The universe demands a fiction of origin; even the most rigorous sciences must invent a zero-point, a celestial coordinate where the clock strikes Nought, pretending that time began there. Poetry, the grander sister, claims to begin in the middle, yet she too is merely counting backward and forward, dividing her units until she arrives *in medias res*. There is no true genesis to be found in retrospect. Whether our prologue unfolds in heaven or on earth, it remains but a fragment of the presupposed fact that anchors our narrative.

Was she beautiful? Or was the secret of her form a dynamic tension rather than static charm? Did the good or the evil genius preside over those beams? Likely the latter; otherwise, why did her presence evoke unrest rather than undisturbed delight? Why did the urge to look again feel like coercion, a demand upon the senses, rather than a willing longing of the soul?

The woman posing these questions in Daniel Deronda's mind was currently engaged in gambling. Not in the open air beneath a southern sun, tossing copper coins against a crumbling wall with rags wrapped around her limbs, but within one of those magnificent resorts that the accumulated enlightenment of ages has prepared for such pleasures at a steep price. The room was a condenser of gilt moldings, dark tones, and chubby nudes, designed to hold the heavy, humid breath of high fashion—a breath rarely available in equal proportions elsewhere, certainly not to those of lesser station.

It was nearly four o'clock on a September day, the atmosphere brewed into a visible haze. Deep stillness reigned, broken only by the light rattle and chink of chips, the sweep of a brush, and the occasional monotone of French spoken by an ingeniously constructed automaton. Two long tables hosted serried crowds, all faces bent toward the felt except for one. A melancholy little boy, clad in epidermis for his legs but dressed in finery for the rest, stood facing the doorway. With the blank gaze of a masquerading advertisement on an itinerant show's platform, he watched the entrance, positioned directly behind a lady deeply engrossed at the roulette wheel.

Around this table, fifty or sixty souls were assembled. In the outer rows, mere spectators occasionally deposited a five-franc note with a simpering air, curious to taste the passion of gambling. Those fully absorbed displayed a distant variety of European types: Livonian, Spanish, Graeco-Italian, and miscellaneous German, alongside English aristocrats and plebeians alike. Here was a striking admission of human equality. The jeweled fingers of an English countess hovered inches from a bony, yellow, crab-like hand stretching a bare wrist to clutch a heap of coin. That hand belonged to a figure with a square, gaunt face, deep-set eyes, grizzled brows, and ill-combed hair—a slight metamorphosis of the vulture. Beside her sat a prematurely aged woman, withered after a short bloom like artificial flowers, holding a shabby velvet reticule and occasionally popping the point of a card into her mouth. Nearby, a respectable London tradesman, blonde and soft-handed, maintained his sleek, parted hair, conscious of circulars addressed to the nobility whose patronage allowed him fashionable holidays. He possessed not the gambler's passion that nullifies appetite, but a well-fed leisure. In the intervals of making and spending money, he sought to replicate the cycle in play, reflecting a belief that Providence approved of his amusement, ready to cease only if the sweetness of winning turned to the sourness of losing.

Standing close to his chair was a handsome Italian, calm and statuesque, reaching across to place the first pile of napoleons from a fresh bag delivered by an envoy with a scrolled mustache. Within half a minute, the pile was pushed to an old bewigged woman with eyeglasses pinching her nose. A slight gleam and a faint, mumbling smile touched her lips, but the Italian remained impassive, secure in an infallible system that placed his foot on the neck of chance, immediately preparing a new stack. So did a man resembling an emaciated beau or worn-out libertine, viewing life through a single eyeglass and holding out his hand tremulously for change. His play was driven not by severity of system, but by dreams of white crows or the superstition that the eighth of the month was lucky, inspiring a fierce yet tottering impulsiveness.

Yet, despite their marked differences, every player wore a uniform negativeness of expression, a mask suggesting they had all consumed a root compelling their brains to the same narrow monotony of action.

Deronda's first thought, as his eyes fell upon this scene of dull, gas-poisoned absorption, was that the gambling of Spanish shepherd-boys seemed more enviable. Rousseau might be justified in claiming that art and science had done mankind a poor service. Suddenly, however, the moment became dramatic. His attention was arrested by a young lady standing at an angle nearby, the last to whom his eyes traveled. She was bending to speak English to a middle-aged lady seated at play beside her, then instantly returning to the game. She revealed the full height of a graceful figure, possessing a face that might possibly be looked at without admiration, but could hardly be passed with indifference.

The inward debate she sparked in Deronda gave his eyes a growing expression of scrutiny, drifting further from the glow of undefined sensibility into pure analysis. At moments, he tracked the movement of her arms and hands as this problematic sylph bent to deposit her stake with an air of firm choice; the next, his gaze returned to her face, steady and unaffected by beholders, directed solely toward the game. The sylph was winning. As her tapering fingers, delicately gloved in pale gray, adjusted the coins pushed toward her to pass them back to the winning point, she looked around with a survey too cold and neutral to lack a trace of art concealing inward exultation.

Then, in the course of that survey, her eyes met Deronda's. Instead of averting them as she would have desired, she was unpleasantly conscious that they were arrested. How long? The darting sense that he was measuring her, looking down on her as an inferior, that he was of a different quality from the human dross surrounding her, that he felt himself in a region outside and above her, examining her as a specimen of a lower order, roused a tingling resentment. It did not flush her cheeks but drained the blood from her lips. Controlling herself through an inward defiance, she showed no other sign of emotion than this pallor before turning back to her play. But Deronda's gaze had acted as an evil eye. Her stake vanished. No matter; she had been winning since she began with a few napoleons and held a considerable reserve. She had begun to believe in her luck; others had begun to believe in it. She envisioned a *cortège* following her, worshipping her as a goddess of fortune, watching her play as a directing augury. Such things had been known of male gamblers; why not a woman? Her friend and chaperon, who had initially opposed her playing, was now approving, offering the prudent advice to stop at the right moment and return to England. Gwendolen had replied that she cared for the excitement, not the winnings. On that supposition, this moment ought to have been the flood-tide of her eager experience. Yet, when her next stake was swept away, she felt the orbits of her eyes grow hot. The certainty of that man still watching her was a pressure beginning to torture. The more reason she should not flinch, but continue playing as if indifferent to loss or gain.

Her friend touched her elbow, proposing they quit. In reply, Gwendolen put ten louis on the same spot. She was in a mood of defiance where the mind loses sight of any end beyond the satisfaction of enraged resistance; the puerile stupidity of a dominant impulse included luck among its objects of defiance. Since she was not winning strikingly, the next best thing was to lose strikingly. She controlled her muscles, showing no tremor of mouth or hands. Each time her stake was swept off, she doubled it. Many were now watching her, but the sole observation she registered was Deronda's, who, though she never looked toward him, she was sure had not moved away.

Such a drama requires no long time to play out; development and catastrophe can often be measured by nothing clumsier than the moment-hand. "Faites votre jeu, mesdames et messieurs," said the automatic voice of destiny from between the mustache and imperial of the croupier. Gwendolen's arm stretched to deposit her last poor heap of napoleons. "Le jeu ne va plus," said destiny. In five seconds, Gwendolen turned from the table, but turned resolutely with her face toward Deronda and looked at him. There was a smile of irony in his eyes as their glances met; yet it was better that he should keep his attention fixed on her than disregard her as one of an insect swarm lacking individual physiognomy. Besides, in spite of his superciliousness and irony, it was difficult to believe he did not admire her spirit as well as her person. He was young, handsome, distinguished in appearance—not one of those ridiculous, dowdy Philistines who thought it incumbent on them to blight the gaming-table with a sour look of protest. The general conviction that we are admirable does not easily yield to a single negative; rather, when members of Vanity's large family find their performance received coldly, they are apt to believe that a little more of it will win over the unaccountable dissident. In Gwendolen's habits of mind, it had been taken for granted that she knew what was admirable

This man contrives a secret 'twixt us two, That he may quell me with his meeting eyes Like one who quells a lioness at bay. These lines haunted Gwendolen as she scanned the crumpled sheet resting on her dressing table, the ink blurring slightly under the harsh glare of the gaslight.

DEAREST CHILD.—I have been expecting to hear from you for a week. In your last you said the Langens thought of leaving Leubronn and going to Baden. How could you be so thoughtless as to leave me in uncertainty about your address? I am in the greatest anxiety lest this should not reach you. In any case, you were to come home at the end of September, and I must now entreat you to return as quickly as possible, for if you spent all your money it would be out of my power to send you any more, and you must not borrow of the Langens, for I could not repay them. This is the sad truth, my child—I wish I could prepare you for it better—but a dreadful calamity has befallen us all. You know nothing about business and will not understand it; but Grapnell & Co. have failed for a million, and we are totally ruined—your aunt Gascoigne as well as I, only that your uncle has his benefice, so that by putting down their carriage and getting interest for the boys, the family can go on. All the property our poor father saved for us goes to pay the liabilities. There is nothing I can call my own. It is better you should know this at once, though it rends my heart to have to tell it you. Of course we cannot help thinking what a pity it was that you went away just when you did. But I shall never reproach you, my dear child; I would save you from all trouble if I could. On your way home you will have time to prepare yourself for the change you will find. We shall perhaps leave Offendene at once, for we hope that Mr. Haynes, who wanted it before, may be ready to take it off my hands. Of course we cannot go to the rectory—there is not a corner there to spare. We must get some hut or other to shelter us, and we must live on your uncle Gascoigne's charity, until I see what else can be done. I shall not be able to pay the debts to the tradesmen besides the servants' wages. Summon up your fortitude, my dear child; we must resign ourselves to God's will. But it is hard to resign one's self to Mr. Lassman's wicked recklessness, which they say was the cause of the failure. Your poor sisters can only cry with me and give me no help. If you were once here, there might be a break in the cloud—I always feel it impossible that you can have been meant for poverty. If the Langens wish to remain abroad, perhaps you can put yourself under some one else's care for the journey. But come as soon as you can to your afflicted and loving mamma, FANNY DAVILOW.

The first effect of this letter on Gwendolen was half-stupefying. The implicit confidence that her destiny must be one of luxurious ease, where any trouble that occurred would be well clad and provided for, had been stronger in her own mind than in her mamma's, being fed there by her youthful blood and that sense of superior claims which made a large part of her consciousness. It was almost as difficult for her to believe suddenly that her position had become one of poverty and of humiliating dependence, as it would have been to get into the strong current of her blooming life the chill sense that her death would really come. She stood motionless for a few minutes, then tossed off her hat and automatically looked in the glass. The coils of her smooth light-brown hair were still in order perfect enough for a ball-room; and as on other nights, Gwendolen might have looked lingeringly at herself for pleasure (surely an allowable indulgence); but now she took no conscious note of her reflected beauty, and simply stared right before her as if she had been jarred by a hateful sound and was waiting for any sign of its cause.

By-and-by she threw herself in the corner of the red velvet sofa, took up the letter again and read it twice deliberately, letting it at last fall on the ground, while she rested her clasped hands on her lap and sat perfectly still, shedding no tears. Her impulse was to survey and resist the situation rather than to wail over it. There was no inward exclamation of "Poor mamma!" Her mamma had never seemed to get much enjoyment out of life, and if Gwendolen had been at this moment disposed to feel pity she would have bestowed it on herself—for was she not naturally and rightfully the chief object of her mamma's anxiety too? But it was anger, it was resistance that possessed her; it was bitter vexation that she had lost her gains at roulette, whereas if her luck had continued through this one day she would have had a handsome sum to carry home, or she might have gone on playing and won enough to support them all. Even now was it not possible? She had only four napoleons left in her purse, but she possessed some ornaments which she could sell: a practice so common in stylish society at German baths that there was no need to be ashamed of it; and even if she had not received her mamma's letter, she would probably have decided to get money for an Etruscan necklace which she happened not to have been wearing since her arrival; nay, she might have done so with an agreeable sense that she was living with some intensity and escaping humdrum. With ten louis at her disposal and a return of her former luck, which seemed probable, what could she do better than go on playing for a few days? If her friends at home disapproved of the way in which she got the money, as they certainly would, still the money would be there.

Gwendolen's imagination dwelt on this course and created agreeable consequences, but not with unbroken confidence and rising certainty as it would have done if she had been touched with the gambler's mania. She had gone to the roulette-table not because of passion, but in search of it: her mind was still sanely capable of picturing balanced probabilities, and while the chance of winning allured her, the chance of losing thrust itself on her with alternate strength and made a vision from which her pride sank sensitively. For she was resolved not to tell the Langens that any misfortune had befallen her family, or to make herself in any way indebted to their compassion; and if she were to part with her jewelry to any observable extent, they would interfere by inquiries and remonstrances. The course that held the least risk of intolerable annoyance was to raise money on her necklace early in the morning, tell the Langens that her mother desired her immediate return without giving a reason, and take the train for Brussels that evening. She had no maid with her, and the Langens might make difficulties about her returning home, but her will was peremptory.

Instead of going to bed she made as brilliant a light as she could and began to pack, working diligently, though all the while visited by the scenes that might take place on the coming day—now by the tiresome explanations and farewells, and the whirling journey toward a changed home, now by the alternative of staying just another day and standing again at the roulette-table. But always in this latter scene there was the presence of that Deronda, watching her with exasperating irony, and—the two keen experiences were inevitably revived together—beholding her again forsaken by luck. This importunate image certainly helped to sway her resolve on the side of immediate departure, and to urge her packing to the point which would make a change of mind inconvenient.

It had struck twelve when she came into her room, and by the time she was assuring herself that she had left out only what was necessary, the faint dawn was stealing through the white blinds and dulling her candles. What was the use of going to bed? Her cold bath was refreshment enough, and she saw that a slight trace of fatigue about the eyes only made her look the more interesting. Before six o'clock she was completely equipped in her gray traveling dress even to her felt hat, for she meant to walk out as soon as she could count on seeing other ladies on their way to the springs. And happening to be seated sideways before the long strip of mirror between her two windows she turned to look at herself, leaning her elbow on the back of the chair in an attitude that might have been chosen for her portrait. It is possible to have a strong self-love without any self-satisfaction, rather with a self-discontent which is the more intense because one's own little core of egoistic sensibility is a supreme care; but Gwendolen knew nothing of such inward strife. She had a naïve delight in her fortunate self, which any but the harshest saintliness will have some indulgence for in a girl who had every day seen a pleasant reflection of that self in her friends' flattery as well as in the looking-glass. And even in this beginning of troubles, while for lack of anything else to do she sat gazing at her image in the growing light, her face gathered a complacency gradual as the cheerfulness of the morning. Her beautiful lips curled into a more and more decided smile, till at last she took off her hat, leaned forward and kissed the cold glass which had looked so warm. How could she believe in sorrow? If it attacked her, she felt the force to crush it, to defy it, or run away from it, as she had done already. Anything seemed more possible than that she could go on bearing miseries

"Let no flower of the spring pass by us; let us crown ourselves with rosebuds before they be withered." —Book of Wisdom.

What a pity that Offendene was not the soil of Miss Gwendolen Harleth's childhood, nor a place sanctified by the roots of family memory! For a human life requires such anchorage—a spot in the native land where the love of tender kinship takes hold of the very face of the earth, of the labors men undertake, of the haunting sounds and accents that define existence. It is a place where the definiteness of early memories becomes inwoven with affection, where acquaintance with neighbors, even to the dogs and donkeys, spreads not through sentimental effort but as a sweet, instinctive habit of the blood. At five years old, mortals are ill-equipped to be citizens of the world, to be stirred by abstract nouns, or to soar above preference into impartiality; the prejudice in favor of milk with which we blindly begin is merely a type of the way body and soul must be nourished, at least for a time. The best introduction to astronomy is to conceive of the nightly heavens as a little lot of stars belonging exclusively to one's own homestead.

Yet this blessed persistence, in which affection can truly take root, had been absent from Gwendolen's life. It was only a year prior to her recall from Leubronn that Offendene was selected as her mother's home, chosen simply for its proximity to Pennicote Rectory. Thus, Mrs. Davilow, Gwendolen, and her four half-sisters—followed by the governess and the maid in a separate vehicle—were driven along the avenue for the first time on a late October afternoon. Above them, rooks crowed loudly, and yellow elm-leaves whirled in the wind.

The season suited the aspect of the old oblong red-brick house, a structure rather too anxiously ornamented with stone at every line, not excluding the double row of narrow windows and the large square portico. The stone encouraged a greenish lichen, the brick a powdery gray, so that though the building was rigidly rectangular, there was no harshness in the physiognomy it presented to the three avenues cutting east, west, and south through the hundred yards' breadth of old plantation encircling the immediate grounds. One would have preferred the house to stand on a knoll, lifted above its own little domain to gaze upon the long thatched roofs of distant villages, the church towers, the scattered homesteads, and the gradual rise of surging woods, alongside the green breadths of undulating park that formed the beautiful face of the earth in that part of Wessex. But standing thus behind a screen amid flat pastures, it possessed on one side a glimpse of the wider world in the lofty curves of the chalk downs, grand steadfast forms played over by the changing days.

The house was barely large enough to be called a mansion, moderately rented, lacking a manor attachment, and proving difficult to let due to its sombre furniture and faded upholstery. Yet, inside and out, it was something no beholder could suppose to be inhabited by retired trades-people—a certainty worth many conveniences to tenants who possessed the taste that shrinks from new finery and reside in that border-territory of rank where annexation is a burning topic. To take up residence in a house that had once sufficed for dowager countesses imparted a perceptible tinge to Mrs. Davilow's satisfaction in finally possessing an establishment of her own. This possibility appeared suddenly, and mysteriously to Gwendolen, upon the death of her stepfather, Captain Davilow, who for the last nine years had joined his family only in brief, fitful bursts, enough to reconcile them to his long absences. But Gwendolen cared much more for the fact than for the explanation. All her prospects had become more agreeable in consequence. She had disliked their former way of life, roving from one foreign watering-place or Parisian apartment to another, always feeling new antipathies to new suites of hired furniture and meeting new people under conditions that rendered her appearance of little importance. The variation of having spent two years at a showy school, where, on all occasions of display, she had been placed foremost, had only deepened her conviction that so exceptional a person as herself could hardly remain in ordinary circumstances or in a social position less than advantageous. Any fear of this latter evil was banished now that her mother was to have an establishment; for regarding the point of birth, Gwendolen was quite easy. She had no notion how her maternal grandfather acquired the fortune inherited by his two daughters, but he had been a West Indian—which seemed to exclude further question; and she knew that her father's family was so high as to take no notice of her mother, who nevertheless preserved with much pride the miniature of a Lady Molly in that connection. She would probably have known much more about her father but for a little incident that happened when she was twelve years old. Mrs. Davilow had brought out, as she did only at wide intervals, various memorials of her first husband, and while showing his miniature to Gwendolen, recalled with a fervor that seemed to count on a peculiar filial sympathy, the fact that dear papa had died when his little daughter was in long clothes. Gwendolen, immediately thinking of the unlovable stepfather whom she had been acquainted with the greater part of her life while her frocks were short, said,

"Why did you marry again, mamma? It would have been nicer if you had not."

Mrs. Davilow colored deeply, a slight convulsive movement passing over her face, and straightway shutting up the memorials, she said, with a violence quite unusual in her,

"You have no feeling, child!"

Gwendolen, who was fond of her mother, felt hurt and ashamed, and had never since dared to ask a question about her father.

This was not the only instance in which she had brought upon herself the pain of some filial compunction. It was always arranged, when possible, that she should have a small bed in her mother's room; for Mrs. Davilow's motherly tenderness clung chiefly to her eldest girl, who had been born in her happier time. One night, under an attack of pain, she found that the specific regularly placed by her bedside had been forgotten, and begged Gwendolen to get out of bed and reach it for her. That healthy young lady, snug and warm as a rosy infant in her little couch, objected to stepping out into the cold, and lying perfectly still, grumbled a refusal. Mrs. Davilow went without the medicine and never reproached her daughter; but the next day Gwendolen was keenly conscious of what must be in her mother's mind, and tried to make amends by caresses which cost her no effort. Having always been the pet and pride of the household, waited on by mother, sisters, governess, and maids as if she had been a princess in exile, she naturally found it difficult to think her own pleasure less important than others made it, and when it was positively thwarted, felt an astonished resentment apt, in her cruder days, to vent itself in one of those passionate acts which look like a contradiction of habitual tendencies. Though never even as a child thoughtlessly cruel, nay, delighting to rescue drowning insects and watch their recovery, there remained a disagreeable silent remembrance of her having strangled her sister's canary-bird in a final fit of exasperation at its shrill singing, which had again and again jarringly interrupted her own. She had taken pains to buy a white mouse for her sister in retribution, and though inwardly excusing herself on the ground of a peculiar sensitiveness which was a mark of her general superiority, the thought of that infamous murder had always made her wince. Gwendolen's nature was not remorseless, but she liked to make her penances easy, and now that she was twenty and more, some of her native force had turned into a self-control by which she guarded herself from penitential humiliation. There was more show of fire and will in her than ever, but there was more calculation underneath it.

On this day of arrival at Offendene, a place not even Mrs. Davilow had seen before—the location having been secured for her by her brother-in-law, Mr. Gascoigne—when all had descended from the carriage and were standing under the porch in front of the open door, so that they could have a general view of the place and a glimpse of the stone hall and staircase hung with sombre pictures, but enlivened by a bright wood fire, no one spoke. Mamma, the four sisters, and the governess all looked at Gwendolen, as if their feelings depended entirely on her decision. Of the girls, from Alice in her sixteenth year to Isabel in her tenth, hardly anything could be said on a first view, save that they were girlish, and that their black dresses were getting shabby. Miss Merry was elderly and altogether neutral in expression. Mrs. Davilow's worn beauty seemed the more pathetic for the look of entire appeal which she cast at Gwendolen, who was glancing round at the house, the landscape, and the entrance hall with an air of rapid judgment. Imagine a young racehorse in the paddock among untrimmed ponies and patient hacks.

"Well, dear, what do you think of the place?" said Mrs. Davilow at last, in a gentle, deprecatory tone.

"I think it is charming," said Gwendolen, quickly. "A romantic place; anything delightful may happen in it; it would be a good

The rector of Pennicote, surveying the landscape of his niece's future with the detached optimism of a man accustomed to viewing life from every angle, found it difficult to fault his own vision. To see Gwendolen as a candidate for a brilliant marriage was not a flaw of character but a reflection of the era; why should he, a man of sound intentions, wish her a fate inferior to the consensus of his contemporaries? His approval stemmed from a genuinely good-natured desire for her welfare, devoid of the grotesque fantasies that plagued lesser minds. He did not calculate that she required a gown as tattered as Griselda's to lure a marquis, nor did he scheme to break her leg on a spirited horse so a wealthy savior might swoop in. His strategy was purely rational: ensure she presented herself to the neighborhood's elite in the best possible light.

Yet, this external orchestration mirrored an internal truth Gwendolen already possessed. While she accepted the inevitability of marriage and rejected the notion of settling for a mediocre match, she viewed matrimony not as the crown of her ambition but as a necessary, albeit tedious, constraint. To her, the domestic sphere promised a life where agency was surrendered to the whims of husbandry and child-rearing, a dreary immersion in the mundane that stifled the spirit. Marriage was social promotion, yes, but promotions often demanded bitter herbs; a title could not substitute for the genuine act of leading. Gwendolen, a delicate sylph of twenty, intended to lead. Though her passions were confined strictly to the realm of feminine aesthetics and lacked any grandiose intent to reshape the constitution or advance learning, she harbored a fierce desire to command attention.

"Gwendolen will not rest until the world lies at her feet," declared Miss Merry, the governess, using hyperbole that had long since softened to denote mere popularity within a genteel suburb. Yet, for Gwendolen, perched high on the precipice of youthful exultation, these words suggested a vast, hazy horizon. Unlike others who drifted like empty vessels at the mercy of circumstance, she resolved to seize control. Settled soon at Offendene, surrounded by the notices of Lady Brackenshaw and the invitations of the Arrowpoints, her prospects might seem limited to the local aristocracy, but her confidence rested squarely on her own perceived capabilities. She believed herself educated beyond reproach, armed with the rigid starch of schoolroom rules and the expansive wisdom gleaned from novels and plays. Her French and music were flawless, bolstered by an innate, spontaneous certainty that she could judge anything she touched.

While she dismissed the majority of worldly subjects as inherently stupid—much as the elderly find light dull—she knew she would navigate any conversation with ease. No one had ever disputed her superiority. From her arrival at Offendene onward, she was treated as a sovereign in exile. Whether it was the creaking boots of a footman, the unsatisfactory laundry, or the smoking fireplace, the household operated on a principle of anticipatory apology directed solely at her. When she entered the room, radiant with freshly brushed hair and eyes like polished onyx, the atmosphere shifted; she alone bore the burden of tolerance, gently correcting the posture of servants and dismissing the intrusive curiosity of peers.

How was this domestic empire achieved? The answer seemed obvious: her beauty, her unusual presence, and the decisive will that animated her graceful movements. Even in moments of collective lethargy, her entrance provided a sufficient reason to maintain the forms of civilized life, prompting waiters to scrub crumbs with renewed vigor. Furthermore, as the eldest daughter of a mother perpetually contrite for the hardships inflicted by a stepfather, she commanded a protective, servile devotion.

However, caution dictates that one does not accept surface explanations without scrutiny. One recalls seeing similar assiduous service rendered to those neither beautiful nor unusual, whose firmness was less graceful but equally effective. These individuals shared a singular trait: a relentless determination to secure pleasure, coupled with a terrifying fearlessness in becoming disagreeable or dangerous when denied. Consider the unscrupulous male, capable of wreaking havoc elsewhere if unchecked at home, who finds households trembling before him. Thus, even stripped of her charm and privileged status, Gwendolen might have ruled as a queen in exile, driven by an inborn energy of egoistic desire and the power to inspire dread regarding her potential actions.

Nevertheless, she possessed the charm, and those who feared her were simultaneously drawn to her. This dynamic was amplified by the iridescent complexity of her character—a play of contradictory tendencies that defied the rigid logic of action. Just as Macbeth lamented the impossibility of being many things at once, referring only to clumsy physical necessities, the subtler realms of feeling allow for paradox. A moment is wide enough to harbor both loyalty and baseness, to contain the flash of a murderous thought followed immediately by the sharp recoil of repentance. In Gwendolen, these opposing forces danced together, creating a figure who was both adored and formidable, destined to navigate the intricate tapestry of her own making.

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Gwendolen Harleth, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of London itself. She sat at her mahogany desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from the neighborhood, detailing her triumphant reception. From Brackenshaw Castle to the Firs at Wanchester, she was welcomed with manifest admiration. Even those ladies who did not quite like her felt a comfort in having a new, striking girl to invite; for hostesses who entertain much must make up their parties as ministers make up their cabinets, on grounds other than personal liking. Then, in order to have Gwendolen as a guest, it was not necessary to ask anyone who was disagreeable, for Mrs. Davilow always made a quiet, picturesque figure as a chaperon, and Mr. Gascoigne was everywhere in request for his own sake.

Among the houses where Gwendolen was not quite liked, and yet invited, was Quetcham Hall. One of her first invitations was to a large dinner-party there, which made a sort of general introduction for her to the society of the neighborhood; for in a select party of thirty and of well-composed proportions as to age, few visitable families could be entirely left out. No youthful figure there was comparable to Gwendolen's as she passed through the long suite of rooms adorned with light and flowers, and, visible at first as a slim figure floating along in white drapery, approached through one wide doorway after another into fuller illumination and definiteness. She had never had that sort of promenade before, and she felt exultingly that it befitted her: anyone looking at her for the first time might have supposed that long galleries and lackeys had always been a matter of course in her life; while her cousin Anna, who was really more familiar with these things, felt almost as much embarrassed as a rabbit suddenly deposited in that well-lit space.

"She is that with Gascoigne?" said the Archdeacon, neglecting a discussion of military maneuvers on which, as a clergyman, he was naturally appealed to. And his son, on the other side of the room—a hopeful young scholar, who had already suggested some "not less elegant than ingenious" emendations of Greek texts—said nearly at the same time, "By George! Who is that girl with the awfully well-set head and jolly figure?"

But to a mind of general benevolence, wishing everyone to look well, it was rather exasperating to see how Gwendolen eclipsed others: how even the handsome Miss Lawe, explained to be the daughter of Lady Lawe, looked suddenly broad, heavy, and inanimate; and how Miss Arrowpoint, unfortunately also dressed in white, immediately resembled a *carte-de-visite* in which one would fancy the skirt alone to have been charged for. Since Miss Arrowpoint was generally liked for the amiable unpretending way in which she wore her fortunes, and made a softening screen for the oddities of her mother, there seemed to be some unfitness in Gwendolen's looking so much more like a person of social importance.

"She is not really so handsome if you come to examine her features," said Mrs. Arrowpoint, later in the evening, confidentially to Mrs. Vulcany. "It is a certain style she has, which produces a great effect at first, but afterward she is less agreeable."

In fact, Gwendolen, not intending it, but intending the contrary, had offended her hostess, who, though not a splenetic or vindictive woman, had her susceptibilities. Several conditions had met in the Lady of Quetcham which to the reasoners in that neighborhood seemed to have an essential connection with each other. It was occasionally recalled that she had been the heiress of a fortune gained by some moist or dry business in the city, in order fully to account for her having a squat figure, a harsh parrot-like voice, and a systematically high head-dress; and since these points made her externally rather ridiculous, it appeared to many only natural that she should have what are called literary tendencies. A little comparison would have shown that all these points are to be found apart; daughters of aldermen being often well-grown and well-featured, pretty women having sometimes harsh or husky voices, and the production of feeble literature being found compatible with the most diverse forms of *physique*, masculine as well as feminine.

Gwendolen, who had a keen sense of absurdity in others, but was kindly disposed toward anyone who could make life agreeable to her, meant to win Mrs. Arrowpoint by giving her an interest and attention beyond what others were probably inclined to show. But self-confidence is apt to address itself to an imaginary dullness in others; as people who are well off speak in a cajoling tone to the poor, and those who are in the prime of life raise their voice and talk artificially to seniors, hastily conceiving them to be deaf and rather imbecile. Gwendolen, with all her cleverness and purpose to be agreeable, could not escape that form of stupidity: it followed in her mind, unreflectingly, that because Mrs. Arrowpoint was ridiculous she was also likely to be wanting in penetration, and she went through her little scenes without suspicion that the various shades of her behavior were all noted.

"You are fond of books as well as of music, riding, and archery, I hear," Mrs. Arrowpoint said, going to her for a *tête-à-tête* in the drawing-room after dinner. "Catherine will be very glad to have so sympathetic a neighbor." This little speech might have seemed the most graceful politeness, spoken in a low, melodious tone; but with a twang, fatally loud, it gave Gwendolen a sense of exercising patronage when she answered, gracefully:

"It is I who am fortunate. Miss Arrowpoint will teach me what good music is. I shall be entirely a learner. I hear that she is a thorough musician."

"Catherine has certainly had every advantage. We have a first-rate musician in the house now—Herr Klesmer; perhaps you know all his compositions. You must allow me to introduce him to you. You sing, I believe. Catherine plays three instruments, but she does not sing. I hope you will let us hear you. I understand you are an accomplished singer."

"Oh, no!—'die Kraft ist schwach, allein die Lust ist gross,' as Mephistopheles says."

"Ah, you are a student of Goethe. Young ladies are so advanced now. I suppose you have read everything."

"No, really. I shall be so glad if you will tell me what to read. I have been looking into all the books in the library at Offendene, but there is nothing readable. The leaves all stick together and smell musty. I wish I could write books to amuse myself, as you can! How delightful it must be to write books after one's own taste instead of reading other people's! Home-made books must be so nice."

For an instant Mrs. Arrowpoint's glance was a little sharper, but the perilous resemblance to satire in the last sentence took the hue of girlish simplicity when Gwendolen added,

"I would give anything to write a book!"

"And why should you not?" said Mrs. Arrowpoint, encouragingly. "You have but to begin as I did. Pen, ink, and paper are at everybody's command. But I will send you all I have written with pleasure."

"Thanks. I shall be so glad to read your writings. Being acquainted with authors must give a peculiar understanding of their books: one would be able to tell then which parts were funny and which serious. I am sure I often laugh in the wrong place." Here Gwendolen herself became aware of danger, and added quickly, "In Shakespeare, you know, and other great writers that we can never see. But I always want to know more than there is in the books."

"If you are interested in any of my subjects I can lend you many extra sheets in manuscript," said Mrs. Arrowpoint—while Gwendolen felt herself painfully in the position of the young lady who professed to like potted sprats.

"These are things I dare say I shall publish eventually: several friends have urged me to do so, and one doesn't like to be obstinate. My Tasso, for example—I could have made it twice the size."

"I dote on Tasso," said Gwendolen.

"Well, you shall have all my papers, if you like. So many, you know, have written about Tasso; but they are all wrong. As to the particular nature of his madness, and his feelings for Leonora, and the real cause of his imprisonment, and the character of Leonora, who, in my opinion, was a cold-hearted woman, else she would have married him in spite of her brother—they are all wrong. I differ from everybody."

"How very interesting!" said Gwendolen. "I like to differ from everybody. I think it is so stupid to agree. That is the worst of writing your opinions; you make people agree with you." This speech renewed a slight suspicion in Mrs. Arrowpoint, and again her glance became for a moment examining. But Gwendolen looked very innocent, and continued with a docile air:

"I know nothing of Tasso except the *Gerusalemme Liberata*, which we read and learned by heart at school."

"Ah, his life is more interesting than his poetry, I

The sting of Fontenelle's critique—that one cannot claim humility for having learned the earth revolves around the sun—lingered in Gwendolen's mind, transforming into a fresh, peculiar ache. She refused to acknowledge the misfortune of lacking Miss Arrowpoint's musical tutelage, a deficit that robbed her of the authority to judge Herr Klesmer's taste with the confidence of true expertise. Even more intolerable was the admission, forbidden even to her own conscience, that every encounter with Miss Arrowpoint ignited a sudden, unwonted spark of jealousy within her. It was not the disparity of wealth that fueled this fire; rather, it was the infuriating precision of a girl whose physical presence—one might describe as slight, of medium height, with small features, tolerable eyes, and a sallow complexion—nevertheless radiated a mental superiority that defied explanation. Miss Arrowpoint possessed an exasperating thoroughness in her musical command and a fastidious discernment in her general tastes that rendered her admiration impossible to force, keeping others in a perpetual state of awestruck deference. This seemingly insignificant twenty-four-year-old, whom any observer would casually overlook were it not for her title, harbored a secret conviction that Miss Harleth's accomplishments belonged to the realm of the common. Such a thought was unbearable, especially when cloaked beneath Miss Arrowpoint's impenetrable veneer of perfect kindness.

Gwendolen shunned dwelling on facts that cast such an unfavorable shadow upon her own reflection. The Musical Magus, who had so abruptly expanded her horizons, was not a constant presence; his frequent shuttling between London and Quetcham soon appeared to offer convenient openings for converting him to a more admiring disposition. In the meantime, the evident pleasure her singing elicited at Brackenshaw Castle, the Firs, and elsewhere restored her equilibrium. She inclined toward believing approval more trustworthy than objection, certainly not among those rare souls who suffer from a parching thirst for a perfection their neighbors do not demand. Perhaps it would have been presumptuous to claim she was exceptionally unique in her interiority, beyond her rare grace of movement and bearing, and a certain daring that lent piquancy to a very common egoistic ambition—one that thrives beneath clumsy exteriors and remains unnoticed. After all, the tilt of the head does not truly dictate the inner self's hunger for supremacy; it merely alters the method by which that supremacy is pursued and the degree to which it can be achieved. This dynamic holds particularly true for a girl, whose passion for achieving the remarkable finds its ideal limit in consistency with the highest breeding and a complete freedom from the sordid necessity of income. Gwendolen was inwardly rebellious against the restraints of family conditions, ready to peer through obligations to her own fundamental lack of feeling for them, as if sustained by the boldest speculations. Yet, she possessed no such speculations whatsoever. She would instantly distinguish herself from any theoretically or practically reforming woman by satirizing them. She rejoiced in feeling exceptional, yet her horizon remained confined to the genteel romance wherein the heroine's soul pours out in journals filled with vague power, originality, and general rebellion, while her actual life moves strictly within the sphere of fashion. Should she wander into a swamp, the pathos lies partly, so to speak, in her wearing satin shoes. Nature and society provide this restraint on the pursuit of striking adventure; thus, a soul burning with a sense of what the universe is not, ready to consume all existence as fuel, remains captive to the ordinary wirework of social forms, accomplishing nothing particular.

This commonplace result was the threat Gwendolen sensed even amidst the novelty of the first winter at Offendene. She was clear that she did not wish to lead the same life as ordinary young ladies, yet she remained unclear on how to begin leading any other, or what specific acts she would assert her freedom through. Offendene remained a suitable backdrop if anything were to occur there, but overall, the neighborhood fell short. Beyond the initial impact of her beauty at a first presentation, there was little excitement to be gleaned from her earliest invitations. Returning home after brief sallies of satire and knowingness—which had previously offended Mrs. Arrowpoint—she filled the intervening days with the most girlish devices. Her strongest assertion of individual claim was to omit Alice's lessons, operating on the principle that Alice was more likely to excel in ignorance, and instead employ her with Miss Merry and the maid rumored to attend to all the ladies. Together, they arranged various dramatic costumes which Gwendolen delighted in preparing for future occasions of acting in charades or theatrical pieces, occasions she intended to summon forth by sheer force of will or contrivance. Though she had never acted professionally—having only posed in *tableaux vivants* at school—she felt assured of her ability to perform well. Having visited the Théâtre Français once or twice and listened to her mother speak of Rachel, her waking dreams and cogitations regarding her destiny often turned on the question of whether she should become an actress like Rachel, given that she was more beautiful than that thin Jewess.

The wet days preceding Christmas were spent pleasantly in the preparation of Greek, Oriental, and composite costumes. Gwendolen attitudined and declaimed before a domestic audience, which included even the housekeeper, who was once pressed into service to swell the notes of applause. However, having demonstrated her unworthiness by observing that Miss Harleth looked far more regal in her own attire than in that baggy garment exposing her arms, she was not invited to participate a second time.

"Do I look as well as Rachel, mamma?" Gwendolen asked one day, displaying herself in her Greek dress to Anna and enacting fragments of scenes with much tragic intent.

"You possess better arms than Rachel," Mrs. Davilow replied. "Your arms would suffice for anything, Gwen. But your voice lacks her tragedy; it is not so deep."

"I can deepen it, if I choose," Gwendolen said provisionally, then added with decision, "I believe a higher voice is more tragic. It is more feminine. And the more feminine a woman is, the more tragic she appears when performing desperate actions."

"There may be truth in that," Mrs. Davilow said languidly. "But I do not understand the utility of making one's blood creep. And if there is anything horrible to be done, I should prefer it left to the men."

"Oh, mamma, you are dreadfully prosaic!" Gwendolen exclaimed. "As if all the great poetic criminals were not women! I consider the men poor, cautious creatures."

"Well, dear, and you—who are afraid to be alone in the night—I do not think you would be very bold in crime, thank God."

"I am not speaking of reality, mamma," Gwendolen said impatiently. Once her mother was called out of the room, she turned quickly to her cousin, seizing the opportunity. "Anna, please ask my uncle to organize some charades at the rectory. Mr. Middleton and Warham could act with us—simply for practice. Mamma insists it is improper for Mr. Middleton to consult and rehearse here. He is a stick, but we could assign him suitable parts. Please ask, or else I will."

"Oh, not until Rex arrives. He is so clever, and such a dear old thing, and he will act as Napoleon surveying the sea. He looks exactly like Napoleon. Rex can do anything."

"I do not in the least believe in your Rex, Anna," Gwendolen laughed at her. "He will turn out to be like those wretched blue and yellow watercolors of his which you hang in your bedroom and worship."

"Very well, you will see," Anna retorted. "It is not that I know what constitutes cleverness, but he has secured a scholarship already, and Papa says he will obtain a fellowship. Nobody is better at games. He is cleverer than Mr. Middleton, and everyone but you considers Mr. Middleton clever."

"So he may be clever in a dark-lantern sort of way. But he _is_ a stick. If he were forced to say, 'Perdition catch my soul, but I do love her,' he would utter it in precisely the same tone as, 'Here endeth the second lesson.'"

"Oh, Gwendolen!" Anna cried, shocked by these promiscuous allusions. "And it is very unkind of you to speak so of him, for he admires you greatly. I heard Warham tell Mama one day, 'Middleton is regularly spooney upon Gwendolen.' She was furious with him; but I know what it means. It is the phrase used at college for being in love."

"How can I help it?" Gwendolen said, rather contemptuously. "Perdition catch my soul if I love _him_."

"No, of course; Papa, I think, would not wish it. And he is departing soon. But it saddens me when you ridicule him."

"What will you do to me when I ridicule Rex?" Gwendolen asked wickedly.

"Now, Gwendolen, dear, you _will not_?" Anna said, her eyes filling with tears. "I could not bear it. But there is truly nothing in him to ridicule. Only you may discover things. For no one ever thought of laughing at Mr. Middleton before you. Everyone said he was good-looking, with perfect manners. I am sure I have always been intimidated by him because of his learning, his square-cut coat, his status as the Bishop's nephew, and all that. But you will not ridicule Rex—promise me." Anna concluded with a beseeching look that touched Gwendolen.

"You are a dear little coz," she said, gently touching the tip

The first breath of the unimagined snowstorm hung in the air like a translucent veil, setting off the azure clarity of the sky. Inside the house, Anna stood in the shadow of Rex's silence. For the first time, the boy she loved had withheld his innermost thoughts, assuming she already knew the weight of his affection, while simultaneously rendering her own confessions impossible. A strange, heavy ache settled in her chest—not merely the pain of seeing him look elsewhere, but a terrifying realization that Gwendolen, her cousin whom she revered with simple, dutiful loyalty, viewed Rex with indifference. To Anna, Gwendolen was a magnificent creature, a wondrous animal of beauty whose nature remained a locked mystery; she feared that this creature might one day devour the small, cherished pets of her own heart. Now, Anna's spirit sank beneath the crushing conviction she dared not voice: that Gwendolen would never return Rex's love. Where Anna found tenderness, Gwendolen found only boredom; where Anna saw a soul to cherish, Gwendolen saw a project to dismiss. Worse still, Gwendolen was forever chasing the extraordinary, while poor Rex, terrified of his father's wrath and deemed too young for such passions by Anna's own reckoning, remained trapped in the role of a future housekeeper. In the prospect of his inevitable suffering, Anna found herself developing a fierce distaste for her cousin's captivating arrogance.

Time seemed to stretch and compress, filled with a tumultuous life visible to all but understood by none. If asked, Rex would claim he harbored no secrets regarding an impending engagement he intended to announce to his father immediately. Yet, for the first time, he was guarded—not just about his feelings, but about his very movements, a shift that struck Anna as profoundly significant. Meanwhile, the elders moved through their days oblivious to the pantomime unfolding around them. They watched the young people with the detached curiosity one reserves for observing lively ants, missing the dramatic tension that played out clearly only in the minds of those living it.

On a gray morning, as her father departed for the sessions with Mrs. Gascoigne, Anna caught sight of Rex donning his antigropelos, the closest approximation to hunting gear he owned.

"Where are you going, Rex?" she asked.

"To see the hounds throw off at the Three Barns," he replied.

"Are you going to take Gwendolen?" she whispered, her voice trembling.

"She told you, did she?"

"No, but I thought... Does papa know?"

"Not that I'm aware of. I shouldn't suppose he'd trouble himself."

"You're going to use his horse?"

"He knows I do that whenever I can."

"Don't let Gwendolen ride after the hounds, Rex," Anna pleaded, her fears granting her a sudden, piercing insight.

"Why not?" Rex smiled, the expression tinged with provocation.

"Papa and Mamma and Aunt Davilow all wish her not to. They think it isn't right for her."

"Why should you suppose she intends to do what is not right?"

"Gwendolen minds nobody sometimes," Anna retorted, emboldened by a flash of anger.

"Then she wouldn't mind me," Rex said, turning her anxiety into a perverse jest.

"Oh, Rex, I cannot bear it. You will make yourself very unhappy." The dam broke, and Anna burst into tears.

"Nannie, Nannie, what on earth is the matter with you?" Rex exclaimed, impatient at being detained, hat in hand and whip poised.

"She will not care for you one bit—I know she never will!" the poor child sobbed, losing all control.

Rex flushed crimson and hurried from the hall, leaving Anna alone with the misery of having spoken in vain. As he rode away, he replayed her words, finding them unwelcome, akin to a bad prophecy even when laughed at. Yet, he quickly rationalized them as mere expressions of little Anna's tender protectiveness, regretting only that he could not soothe her before departing. However, every other emotion on the subject rapidly dissolved into a stubborn resistance to her fears, fueled by a new determination to prove her wrong. This certainty carried just enough kinship to doubt and unease to hasten a confession that safety might have delayed.

When Rex reached the gate, Gwendolen was already mounted, pacing the avenue. Anticipating his delay, she had prepared the groom to wait behind her, unwilling to linger beyond reason. But now, the groom was dismissed, and the pair rode away in a blaze of delightful freedom. Gwendolen was in high spirits, appearing more lovely than Rex had ever seen her; her figure, long white throat, and the curve of her cheek were framed perfectly by the compact simplicity of her riding dress. To Rex, a youthful lover, she seemed the embodiment of the good, the true, and the beautiful—a fundamental identity already manifest in the object of his adoration. Most observers would surely find it equally plausible that a girl should hold such reverent impressions of Rex, for in his handsome face lay nothing to counteract the indefinable, stinging quality—a trace of demon ancestry—that caused some to hesitate in their admiration of Gwendolen.

It was an exquisite January morning, devoid of rain, with a gray sky providing the calmest backdrop for a mild winter scene. The grassy borders of the lanes, hedgerows dotted with red berries and filled with low twitterings, the purple bareness of the elms, and the rich brown of the furrows created a tapestry of peace. The horses' hooves chimed musically, echoing their young voices. Gwendolen laughed at his equipment, noting how he was the reverse of a dandy, and he reveled in her laughter. The freshness of the morning mingled with the freshness of their youth; every sound from their clear throats, every glance exchanged, bubbled forth from a spring of pure joy. It was all morning to them, inside and out. One is tempted, looking back on such moments, to indulge in that futile wishing: *if only things could have been slightly otherwise, so greatly otherwise afterwards*—if only these two beautiful young creatures could have pledged themselves then and there, never to swerve from that vow throughout their lives. Some of the goodness Rex believed in was indeed present. Goodness is a vast, often prospective word, like harvest. At one stage, it lies buried underground, an indeterminate future; is the germ prospering in the darkness? At another, it pushes forth delicate green blades, and soon the trembling blossoms stand ready to be shattered by an hour of rough wind or rain. Each stage carries its peculiar blight, vulnerable to the choking action of the foul soil that rears it or the damage brought from distant corruption.

"Anna had got it into her head that you wanted to ride after the hounds this morning," Rex said, his secret association with Anna's earlier words making the topic feel perilously close to the most momentous of subjects.

"Did she?" Gwendolen laughed. "What a little clairvoyant she is!"

"Shall you?" Rex asked. He hadn't believed she would actually do it if the elders objected, but he trusted she had good reasons.

"I don't know. I can't tell what I'll do until I get there. Clairvoyants are often wrong; they foresee what is likely. I'm not fond of what is likely; it's always dull. I do what is unlikely."

"There, you've told me a secret," Rex remarked. "Once I knew what people generally would likely do, I'd know you'd do the opposite. So you'd come round to a likelihood of your own sort. I'll be able to calculate on you. You couldn't surprise me."

"Yes, I could. I'd turn round and do what was likely for people in general," Gwendolen replied with a musical laugh.

"You see, you can't escape some sort of likelihood. And contradictoriness makes the strongest likelihood of all. You must give up a plan."

"No, I won't. My plan is to do what pleases me." (Should any young lady wish to emulate Gwendolen, let her observe the set of her head and neck: had the angle been different, the chin more protrusive, the cervical vertebrae slightly more curved, ten to one Gwendolen's words would have jarred the sweet-natured Rex. But everything odd in her speech was merely humor and pretty banter, which he eagerly sought to steer toward a single point.)

"Can you manage to feel only what pleases you?" he asked.

"Of course not; that comes from what other people do. But if the world were pleasanter, one would only feel what was pleasant. Girls' lives are so stupid; they never do what they like."

"I thought that was more the case of men. They're forced to do hard things, often dreadfully bored, and knocked to pieces too. And then, if we love a girl very dearly, we want to do as she likes, so after all, you have your own way."

"I don't believe it. I never saw a married woman who had her own way."

"What should you like to do?" Rex asked, quite guileless and genuinely anxious.

"Oh, I don't know! Go to the North Pole, or ride steeple-chases, or go to be a queen in the East like Lady Hester Stanhope," Gwendolen said flightily. Her words sprang from her lips, though she would have been at a loss to provide an answer of deeper origin.

"You don't mean you'd never be married?"

"No; I didn't say that. Only when I marry,

The rectory was gripped by a malaise that felt less like a passing storm and more like a slow, suffocating tide. Rex had returned, but the vibrant spirit that once animated the household had evaporated, leaving behind a hollow shell. He spent his days collapsed upon his bed, a statue of apathy that persisted until dawn, when the shadows of illness would finally pierce through his lethargy. The prospect of traveling to Southampton, once a beacon of hope, had curdled into a nightmare; instead, his mother and Anna found themselves besieged by the urgent, terrifying task of tending to a patient who refused to heal. The boy who had been the brightest, most grateful luminary of the house had metamorphosed into a dull-eyed, unresponsive creature, meeting every tender attempt at connection with a weary murmur: "Let me alone."

Mr. Gascoigne viewed the crisis with the detached pragmatism of a man who sees a leak in the roof and simply waits for the rain to stop, believing this withdrawal to be the quickest exit from an unfortunate entanglement. Yet, beneath his stoicism lay a quiet sorrow. He would drift into the room occasionally, sitting in silence beside the bed, pressing a gentle hand against Rex's blank forehead before whispering, "God bless you, my boy." The younger children, Warham and the siblings, would creep to the doorframe, mesmerized by the spectacle of their lively brother laid low, only to be shooed away with a sharp shake of a finger. Anna remained the constant guardian, her small hand resting within her brother's, though he offered no reciprocation. Her soul was fractured, torn between the agonizing pain for Rex and a burning, silent reproach directed at Gwendolen. "Perhaps it is wicked of me," she confessed to the empty air, "but I think I never *can* love her again." Even Mrs. Gascoigne harbored a fierce, apologetic anger toward her niece, venting it to her husband. "Henry, I cannot help thinking she is hard; she has the heart of a coquette," she lamented. "She must have led him on, or the disappointment would not have seized him so violently. And poor Fanny... she is quite blind regarding that girl." Mr. Gascoigne cut her off imperatively. "The less said on that point the better, Nancy. I ought to have been more awake myself. Be thankful if nothing worse happens to the boy. Let the matter die out as quickly as possible, and especially concerning Gwendolen—treat it as if it had never happened." The rector's prevailing sentiment was one of relief; a mutual love between Rex and Gwendolen would have presented a labyrinthine problem, a knot too tight for his hands to untangle.

Then, on a crisp morning, the atmosphere shifted. Rex requested his bath and prepared for the day with the mechanical precision of a clockwork toy. Anna, electrified by this sign of life, stood at the foot of the stairs, listening intently. When his footsteps finally echoed, she rushed forward to greet him. For the first time since his decline, Rex offered a faint smile, yet it was etched onto his pallid face with such profound melancholy that Anna could barely suppress a sob. "Nannie!" he called softly, seizing her hand and guiding her slowly down the hall to the drawing-room. His mother awaited them, and when she leaned to kiss him, he muttered, "What a plague I am!" He settled into a chair, staring out the bow-window at the lawn and shrubs dusted with hoarfrost, where the sun sent only sporadic, weak gleams across the white expanse. Anna watched him, noting the resemblance of that sad, distant smile to the ghost of his former self. He felt as though he had been resurrected into a strange, unfamiliar world, adrift among ruins of his old interests.

Anna sat nearby, feigning work while her eyes tracked him with yearning. Through the garden hedge, a road wound past, occasionally traversed by farm wagons. A gap in the hedge revealed the upland pasture, bordered by woods and a cluster of ash trees standing stark against the sky. Suddenly, a wagon laden with timber lumbered along. The horses strained their magnificent muscles, and the driver, cracking his whip, darted ahead to guide the lead horse, anxious to prevent a swerve. Rex seemed shaken from his stupor; he rose, watching intently until the last quivering trunk of timber vanished, then paced the floor twice. Mrs. Gascoigne had departed, and when Rex sat again, Anna, sensing a flicker of speech returning to his eyes, could not resist bringing a small stool and seating herself against his knee, looking up with an imploring expression that whispered, *Speak to me.*

"I'll tell you what I'm thinking of, Nannie," he began, his voice tentative. "I will go to Canada, or somewhere of that sort." (Rex had not yet scrutinized the complexities of the British Empire's colonial holdings.) "Oh, Rex, not forever!" "Yes, to earn my bread there. I'd like to build a hut, work hard clearing the land, have everything wild around me, and a vast, wide quiet." "And not take me?" Anna asked, big tears streaming rapidly down her cheeks. "How could I?" "I should like it better than anything; settlers go with their families. I'd rather go there than stay here in England. I could make the fires, mend the clothes, cook the food; I could learn to bake bread before we left. It would be nicer than anything—like playing at life over again, as we used to do with our tent and the drugget, with our little plates and dishes." "Father and mother wouldn't let you go." "Yes, I think they would, if I explained everything. It would save money; Papa would have more to support the boys." Practical discussions followed at intervals, culminating in Rex consenting that Anna accompany him, provided he secured his father's approval. Naturally, this negotiation took place only when the rector was alone in his study. Their mother would eventually reconcile to whatever decision he made, but mentioning the plan to her first would only distress her.

"Well, my children!" Mr. Gascoigne greeted them cheerfully as they entered. It was a comfort to see Rex moving again. "May we sit down with you a little, Papa?" Anna asked. "Rex has something to say." "With all my heart." These three formed a striking tableau, each face sharing a distinct structural lineage: the straight brow, the nose abruptly straightened from an intended aquiline curve, the short upper lip, the short but strong, well-hung chin. Their complexions matched, their gazes aligned. The gray-haired father appeared both massive and keen, a vertical line running down his brow deepening whenever he spoke with force, lending him an air of reserved authority. Rex would have been a vision of his father's youth if one could imagine Mr. Gascoigne stripped of his plans and commands, smitten with heart-sorrow and possessing no more capacity for concealment than a sick animal. Anna was a tiny echo of Rex, her hair drawn back and knotted, her face mirroring his expressions as if they shared a single soul.

"You know all about what has upset me, Father," Rex began. Mr. Gascoigne nodded. "I am quite finished with life in this part of the world. I am sure it will be useless for me to return to Oxford. I couldn't do any reading. I would fail, and cause you expense for nothing. I seek your consent to pursue another course, sir." Mr. Gascoigne nodded slowly, the line on his brow deepening, while Anna trembled visibly. "If you would allow me a modest outfit, I should like to go to the colonies and work the land there." Rex deemed the vagueness of the word "colonies" prudent; it embraced broader possibilities and was less vulnerable to rebuttal on specific grounds than naming a particular settlement. "Oh, and with me, Papa," Anna interjected, unable to bear exclusion from the proposal even momentarily. "Rex needs someone to care for him, you know—someone to manage the household. Neither of us will ever marry. I would cost nothing, and I would be so happy. I know it is hard to leave you and Mamma, but there are the others to raise, and the two of us would be no trouble to you anymore." Anna had risen, employing the feminine tactic of physical proximity as she spoke. Mr. Gascoigne did not smile; instead, he drew her onto his knee and held her there, effectively removing her from the equation while addressing Rex.

"You will admit that my experience grants me some power to judge for you, and that I can likely guide you in practical matters better than you can guide yourself?" Rex was forced to concede, "Yes, sir." "And perhaps you will admit—though I do not wish to press this—that you are bound in duty to consider my judgment and wishes?" "I have never yet placed myself in opposition to you, sir." Secretly, Rex did not feel bound to reject the colonies in favor of returning to Oxford, which was the actual point of contention. "But you will do so if you persist in pursuing a rash and foolish procedure, blinding yourself to considerations assured to me by my experience of life. Do you suppose, I ask, that you have suffered a shock that has altered all your inclinations, stupefied your brain, unfitting you for anything but manual labor, and instilling a hatred of society? Is that your belief?" "Something like that. I shall never be capable of the work required to live

A silly child, quivering with joy, casts its little mimic fishing-line baited with loadstone into the salt ocean, dreaming of a bowl of toys. So are men's hopes. Eight months after the family's arrival at Offendene, in the late heat of June, a rumor rippled through the countryside, igniting a fire of speculative delight that burned equally bright among the corn-factors, brewers, and horse-dealers of Wanchester. To the blacksmith of Diplow, it signaled a turning tide; to the wives of laborers, a sudden opening for their nimble sons; to the farmers, a glimmer of hope for a fairer market for their hay and straw. Yet for those of higher station, the rumor promised far more than economic relief; it conjured a floating, indeterminate vision of matrimony.

The news was specific: Diplow Hall, Sir Hugo Mallinger's estate, had ceased its painful, wall-eyed silence regarding its fine elms and beeches, its lilied pools, and its deer-speckled acres. It was being prepared for a tenant, to be inhabited in fitting style throughout the summer and the hunting season. Not by Sir Hugo, however, but by his nephew, Mr. Mallinger Grandcourt, the presumptive heir. Fortune had smiled upon young Grandcourt doubly: the baronetcy awaited him through his father, while his mother had infused his blood with a baronial streak, promising a peerage should certain distant relatives fade into memory. Nature, in her uneven allotment, grants the tuft only to the male bird, yet here, the potential for a shared crown hung in the air. A wife for such a man, currently unchosen, was a prize to be coveted by anyone with a sympathetic interest in securing a well-provided-for woman.

Some might deem it incredulous that matrimonial prospects should be constructed on mere report, dismissing such speculation as the gall of the idle. They would claim their minds are not so unbridled, that human nature abhors such fallacies. But this narrative concerns not humanity in general, but a select corner of Wessex, where reputations were unimpeached and social circles intertwined with the highest ranks. Consider the Arrowpoints at Quetcham. With a fortune sufficient to leave their daughter Catherine half a million pounds, they possessed no sordid motives, yet their affectionate anxiety for her position—having rejected Lord Slogan, an Irish peer whose estate lacked drainage and population—led them to wonder if Mr. Grandcourt was handsome, sound, virtuous, or at least reformed. Was he a liberal-conservative of the right degree? His succession to the title was desired, provided it did not require the death of anyone.

Similarly, Mr. Gascoigne, though a clergyman, felt the weight of parental and guardian anxiety acutely. He and his wife, Mrs. Gascoigne, had long felt overburdened by the management of young creatures that defied bit and bridle, resisting any metaphor of judicious advice. Naturally, they did not broadcast every thought concerning Grandcourt's advent. Openness on such subjects is rarely prudent; one risks having one's ingenious theories forestalled or one's differing views on the fixed stars used as grounds for censure. Mr. Gascoigne did not inquire of Mr. Arrowpoint for trustworthy sources on Grandcourt as a husband for a charming girl, nor did Mrs. Arrowpoint whisper to Mrs. Davilow that if the possible peer sought a wife nearby, Catherine was the only reasonable prospect, though she would refuse unless secured for happiness. Even to his wife, the rector remained silent on the matrimonial implications, anticipating that Grandcourt might see Gwendolen at the upcoming Archery Meeting, though Mrs. Gascoigne's mind raced in that direction with greater fervor. She had once exclaimed to her sister, "It would be a mercy, Fanny, if that girl were well married!" Mrs. Davilow, sensing a critique of her darling in the fervor of the wish, withheld an audible reply, thinking inwardly, "You will not get her to marry for your pleasure," the mild mother growing saucy when identifying with her daughter's autonomy.

To her husband, Mrs. Gascoigne remarked, "I hear Mr. Grandcourt has got two places of his own, but he comes to Diplow for the hunting. It is to be hoped he will set a good example in the neighborhood. Have you heard what sort of a young man he is, Henry?" Mr. Gascoigne confessed he had heard nothing, or rather, he was disposed to ignore the gossip of his male acquaintances. He deemed it futile to investigate the past of a young man whose birth, wealth, and leisure rendered many habits venial. Whatever Grandcourt had done, he had not ruined himself; indeed, in the realm of gambling, a man who possesses the strength of mind to cease when he has ruined others is a reformed character. This was merely an illustration; Mr. Gascoigne had not heard Grandcourt was a gambler. One cannot pronounce him singular for feeling that a landed proprietor with noble blood need not be subjected to the suspicious inquiry reserved for a reformed character offering himself as a butler. Where a man can afford to do without reformation, it is hardly anything but genuine. Furthermore, it was uncertain if Grandcourt required reform more than other youths of thirty-five, and the significance of his past must yield to the determination of his present self.

Mrs. Davilow, though unwilling to voice her sister's pregnant remarks, could not remain inwardly indifferent to an advent promising a brilliant lot for Gwendolen. Speculation on "what may be" arises naturally, inevitably forming images when unknown persons are mentioned. First, Mr. Grandcourt appeared to her as a handsome, accomplished, excellent young man, perfectly suited as a husband. Then, the image shifted: would Gwendolen be satisfied? There was no knowing what would meet that girl's taste or touch her affections; it might be something other than excellence. Thus, the perfect suitor gave way to a fluctuating combination of qualities imagined to win Gwendolen's heart. In the difficulty of pinpointing the exact combination, Mrs. Davilow told herself, "It would not signify about her being in love, if she would only accept the right person." For whatever marriage had been for herself, how could she desire it less for her daughter? The difference lay in her own misfortunes; she never dared dwell much on the desirableness of marriage with Gwendolen, fearing an answer akin to that of the future Madame Roland, when her gentle mother urged the acceptance of a suitor, saying, "Tu seras heureuse, ma chère." "Oui, maman, comme toi."

Regarding the problematic Mr. Grandcourt, Mrs. Davilow would least of all willingly drop a hint of the aerial castle-building she felt ashamed of, for such a hint might give an adverse poise to Gwendolen's thoughts, making her detest the desirable husband beforehand. Since the scene after poor Rex's farewell visit, the mother had felt a new sense of peril in touching the mystery of her child's feelings, rashly determining what constituted her welfare, which she could conceive of only in the shape of marriage.

Yet the discussion of Gwendolen's attire for the Archery Meeting proved a relevant diversion. When it was decided that pale green, a touch of color on her white cashmere, was superior for her complexion—a choice confirmed as Gwendolen tried a feather in her hat before the mirror—Mrs. Davilow felt her ears tingle when Gwendolen suddenly threw herself into the attitude of drawing her bow, speaking with a look of comic enjoyment: "How I pity all the other girls at the Archery Meeting—all thinking of Mr. Grandcourt! And they have not a shadow of a chance."

Mrs. Davilow lacked the presence of mind to answer immediately. Gwendolen turned quickly, saying wickedly, "Now you know they have not, mamma. You and my uncle and aunt—you all intend him to fall in love with me." Piqued into a little stratagem, Mrs. Davilow replied, "Oh, my dear, that is not so certain. Miss Arrowpoint has charms which you have not."

"I know, but they demand thought. My arrow will pierce him before he has time for thought. He will declare himself my slave—I shall send him round the world to bring me back the wedding ring of a happy woman—in the meantime all the men who are between him and the title will die of different diseases—he will come back Lord Grandcourt—but without the ring—and fall at my feet. I shall laugh at him—he will rise in resentment—I shall laugh more—he will call for his steed and ride to Quetcham, where he will find Miss Arrowpoint just married to a needy musician, Mrs. Arrowpoint tearing her cap off, and Mr. Arrowpoint standing by.
