Exit Lord Grandcourt, who returns to Diplow, and, like M. Jabot, *change de linge*."

Was ever any young witch like this? One thought of hiding things from her, of sitting upon a secret and looking innocent, only to discover by the corner of her eye that it was exactly five pounds ten shillings one was sitting on! As well turn the key to keep out the damp! By dint of divination, she likely already knew more of Mr. Grandcourt than anyone else. This idea prompted a question in Mrs. Davilow's mind, arising without apparent reason save the faculty of speech and the uncertainty of what to do with it. "Why, what kind of a man do you imagine him to be, Gwendolen?"

"Let me see!" said the witch, putting her forefinger to her

The debate over the essence of femininity unfolded with the crisp precision of a printed pamphlet, a dialogue where logic was wielded as a weapon and social convention served as the ammunition. The first gentleman posited that the definition of a woman must be derived solely from the appetites of the marriageable male, a proposition he illustrated with the grand industrial metaphor of the era: iron, cotton, wool, and chemicals were all shaped by the market's pulse, responding to demand with mechanical obedience. Thus, he argued, daughters were destined to be wives, molded into whatever shape the masculine palate required, their value measured by the whims of their suitors. Yet, the second gentleman countered with a twist of rhetorical elegance, demanding the tables be turned. If taste dictated the menu, he asked, did the Roman epicurean standards of the day dictate that a gentleman must consume specific delicacies to earn the dignity of dining? The conversation hung in the air, a delicate dance of syntax and semantics, before dissolving into the ambient noise of the gathering.

Beyond the immediate circle of discourse lay the landscape of Brackenshaw Park, a verdant amphitheater carved from gentle heights overlooking the neighboring valley. From this vantage point, the eye swept across the outlying eastern downs and the broad, slow rise of cultivated fields, which hung in the distance like a vast, textured curtain drawn against the western sky. Dominating the highest platform of the clustered hills stood the castle, a fortress of rough-hewn limestone, its surface etched with the dark dust of lichens and the translucent washings of recent rain. Masses of beech and fir trees sheltered the structure on the north, cascading down the green slopes like flocks of birds seeking the shimmering water below. At the farthest end of the park, separated by a protective barrier of tall elms and a thick screen of holly, lay the archery ground—a meticulously maintained enclosure of table-land where the targets stood bathed in agreeable afternoon shade. The Archery Hall, with its colonnade facing forward, rose like a pristine white temple against the encroaching greenery.

This pastoral theater provided the perfect backdrop for the floral procession of ladies, who moved and bowed with the languid grace of lilies suddenly endowed with locomotion. Even as the military band from Wanchester fell silent, the atmosphere remained charged with a symphony of pleasant sounds: musical laughter rippling through various registers, harmonious exchanges of friendly speech that swelled into mild excitement before settling back into a contented murmur. Unlike the noisy, crowded spectacles that spoiled modern pleasures, this open-air amusement was curiously exclusive. The number of guests accompanying the club members was strictly limited by ticket awards, ensuring the crowd remained manageable for the subsequent dinner and ball within the castle walls. No plebeian spectators were admitted save for Lord Brackenshaw's tenants and their families, and even among them, it was primarily the women who exercised this privilege, accompanied by small children or younger siblings. While the young males engaged in imaginative betting on their favored archers, wagering "anything you like," the young maidens observed with a different, more discerning eye. They scrutinized the assembly, mentally cataloging which of the sweetly dressed ladies they would aspire to emulate if given the choice. Though the rural souls might have coveted a form more flushed with pink or crowned with fashionable yellow hair, the consensus among the male judges surrounding Gwendolen was unanimous: she was the finest girl present.

It was no wonder she basked in the radiance of that July day. Pre-eminence is a sweet intoxication, even when the circumstances are merely mediocre. Perhaps it was not entirely mythical that a slave takes pride in being bought first, nor that a barnyard fowl, unaware of its own status, might strut with a self-conscious awareness of being the best of a bad lot. But for true enjoyment, the external circumstance and internal conviction must align perfectly. And for Gwendolen, this concurrence was absolute. Who could deny that bows and arrows constitute among the most aesthetically pleasing instruments for feminine forms to manipulate? They invite attitudes of grace and power, liberating the fine concentration of energy found in marksmanship from the grim associations of bloodshed. The time-honored British tradition of "killing something" had evolved; bands defending their passes fought under different banners, and poisoned darts survived only as harmless metaphors or curiosities in distant lands. Archery possessed no sulfurous odor, broke no shins, and bred no monstrous athletes; its sole peril was failure, a challenge sufficient to mold skilled action in generous hearts. Furthermore, the prizes awarded by the Brackenshaw Archery Club were imbued with noble symbolism. They were not parcels to be hoarded, degrading honor into mere gain, but the gold arrow, the silver arrow, the gold star, and the silver star—tokens to be worn for a long time as badges of achievement before passing to the next worthy contender. These insignia offered the glory of wreaths without their physical inconvenience, avoiding the potential melancholy of heavy foliage in the stifling heat of the ballroom. Indeed, the entire institution was framed with impeccable taste, designed to eschew any ridiculous incident.

Today, every incalculable element aligned in its favor. The air held a mild warmth, undisturbed by wind that might ruffle hair, disrupt drapery, or deflect the flight of an arrow. Skillful preparation met fair play, and when the general march began to retrieve the arrows, the promenade of joyous young creatures, speaking lightly and laughing, moving gracefully toward a common objective, created a spectacle worth witnessing. Here, Gwendolen appeared as a Calypso among her nymphs; her attitudes and movements commanded universal admission of her surpassing charm.

"That girl is like a high-mettled racer," remarked Lord Brackenshaw to young Clintock, one of the invited spectators.

"First chop! Tremendously pretty too," replied the elegant Grecian, who had paid her assiduous attention; "I never saw her look better."

Perhaps she had never looked so radiant. Her face beamed with a young pleasure devoid of any malignant ray of discontent. Satisfied with her own prospects, she felt kindly toward everyone and content with the universe itself. Lacking the highest distinction in rank or the label of heiress like Miss Arrowpoint, she found an added triumph in eclipsing those advantages. Regarding her personal entourage, she would not have traded her family group for any other: her mother's appearance suited an amiable duchess, while her uncle and aunt Gascoigne, alongside Anna, formed equally gratifying figures in their respective roles. Gwendolen was too overflowing with joyous belief in herself to harbor the slightest jealousy, even as Miss Arrowpoint stood as one of the premier archeresses.

Even the unexpected reappearance of the formidable Herr Klesmer, which caused a ripple of surprise among the company, seemed to cater to Gwendolen's inclination for amusement. Short of Apollo himself, what greater musical maestro could possibly command attention at an archery meeting? A satirical light danced in Gwendolen's eyes as she watched the Arrowpoint party enter, observing the stark contrast between Klesmer and the average group of English country folk. The disparity reached its zenith in the close proximity of his hosts—or, as Mrs. Arrowpoint preferred to term them, "patrons," a title she hoped would allow her to deny the possibility of ever truly patronizing genius, acknowledging instead its universally recognized royalty. Such a contrast might have delighted a graver observer, but Gwendolen viewed it with detached delight. The English, a miscellaneous people, often display varied architectures of animalism or facial ornamentation in random groups of fifty, yet their prevailing expression lacks the liveliness of an impassioned race preoccupied with the ideal. The strong point of the pure English gentleman lies in the easy style of his figure and clothing; he objects to marked irregularities in his attire and, crucially, objects to appearing inspired.

Imagine an assembly where the men bore the ordinary stamp of the well-bred Englishman, watching the entrance of Herr Klesmer. His mane of hair floated backward in massive inconsistency with the chimney-pot hat perched atop his head, a hat that seemed affixed for a joke above his pronounced, well-modeled features and powerful, clear-shaven jaw. His tall, thin figure was clad in a manner that, lacking strict English conformity, emphasized its intentionality all the more sharply. Draped in a loose garment with a Florentine *berretta* on his head, he would have been fitting beside Leonardo da Vinci. Yet, how did he fare presenting himself in trousers that defied English sensibilities regarding the knee? How did the fire evident in his glances and the animated movements of his head transform into comedy when capped by a hat dictating that mankind should possess well-cropped hair and a staid demeanor, such as that worn by Mr. Arrowsmith, whose nullity of face and perfect tailoring could pass anywhere without ridicule? One understands why it is often preferable for greatness to be dead, shedding the burden of the outward man.

Many present knew Klesmer, or at least heard of him, but only from candle-lit occasions where he appeared simply as a musician. He had not yet attained that supreme, world-wide celebrity that renders an artist great to the most ordinary people through the sheer knowledge of his exorbitant cost. It was literally a new light for them to see him in—unexpectedly presented on this July afternoon within an exclusive society. Some were inclined to laugh; others felt a pang of disgust at the lack of judgment displayed by the Arrowpoints in utilizing an introductory card for such an arrival.

"What extreme guys those artistic fellows usually are!" exclaimed young Clintock to Gwendolen. "Do look at the figure

The initiation of any acquaintance, whether with a person or an object, begins with the desperate attempt to sketch a definitive outline for one's ignorance. For Gwendolen, Mr. Grandcourt's request for introduction possessed no suddenness; it was a predictable tide. Yet, when Lord Brackenshaw stepped aside to allow the prefigured stranger to advance, and she found herself face-to-face with the living reality, a sharp shock struck her. It flushed her cheeks and deepened with a vexation born of her own awareness. The impact stemmed from a total reversal of expectation: Grandcourt could scarcely have been more unlike the caricatures she had constructed in her mind.

He stood slightly taller than herself, their eyes seemingly aligned on a horizontal plane. There was not the faintest smile upon his face as he regarded her, nor a trace of self-consciousness or anxiety in his posture. As he raised his hat, he revealed an extensive baldness fringed only by sparse, reddish-blonde hair, yet simultaneously displayed a hand of perfect proportion. The line of features from brow to chin, undisguised by beard, was decidedly handsome, adhering strictly to the vertical with only moderate deviations. Even the slight whisker hung perpendicularly. It was impossible for a human visage to be freer from grimace or solicitous wriggling; equally, it was improbable for a waking man to appear less animated. The stereotypical Englishman, drawing himself up from a bow into rigid stiffness, assenting with severity, projects a suppressed vivacity, hinting at violent release once freed from parade. Grandcourt, however, possessed no such rigidity; his bearing inclined rather toward the flaccid. His complexion held a faded fairness reminiscent of an actress stripped of artificial rouge and powder. His long, narrow gray eyes expressed nothing but a profound indifference.

Attempts at immediate description are futile; who can encapsulate a human being in a single breath? Even in presentation, we merely commence the knowledge of appearance, a process requiring completion through countless impressions under varying circumstances. We recognize the alphabet but remain unsure of the language. Gwendolen perceived this truth illuminated by a prepared contrast within the first minutes of their meeting. They were summarized in the stark realization: *He is not ridiculous.*

Immediately thereafter, Lord Brackenshaw departed, and what is termed conversation commenced. Its primary and constant element was that Grandcourt looked at Gwendolen persistently with a slightly exploring gaze, devoid of any shift in expression, while she returned his attention only occasionally with a flash of observation, softened marginally by coquetry. Following her replies, there ensued a pause, variable in duration, before he spoke again.

"I used to think archery was a great bore," Grandcourt began. His voice carried a fine accent, undercut by a certain broken drawl, suggestive of a distinguished figure suffering from a distinguished chill.

"Are you converted today?" asked Gwendolen.

*(A pause ensued, during which she conjured various degrees and modes of opinion regarding herself that Grandcourt might entertain.)*

"Yes, since I saw you shooting. In matters of this sort, one generally observes people missing and simpering."

"I suppose you are a first-rate shot with a rifle."

*(Another pause, during which Gwendolen, having conducted a rapid survey of Grandcourt, mentally drafted a brief, graphic description of him for an indefinite audience.)*

"I have left off shooting."

"Oh, then you are a formidable person. Those who accomplish things once and abandon them make one feel exceedingly contemptible, as if one were utilizing discarded fashions. I trust you have not abandoned all follies, for I practice a great many."

"What do you term 'follies'?"

"Well, in general, I believe whatever is agreeable is classified as a folly. However, I understand you have not ceased hunting, I hear."

*(Here, Gwendolen recalled reports concerning Grandcourt's standing and concluded he was the most aristocratically appearing man she had ever encountered.)*

"One must do something."

"And do you care for the turf—or is that among the pursuits you have relinquished?"

*(In this pause, Gwendolen reasoned that a man of extremely calm, cold manners might prove less disagreeable as a husband than others, unlikely to interfere with his wife's preferences.)*

"I run a horse now and then; but I do not engage in the sport as some men do. Are you fond of horses?"

"Yes, indeed; I never enjoy my life so fully as when I am on horseback, having a great gallop. I think of nothing. I only feel myself strong and happy."

*(Gwendolen paused, wondering if Grandcourt approved of her statement, yet reassuring herself that she would not disguise her tastes.)*

"Do you like danger?"

"I do not know. When I am on horseback, I never consider danger. It seems to me that if I broke my bones, I should not feel it. I should go at anything that comes in my way."

"You would perhaps enjoy tiger-hunting or pig-sticking. I witnessed some of that for a season or two in the East. Everything here is poor stuff compared to that."

"_You_ are fond of danger, then?"

*(Gwendolen speculated on the probability that men of the coldest manners were the most adventurous, feeling the strength of her own insight as if the question required judicial decision.)*

"One must have something or other. But one gets accustomed to it."

"I begin to think I am very fortunate, because everything is new to me: it is only that I cannot get enough of it. I am not used to anything except being dull, which I should like to leave off as you have left off shooting."

*(It occurred to Gwendolen that a man of cold, distinguished manners might potentially be a dull companion; yet, on the other hand, she considered that most persons were dull, that she had not observed husbands to function as companions—and that, after all, she was not going to accept Grandcourt.)*

"Why are you dull?"

"This is a dreadful neighborhood. There is nothing to be done in it. That is why I practiced my archery."

*(Gwendolen reflected that the life of an unmarried woman, unable to move freely and lacking command over anything, must inevitably become dull through all degrees of comparison as time progresses.)*

"You have made yourself queen of it. I imagine you will carry the first prize."

"I do not know that. I have great rivals. Did you not observe how well Miss Arrowpoint shot?"

*(Gwendolen thought that men had been known to choose someone other than the woman they most admired, recalling several instances of this phenomenon in novels.)*

"Miss Arrowpoint. No—that is, yes."

"Shall we go now and hear what the scoring declares? Everyone is proceeding to the other end now—shall we join them? I think my uncle is looking toward me. He perhaps wishes to speak with me."

Gwendolen found relief in altering the situation. Not that the tête-à-tête was entirely disagreeable to her; rather, while it persisted, she apparently could not dispel the unwonted flush in her cheeks and the sense of surprise that rendered her less mistress of herself than usual. This Mr. Grandcourt, who seemed to feel his own importance far more acutely than hers—a sort of unreasonableness few of us can tolerate—must not presume that he was of great moment to her, or that because others speculated on him as a desirable match, she placed herself wholly at his beck. How Grandcourt had filled the pauses would become more evident later.

"You have just missed the gold arrow, Gwendolen," said Mr. Gascoigne. "Miss Juliet Fenn scores eight points above you."

"I am very glad to hear it. I should have felt that I was making myself too disagreeable—taking the best of everything," said Gwendolen, quite easily.

It was impossible to harbor jealousy toward Juliet Fenn, a girl as mediocre as a midday market in every attribute save her archery and plainness, in which latter trait she was noticeable, much like her father: underhanged and possessing a receding brow resembling that of the more intelligent fish. (Certainly, considering the significance accorded to such accidents in female offspring, marriageable men—or what the new English term "intending bridegrooms"—should examine themselves dispassionately in the mirror, since their natural selection of a mate prettier than themselves is not guaranteed to negate the effect of their own ugliness.)

A lively movement rippled through the mingling groups, carrying the conversation along with it. Everyone spoke to everyone else in turn, and Gwendolen, choosing to observe the surroundings, noted that Grandcourt was having Klesmer presented to him by someone unknown to her—a middle-aged man with a dark, full face and fat hands, who appeared on the easiest terms with both parties, and soon led the way to join the Arrowpoints, whose acquaintance had already been established by both him and Grandcourt. Who this stranger was mattered little to her; rather, she wished to observe Grandcourt's manner toward others besides herself. It was precisely the same: except that he did not look much at Miss Arrowpoint, but rather at Klesmer, who spoke with animation—stretching out his long fingers horizontally, pointing downward with his forefinger, folding his arms and tossing his mane, addressing himself alternately to one and then the other, including Grandcourt, who listened with an impassive face and narrow eyes, his left forefinger

On the second day following the archery contest, the morning light filtered through the open casements of the breakfast room, carrying with it the crisp, invigorating scent of the summer air and the distant, verdant hum of the park. Beyond the window, the landscape unfolded in soft, purplish hues, merging into a dense, bordering woodland that stood as a silent sentinel to the estate's tranquility. Inside, the atmosphere was one of deliberate stillness, preserved by the room's sober, antique elegance; it possessed a conscious, well-bred silence that stood in stark contrast to the chaotic restlessness of vulgar furnishings. Yet, beneath this veneer of decorum, the dynamic between the two gentlemen occupying the space was far from harmonious.

Mr. Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt had arranged his chair to face the lawn, adopting a posture of calculated nonchalance: his left leg draped casually over the back of another seat, his right elbow propped upon the table, a large cigar burning steadily between his fingers. Beside him, Mr. Lush continued to eat, his movements lacking the same theatrical deliberation. Between them moved a motley pack of six dogs, creatures of considerable privilege who treated hunger as a game, selectively ignoring delicacies unless presented with the specific deference they demanded. Their attention flickered vacillatingly between the two masters, until the focus settled on Fetch, a magnificent liver-colored water spaniel. Unlike her peers, Fetch sat with unwavering constancy, her forepaws planted firmly, her expressive brown gaze locked onto Grandcourt.

In Grandcourt's lap rested a tiny Maltese terrier, adorned with a miniature silver collar and bell, a small parcel of animal warmth that he tended with a detached affection whenever his hand was free from the cigar or coffee cup. Fetch, sensing the slight, extended her large, silky paw onto his leg. For half a minute, Grandcourt regarded her with an impassive countenance before finally laying down his cigar to lift the diminutive Fluff to his chin, bestowing gentle pats while maintaining a grave scrutiny of Fetch. The spaniel, seemingly unable to endure the neglect, whimpered intermittently, attempting to suppress her visible discontent before resting her head beside the appealing paw, her expression one of pitiful supplication. A lover of dogs might have interpreted this as a plea for acknowledgment, and indeed, Grandcourt's reputation for canine adoration was built upon such interpretations. However, when Fetch's suppressed anguish erupted into a howling bark, Grandcourt reacted with swift irritation. He shoved the spaniel down without a word, deposited Fluff carelessly onto the table—where the dog's black nose momentarily dominated a salt cellar—and returned his attention to his cigar, annoyed that the beast had extinguished it.

Fetch, finding herself in the throes of a lament that proved difficult to cease, let out a second, even louder howl followed by a third. "Turn out that brute, will you?" Grandcourt commanded Lush, his voice devoid of inflection, his eyes fixed forward as if expecting the command to be read in the air alone. Lush rose immediately, lifting the heavy spaniel despite his aversion to stooping, and carried her out, disappearing for a couple of minutes before returning. He then relit his own cigar, settling into an angled position that allowed him to observe Grandcourt's profile without turning fully, and asked, "Shall you ride or drive to Quetcham today?"

"I am not going to Quetcham," Grandcourt replied.

"You did not go yesterday either."

Grandcourt smoked in silence for a moment before stating, "I suppose you sent my card and inquiries."

"I went myself at four," Lush explained calmly. "I informed them you were due shortly. Naturally, they assumed some accident had prevented you from fulfilling your intention, especially if you intend to go today."

A silence ensued, broken only by the crackle of tobacco. Finally, Grandcourt asked, "What men are invited here with their wives?"

Lush consulted a notebook. "The Captain and Mrs. Torrington arrive next week. Then there are Mr. Hollis and Lady Flora, along with the Cushats and the Gogoffs."

"A rather ragged assortment," Grandcourt observed after a pause. "Why did you invite the Gogoffs? When you draft invitations in my name, you ought to provide a list beforehand, rather than descending upon me with a giantess without my knowledge. She ruins the aesthetic of the room."

"You invited the Gogoffs yourself when you encountered them in Paris," Lush countered.

"What does my encounter with them in Paris have to do with this?" Grandcourt snapped, his tone shifting instantly from a languid drawl suggestive of ennui to a subdued, peremptory command that Lush recognized as the voice of absolute will. "I instructed you to give me a list."

Like many men of his station, Grandcourt possessed two distinct vocal registers. Until now, he had spoken with a superficial, interrupted drawl, but this shift revealed the underlying rigidity of his character. "Are there any other couples you would like to invite?"

"Yes," Lush replied, seizing the opening. "Consider inviting some respectable families with daughters. And one of your damned musicians, though certainly not a comic performer."

"I wonder if Klesmer would consent to join us once he departs Quetcham. Nothing but first-class music will suffice for Miss Arrowpoint."

Lush spoke lightly, yet his eyes narrowed with observation. "You need not trouble yourself excessively, perhaps. However, certain formalities must be observed before a man can marry a fortune."

"Very likely. But I have no intention of marrying a fortune."

"That is a pity—to squander such an opportunity and dismantle your own ambitions."

"_Your_ plans, I presume you mean," Grandcourt retorted.

"You carry debts, you know. Things may prove inconvenient eventually. The heirship is not absolutely secure."

Grandcourt offered no response. Lush pressed on. "Truly, it is a splendid opportunity. The parents demand nothing less than perfection, and the daughter's appearance and demeanor require no deductions whatsoever, as if she possessed not a single penny. She lacks conventional beauty, yet she possesses the bearing to sustain any rank. Furthermore, she is unlikely to reject the prospects you can offer."

"Perhaps not."

"The parents would permit you to do whatever you wished with them."

"But I should not wish to do anything with them."

Here, Lush paused, gathering his composure before speaking in a deep, reproachful tone. "Good heavens, Grandcourt! After your experiences, will you allow a mere whim to disrupt your comfortable settlement in life?"

"Spare your oratory. I know precisely what I intend to do."

"What?" Lush lowered his cigar, thrusting his hands into his side pockets as if bracing for an exasperating revelation, though striving to maintain his temper. "I am going to marry the other girl."

"Have you fallen in love?" The question carried a heavy sneer.

"I am going to marry her."

"You have already made her an offer, then?"

"No."

"She is a young woman with a formidable will, I suspect. She is extremely well-suited to create a scene. She knows exactly what she desires."

"She does not like you," Grandcourt stated, a ghost of a smile touching his lips.

"Quite true," Lush agreed, adding with marked derision, "Nevertheless, if you and she are devoted to one another, that shall suffice."

Grandcourt ignored this remark, sipping his coffee, rising, and strolling out onto the lawn, trailed by the entire pack of dogs. Lush watched him depart for a fleeting moment before resuming his cigar. He smoked slowly, running his fingers through his beard with an inspector's scrutiny, until he finally stroked it with the air of someone who had reached a definitive conclusion. "Check, old boy!"

Having known Grandcourt for fifteen years, Lush understood perfectly which measures were futile, though the efficacy of others remained a matter of doubt. Early in his career, Lush had held a fellowship and nearly taken holy orders for the sake of a college living, but, lacking a vocation for that path, he accepted the role of traveling companion first to a marquess, and subsequently to the young Grandcourt. Having lost his father prematurely, Grandcourt found Lush so conveniently adept that he allowed him to serve as prime minister in all his personal affairs. Fifteen years of this symbiotic relationship had rendered Grandcourt increasingly dependent on Lush's dexterity, while Lush grew equally reliant on the lazy luxury that his transactions afforded Grandcourt without demanding significant recompense.

One could not claim that this prolonged association had intensified Grandcourt's inherent lack of respect for his companion, for that disdain had been absolute from the outset. Rather, it had reinforced Grandcourt's conviction that he might strike Lush at will—though he never actually chose to kick any animal, for the act of kicking is a compromising posture, and a gentleman's dogs should be flogged by his servants. Instead, Grandcourt employed verbal barbs designed to expose himself to retaliation should his confidant possess an independent spirit. Yet, what son of a vicar, who has starved his wife and daughters of calico to fund his male offspring's education at Oxford, can retain an independent spirit when bent on dining with refined discrimination, riding superior horses, and living amidst the most luxuriant honey-blossomed clover—all without labor?

Lush had once been considered a scholar and retained a vestige of scholarly sensibility when not struggling to recall facts. However, the bachelor arts that soften manners are a time-honored preparation for sinecures, and Lush

"Philistia, rejoice in my presence!"

Having resolved to wed Miss Glanmore, Grandcourt displayed a formidable capacity for aligning means with ends. For the ensuing fortnight, scarcely a day passed without his arranging a meeting or demonstrating, through emphatic attentions, that she occupied his singular thoughts. With his cousin, Mrs. Torrington, now presiding over the household, Mrs. Davilow and Gwendolen were invited to a grand assembly at Diplow, where numerous witnesses observed how the host lavished distinction upon the dowerless beauty while displaying a conspicuous lack of concern for the heiress. The world—Mr. Gascoigne and all families of consequence within visiting distance of Pennicote—felt a settled assurance regarding the matter, a conviction that transformed in the rector's mind into a solemn resolve to fulfill his duty to his niece and ensure the settlements were adequate. Indeed, the wonder that persisted among them, and which Grandcourt himself shared, was that an offer, for which so many suitable occasions presented themselves, had not already been made. When he had declared his resolution to Lush, he had anticipated a swift conclusion; to his own surprise, he had repeatedly promised himself in the mornings that he would grant Gwendolen the opportunity to accept him that very day, only to find in the evenings that the necessary formality remained unaccomplished. This remarkable stagnation served only to heighten his determination for another day. He had never admitted to himself that Gwendolen might refuse him, yet—help us all!—we are often powerless to act upon our certainties. Our aversion to a contrary outcome, were it possible, rises like a spectral illusion between us and our truth; we are rationally assured that the blind worm cannot bite us mortally, yet the thought of being bitten is so intolerable, and the creature possesses such a menacing aspect, that we decline to handle it.

He had requested permission to bring a magnificent horse for Gwendolen to ride. Mrs. Davilow was to accompany her in the carriage, and they were to proceed to Diplow for lunch, conducted by Grandcourt. It was a fine mid-harvest season, neither too warm nor too cool for a noonday ride of five miles to be truly delightful. Poppies glowed along the borders of the fields; a gentle breeze moved softly like a social spirit among the ears of uncut corn, winging the shadow of a cloud across the soft gray downs. Here, the sheaves stood tall; there, horses strained their muscles beneath the last loads from wide expanses of stubble, while everywhere the green pasture provided a broader setting for the cornfields, and cattle rested beneath wide-spreading branches. The road wound through a corner of the countryside where dairy farms appeared much as they had in the days of our forefathers—a realm where peace and permanence seemed to find a sanctuary away from the busy changes that sent railway trains flying in the distance.

Yet the spirit of peace and permanence failed to penetrate the mind of poor Mrs. Davilow sufficiently to overcome her habitual unease. Watching Gwendolen and Grandcourt canter ahead of her, then slackening their pace to a conversational walk until the carriage caught up again, presented a gratifying sight; yet it served chiefly to fuel the conflict of hopes and fears concerning her daughter's future. Here lay an irresistible opportunity for a lover to speak and dissolve all uncertainties, and Mrs. Davilow could only hope, with trembling heart, that Gwendolen's decision would be favorable. Certainly, if Rex's love had been repugnant to her, Mr. Grandcourt possessed the distinct advantage of being a complete antithesis to him; and that he had produced some quite novel impression upon her seemed evident in her marked abstinence from satirical observations, nay, her total silence regarding his characteristics—a silence which Mrs. Davilow dared not break. "Is he a man she would be happy with?" was a question that inevitably arose in the mother's mind. "Well, perhaps as happy as she would be with anyone else—or as most other women are"—was the answer with which she tried to quiet herself; for she could not imagine Gwendolen under the influence of any feeling that would make her satisfied in what we traditionally term "mean circumstances."

Grandcourt's own thoughts were looking in the same direction: he sought to be done with the uncertainty belonging to his failure to speak. As to any further uncertainty—well, it was something without reasonable basis, a quality in the air acting as an irritant to his desires.

Gwendolen enjoyed the riding, but her pleasure did not burst forth in the girlish, unpremeditated chatter and laughter it had shown that morning with Rex. She spoke a little, and even laughed, but with a lightness akin to a far-off echo; for there was, for her too, some peculiar quality in the air. She was sure it was not any subjugation of her will by Mr. Grandcourt, nor the splendid prospects he intended to offer; for Gwendolen desired everyone, including that dignified gentleman himself, to understand that she was going to do exactly as she liked, and that they had better not calculate on her pleasing them. If she chose to take this husband, she would have him know that she was not going to renounce her freedom, or, according to her favorite formula, "not going to do as other women did."

Grandcourt's speeches that morning were, as usual, of that brief sort which never fails to make a conversational figure when the speaker is held important in his circle. By stopping so soon, they gave signs of a suppressed and formidable ability to say more, and possessed the meritorious quality of allowing lengthiness to others.

"How do you like Criterion's paces?" he said after they had entered the park and slowed from a canter to a walk.

"He is delightful to ride. I should like to have a leap with him, if it would not frighten mamma. There was a good wide channel we passed five minutes ago. I should like to have a gallop back and take it."

"Pray do. We can take it together."

"No, thanks. Mamma is so timid—if she saw me it might make her ill."

"Let me go and explain. Criterion would take it without fail."

"No—indeed—you are very kind—but it would alarm her too much. I dare take any leap when she is not by; but I do it and don't tell her about it."

"We can let the carriage pass and then set off."

"No, no, pray don't think of it any more: I spoke quite randomly," said Gwendolen; she began to feel a new objection to carrying out her own proposition.

"But Mrs. Davilow knows I shall take care of you."

"Yes, but she would think of you as having to take care of my broken neck."

A considerable pause ensued before Grandcourt said, looking toward her, "I should like to have the right always to take care of you."

Gwendolen did not turn her eyes on him; it seemed to her a long while that she was first blushing, and then turning pale, but to Grandcourt's rate of judgment she answered soon enough, with the lightest flute-tone and a careless movement of the head, "Oh, I am not sure that I want to be taken care of: if I chose to risk breaking my neck, I should like to be at liberty to do it."

She checked her horse as she spoke, and turned in her saddle, looking toward the advancing carriage. Her eyes swept across Grandcourt as she made this movement, but there was no language in them to correct the carelessness of her reply. At that very moment she was aware that she was risking something—not her neck, but the possibility of finally checking Grandcourt's advances, and she did not feel contented with the possibility.

"Damn her!" thought Grandcourt, as he too checked his horse. He was not a wordy thinker, and this explosive phrase stood for mixed impressions which eloquent interpreters might have expanded into sentences full of an irritated sense that he was being mystified, and a determination that this girl should not make a fool of him. Did she want him to throw himself at her feet and declare that he was dying for her? It was not by that gate that she could enter on the privileges he could give her. Or did she expect him to write his proposals? Equally a delusion. He would not make his offer in any way that could place him definitely in the position of being rejected. But as to her accepting him, she had done it already in accepting his marked attentions: and anything which happened to break them off would be understood to her disadvantage. She was merely coquetting, then?

However, the carriage came up, and no further tête-à-tête could well occur before their arrival at the house, where there was abundant company. To whom Gwendolen, clad in riding-dress with her hat laid aside, and clad also in the repute of being chosen by Mr. Grandcourt, was naturally a center of observation; and since the objectionable Mr. Lush was not there to look at her, this stimulus of admiring attention heightened her spirits and dispersed, for the time, the uneasy consciousness of divided impulses which threatened her with repentance of her own acts. Whether Grandcourt had been offended or not there was no judging: his manners were unchanged, but Gwendolen's acuteness had not gone deeper than to discern that his manners were no clue for her, and because these were unchanged she was not the less afraid of him.

The morning sun broke over Cardell Chase not merely as light, but as a violent exorcism of the previous night's gloom. Gwendolen Harleth emerged from her chamber transformed; the self-doubt that had clung to her like wet wool was evaporated, replaced by a vibrant, almost predatory vitality. She stood tall and luminous, a newly opened lily unfurling its petals to the harsh, clarifying air. Yesterday's hesitation felt now like a phantom tremor on the surface of a roaring river, insignificant and washed away. The prospect of the roving archery match ignited a primal joy within her. She envisioned herself darting beneath the beech trees, a wood-nymph in motion, surrounded by admiring gazes. And there, inevitably, was Grandcourt. Not the tragic, lyrical Daphnis of her feverish dreams, but a man of substance, a hunter of a different order. Today, she anticipated his slow, deliberate approach, a calculated convergence of wills, and she prepared to meet it with the rational, cool encouragement she had rehearsed with her uncle.

Descending to breakfast, the table was silent save for the rustling of Mrs. Davilow's fan. A stack of letters lay upon Gwendolen's plate. She picked one up, a smile blooming gradually across her face until it reached her eyes, and passed it to her mother. Mrs. Davilow returned it, her own features lighting up with a renewed cheerfulness born of her daughter's radiant spirits.

"You don't feel inclined to flee a thousand miles away?" she asked gently.

"Not exactly so far," Gwendolen replied, her voice crisp.

"It was a grievous oversight not to write again before this departure. Cannot you scribble a line now, before we set out?"

"It is not urgent. Tomorrow will suffice. You see, they leave town today. I must write to Dover; they will remain there until Monday."

"Shall I write for you, dear, if it tires you?"

Gwendolen paused, lifting her cup for a sip of coffee before answering with a brisk dismissal. "Oh, no, let it be. I shall write tomorrow." Yet, a flicker of guilt touched her conscience, and she softened her tone, looking up with playful affection. "Dear, old, beautiful mamma!"

"Old, child, truly," Mrs. Davilow countered, her voice warm.

"Please do not, mamma! I meant 'old' as in 'darling.' You are hardly twenty-five years older than I am. When you speak thus, my life seems to shrivel before me."

"One can find immense happiness in twenty-five years, my dear."

"I must seize every moment," Gwendolen declared merrily. "The sooner I secure my palaces and coaches, the better."

"And a devoted husband who worships you, Gwen," Mrs. Davilow added, her encouragement palpable.

Gwendolen pouted saucily, saying nothing, her heart swelling with the anticipation of acquisition.

However, a minor cloud marred the brilliance of her start: the rector was detained by magistrate's business and likely would not reach Cardell Chase that day. She cared little that Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna chose to stay behind without him, nor did she mind the absence of the clergyman. But her uncle's presence would have rendered the decision to proceed inevitable. Decision itself had become a formidable entity. Having nearly succumbed to Grandcourt, Gwendolen felt this sudden abundance of options coalescing into something too definitive, too heavy. When one begins to crave so fervently, whatever is obtained quickly transforms into a cage of limitations and exclusions. Yet, a comforting thought persisted: marriage was the gateway to a vast, liberated freedom.

The rendezvous was set at Green Arbor, a grassy clearing where a cluster of overhanging branches formed a natural, sheltering amphitheater. Here, the coachload of servants with provisions would prepare the picnic, and the warden of the Chase would guide the roving archers, ensuring they remained within the prescribed boundaries—a curved path defined by landmarks such as the Double Oak, the Whispering Stones, and the High Cross. The plan was for a brief stroll before luncheon, saving the main expedition for the exquisite, golden light of the afternoon. The muster was swift, sparing everyone the tedium of waiting. As the groups scattered into the dappled shade of the beeches and the deeper gloom of the oaks, the scene possessed a painterly perfection. This roving archery was far more picturesque than the static games, though success at moving targets relied less on practiced precision and more on chance. Consequently, the hits were distributed unevenly among the volunteers. Perhaps for this reason, compounded by her own divided attention between preoccupation and the desperate need to hide it, Gwendolen did not excel in these initial trials. Unless one counted her lively grace in the face of comparative failure, she remained unremarkable. She wore white and green, the same attire that had marked the epoch-making introduction to Grandcourt. Now, he was constantly by her side, yet it would have been difficult to discern from mere appearance that their relationship had shifted irrevocably since their first conversation. Still, the air around them crackled with implication; most observers concluded they were either betrothed or on the precipice of it. Gwendolen believed this herself.

As the groups drifted back toward Green Arbor, chatting aimlessly rather than aiming, words were exchanged that signaled the dawn of her surrender. Grandcourt spoke, breaking the rhythm of their walk. "Do you know how long it has been since I first saw you in this dress?"

"The archery meeting was on the 25th, and this is the 13th," Gwendolen laughed lightly. "I am not good at calculation, but I dare say it must be nearly three weeks."

A pause hung in the air, heavy and expectant. Then, "That is a great loss of time."

"That your knowing me has caused?" Gwendolen retorted, her smile sharpening. "Pray, do not be uncomplimentary; I dislike it."

Another pause. "It is because of the gain that I feel the loss."

This time, Gwendolen allowed the silence to stretch. She was thinking, *He is remarkably ingenious. He never speaks foolishly.* Her unusual quietude seemed the ultimate affirmative, and Grandcourt pressed on. "The gain of knowing you makes me acutely aware of the time lost in uncertainty. Do *you* like uncertainty?"

"I think I do, rather," Gwendolen said suddenly, beaming at him with a playful intensity. "There is more in it."

Grandcourt met her laughing eyes with a slow, steady gaze that pierced directly into her soul, an abstract vision of possession. "Do you mean more torment for me?"

Something profoundly strange struck Gwendolen in that instant, shaking her loose from her usual self-conscious armor. Blushing, she turned her eyes away. "No, that would make me sorry."

Grandcourt might have seized upon this answer, which her shift in demeanor rendered decisively favorable, but he was not so overwhelmed as to ignore their surroundings. They were descending into Green Arbor, visible to all, at a point where the slope became inconveniently steep. This necessitated his offering his hand in the literal sense. She took it, and they descended in silence, watched intently by those already on the level—among them Mrs. Arrowpoint, standing beside Mrs. Davilow. That lady had resolved that Grandcourt's merits were insufficient to sway Catherine, given her high standards that had previously rejected Lord Slogan. Thus, she regarded the tenant of Diplow with detached scrutiny.

"Mr. Grandcourt is not equal as a man to his uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger—too languid. True, Mr. Grandcourt is younger, but I should not be surprised if Sir Hugo outlives him, despite the age gap. It is ill-calculating on successions," Mrs. Arrowpoint concluded, her voice carrying too loudly.

"It is indeed," Mrs. Davilow agreed with quiet cheerfulness, her habitual melancholy regarding the unsatisfactory state of affairs completely suspended by her satisfaction with the current situation.

I need not recount the sumptuous food consumed in that green refectory, nor dwell on the tales of the forest scenery spreading beyond the hollow's rim. My concern is with a specific phase of life where the blissful beauty of earth and sky filtered in only through narrow, oblique cracks, while consciousness was consumed by a petty social drama, insulated from wider realities as if it were a puppet show. The food and champagne were of the finest caliber; the talk and laughter belonged to the elite, where no one displayed envy, and the advantages of the world were accepted with a high-born indifference born of familiarity. Some gentlemen strolled and smoked cigars during the interval before four o'clock, the hour designated for resuming the roam. Strangely, Grandcourt was among them, yet not Mr. Lush. Seeming to enjoy the day generously, Lush busied himself serving everyone, ordering drinks and managing logistics, thereby becoming an increasingly glaring blemish on the scene for Gwendolen, though he maintained an amiably distant posture, never casting an obvious glance in her direction.

When the group moved to prepare for departure, it transpired that the bows had been entrusted to Lord Brackenshaw's valet. Mr. Lush stepped forward to relieve the ladies of the burden of retrieving their equipment from the carriages. He did not intend to fetch Gwendolen's, but fearing he might, she hurried to retrieve it herself. The valet, anticipating her

"Festina lente"—celerity tempered with delay.

Gwendolen had spent her time abroad intoxicated by the new thrill of gambling, conjuring herself as an empress of fortune. From her recent experiences, she carried a vague conviction that in this chaotic world, actions held no intrinsic meaning; they were merely diversions. Alongside her, a shadowy entity known as Grapnell & Co. had sought to reign in the realm of luck, equally intent on amusement regardless of cost, thereby orchestrating a painful upheaval in her domestic sphere. Thus, she returned home, dragging behind her, against her will, a necklace she had pawned and which someone else had subsequently redeemed.

As Gwendolen journeyed back to England, Grandcourt was approaching to claim her. Yet he approached in his own distinct fashion—devoid of the frantic haste one might expect via express train from Diplow to Leubronn, where her whereabouts were rumored. Instead, he lingered at Baden-Baden, entangled by the company of Russian acquaintances, making various appointments he ultimately broke when the pull of Leubronn became irresistible. Grandcourt's passions were intermittent, flickering flames that rarely burned hot. Yet vast quantities of life proceed without such fervor: countless cravats are meticulously tied, dinners attended, speeches delivered extolling the virtues of august personages, all devoid of the zest born of intense desire. A man can maintain a formidable appearance in high society, feigning classical knowledge, scientific reserves, political opinions, and the full sentimentality of the English gentleman, all for a negligible expenditure of vital energy. Furthermore, he may display stubborn persistence at a low level, occasionally manifesting sudden impulses that possess a deceptive aura of demonic strength simply because they appear inexplicable. Their secret, however, often lies merely in the absence of regulated channels for the soul to flow; without the ducts of habit, human nature easily devolves into ooze and mud, yielding under pressure only a spurt or a puddle.

Grandcourt was not entirely displeased by Gwendolen's flight from the splendid opportunity he had proffered. The act possessed a certain piquancy. He preferred to believe it stemmed from resentment regarding his careless conduct in Cardell Chase, a behavior which, upon reflection, appeared rather cool. To have drawn her so near to a tender admission, only to watch her walk headlong away from further chances of securing her consent—which he had made clear he was requesting—was enough to provoke a spirited girl. And to be worthy of mastering such a spirit, she required just that spark. Undoubtedly, she expected him to pursue her, and so did he. Yet for a full week, he took no steps toward departure, nor did he inquire of Mr. Lush where Miss Harleth had vanished. Mr. Lush felt a triumph tinged with deep suspicion; Grandcourt had uttered not a word to him about her, maintaining a neutrality akin to an alligator, leaving one unable to predict what might churn within his slowly rotating mind. Nevertheless, postponing a decision created space for the dissipation of Grandcourt's energy.

The guests at Diplow harbored greater curiosity than their host. Silence regarding Miss Harleth was deafening. Could it be credible that she had refused Mr. Grandcourt? Lady Flora Hollis, a vivacious middle-aged woman brimming with inquiry, suddenly felt compelled to embark on a round of calls with Mrs. Torrington, visiting the rectory, Offendene, and Quetcham. Through this circuit, she not only doubled her efforts but also gleaned from the Arrowpoints that Miss Harleth had departed for Leubronn to join old friends, the Baron and Baroness von Langen. The immediate agitation and disappointment of Mrs. Davilow and the Gascoignes had crystallized into a collective wish that Gwendolen's disappearance should not be construed as eccentric or requiring secrecy. Indeed, the rector's mind entertained the possibility that the marriage was merely deferred; Mrs. Davilow had hesitated to reveal the bitter determination with which Gwendolen had spoken. Despite his practical acumen, some of his experience had hardened into maxims and quotations. As Amaryllis fled, she desired her hiding place remain unknown; yet in this age of steam, the aphorism that love finds a way "over the mountain and over the wave" holds truth without hyperbole. Gwendolen, the rector concluded, was an Amaryllis of excellent sense but coquettish daring; the question remained whether she had dared too much.

Lady Flora, returning laden with news of Miss Harleth, saw no impediment to attempting to electrify Mr. Grandcourt by whispering the rumor of his thwarted adoration during dinner. She dropped a few suggestive hints, and Grandcourt listened with quiet attentiveness. The following day, he instructed Lush to engineer a polite pretext for dissolving the party at Diplow within another week, citing an impending yacht trip to the Baltic or beyond. It was impossible to remain at Diplow like a prisoner on parole amidst a crowd he had never desired. Lush required no clearer signal that Grandcourt was bound for Leubronn, though he might travel with the sluggish momentum of a creeping billiard ball, sticking to the path. Mr. Lush intended to render himself indispensable to accompany his patron, a strategy that succeeded; Gwendolen's repulsion for him was a fact that only amused Grandcourt, rendering him all the more willing to keep Lush perpetually at hand.

Thus, it came to pass that Grandcourt arrived at the *Czarina* on the fifth day after Gwendolen had left Leubronn, discovering there his uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger, accompanied by his family, including Deronda. It is seldom a pleasure, for either the reigning power or the heir presumptive, when their separate affairs—a touch of gout in one, a streak of willfulness in the other—conspire to bring them to the same locale. Sir Hugo was a man of easy temper, tolerant of differences and defects alike. However, a divergent viewpoint regarding the settlement of family estates fretted him more than disputes over church discipline or the ballot; faults were less forgivable when committed by someone whose existence was inconvenient. In no scenario could Grandcourt have been a nephew to Sir Hugo's liking; yet as the presumptive heir to the Mallinger estates, he embodied the baronet's chief grievance: the lack of a son to inherit the lands, in no portion of which did Sir Hugo hold more than a life-interest. For in the ill-advised settlement dictated by his father, Sir Francis, even Diplow, with its modest acreage, had been left under identical conditions as the ancient, wide inheritance of the two Toppings—Diplow, where Sir Hugo had hunted and lived through many seasons in his youth, and where his wife and daughters ought to have retired upon his death.

This grievance had intensified with the passing years. Lady Mallinger, after bearing three daughters in rapid succession, had remained childless for eight years, now surpassing forty without producing another girl. Sir Hugo, nearly twenty years her senior, stood at an age where, despite the fashionable retardation of everything from dinners to marriages, a man's hopefulness begins to show signs of wear, unless restored by a second childhood.

Indeed, he had begun to despair of a son. Consequently, the confirmation of Grandcourt's interest in the estates rendered his image and presence increasingly unwelcome. Yet, paradoxically, this circumstance inclined Sir Hugo to ensure their relationship remained as cordial as possible. It spurred him to dwell on a plan that had grown alongside his disappointment: to secure Diplow as a future residence for Lady Mallinger and her daughters, preserving this pretty fragment of family inheritance for his own offspring despite the lack of a male heir. His knowledge of his nephew's disposition encouraged the belief that Grandcourt might consent to a transaction granting him a substantial sum of ready money in exchange for his prospective interest in the domain and its attached lands. Should the unhoped-for son eventually be born, the money would have been squandered, and Grandcourt would have been compensated for relinquishing interests that proved worthless; yet Sir Hugo dismissed this risk as nil, having recently husbanded his fortune through mining operations and lease sales, preparing himself for such an outlay.

This objective made him vigilant against quarreling with Grandcourt. Years prior, while improving the Abbey and needing Grandcourt's concurrence to fell an obstructive mass of timber on the demesne, he had congratulated himself on finding no active spite in his nephew's peculiar mind. Nothing had since occurred to deepen their hatred beyond what was compatible with perfect politeness or mutually beneficial accommodation.

On his side, Grandcourt viewed his uncle as a superfluity and a bore, believing the world would improve once Sir Hugo was expunged from the ledger. Yet, informed through Lush—a useful medium—of the baronet's inclinations regarding Diplow, he was gratified to have the option of the money in his mind. Even if he deemed acceptance unlikely, his sense of power would be flattered by the capacity to refuse what Sir Hugo desired. The hinted transaction influenced his motives for requesting a year's tenancy of Diplow, a request that had annoyed Sir Hugo, for the excellent hunting in the vicinity might deter Grandcourt from parting with his chance of future possession; a man possessing two places, one with inferior hunting, naturally desires a third where the game is better. Moreover, Lush had suggested to Sir Hugo the probability that Grandcourt would woo and win Miss Arrowpoint, in which case ready money might lose its allure. Hence, at this unexpected meeting at Leubronn, the baronet felt a keen curiosity regarding the developments at Diplow, bent on civility toward his nephew, and looking

The universe of human existence mirrors the cosmos: every individual possesses a visible trajectory charted by the stars and an invisible history woven through the dark ether. While astronomers deduce the path of a wandering planet with cold precision, mapping every arc against the backdrop of space, the chronicler of human souls must navigate far deeper currents. Their task requires threading the hidden pathways of emotion and cognition that culminate in every decisive act, illuminating those moments of agonizing intensity where suffering transforms into action—much like the cry of Prometheus, whose bound torment radiates a ferocity surpassing the very sea and sky he summons and the god he defies.

For Daniel Deronda, destiny had carved a singular, searing epoch into his youth. It was a day drenched in July sunlight, where large pink roses shed their final petals onto a grassy courtyard hemmed in by a Gothic cloister. Picture the scene: a boy of thirteen lying prone upon the cool grass within the shade, his curly head resting on his folded arms above an open book, while his tutor, similarly engrossed, sat on a camp-stool beneath the shelter of the arcade. In his hands lay Sismondi's *History of the Italian Republics*; the lad possessed a voracious hunger for history, desperate to comprehend how time had been filled since the Great Flood and how civilization persisted through its dull, stagnant intervals. Suddenly, he lowered his left arm and glanced at his tutor, his voice ringing out in the purest tones of boyhood.

"Mr. Fraser, how was it that popes and cardinals always had so many nephews?"

His tutor, an able young Scot serving as Sir Hugo Mallinger's secretary, roused himself reluctantly from his studies in political economy. With the clear, emphatic cadence characteristic of Scottish speech—which renders a truth doubly potent—he replied, "Their own children were called nephews."

"Why?" asked Deronda.

"It was simply for the propriety of the matter," the tutor explained, his tone sharpening slightly. "As you know very well, priests cannot marry, and therefore the children were illegitimate."

Mr. Fraser thrust out his lower lip, emphasizing the final word with a hint of impatience at being interrupted, before returning his gaze to his text. Yet, as if stung by a sudden insect, Deronda sprang upright into a sitting position, his back turned sharply toward the tutor.

He had always addressed Sir Hugo Mallinger as his uncle. When the question of his parents had once arisen, the baronet had responded with a vague dismissal: "You lost your father and mother when you were quite a little one; that is why I take care of you." At that time, straining to pierce the haze of early twilight, Daniel retained only a dim sensation of being kissed profusely, enveloped in thin, clouded, scented drapery, until his fingers brushed against something hard that caused pain, triggering a burst of tears. All other memories were confined to the little world in which he currently resided. At that age, he cared little to learn more, for his affection for Sir Hugo was so profound that he felt no sorrow for the loss of unknown parents. Life remained delightfully radiant for the lad, guided by an uncle who was perpetually indulgent and cheerful—a magnificent figure in the bright noon of life, whom Daniel deemed absolutely perfect. Sir Hugo's domain was one of England's finest seats, a place that was simultaneously historical, romantic, and intimately domestic: a picturesque architectural extension rising from an abbey, retaining remnants of its ancient monastic core.

Diplow lay in another county, a comparatively landless estate acquired by the family from a wealthy Restoration-era lawyer on the maternal side, a woman noted for wearing the powdered wig of the era. By contrast, the Mallingers held the grant of Monk's Topping dating back to Henry VIII, possessing ancestral rights to the neighboring lands of King's Topping. Their lineage traced directly to a certain Hugues le Malingre, who arrived with William the Conqueror—and seemingly inherited a pallid complexion that had fortunately been corrected in his descendants.

Two rows of descendants, direct and collateral, stared down from the gallery overlooking the cloisters as Daniel walked below: men in armor with pointed beards and arched brows, pinched ladies trapped in hoops and ruffs with faces obscured by fabric; solemn figures in black velvet with padded hips, and pale, frightened women clutching small boys by the hand; smiling politicians sporting magnificent wigs, and ladies of the prize-animal variety, with rosebud mouths and heavy-lidded eyes, rendered in the style of Lely. Then came generations whose features were revised and embellished in the tastes of Kneller, followed by refined iterations of the family type during the eras of Reynolds and Romney, until the line terminated with Sir Hugo and his younger brother, Henleigh. This latter brother had married Miss Grandcourt, adopting her surname alongside her vast estates, thereby forging a union between two equally ancient houses. He impaled the three Saracen heads and three bezants of his own coat of arms with the tower and argent falcons of hers, effectively merging their highest advantages in the prospects of Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt—a man who, coincidentally, had become more of an acquaintance to the narrative observer than either Sir Hugo or his nephew, Daniel Deronda.

In Sir Hugo's youthful portrait, executed by Sir Thomas Lawrence with a rolled collar and high cravat, the artist captured the agreeable alacrity of expression and sanguine temperament evident in the original subject. However, Lawrence went beyond mere fidelity, slightly elongating the nose, which was in reality shorter than one might expect in a Mallinger. Fortunately, the distinctive nasal profile of the family reappeared in his younger brother, manifesting in all its refined regularity in his nephew, Mallinger Grandcourt. Yet, in the nephew Daniel Deronda, the myriad family faces displayed on the gallery walls found no reflection. Nevertheless, he was handsomer than any of them. At thirteen, he might have served as the ideal model for any painter seeking to capture the essence of the most memorable of boys; one could scarcely meet his gaze fully without believing that humanity had achieved nobility in times past and might achieve even greater heights in the future. The finest childlike faces possess this consecrating power, causing us to shudder anew at the world's grossness and basely crafted sorrows, fearing they might intrude here and defile such purity.

But amidst the rose-petal-strewn grass at that precise moment, Daniel Deronda was encountering those very griefs for the first time. A novel idea had invaded his mind, altering the landscape of his habitual happiness as surely as a sudden threat clouds the sky and changes the mood of carefree voyagers. He sat motionless with his back to the tutor, his face reflecting a rapid internal metamorphosis. The deep blush that had risen upon his initial start-up gradually faded, yet his features retained an indescribable look of subdued activity, often accompanying a fresh mental survey of familiar realities.

Having never associated with other boys, his mind exhibited a unique synthesis of childish ignorance and startling knowledge, a trait more commonly observed in bright girls. Having devoured Shakespeare alongside extensive histories, he could have discoursed with the wisdom of a bookish child regarding men born out of wedlock, who were deemed unfortunate due to the disadvantages necessitating heroic efforts to attain equality with their legally born brothers. Yet, he had never linked such knowledge to his own station, which had been so effortless that it never warranted contemplation—until this moment. With the magic of swift comparison, the possibility had darted into his consciousness: that here lay the secret of his own birth, and that the man he called uncle was, in truth, his father.

Some children, even younger than Daniel, recognize the first arrival of anxiety as an ominous, irremovable guest in their tender lives upon discovering that their parents, whom they imagined capable of purchasing anything, were actually poor and drowning in financial distress. Daniel felt the presence of a new guest arriving with an enigmatic, veiled face, carrying dimly conjectured, dreaded revelations. The ardor he had poured into the imaginary worlds of his books suddenly surged toward his own history, expending its pictorial energy there to explain what he knew and represent the unknown. The uncle he loved so dearly assumed the aspect of a father harboring secrets about him—a father who had committed a wrong, yes, a grievous wrong. And what had become of his mother, for whom he must have been taken away? Secrets surrounding which he, Daniel, could never inquire; for speaking or listening to such new thoughts felt akin to falling flakes of fire burning his imagination. Those who have known an impassioned childhood understand this dread of utterance regarding any shame connected to one's parents.

This impetuous influx of new images seized him with the force of absolute fact, leaving him no immediate capacity to reflect that he might be trembling at a mere fiction of his own making. The terrible sense of collision between a powerful rush of feeling and the terror of its betrayal finally found release in big, slow tears that fell without restraint until the voice of Mr. Fraser broke the silence: "Daniel, do you see that you are sitting on the bent pages of your book?"

Daniel immediately shifted the book without turning around, held it before him for an instant, then rose with it and walked away into the open grounds, where he could dry his tears unseen. Once the initial shock of suggestion had passed, he remembered that he possessed no certainty regarding how things truly had been. He realized he had been constructing conjectures about his own history, much as he had often fabricated tales about Pericles or Columbus simply to fill the blanks before they became famous. Yet, certain facts returned with an obstinate reality, almost like fragments of a broken bridge, unmistakably indicating how

This is true, the poet sings, that a sorrow's crown of sorrow / Is remembering happier things.

On a fine evening near the end of July, Deronda rowed alone upon the Thames. It had been nearly a year since his return to England, a return marked by the formal conclusion of his education and the pressing expectation that he assume his station within the rigid architecture of English society. Though he had yielded to Sir Hugo's wishes, fencing off his potential idleness with the study of law, this decision had achieved nothing but to entrench his indecision deeper. Yet, now that he was in town with the Mallingers, his old passion for boating had surged with renewed vigor. Nowhere else could he secure the profound, still seclusion the river afforded. He possessed a boat of his own at Putney, and whenever Sir Hugo did not require his presence, it became his primary sanctuary to row until the sun surrendered the sky to the stars.

He was not in a sentimental phase, nor was he lost in the romantic haze of youth. Rather, he inhabited a contemplative state increasingly common among the young men of his generation: a skeptical inquiry into the utility of engaging in the world's great battles. Specifically, he questioned the value of participating in a struggle sustained by the unproductive labor of questioning, fueled by a mere three or five percent return on capital amassed by others' toil. This hesitation puzzled Sir Hugo, who found it baffling that a figure presenting such a striking contrast to the sickly, whining elements of society should be hamstrung by ideas that, to an accomplished Whig, appeared as spectral illusions. Especially since Deronda rejected authorship—a profession widely believed to convert foolish thinking into financial assets.

Clad in a dark-blue shirt and skull-cap, his curls closely cropped and his mouth framed by abundant, soft waves of beard, Deronda carried only disguised traces of the seraphic boy once described as trailing clouds of glory. Yet, even someone who had not seen him since childhood might regard him with a slow, dawning recognition, perhaps due to the peculiar intensity of the gaze Gwendolen had once termed "dreadful," though it possessed a genuinely mild, scrutinizing quality. His voice, occasionally audible in subdued fragments of song, proved to be merely a high baritone; indeed, observing his lithe, powerful frame and the firm gravity of his countenance would suffice for an experienced observer to deduce that he lacked the rare, ravishing tenor that nature reluctantly gifts at some sacrifice. Look at his hands: they were neither small and dimpled with tapering fingers suggesting a deprecating touch, nor were they clumsy. They were long, flexible, and firmly grasping, reminiscent of the hands Titian painted when aiming to depict the synthesis of refinement and raw force. There was a kinship, too, between the faces that belonged to such hands—in both, the uniform pale-brown skin, the perpendicular brow, and the calmly penetrating eyes. No longer seraphic, he was thoroughly terrestrial and manly; yet, he retained a quality capable of inspiring belief in a human dignity that can afford to recognize its poorer relations.

Such types appear sporadically amidst average conditions; consider, for instance, a workman whistling over a measurement, lifting his eyes to answer a query about the road. Often, the grand meanings embedded in faces, as well as in written words, reside chiefly in the impressions that happen to hold immediate importance relative to Deronda. Rowing on the Thames in very ordinary attire for a young Englishman at leisure, he passed beneath Kew Bridge with no thought of an adventure where his appearance might play a role. Indeed, he vehemently objected to the notion, which others had failed to allow him to dismiss, that his visage was inherently attention-grabbing. Hints of this, intended as compliments, resonated angrily within him, stemming from mixed experiences to which a clue had previously been provided. For many years, his reflection in the glass had been inextricably linked in his mind to thoughts of someone he must resemble—a figure regarding whose character and fate he continually wondered, yet never dared to inquire.

Between six and seven o'clock in the vicinity of Kew Bridge, the river was no longer a solitude. Several persons sauntered along the towing-path, and boats plied here and there. Deronda had rowed swiftly to bypass this congestion, only to guide his vessel aside and rest his oar within a couple of yards of the riverbank as a great barge advanced toward him. Throughout this maneuver, he unconsciously continued the low-toned chant that had haunted his throat all the way upstream—the gondolier's song from *Otello*, where Rossini worthily set to music the immortal words of Dante:

"Nessun maggior dolore / Che ricordarsi del tempo felice / Nella miseria."

And as he rested on his oar, the pianissimo fall of the melodic wail "nella miseria" drifted distinctly onto the water's brink. Three or four bystanders had paused to watch the barge pass the bridge, and undoubtedly, their glances included the young gentleman in the boat; yet, presumably, the low vocal sounds registered with greater significance to only one ear, cutting through the sum of current noises like an insect-murmur. Deronda, waiting for the barge, turned his head toward the riverside and spotted a figure a few yards distant that might have been an impersonation of the misery he was unconsciously voicing: a girl hardly older than eighteen, of low, slim stature, with a most delicate face. Her dark curls were pushed behind her ears beneath a large black hat, and a long woolen cloak draped her shoulders. Her hands hung down, clasped before her, her eyes fixed on the river with an immovable, statue-like despair.

This arresting stillness caused him to cease singing. Apparently, his voice had entered her inner world without her registering its origin, for when it abruptly stopped, she shifted her stance slightly. Looking around with a frightened glance, she met Deronda's face. It lasted but a couple of moments, yet felt like an eternity for two souls locked in mutual scrutiny. Her expression resembled that of a fawn or other gentle creature before it turns to flee: no blush, no specific alarm, only a timidity that nonetheless could not prevent her from sustaining a prolonged gaze before averting her eyes. In truth, it seemed to Deronda she was only half-conscious of her surroundings; was she starving, or was there some other cause for her bewilderment? A surge of interest and compassion leaped within him; yet, the next instant, she turned and walked away to a nearby bench beneath a tree. He had no right to linger and watch her; poorly dressed, melancholy women were common sights. It was only the delicate beauty, the picturesque lines and coloring of the image that were exceptional, rendering it all the more impossible for him to intrude his interest upon her.

He began to row away, soon finding himself far up the river, yet no other thoughts were vigorous enough to expel that pale image of unhappy girlhood. He fell repeatedly into speculating on the probable romance lurking behind that loneliness and look of desolation, then smiled at his own complicity in the prejudice that interesting faces must harbor interesting adventures, and finally justified his feeling that sorrow was more tragic when it befell delicate, childlike beauty.

"I should not have forgotten the look of misery if she had been ugly and vulgar," he muttered to himself. Yet, there was no denying that the attractiveness of the image ensured its persistence. It was clear to him as an onyx cameo: the brown-black drapery, the white face with minute features and dark, long-lashed eyes. His mind swept over the girl-tragedies unfolding in the world, hidden and unheeded, as if they were merely dramas of the copse or hedgerow, where the helpless dragged wounded wings forsakenly and stained the shadowed moss with the red moment-hand of their own death. Lately, in his solitary excursions, Deronda had been preoccupied chiefly with uncertainties regarding his own course. But these uncertainties, having ample leisure, tended to forge wide-sweeping connections with all life and history, allowing the new image of helpless sorrow to blend seamlessly with what seemed to him the strong array of reasons why he should recoil from entering the routine of the world that forces men to apologize for all its wrongdoing, treating opinions as mere professional equipment—and why he should not draw strongly at any thread in the hopelessly entangled scheme of things.

He used his oars sparingly, content to drift with the tide and be returned by it. It was his habit to indulge in that solemn passivity that naturally arises with lengthening shadows and mellow light, when thinking and desiring melt together imperceptibly, transforming arguments into passionate visions. By the time the tide carried him back past Richmond Bridge, the sun was nearing its setting. The approach of his favored hour—with its deepening stillness and darkening masses of trees and buildings caught between the double glow of sky and river—disposed him to linger as if they were an unfinished strain of music. He sought a perfectly solitary spot where he could moor his boat against the bank, throw himself backward with his head propped on the cushions, and watch the fading light of sunset and the opening of that bead-roll which some Oriental poet describes as God's call to the little stars, each answering, "Here am I."

He selected a location in the bend of the river directly opposite Kew Gardens, where a vast expanse of water reflected the sky's glory while he remained in shadow. Lying with his hands behind his head, propped level with the boat's edge, he could see all around yet remain unseen by anyone a few yards distant. For a long while, he never turned his eyes from the view directly ahead.

Life is a various mother: sometimes she adorns herself in plumes and brilliants, ascending marble staircases with head held high, refusing to cast a glance upon the lackeys who attend her; at other times, she dwells grim-clad within dark alleys, breathing the fumes of hot gin amidst the screaming chaos of pauper riots. Yet tonight, she appeared as a frugal matron, neat and deft, possessing cheerful morning thoughts and a quick ingenuity to find abundance in the smallest measure.

Mrs. Meyrick's house was not noisy.
