The front parlor overlooked the river, while the rear faced the gardens, allowing the window to remain open even as she read aloud to her daughters, thereby freshening the air of the small double room where a single lamp and two candles burned. These candles rested on a separate table for Kate, who was sketching illustrations for a publisher; the lamp illuminated not only the reader but also Amy and Mab, who were embroidering satin cushions destined for "the great world."

Outside, the house appeared narrow and shabby, the bright light filtering through the holland blind revealing the heavy, old-fashioned window frame. Yet there is a peculiar comfort in knowing that countless such grim-walled slices of space in our foggy London have been—and still are—the sanctuaries of a culture far freer from vulgarity precisely because poverty renders every display an impersonal necessity, transforming the grand spectacles of the world into mere phenomena that stir only petty rivalry or vain efforts at possession. The Meyricks' home belonged to this category; indeed, they clung to this specific row-house because its interior was filled with objects permanently stationed in their accustomed places. For the mother, these items held memories of her marriage; for the young ones, they seemed as essential and unchallenged a component of their universe as the stars of the Great Bear viewed from the back windows.

Mrs. Meyrick had endured significant privation in other areas solely to preserve certain engravings specially cherished by her husband. Consequently, the narrow stretches of wall displayed a condensed world history in scenes and portraits that the children had memorized since infancy. The chairs and tables were likewise old acquaintances preferred to any new acquisitions. In these two modest parlors, devoid of furniture a broker would deign to cheapen save for the prints and the piano, resided the apparatus for a wide-glancing, carefully selected life, opened to the loftiest realms of music, painting, and poetry.

One cannot be certain that during the times of greatest scarcity, prior to Kate securing paid employment, these ladies always possessed a servant to tend their fires and sweep their floors. Nevertheless, they remained fastidious in certain particulars and refused to accept the notion that the manners of ladies in the fashionable world were saturated with the coarse selfishness, petty quarreling, and slang depicted in so-called literary photographs. The Meyricks possessed their own peculiarities, streaks of eccentricity flowing from both maternal and paternal blood, their minds resembling medieval houses with unexpected recesses and openings connecting one chamber to another, complete with flights of steps and sudden vistas.

Yet mother and daughters were bound together by a triple cord: family love, an admiration for the finest works and noblest actions, and a habit of industry. Hans's desire to expend some of his funds in rendering their lives more luxurious had been firmly resisted by all. Thus, both parties were spared the regret of threatened triumphs over his yearning for art in favor of the allurements of secure income—a victory that would eventually compel him to relinquish his fellowship. They could all afford to laugh at his Gavarni caricatures and regard him as blameless for following a natural inclination, an impulse their unselfishness and independence had left unhindered. It sufficed for them to continue in their established manner, punctuated only by a grand treat of opera-going (from the gallery) whenever Hans returned home for a visit.

Observing the group they formed this evening, one could scarcely wish for any alteration to their mode of existence. They were all similarly diminutive, perfectly scaled to their miniature rooms. Mrs. Meyrick was reading aloud from a French volume; she was a lively little woman, half-French, half-Scotch, possessing a pretty articulateness of speech that seemed to conjure daylight within her listener's understanding. Though not yet fifty, her rippling hair, concealed beneath a Quakerish net cap, was chiefly gray, while her eyebrows remained brown, matching the bright eyes below them. Her black dress, resembling a priest's cassock with its rows of buttons, suited a neat figure barely five feet tall.

The daughters mirrored their mother, save that Mab inherited Hans's light hair and complexion, accompanied by a bossy, irregular brow and other quaint features reminiscent of him. Everything about them was compact: from the firm coils of their hair, fastened back *à la Chinoise*, to their gray skirts standing in Puritan nonconformity to the prevailing fashion, which at that time would have demanded that four feminine circumferences occupy every inch of free space in the front parlor. If they had been waxworks, all four might have been packed effortlessly into a fashionable lady's traveling trunk. Their faces seemed brimming with speech, as if their minds had been shelled, akin to horse-chestnuts, becoming brightly visible.

The only large entity of its kind in the room was Hafiz, the Persian cat, comfortably perched on the brown leather back of a chair, occasionally opening his large eyes to ensure the lower animals were not causing mischief. The book Mrs. Meyrick held was Erckmann-Chatrian's *Histoire d'un Conscrit*. Having just concluded reading it aloud, Mab—who had dropped her work to the floor while stretching her head forward to fix her gaze upon the reader—exclaimed, "I think that is the finest story in the world."

"Of course, Mab!" replied Amy. "It is the last you have heard. Whatever pleases you is the best in its turn."

"It is hardly to be called a story," corrected Kate. "It is a fragment of history brought near us with a powerful telescope. We can discern the soldiers' faces; no, it is more than that—we can hear everything—we can almost hear their hearts beating."

"I don't care what you call it," said Mab, flicking away her thimble. "Call it a chapter in Revelations. It compels me to do something good, something grand. It fills me with sorrow for everyone. It makes me like Schiller—I want to take the world in my arms and kiss it. I must kiss you instead, little mother!" She flung her arms around her mother's neck.

"Whenever you are in that mood, Mab, down goes your work," Amy remarked. "It would be doing something good to finish your cushion without soiling it."

"Oh—oh—oh!" groaned Mab as she stooped to retrieve her work and thimble. "I wish I had three wounded conscripts to care for."

"You would spill their beef tea while you were talking," Amy retorted.

"Poor Mab! Don't be hard on her," interjected the mother. "Give me the embroidery now, child. You proceed with your enthusiasm, and I will continue with the pink and white poppy."

"Well, ma, I think you are more caustic than Amy," Kate observed, pulling her head back to examine her drawing.

"Oh—oh—oh!" cried Mab once more, rising and stretching her arms. "I wish something wonderful would happen. I feel like the deluge. The waters of the great deep are broken up, and the windows of heaven are opened. I must sit down and play the scales."

Mab began opening the piano while the others laughed at this dramatic climax, when suddenly a cab halted before the house, followed immediately by a rapid rap of the knocker.

"Dear me!" exclaimed Mrs. Meyrick, springing to her feet. "It is after ten, and Phoebe is already asleep." She hastened outward, leaving the parlor door ajar.

"Mr. Deronda!" The girls heard this exclamation from their mother. Mab clasped her hands, whispering loudly, "There now! Something _is_ going to happen." Kate and Amy abandoned their work in astonishment. However, Deronda's tone in reply was so low they could not catch his words, and Mrs. Meyrick promptly closed the parlor door.

"I know I am relying on your goodness in a most extraordinary manner," Deronda continued after delivering his brief narrative. "But you can imagine how helpless I feel with a young creature like this on my hands. I could not accompany her among strangers, and in her nervous state, I should dread bringing her into a house full of servants. I have placed my trust in your mercy. I hope you will not deem my act unwarrantable."

"On the contrary," Mrs. Meyrick responded. "You have honored me by trusting me. I perceive your difficulty. Please bring her in. I shall go and prepare the girls."

While Deronda retreated to the cab, Mrs. Meyrick re-entered the parlor and declared, "Here is someone to care for instead of your wounded conscripts, Mab: a poor girl who intended to drown herself in despair. Mr. Deronda discovered her only in time to save her. He brought her along in his boat and knew nothing else safe to do with her, so he has entrusted us and brought her here. It appears she is a Jewess, but quite refined, he says—she knows Italian and music."

The three girls, wondering and expectant, moved forward, standing near one another in a mute confidence that they shared the same feelings under this appeal to their compassion. Mab looked somewhat awe-stricken, as if

Deronda was not a man defined by the label of romantic; indeed, to pin that description upon him would be a distortion of his essential nature. Yet, beneath his surface of calm composure and self-imposed restraint burned a fervent fire, a capacity to distill poetry and romance from the mundane fabric of daily existence. Such poetic resonance exists everywhere, abundant as ever, save for those phlegmatic souls who, regardless of the era, dismiss it as a delusion born of error. These sentiments thrive alongside the microscope and within the rattling confines of railway carriages; it is only the sterile vacuum of polite society, filled with gentlemen and ladies clad in propriety, that banishes them. How else could the vast apparatus of heaven and earth—from the furthest reaches of the firmament down to the tender, nurturing bosom of a mother—fail to generate poetry for a mind devoid of awe and tenderness, lacking that electric sense of fellowship that vibrates between the near and the distant? For Deronda, the discovery of Mirah struck with the same heart-shattering force as the fates visited upon Orestes or Rinaldo. He remained awake for half the night, reliving the moments since he first glimpsed her on the riverbank, revisiting them with a freshness and vividness unique to emotive memory.

When he attempted to quell this urgent inner vision by opening a book, the printed words dissolved into mere scaffolding. Through this network of ink, he perceived everything with startling clarity—not only the tangible events of the preceding two hours but also the swirling possibilities of what had been and what might be, scenarios fed by the warm, pulsing blood of passionate hope and fear. A specific current within his own experience caused Mirah's desperate search for her mother to seize his imagination with peculiar intensity. His initial impulse was pure sympathy: if living relatives existed in London, methods akin to scientific experimentation could locate them, provided the necessary machinery was engaged. However, the complex emotions rooted in his own kinship anxieties immediately infused his concern for Mirah with a darker hue. The desire to uncover his own mother, or even learn of her, was perpetually shadowed by dread. In projecting this onto Mirah, he realized that locating the mother and brother from whom she had been torn away as an infant could prove to be a calamity rather than a cure. While she spoke in the boat of their goodness, that virtue might have stemmed solely from her ignorant innocence and yearning recollection; the decade and a half since their separation was ample time for corruption and decay.

Despite his natural inclination to side with the victims of prejudice, Deronda's practical interest had never truly extended toward existing Jews. Whether they paraded in fine apparel or lurked in shadowed by-streets, the facts he knew were largely repugnant. He assumed that learned and accomplished Jews had abandoned their faith to merge with the peoples of their adopted lands. While scorn cast specifically at a Jew for individual failings would rouse his deepest sympathies, the indiscriminate hatred of a race often strikes a specimen who has well-earned it, deserving to be gibbeted as a rascal son of Adam. One need look no further than the Caribs, who know little of theology, regarding theft as a practice peculiarly linked to Christian tenets; they likely possess experimental grounds for such an opinion. Deronda could not escape knowing the ugly stories surrounding Jewish characteristics and occupations. Though he protested against the severance of past and present history, he shared the common failure of never caring to draw special conclusions beyond viewing Jews as retaining the virtues and vices of a long-oppressed race. Now, however, Mirah's longing forced a closer scrutiny of details, summoning disagreeable images of what it might mean to discover this middle-aged Jewess and her son. Granted, there was the exquisite refinement and charm of Mirah herself, creating a presumption in favor of her immediate kin—but he must wait. Perhaps through Mrs. Meyrick he could gather guiding hints directly from Mirah's lips. Her voice, her accent, her looks—all the sweet purity that clothed her like a consecrating garment—made him shrink even further from associating her, either ideologically or practically, with anything hateful or contaminating.

Yet these fine words, used to fumigate and obscure unpleasant realities, are not the language of thought. Deronda's mind raced through rapid images of potential futures: he saw himself guided by an official scout into a dingy alleyway, entering a dim doorway where a hawk-eyed, rough-haired, unwashed woman bargained down a hungry girl's last scrap of finery. Or, in a quarter rendered more hideous by its smartness, he found himself breathing the air of a young Jew, talkative and familiar, eager to display his acquaintance with gentlemanly tastes and unfastidious in any transaction they favored. This was the brief chapter of his experience in this realm. Do excuse him; his mind was not prone to spontaneously conjure insulting ideas or to practice a wit that equates Moses with an advertisement sheet. But he was currently governed by dread. If Mirah's parents had been Christian, the sole difference would be that his forebodings would have been fed by wider knowledge. It was the habit of his mind to link dread with unknown parentage, and in this instance, as in his own, the connection was entirely reasonable.

But what was to be done with Mirah? She required shelter and protection in the fullest sense. All his chivalrous sentiment rallied to insist that the sooner and more fully he could secure the interest of others beside himself, the better he fulfilled her claims upon him. He possessed no right to provide for her entirely, though he might be capable of doing so; the sheer depth of the impression she had made compelled him to believe she understood herself to be entirely independent of him. Vague visions of the future, which he tried to dispel as fantastical, left behind an anxiety stronger than any rational motive he could articulate: that those observing his actions closely should be acquainted from the outset with the full history of his relation to Mirah. He had learned to hate secrecy regarding the grand ties and obligations of his life, hating it all the more because a powerful spell of interwoven sensibilities hindered him from breaking such silences. Deronda had vowed to himself that, since the truths which disgrace mortals are not always of their own making, he would ensure the truth never became a disgrace to another through his own act. He lived in constant terror of breaking this vow, falling into the apologetic philosophy that explains the world away as containing nothing better than one's own conduct.

At one moment, he resolved to recount the entire adventure to Sir Hugo and Lady Mallinger the following morning at breakfast. Then, the possibility that something entirely new might reveal itself during his next visit to Mrs. Meyrick checked this impulse. Finally, he succumbed to sleep, concluding that he would wait until that visit had been made.

"It will hardly be denied that even in this frail and corrupted world, we sometimes meet persons who, in their very mien and aspect, as well as in the whole habit of life, manifest such a signature and stamp of virtue, as to make our judgment of them a matter of intuition rather than the result of continued examination." —Alexander Knox: quoted in Southey's Life of Wesley.

Mirah claimed she had slept well that night. When she descended in Mab's black dress, her dark hair curling in fresh, damp fibrils as it dried from its recent bath, she possessed the appearance of someone reclaiming comfort after a long season of sorrow and vigilance that had pale her cheeks and etched blue semicircles beneath her eyes. It was Mab who carried her breakfast and escorted her downstairs, wearing a distinct pride in the effect of a pair of tiny felt slippers she had hurried out to purchase, for there were no shoes in the house small enough for Mirah. Her borrowed dress ended just above her ankles, exposing the cheap garments that molded themselves to her feet, appearing to Mab as an adornment as exquisite as the sheaths of budding flowers. The farthing buckles were jewels.

"Oh, if you please, mamma?" cried Mab, clasping her hands and bowing toward Mirah's feet as they entered the parlor. "Look at the slippers, how beautifully they fit! I declare she is like Queen Budoor—'two delicate feet, the work of the protecting and all-recompensing Creator, support her; and I wonder how they can sustain what is above them.'"

Mirah glanced down at her own feet with a childlike curiosity and then offered a smile to Mrs. Meyrick. The mother said inwardly, *One could hardly imagine this creature harboring an evil thought. Yet, wise counsel dictates caution.* She returned Mirah's smile and replied, "I fear the feet have borne their burden a little too often lately. But today she shall rest and be my companion."

"And she will tell you so many things, and I shall not hear them," grumbled Mab, feeling herself thrust into the first volume of a delightful romance, forced to skip chapters because she had duties to attend to among her pupils.

Kate had already departed to sketch along the river, and Amy was away on business errands. This separation was precisely what the mother desired: solitude with this stranger, whose narrative must inevitably be one of sorrow, yet one that demanded to be heard.

The small front parlor stood that morning as a sanctuary. Sunlight spilled onto the river, and a soft breeze drifted through the open window. The walls bore witness to a glorious, silent cloud of observers: the Virgin soaring amidst her cherubic escort; Grand Melancholia with her solemn universe; the Prophets and Sibyls; the School of Athens; the Last Supper; mystic groups where distant ages condensed into a single breath; the grave heads of Holbein and Rembrandt; the Tragic Muse; children of the previous century lost in reverie or play; Italian poets—all present through the medium of black and white. The neat mother, having weathered her trials and emerged with a face still cheerful, sorted colored wools for her embroidery. Hafiz purred on the window-ledge; the clock on the mantelpiece ticked without haste; and the occasional rumble of wheels seemed to lie beyond the more massive, central quiet. Mrs. Meyrick perceived this silence as the most potent invitation for her companion to speak, choosing not to disrupt it with remark.

Mirah sat opposite in her former posture, hands clasped on her lap, ankles crossed, her eyes initially drifting slowly over the surrounding objects before settling with a sort of placid reverence upon Mrs. Meyrick. Finally, she began to speak softly.

"I remember my mother's face better than anything else; yet I was not seven when I was taken away, and I am nineteen now."

"I can understand that," said Mrs. Meyrick. "Some earliest impressions endure the longest."

"Oh, yes, it was the earliest. I think my life began with waking up and loving my mother's face; it was so near to me, and her arms were round me, and she sang to me. One hymn she sang so often, so often: and then she taught me to sing it with her; it was the first song I ever knew. They were always Hebrew hymns she sang; and because I never knew the meaning of the words, they seemed filled with nothing but our love and happiness. When I lay in my little bed and it was all white above me, she would bend over me, between me and the white ceiling, and sing in a sweet, low voice. I can dream myself back into that time when I am awake, and it often returns to me in my sleep—my hand is very small; I raise it to her face, and she kisses it. Sometimes in my dreams I begin to tremble and think that we are both dead; but then I wake up, and my hand lies like this, and for a moment I hardly know myself. But if I could see my mother again, I should know her instantly."

"You must expect some change after twelve years," said Mrs. Meyrick, gently. "See my grey hair: ten years ago it was bright brown. Days and months pace over us like restless little birds, leaving the marks of their feet backward and forward; especially when they are birds with heavy hearts—then they tread heavily."

"Ah, I am sure her heart has been heavy for want of me. But to feel her joy if we could meet again, and I could make her know I love her and give her deep comfort after all her mourning! If that were possible, I should mind nothing; I should be glad that I have lived through my trouble. I did despair. The world seemed miserable and wicked; no one helped me so that I could bear their looks and words; I felt that my mother was dead, and death was the only path to her. But then, in the last moment—yesterday, when I longed for the water to close over me—and I thought that death was the best image of mercy—goodness came to me alive, and I felt trust in the living. And—it is strange—but I began to hope that she was living too. And now, with you—here—this morning, peace and hope have flooded into me. I want nothing; I can wait; because I hope and believe and am grateful—oh, so grateful! You have not thought evil of me—you have not despised me."

Mirah spoke with low-toned fervor, sitting as still as a painting throughout.

"Many others would have felt as we do, my dear," said Mrs. Meyrick, feeling a mist rise in her eyes as she looked at her embroidery.

"But I did not meet them—they did not come to me."

"How was it that you were taken from your mother?"

"Ah, I am a long while coming to that. It is dreadful to speak of, yet I must tell you—I must tell you everything. My father—he was the one who took me away. I thought we were only going on a little journey; and I was pleased. There was a box containing all my little things. But we boarded a ship and drifted farther and farther from the land. Then I became ill; and I thought it would never end—it was the first misery, and it seemed endless. But at last we landed. I knew nothing then, and believed whatever my father said. He comforted me, telling me I should return to my mother. But it was America we had reached, and it was many long years before we returned to Europe. At first, I often asked my father when we were going back; and I tried to learn writing quickly, for I wanted to write to my mother. But one day, when he found me attempting to compose a letter, he lifted me onto his knee and told me that my mother and brother were dead—that was why we did not return. I remember my brother a little; he carried me once, but he was not always at home. I believed my father when he said they were dead. I saw them under the earth when he said they were there, with their eyes forever closed. I never doubted its truth; and I used to cry every night in my bed for a long while. Then, when she visited me so often in my sleep, I thought she must be living nearby, though I could not always see her, and that comforted me. I was never afraid in the dark because of that; and very often during the day I would shut my eyes, bury my face, and try to see her and hear her singing. I eventually came to do that without closing my eyes."

Mirah paused, her face bearing a sweet contentment, as if she were inhabiting her happy vision while gazing out toward the river.

"Still, your father was not unkind to you, I hope," said Mrs. Meyrick after a moment, anxious to draw her back from the memory.

"No; he petted me and took pains to teach me. He was an actor; and I discovered later that the 'Coburg' I used to hear him mention at home was a theater. But he had more involvement with the theater than mere acting. He had not always been an actor; he had been a teacher and knew many languages. His acting was not particularly good, I think, but he managed the stage, wrote, and translated plays. An Italian lady, a singer, lived with us for a long time. Both of them taught me, and I also had a master who made me memorize and recite. I worked quite hard, though I was so small; and I was not nine when I first stepped onto the

Knowledge is power, a maxim often repeated until it loses its sting, yet who has ever truly weighed the crushing gravity of Ignorance? While knowledge patiently constructs empires over centuries, accumulating records and refining skills, ignorance acts with the sudden, destructive velocity of a wildfire. In a single hour, it consumes the libraries built by ages, feeding its flames on the charred bones of generations and roasting its meager meal on the ashes of history. Knowledge expands the horizon, transforming necessity into artistry; ignorance, drunk on the seventh day of creation, brings only a flint and a match to extinguish the rainbow, shrinking the vibrant cosmos into a singular, suffocating blackness. True knowledge walks with a conscience, aware of the boundaries between what *must* be and what *may* be; ignorance, however, is a blind titan. Left unchecked, it delights in toppling the pillars of human goodness, plunging the sanctuaries of joy into the darkness of a buried Babylon. When one examines the trajectory of a single life, it becomes evident that a lack of understanding regarding the true bonds of causality—a delusion akin to optical failure that mistakes distant horizons for immediate grasp—drives the misguided soul straight into ruin.

It was half-past ten in the morning when Gwendolen Harleth emerged from her gloomy journey from Leubronn, arriving at the station destined for Offendene. No carriage awaited her, nor any friend; a telegram sent from Dover had specified a later train, and in her impatience to escape the stagnation of London, she had departed without envisioning the solitude of an unannounced arrival at a remote halt—one of those stations positioned not for convenience, but as an equidistant point from nowhere. Abandoned as a *femme sole* amidst her towering trunks, she waited in vain for a vehicle summoned from the Railway Inn. Inside the waiting-room, the atmosphere was a compound of decay: the walls were stained with dirt, a decanter of flat water stood dusty and useless, and posters in bold, mocking letters urged passersby to repent and convert. These elements coalesced into a dreary tableau that mirrored the domestic catastrophe unfolding in her heart. Hurrying toward the outer door, she sought the lane and the fields, yet even the sunlight seemed tainted with melancholy. Autumnal leaves trembled in the chill breeze, and the grass shivered beneath the wind, which ruffled the feathers of a cock and two hens—stragglers who had lost their brood and wandered aimlessly, unable to comprehend their displacement.

The railway official, a newcomer to the district, observed her luggage with an innocent expression that quickly curdled into condescension. His gaze suggested he viewed her not as a lady of standing, but as someone of negligible consequence, a judgment reinforced by her solitary status. Outside, a dilapidated barouche was being wheeled forward by an elderly laborer, its condition contemptible yet emblematic of the turn of fate. Such trivialities, though seemingly insignificant, possess a cumulative weight capable of shaping the entire architecture of a life. They enter the psyche with the force of a theory, molding mood and motive. Philosophy itself is not immune to such determinants; imagine a subtle thinker stranded alone at a bleak, irrelevant outpost, devoid of income, forced to speculate on the origins of existence and the rationale behind a world that treats intellect as currency. How much more potent must these trifles be for a young woman of fastidious taste, draped in an Indian shawl, surrounded by twenty cubic feet of luggage, and suddenly acutely conscious of poverty? These details crushed Gwendolen, quelling her resistance. Why endure hardship, ugliness, and humiliation? This was the threshold of returning home, and the sample provided was grim.

As she settled into the uneven ride, the discomfort of the barouche—with one trunk squeezing the driver and another tethered to the seat before her—became the backdrop for her internal monologue. From Leubronn, her vision had been one of continued exile abroad, sustained by a meager allowance. She feared a dull existence overseas, imagining herself trapped in the tedious monotony of foreign poverty alongside Alice, Bertha, Fanny, and Isabel, while her mother descended further into melancholy. Yet, she refused to submit. Weariness and disgust, however, had begun to mimic an uncomfortable awakening, far more distressing than the uneasy dreams preceding her flight. The self-satisfaction she had once derived from admiring her reflection in the mirror had evaporated, replaced by a hollow realization of futility. Was there any value in being charming, clever, or resolute? Events seemed to conspire against her, and men, she concluded with a surge of bitterness, were hateful. Or rather, she hated them specifically for being obstacles that prevented her from utilizing stolen goods. Anger had shifted its target; she was furious with Grandcourt not merely for his actions, but for being the barrier that denied her the marriage she coveted, the catalyst for her current misery.

The drive drew to a close as the vehicle crested the avenue, bringing the house into view. A figure appeared beneath the portico, triggering a rush of emotion that transcended her own grievances. As Gwendolen leaped from the carriage, she beheld her mother's face, radiant yet etched with fresh lines of sorrow. Instinctively, Gwendolen threw her arms around her mother's neck, momentarily dissolving her own pain into the shared grief of her parent. Behind them stood the four "superfluous" girls, each carrying a private world of insignificance to the outside observer, yet all sensing Gwendolen's presence as a reprieve from their own despair. Where Gwendolen was, something interesting might occur; even her brisk dismissal—"Now go away, girls"—carried the comforting authority of decisive action. Miss Merry, usually marked by a meek depression that defied explanation given her attachment to the family, accepted the shift in circumstances without expectation of greeting, busy instead with the trunks and the coachman's wages. Mrs. Davilow and Gwendolen ascended the stairs, retreating into the black-and-yellow bedroom.

"Never mind, mamma dear," Gwendolen whispered, pressing a handkerchief against her mother's streaming tears. "I don't mind. I will do something. I will be something. Things will come right. It seemed worse because I was away. Come now! You must be glad I am here."

Every word resonated with a sudden, fierce clarity. A wave of compassionate resolve swept through her, crystallizing vague ambitions into concrete plans. In that fleeting moment, she perceived a path to significance. Her mother, oblivious to the depths of their financial abyss, regarded her daughter with adoration. "Bless you, my good, good darling! I can be happy, if you can!"

Yet, as the day progressed, the initial euphoria ebbed. The slippery rocks and weedy corners of reality reasserted themselves. Courage waned as misfortune transformed from a mere announcement into a tyrannical occupant. Initially, the grandeur of the house still offered a shield of personal ease: the spacious silence, the effortless maintenance of her toilette, and a private tray bearing her favorite foods. She had requested seclusion, asking her mother to keep everyone away so they might be alone.

Descending into the drawing-room, Gwendolen presented herself as fresh as a newly dipped swan, leaning against the cushions beside her mother. The crisis had not yet fully confronted her. With deliberate intent, she asked, "What have you thought of doing, exactly, mamma?"

"Oh, my dear, the next step is to leave this house. Mr. Haynes is delighted to receive it now, just as he was when we took it. Lord Brackenshaw's agent, Bazley, will arrange everything to our advantage. He is not an ill-natured man."

"I cannot help thinking that Lord Brackenshaw would let you stay here rent-free, mamma," Gwendolen remarked, applying her talents not to commerce, but to discerning the admiration her charms inspired.

"My dear child, Lord Brackenshaw is in Scotland and knows nothing of us. Neither your uncle nor I would dare approach him. Moreover, what could we do in this house without servants or funds to heat it? The sooner we depart, the better. We have nothing to carry but our clothes."

"I suppose you mean to go abroad, then?" Gwendolen asked, clinging to the scenario she had rehearsed.

"Oh, no, dear, no. How could we travel? You never learned anything about income and expenses," Mrs. Davilow replied, forcing a smile before adding mournfully, "which makes it so much harder for you, my pet."

"But where are we to go?" Gwendolen pressed, a new current of fear coursing through her veins.

"It is all decided. Some furniture will be moved from the rectory—all that can be spared." Mrs. Davilow hesitated, dreading the shock she must inflict. Gwendolen watched her with tense expectancy, falling silent.

"It is Sawyer's Cottage we are to go to."

Initially, Gwendolen remained motionless, her face draining of color with justified rage. Then, with haughty defiance, she declared, "That is impossible. Something else ought to have been considered. My uncle ought not to allow that. I will not submit to it."

"My sweet child, what else could have been arranged? Your uncle is as kind as he can be, but he suffers greatly; he has a family to raise. Do you understand? We have nothing. Absolutely nothing, except what he and my sister provide. They have acted wisely and actively, and we must strive to earn something. Your mother

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Lady Violet Lebas, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of London itself. She sat at her mahogany desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from Mr. Andrew Lang, his sprawling, confident script detailing his latest thoughts on literature.

Violet sighed, tracing the edge of the parchment with a silver letter-opener. Lang was a man of undeniable erudition, yet his letters were suffocated by the very patriarchal complacency she so deeply despised. He wrote to her of Virgil, of modern English poetry, of Fielding and Rochefoucauld, positioning himself as the benevolent instructor to her "undefeated resolution to admire only the right things."

"The right things," she murmured aloud, the words tasting like ash. What he meant, of course, was the established canon—the texts elevated by centuries of wealthy, privileged men who saw their own reflections in the exploits of conquerors and the musings of comfortable philosophers.

She turned to his pages on Fielding. Lang excused the novelist's coarse morality, arguing that men must be pardoned the "vices of young blood" so long as they were brave and generous. He painted a nostalgic picture of a "lusty old militant world," entirely blind to the fact that this merry England was built upon the bruised backs of women and the indentured silence of the poor. He dismissed the systemic inequalities that left women like Sophia Western with no recourse but to forgive the betrayals of their husbands.

In this domestic theater, where the loom of daily life spun its accustomed patterns while we weave ideal webs of fantasy, the truth often appears as a startling anomaly. Consider the Arrowpoints. Their magnificent mansion, fitted with regard to the greatest expense, had become too hot for Klesmer, much like a politician's office during an insurrection against the established order. The owners, astonished by the uprising they had prepared under their very noses, watched as the plain people perceived the cracks in their foundation.

Among the many guests in the house stood a new pretender to Catherine's hand: Mr. Bult, a political man of good family who confidently expected a peerage and felt on public grounds that he required a larger fortune to support the title properly. Heiresses vary; persons interested in one beforehand are prepared to find her too yellow or too red, tall and toppling or short and square, violent and capricious or moony and insipid. Yet, in every case, it is taken for granted that she will consider herself an appendage to her fortune, marrying where others think her fortunes ought to go. Nature, however, not only accommodates herself ill to our favorite practices by making "only children" daughters, but also now and then endows the misplaced daughter with a clear head and a strong will. The Arrowpoints had already felt some anxiety owing to these endowments of their Catherine. She would not accept the view of her social duty which required her to marry a needy nobleman or a commoner on the ladder toward nobility; and they were not without uneasiness concerning her persistence in declining suitable offers. As to the possibility of her being in love with Klesmer, they were not at all uneasy—a very common sort of blindness. For in general mortals have a great power of being astonished at the presence of an effect toward which they have done everything, and at the absence of an effect toward which they had done nothing but desire it. Parents are astonished at the ignorance of their sons, though they have used the most time-honored and expensive means of securing it; husbands and wives are mutually astonished at the loss of affection which they have taken no pains to keep; and all of us in our turn are apt to be astonished that our neighbors do not admire us. In this way it happens that the truth seems highly improbable. The truth is something different from the habitual lazy combinations begotten by our wishes. The Arrowpoints' hour of astonishment was come.

When there is a passion between an heiress and a proud independent-spirited man, it is difficult for them to come to an understanding; but the difficulties are likely to be overcome unless the proud man secures himself by a constant alibi. Brief meetings after studied absence are potent in disclosure: but more potent still is frequent companionship, with full sympathy in taste and admirable qualities on both sides; especially where the one is in the position of teacher and the other is delightedly conscious of receptive ability which also gives the teacher delight. The situation is famous in history, and has no less charm now than it had in the days of Abelard.

But this kind of comparison had not occurred to the Arrowpoints when they first engaged Klesmer to come down to Quetcham. To have a first-rate musician in your house is a privilege of wealth; Catherine's musical talent demanded every advantage; and she particularly desired to use her quieter time in the country for more thorough study. Klesmer was not yet a Liszt, understood to be adored by ladies of all European countries with the exception of Lapland: and even with that understanding it did not follow that he would make proposals to an heiress. No musician of honor would do so. Still less was it conceivable that Catherine would give him the slightest pretext for such daring. The large check that Mr. Arrowpoint was to draw in Klesmer's name seemed to make him as safe an inmate as a footman. Where marriage is inconceivable, a girl's sentiments are safe.

Klesmer was eminently a man of honor, but marriages rarely begin with formal proposals, and moreover, Catherine's limit of the conceivable did not exactly correspond with her mother's. Outsiders might have been more apt to think that Klesmer's position was dangerous for himself if Miss Arrowpoint had been an acknowledged beauty; not taking into account that the most powerful of all beauty is that which reveals itself after sympathy and not before it. There is a charm of eye and lip which comes with every little phrase that certifies delicate perception or fine judgment, with every unostentatious word or smile that shows a heart awake to others; and no sweep of garment or turn of figure is more satisfying than that which enters as a restoration of confidence that one person is present on whom no intention will be lost. What dignity of meaning goes on gathering in frowns and laughs which are never observed in the wrong place; what suffused adorableness in a human frame where there is a mind that can flash out comprehension and hands that can execute finely! The more obvious beauty, also adorable sometimes—one may say it without blasphemy—begins by being an apology for folly, and ends like other apologies in becoming tiresome by iteration; and that Klesmer, though very susceptible to it, should have a passionate attachment to Miss Arrowpoint, was no more a paradox than any other triumph of a manifold sympathy over a monotonous attraction. We object less to be taxed with the enslaving excess of our passions than with our deficiency in wider passion; but if the truth were known, our reputed intensity is often the dullness of not knowing what else to do with ourselves. Tannhäuser, one suspects, was a knight of ill-furnished imagination, hardly of larger discourse than a heavy Guardsman; Merlin had certainly seen his best days, and was merely repeating himself, when he fell into that hopeless captivity; and we know that Ulysses felt so manifest an ennui under similar circumstances that Calypso herself furthered his departure. There is indeed a report that he afterward left Penelope; but since she was habitually absorbed in worsted work, and it was probably from her that Telemachus got his mean, pettifogging disposition, always anxious about the property and the daily consumption of meat, no inference can be drawn from this already dubious scandal as to the relation between companionship and constancy.

Klesmer was as versatile and fascinating as a young Ulysses on a sufficient acquaintance—one whom nature seemed to have first made generously and then to have added music as a dominant power using all the abundant rest, and, as in Mendelssohn, finding expression for itself not only in the highest finish of execution, but in that fervor of creative work and theoretic belief which pierces the whole future of a life with the delight of congruous devoted purpose. His foibles of arrogance and vanity did not exceed such as may be found in the best English families; and Catherine Arrowpoint had no corresponding restlessness to clash with his: notwithstanding her native kindliness she was perhaps too coolly firm and self-sustained. But she was one of those satisfactory creatures whose intercourse has the charm of discovery; whose integrity of faculty and expression begets a wish to know what they will say on all subjects or how they will perform whatever they undertake; so that they end by raising not only a continual expectation but a continual sense of fulfillment—the systole and diastole of blissful companionship. In such cases the outward presentment easily becomes what the image is to the worshipper. It was not long before the two became aware that each was interesting to the other; but the "how far" remained a matter of doubt. Klesmer did not conceive that Miss Arrowpoint was likely to think of him as a possible lover, and she was not accustomed to think of herself as likely to stir more than a friendly regard, or to fear the expression of more from any man who was not enamored of her fortune. Each was content to suffer some unshared sense of denial for the sake of loving the other's society a little too well; and under these conditions no need had been felt to restrict Klesmer's visits for the last year either in

The inheritance of art, much like the apportionment of the Promised Land, demands a war of attrition from every claimant; the divine dispensation offers only a title, a prophetic promise devoid of actual possession. To clutch a map of an estate one does not yet hold is to possess a flimsy copyhold tenure, and to imagine casting one's shoe over Eden provides no guarantee that one will ever plant a boot upon a single acre of that soil.

The most stubborn delusions mortals harbor regarding themselves stem from a lack of evidence beyond a perpetual, spontaneous throbbing of self-satisfaction—a latent seed of madness, a delirious certainty that they can shift the globe without understanding their leverage or their footing.

"Please go to church, Mamma," Gwendolen declared the following morning. "I prefer seeing Herr Klesmer alone." (He had replied to her note stating he would meet her at eleven.)

"That seems hardly proper, I believe," Mrs. Davilow interjected, her anxiety palpable.

"Our concerns are too weighty for us to adhere to such nonsensical regulations," Gwendolen retorted, her voice dripping with disdain. "They are insulting as well as absurd."

"You wouldn't mind if Isabel sat with you? She could read in a corner."

"No; she cannot. She would gnaw her nails and stare. It would be intolerably irritating. Trust my judgment, Mamma; I must be alone. Send them all to church."

As was inevitable, Gwendolen prevailed. Only Miss Merry and two of the girls remained behind, staying inside to lend the house the appearance of habitation by lingering at the dining-room windows.

It was a resplendent Sunday morning. The melancholy, fading sunlight of autumn lay heavy upon the half-mown grass, filtering gently through the panes in slanted ribbons of brilliance. These beams illuminated the antique furniture, the glass panels reflecting the room's interior, the tapestried chairs adorned with their faded floral wreaths, the dark, enigmatic paintings, and the superannuated organ at which Gwendolen had delighted in playing Saint Cecilia upon her first triumphant arrival. Through the open doors of the antechamber, a crowd of pallid, dusty trinkets caught the eye—the very place where she had previously enacted the role of Hermione in her Greek dress. This final memory was vividly active in her mind; surely Herr Klesmer had been struck by the admiration of her posture and expression then? Regardless of what he had actually said, or what she imagined he had thought, the possibility was now scrutinized with the sharpest intensity. Perhaps she had never in her life felt so inwardly dependent, so acutely aware of her need for another's validation. A fresh fluttering animated her spirit, introducing a new element of deliberation into her self-assessment, a quality that had previously been a blissful, intuitive gift. Yet, the recurring burden of her internal monologue remained: Klesmer had seen very little of her, and any unfavorable conclusion he reached would rest on a foundation too narrow to support it. She truly believed herself capable of anything.

To pass the time, she gathered her volumes and musical scores, arranging them atop the piano, intent on classifying them. Then, catching the reflection of her movements in the glass panel, she was drawn into contemplating her own image and walked toward it. Clad in black, devoid of ornament, with the warm whiteness of her skin framed between her light-brown crown of hair and her square-cut bodice, she might have tempted an artist to attempt the Roman trick of a statue carved from black, white, and tawny marble. Watching her reflection advance slowly, she thought, *I am beautiful*—not with exultation, but with grave resolve. Beauty, after all, was the condition upon which she most desperately required external testimony. Should anyone object to the turn of her nose or the contour of her neck and chin, she lacked the sense that she could immediately demonstrate her power of attainment in these branches of feminine perfection.

There was little time to waste in such contemplation before the clatter of wheels, the loud ringing of bells, and the swinging of doors confirmed she was not, by some accident, to be disappointed. This slight intensification of her inner flutter was accompanied by a dread, despite her self-confidence. She feared Klesmer as part of that unmanageable world existing independently of her wishes—a corrosive force that would continue to burn regardless of whether she smiled or frowned at it. Poor creature! She stood at a higher crisis of her woman's fate than in her last encounter with Grandcourt. Previously, the question had been whether she should accept a specific man as a husband. Now, the deepest fold of her inquiry was whether she needed a husband at all—whether she could not achieve substantial independence and know gratified ambition without bondage.

Herr Klesmer executed his most deferential bow in the wide doorway of the antechamber, displaying simultaneously the respect due to the finest gray kerseymere trousers and immaculate gloves (for the 'masters of those who know' are, happily, altogether human). Gwendolen received him with unusual gravity, extending her hand. "It is most kind of you to come, Herr Klesmer. I hope you have not thought me presumptuous."

"I took your wish as a command that did me honor," Klesmer replied with matching seriousness. He was indeed setting aside his own affairs to devote his utmost attention to whatever Gwendolen might have to say; yet his temperament remained in a state of excitation from the events of the previous day, likely lending his expressions a sharper, more biting edge than usual.

For once, Gwendolen was under such a strain of emotion that she forgot formalities. Standing near the piano, she waited, and Klesmer positioned himself near the opposite end, his back to the light, his terribly omniscient eyes fixed upon her. No affectation could avail her, and she began without delay.

"I wish to consult you, Herr Klesmer. We have lost all our fortune; we have nothing. I must earn my own bread, and I desire to provide for my mother, saving her from any hardship. The only path I can conceive—and I should prefer it above anything else—is to become an actress, to go on the stage. But, naturally, I aspire to a high position, and I thought—if you believe I could"—here Gwendolen grew slightly nervous—"it would be preferable for me to be a singer, to study vocal arts as well."

Klesmer placed his hat upon the piano and folded his arms as if concentrating his entire being.

"I know," Gwendolen resumed, shifting from pale to pink and back again—"I know that my method of singing is extremely defective; I have been ill-taught. I could be better taught; I could study. And you will understand my wish: to sing and act, like Grisi, represents a much higher station. Naturally, I should wish to attain the highest rank possible. I can rely on your judgment. I am certain you will tell me the truth."

Gwendolen possessed a conviction that now, making this serious appeal, the truth would prove favorable.

Yet Klesmer remained silent. He swiftly removed his gloves, tossed them into his hat, rested his hands on his hips, and walked to the far end of the room. He was filled with compassion for this girl; he sought to place a guard around his speech. When he turned again, he regarded her with a mild frown of inquiry. "You have never seen anything, I think, of artists and their lives?—I mean of musicians, actors, artists of that kind?"

"Oh, no," Gwendolen replied, unperturbed by a reference to this obvious fact in the biography of a young lady hitherto well-provided-for.

"You are—pardon me," Klesmer said again, pausing near the piano—"coming to a conclusion on a matter of such magnitude; everything must be taken into consideration. Are you perhaps twenty?"

"I am twenty-one," Gwendolen said, a faint fear rising within her. "Do you think I am too old?"

Klesmer pursed his lower lip and shook his long fingers upward in a manner utterly enigmatic.

"Many persons begin later than others," Gwendolen remarked, betrayed by her habitual consciousness of possessing valuable information to dispense.

Klesmer paid her no heed, instead speaking with even greater studied gentleness. "You have probably not considered an artistic career until now: you did not entertain the notion, the longing—what shall I say?—you did not wish to be an actress, or anything of that sort, until this present trouble?"

"Not exactly: but I was fond of acting. I have acted; you saw me, if you remember—you saw me here in charades, and as Hermione," Gwendolen said, genuinely fearing Klesmer had forgotten.

"Yes, yes," he answered quickly, "I remember—I remember perfectly," and once more walked to the other end of the room. It proved difficult for him to restrain this peculiar movement whenever engaged in any argument, audible or silent.

Gwendolen felt herself being weighed. The delay was unpleasant. However, she did not yet conceive that the scales could tip to the wrong side, and it seemed to her only gracious to remark, "I shall be very much obliged to you for taking the trouble to give me your advice, whatever it may be."

"Miss Harleth," Klesmer said, turning toward her and speaking with a slight increase of accent, "I will veil nothing from you in this matter. I should reckon myself guilty if I put a false visage on things—making them appear too

"I question things but do not find One that will answer to my mind: And all the world appears unkind."

Gwendolen breathed a sigh of relief that the interview with Klesmer lay behind her before she faced the inevitable confrontation with her parents. Her resolve had hardened into a defensive shell; she knew only discomfort awaited her now, and she felt a grim capacity to endure whatever humiliation her uncle and aunt might propose.

The meeting did not occur until Monday, when Gwendolen accompanied her mother to the rectory. Their path had led them first to Sawyer's Cottage, where they inspected every cranny of the narrow rooms bathed in the harsh, unfiltered light of midday. Blinds and curtains were absent, exposing the bare walls and floors to the scrutiny of their gaze. The furnishings, intended to be scavenged from the rectory's stores, had not yet arrived.

"How *shall* you endure it, mamma?" Gwendolen asked as they turned back toward the road. She had kept silent during the tour of the dilapidated space—the little garden choked with cabbage stalks, the yew arbor clogged with dust and cobwebs. "You and the four girls confined to that closet of a room, with that sickly green-and-yellow wallpaper pressing against your eyes? And without me?"

"It will be some comfort that you need not share it, dear."

"If it were not for the necessity of earning money, I would sooner be there than go to serve as a governess."

"Do not pre-judge the situation, Gwendolen. If you secure the position at the palace, every luxury will surround you. You know how deeply you have always cherished such things. Do not mistake the hardship of climbing those steep, narrow stairs, listening to crockery clatter through the halls, and overhearing the girls' conversations, for the lesser evil."

"It feels like a nightmare," Gwendolen exclaimed impulsively. "I cannot believe Uncle will allow you to submit to such a fate. He ought to have taken other measures."

"Do not be unreasonable, my child. What else could he have done?"

"That was for him to discover. It strikes me as a peculiar world where people of our standing must sink so precipitously," Gwendolen remarked, her mental landscape shifting instantly to accommodate this new reality. In her imagination, other worlds were constructed with a logic that ensured her future remained agreeable, even amidst ruin.

It was her temperament that shaped her sentences under this crushing weight of adversity. She possessed the rhetorical skill to speak fittingly of vicissitudes in others' lives, though virtue was never her aspiration; she sought only to appear clever—a distinction that often rendered her words sharper than her intentions warranted. Yet, despite the acute awareness of her own bruises, she retained a capacity for contrition when her uncle and aunt greeted her with a warmth they had previously withheld. She was struck by the dignified cheerfulness with which they discussed the necessary economizing of their lifestyle and the education of the boys. Mr. Gascoigne's inherent worth, slightly dimmed by worldly exigencies much as a woman's poetic beauty is obscured by the demands of fashion, shone brightly under this sudden reduction of fortune. Prompt and methodical, he had resolved not only to retire his carriage but to scrutinize his worn suits, to forego meat at breakfast, to abandon periodicals, to withdraw Edwy from school, and to assume direct oversight of the boys' studies, ordering the entire household onto the sparest footing imaginable. For healthy souls, economy possesses its own pleasures, and the rector's spirit had permeated the domestic sphere. Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna, who always held their father as their ideal, genuinely missed nothing they truly valued, and in sincere sorrow, identified the most tragic aspect of their losses as the displacement of Mrs. Davilow and her children.

For the first time, Anna could merge her resentment regarding Rex with her sympathy for Gwendolen. Mrs. Gascoigne hoped that trouble might prove salutary for her niece, sparing herself the duty of adding bitterness to the medicine. Both women had busied themselves devising ways to procure blinds and curtains from the household stores, yet with delicate tact, they relegated these practicalities to the background, focusing instead on Gwendolen's journey and the comfort her presence brought to her mother.

Indeed, there was nothing for Gwendolen to latch onto that would justify extending her discontent toward those immediately surrounding her. Feeling shaken into a state of hyper-alertness, as if summoned to a drill everyone else was obediently attending to, she listened intently when her uncle began, in a voice of firm kindness, to outline the efforts he had undertaken to secure her a situation offering maximum advantage. Mr. Gascoigne had not forgotten Grandcourt, but the vagueness of any potential advances from that quarter was insufficient to dictate his actions to a man of his sound judgment; such uncertainties could not slacken his diligence in providing for his niece under current conditions.

"I felt there was no time to lose, Gwendolen," he said. "A position in a respectable family, where you will receive proper consideration, does not present itself at a moment's notice. However long we wait, we could scarcely find one where you would fare better than at Bishop Mompert's. I am known to both him and Mrs. Mompert, and that is an asset to you. Our correspondence has proceeded favorably, yet I am not surprised that Mrs. Mompert desires to see you before finalizing an engagement. She intends to arrange for you to meet her at Wanchester on her way to town. I daresay you will find the interview somewhat trying, my dear; but you will have time to prepare your mind."

"Do you know *why* she wishes to see me, uncle?" Gwendolen asked, her mind rapidly cataloging the various reasons an imaginary Mrs. Mompert, burdened with three daughters, might entertain—all reasons of a distinctly disagreeable nature for someone presenting herself for inspection.

The rector smiled gently. "Do not be alarmed, my dear. She seeks a more precise impression of you than my report can convey. A mother is naturally scrupulous regarding a companion for her daughters. I have assured her you are very young. However, she exercises close supervision over her daughters' education, which renders her less anxious about age. She is a woman of taste and strict principle, and she objects to having a French person in the house. I am certain she will deem your manners and accomplishments adequate, and regarding the religious and moral tone of your education, she—and indeed the bishop himself—will preside."

Gwendolen dared not respond, but the suppression of her vehement dislike for the entire prospect sent a deep flush racing over her face and neck, receding as swiftly as it arrived. Anna, filled with tender fears, slipped her small hand into her cousin's, and Mr. Gascoigne, too kind-hearted to ignore the trial this sudden shift posed for a girl like Gwendolen, continued in an easy, reassuring tone, addressing her as if dispelling hypothetical objections.

"I hold this position in such high regard that I would have been tempted to pursue it for Anna, had she been likely to meet Mrs. Mompert's requirements. It is truly a home, offering a continuation of education in the highest sense; 'governess' is a misnomer. The bishop's views lean decidedly Low Church compared to my own—he is a close friend of Lord Grampian's—but though privately strict, he is not narrow in public matters. Indeed, he has aroused as little dislike in his diocese as any bishop on the bench. He has remained friendly to me throughout, although prior to his promotion, when he served as an incumbent in this diocese, we engaged in a slight controversy concerning the Bible Society."

The rector's words were laden with meanings that satisfied his own conscience, leading him to underestimate their impact on his niece. "Continuation of education"—"bishop's views"—"privately strict"—"Bible Society"—these phrases landed like loose snakes released for the instruction of ladies who viewed them all as equally equipped with poison bags, ready to bite or sting according to convenience. To Gwendolen, already recoiling from the prospect opening before her, such terms felt like the intensifying heat of a burning glass, rather than the comforting links of persuasive reflection they formed for her good uncle. Desperate, she began to search for an alternative.

"There was another situation, I think, mamma spoke of?" she said, exercising determined self-mastery.

"Yes," the rector replied in a deprecating tone. "But that is in a school. I would derive less satisfaction from your undertaking that role. It involves much harder work, as you are aware, and offers fewer benefits in other respects. Furthermore, you do not possess an equal chance of securing it."

"Oh dear no," Mrs. Gascoigne interjected. "It would be far harder for you, my dear—and far less appropriate. You might not even have a bedroom to yourself." Memories of school flooded Gwendolen's mind, suggesting other particulars that forced her to admit to herself that this alternative offered no relief. Turning back to her uncle, she appeared to accept his proposal.

"When is Mrs. Mompert likely to send for me?"

"That is somewhat uncertain, but she has promised not to entertain any other proposal until she has seen you. She has entered deeply into your position. It will likely be within the next fortnight. But I must depart now; I am going to lease part of my glebe at an exceptionally favorable rate."

The rector concluded with cheerful assurance, leaving the room convinced that Gwendolen would adapt to her circumstances with the sensibility of a reasonable girl. Having spoken appropriately, he naturally assumed the

The mind of a man reduced to the barrenness of fastidious egoism is a landscape where direct desires have dulled and shriveled into a vacillating expectation of motives. It is a terrain composed entirely of moods, where a fitful impulse springs here and there, conspicuous and rank amidst the general weediness. Such a condition is apt to befall a life lived too much at large, unmolded by the crushing pressure of obligation. As the ancients noted, *nam deteriores omnes sumus licentiæ*; or, in a more familiar tongue, "As you like" serves as a terrible finger-post.

Potentates make known their intentions and affect the funds at a small expense of words. So when Grandcourt, after learning that Gwendolen had left Leubronn, incidentally pronounced that resort of fashion a beastly hole, worse than Baden, the remark was conclusive to Mr. Lush that his patron intended straightway to return to Diplow. The execution was sure to be slower than the intention, and indeed, Grandcourt did loiter through the next day without giving any distinct orders about departure—perhaps because he discerned that Lush was expecting them. He lingered over his toilet, and certainly came down with a faded aspect of perfect distinction which made fresh complexions and hands with the blood in them seem signs of raw vulgarity. He lingered on the terrace, in the gambling-rooms, in the reading-room, occupying himself in being indifferent to everybody and everything around him. When he met Lady Mallinger, however, he took some trouble—he raised his hat, paused, and proved that he listened to her recommendation of the waters by replying, "Yes; I heard somebody say how providential it was that there always happened to be springs at gambling places."

"Oh, that was a joke," said innocent Lady Mallinger, misled by Grandcourt's languid seriousness, "in imitation of the old one about the towns and the rivers, you know."

"Ah, perhaps," said Grandcourt, without change of expression. Lady Mallinger thought this worth telling to Sir Hugo, who said, "Oh, my dear, he is not a fool. You must not suppose that he can't see a joke. He can play his cards as well as most of us."

"He has never seemed to me a very sensible man," said Lady Mallinger, in excuse of herself. She had a secret objection to meeting Grandcourt, who was little else to her than a large living sign of what she felt to be her failure as a wife—the not having presented Sir Hugo with a son. Her constant reflection was that her husband might fairly regret his choice, and if he had not been very good might have treated her with some roughness in consequence, gentlemen naturally disliking to be disappointed.

Deronda, too, had a recognition from Grandcourt, for which he was not grateful, though he took care to return it with perfect civility. No reasoning as to the foundations of custom could do away with the early-rooted feeling that his birth had been attended with injury for which his father was to blame; and seeing that but for this injury Grandcourt's prospects might have been his, he was proudly resolute not to behave in any way that might be interpreted into irritation on that score. He saw a very easy descent into mean unreasoning rancor and triumph in others' frustration; and being determined not to go down that ugly pit, he turned his back on it, clinging to the kindlier affections within him as a possession. Pride certainly helped him well—the pride of not recognizing a disadvantage for one's self which vulgar minds are disposed to exaggerate, such as the shabby equipage of poverty: he would not have a man like Grandcourt suppose himself envied by him. But there is no guarding against interpretation. Grandcourt did believe that Deronda, poor devil, who he had no doubt was his cousin by the father's side, inwardly winced under their mutual position; wherefore the presence of that less lucky person was more agreeable to him than it would otherwise have been. An imaginary envy, the idea that others feel their comparative deficiency, is the ordinary cortège of egoism; and his pet dogs were not the only beings that Grandcourt liked to feel his power over in making them jealous. Hence he was civil enough to exchange several words with Deronda on the terrace about the hunting round Diplow, and even said, "You had better come over for a run or two when the season begins."

Lush, not displeased with delay, amused himself very well, partly in gossiping with Sir Hugo and in answering his questions about Grandcourt's affairs so far as they might affect his willingness to part with his interest in Diplow. Also about Grandcourt's personal entanglements, the baronet knew enough already for Lush to feel released from silence on a sunny autumn day, when there was nothing more agreeable to do in lounging promenades than to speak freely of a tyrannous patron behind his back. Sir Hugo willingly inclined his ear to a little good-humored scandal, which he was fond of calling *traits de mœurs*; but he was strict in keeping such communications from hearers who might take them too seriously. Whatever knowledge he had of his nephew's secrets, he had never spoken of it to Deronda, who considered Grandcourt a pale-blooded mortal, but was far from wishing to hear how the red corpuscles had been washed out of him. It was Lush's policy and inclination to gratify everybody when he had no reason to the contrary; and the baronet always treated him well, as one of those easy-handled personages who, frequenting the society of gentlemen, without being exactly gentlemen themselves, can be the more serviceable, like the second-best articles of our wardrobe, which we use with a comfortable freedom from anxiety.

"Well, you will let me know the turn of events," said Sir Hugo, "if this marriage seems likely to come off after all, or if anything else happens to make the want of money pressing. My plan would be much better for him than burdening Ryelands."

"That's true," said Lush, "only it must not be urged on him—just placed in his way that the scent may tickle him. Grandcourt is not a man to be always led by what makes for his own interest; especially if you let him see that it makes for your interest too. I'm attached to him, of course. I've given up everything else for the sake of keeping by him, and it has lasted a good fifteen years now. He would not easily get any one else to fill my place. He's a peculiar character, is Henleigh Grandcourt, and it has been growing on him of late years. However, I'm of a constant disposition, and I've been a sort of guardian to him since he was twenty; an uncommonly fascinating fellow he was then, to be sure—and could be now, if he liked. I'm attached to him; and it would be a good deal worse for him if he missed me at his elbow."

Sir Hugo did not think it needful to express his sympathy or even assent, and perhaps Lush himself did not expect this sketch of his motives to be taken as exact. But how can a man avoid himself as a subject in conversation? And he must make some sort of decent toilet in words, as in cloth and linen. Lush's listener was not severe: a member of Parliament could allow for the necessities of verbal toilet; and the dialogue went on without any change of mutual estimate.

However, Lush's easy prospect of indefinite procrastination was cut off the next morning by Grandcourt's saluting him with the question,

"Are you making all the arrangements for our starting by the Paris train?"

"I didn't know you meant to start," said Lush, not exactly taken by surprise.

"You might have known," said Grandcourt, looking at the burned length of his cigar, and speaking in that lowered tone which was usual with him when he meant to express disgust and be peremptory. "Just see to everything, will you? and mind no brute gets into the same carriage with us. And leave my P. P. C. at the Mallingers'."

In consequence they were at Paris the next day; but here Lush was gratified by the proposal or command that he should go straight on to Diplow and see that everything was right, while Grandcourt and the valet remained behind; and it was not until several days later that Lush received the telegram ordering the carriage to the Wanchester station.

He had used the interim actively, not only in carrying out Grandcourt's orders about the stud and household, but in learning all he could of Gwendolen, and how things were going on at Offendene. What was the probable effect that the news of the family misfortunes would have on Grandcourt's fitful obstinacy he felt to be quite incalculable. So far as the girl's poverty might be an argument that she would accept an offer from him now in spite of any previous coyness, it might remove that bitter objection to risk a repulse which Lush divined to be one of Grandcourt's deterring motives; on the other hand, the certainty of acceptance was just "the sort of thing" to make him lapse hither and thither with no more apparent will than a moth. Lush had had his patron under close observation for many years, and knew him perhaps better than he knew any other subject; but to know Grandcourt was to doubt what he would do in any particular case. It might happen that he would behave with an apparent magnanimity, like the hero of a modern French drama, whose sudden start into moral splendor after much lying and meanness, leaves you

White asses laden with the freight of Tyrian vessels—purple, gold, and balm—are sent to bribe my will; I shall bid them chase him forth, nor let him breathe the taint of his surmise upon my secure resolve. Ay, 'tis secure: and therefore let him come to spread his freight. For firmness hath its appetite and craves the stronger lure, more strongly to resist; would know the touch of gold to fling it off; scent wine to feel its lip the soberer; behold soft byssus, ivory, and plumes to say, "They're fair, but I will none of them," and flout enticement in the very face.

Mr. Gascoigne one day came to Offendene with what he felt to be the satisfactory news that Mrs. Mompert had fixed Tuesday in the following week for her interview with Gwendolen at Wanchester. He said nothing of his having incidentally heard that Mr. Grandcourt had returned to Diplow; knowing no more than she did that Leubronn had been the goal of her admirer's journeying, and feeling that it would be unkind uselessly to revive the memory of a brilliant prospect under the present reverses. In his secret soul he thought of his niece's unintelligible caprice with regret, but he vindicated her to himself by considering that Grandcourt had been the first to behave oddly, in suddenly walking away when there had been the best opportunity for crowning his marked attentions. The rector's practical judgment told him that his chief duty to his niece now was to encourage her resolutely to face the change in her lot, since there was no manifest promise of any event that would avert it.

"You will find an interest in varied experience, my dear, and I have no doubt you will be a more valuable woman for having sustained such a part as you are called to."

"I cannot pretend to believe that I shall like it," said Gwendolen, for the first time showing her uncle some petulance. "But I am quite aware that I am obliged to bear it." She remembered having submitted to his admonition on a different occasion when she was expected to like a very different prospect.

"And your good sense will teach you to behave suitably under it," said Mr. Gascoigne, with a shade more gravity. "I feel sure that Mrs. Mompert will be pleased with you. You will know how to conduct yourself to a woman who holds in all senses the relation of a superior to you. This trouble has come on you young, but that makes it in some respects easier, and there is a benefit in all chastisement if we adjust our minds to it."

This was precisely what Gwendolen was unable to do; and after her uncle was gone, the bitter tears, which had rarely come during the late trouble, rose and fell slowly as she sat alone. Her heart denied that the trouble was easier because she was young. When was she to have any happiness, if it did not come while she was young? Not that her visions of possible happiness for herself were as unmixed with necessary evil as they used to be—not that she could still imagine herself plucking the fruits of life without suspicion of their core. But this general disenchantment with the world—nay, with herself, since it appeared that she was not made for easy pre-eminence—only intensified her sense of forlornness; it was a visibly sterile distance enclosing the dreary path at her feet, in which she had no courage to tread. She was in that first crisis of passionate youthful rebellion against what is not fitly called pain, but rather the absence of joy—that first rage of disappointment in life's morning, which we whom the years have subdued are apt to remember but dimly as part of our own experience, and so to be intolerant of its self-enclosed unreasonableness and impiety. What passion seems more absurd, when we have got outside it and looked at calamity as a collective risk, than this amazed anguish that *I* and not *Thou*, *He* or *She*, should be just the smitten one? Yet perhaps some who have afterward made themselves a willing fence before the breast of another, and have carried their own heart-wound in heroic silence—some who have made their deeds great, nevertheless began with this angry amazement at their own smart, and on the mere denial of their fantastic desires raged as if under the sting of wasps which reduced the universe for them to an unjust infliction of pain. This was nearly poor Gwendolen's condition. What though such a reverse as hers had often happened to other girls? The one point she had been all her life learning to care for was that it had happened to *her*: it was what *she* felt under Klesmer's demonstration that she was not remarkable enough to command fortune by force of will and merit; it was what *she* would feel under the rigors of Mrs. Mompert's constant expectation, under the dull demand that she should be cheerful with three Miss Momperts, under the necessity of showing herself entirely submissive, and keeping her thoughts to herself. To be a queen disthroned is not so hard as some other down-stepping: imagine one who had been made to believe in his own divinity finding all homage withdrawn, and himself unable to perform a miracle that would recall the homage and restore his own confidence. Something akin to this illusion and this helplessness had befallen the poor spoiled child, with the lovely lips and eyes and the majestic figure—which seemed now to have no magic in them.

She rose from the low ottoman where she had been sitting purposeless, and walked up and down the drawing-room, resting her elbow on one palm while she leaned down her cheek on the other, and a slow tear fell. She thought, "I have always, ever since I was little, felt that mamma was not a happy woman; and now I dare say I shall be more unhappy than she has been." Her mind dwelt for a few moments on the picture of herself losing her youth and ceasing to enjoy—not minding whether she did this or that: but such picturing inevitably brought back the image of her mother. "Poor mamma! it will be still worse for her now. I can get a little money for her—that is all I shall care about now."
