And then with an entirely new movement of her imagination, she saw her mother getting quite old and white, and herself no longer young but faded, and their two faces meeting still with memory and love, and she knowing what was in her mother's mind—"Poor Gwen too is sad and faded now"—and then, for the first time, she sobbed, not in anger, but with a sort of tender misery. Her face was toward the door, and she saw her mother enter. She barely saw that; for her eyes were large with tears, and she pressed her handkerchief against them hurriedly. Before she took it away she felt her mother's arms round her, and this sensation, which seemed a prolongation of her inward vision, overcame her will to be reticent; she sobbed anew in spite of herself, as they pressed their cheeks together.

Mrs. Davilow had brought something in her hand which had already caused her an agitating anxiety, and she dared not speak until her darling had become calmer. But Gwendolen, with whom weeping had always been a painful manifestation to be resisted, if possible, again pressed her handkerchief against her eyes, and, with a deep breath, drew her head backward and looked at her mother, who was pale and tremulous.

"It was nothing, mamma," said Gwendolen, thinking that her mother had been moved in this way simply by finding her in distress. "It is all over now."

But Mrs. Davilow had withdrawn her arms, and Gwendolen perceived a letter in her hand.

"What is that letter?—worse news still?" she asked, with a touch of bitterness.

"I don't know what you will think it, dear," said Mrs. Davilow, keeping the letter in her hand. "You will hardly guess where it comes from."

"Don't ask me to guess anything," said Gwendolen, rather impatiently, as if a bruise were being pressed. "It is addressed to you, dear."

Gwendolen gave the slightest perceptible toss of the head.

"It comes from Diplow," said Mrs. Davilow, giving her the letter. She knew Grandcourt's indistinct handwriting, and her mother was not surprised to see her blush deeply; but watching her as she read, and wondering much what was the purport of the letter, she saw the color die out. Gwendolen's lips even were pale as she turned the open note toward her mother. The words were few and formal: *Mr. Grandcourt presents his compliments to Miss Harleth, and begs to know whether he may be permitted to call at Offendene to-morrow after two and to see her alone. Mr. Grandcourt has just returned from Leubronn, where he had hoped to find Miss Harleth.*

Mrs. Davilow read, and then looked at her daughter inquiringly, leaving the note in her hand. Gwendolen let it fall to the floor, and turned away.

"It must be answered, darling," said Mrs. Davilow, timidly. "The man waits."

Gwendolen sank on the settee, clasped her hands, and looked straight before her, not at her mother. She had the expression of one who had been startled by a sound and was listening to know what would come of it. The sudden change of the situation was bewildering. A few minutes before she was looking along

Desire trims the sails, but it is circumstance alone that furnishes the wind to fill them. Outside, Grandcourt rode his sleek black yacht *Yarico*, the groom trailing behind on the *Criterion*, cutting a path of pleasure from Diplow to Offendene. Inside, the air was stiller, charged with the quiet tension of a decision hanging in the balance. Gwendolen sat before the mirror, her mother gathering the cascading mass of her light-brown hair, coiling it with practiced care.

"Only gather it up easily, Mamma," Gwendolen instructed softly.

When the hair was secured, Mrs. Davilow reached for a pair of earrings. "Let me bring you some, Gwen?" she asked, her voice brightening as she caught her daughter's reflection. It was impossible to miss the transformation; the eyes burned with a renewed fire, a shadow seemingly lifted from the face to reveal lines smoothed back into placid youthfulness. The mother drew a hopeful inference, her tone buoyant. "Do you want your earrings?"

"No, Mamma," Gwendolen replied, offering one of her characteristic, enigmatic smiles before rising to shed her dressing gown. "I shall wear none. I'll put on my black silk. Black is the only attire for refusing an offer."

"Suppose the offer isn't made after all?" Mrs. Davilow suggested, a sly note threading her words.

"Then that will simply be because I refused it beforehand," Gwendolen countered, her chin lifting in a proud, defiant toss. When she descended the stairs in her long black robes, she possessed that firm poise and elastic vitality that had recently evaporated from her, like moisture from a parched plant. Her mother watched her pass, thinking, *She is quite herself again. It must be the joy of his coming. Can her mind truly be resolved against him?*

If those words had been spoken aloud, Gwendolen would have bristled with anger, perhaps all the more sharply because for the last twenty hours, with only a fleeting slumber, she had been trapped in a perpetual oscillation of images and arguments regarding her potential union with Grandcourt. The conclusion she had initially drawn had lost its grip; the counterbalancing tides of doubt and desire had washed away any solid ground, leaving her adrift. She intended to refuse him, yes, from the moment the letter arrived, yet this resolve had become hollow, drained of blood, no more vital to her quivering existence than the phrase "God's will be done" spoken by someone eagerly awaiting fate.

She did not mean to accept him. From the first glance at his letter, she had meant to reject him. Still, she could not help but confront the unwelcome truths head-on, stripping them of their awe-inspiring power. Her imagination, fueled by necessity, filled the gaps in her knowledge with various scenarios, reshaping the facts until they seemed to support her stance. By staring at a dubious object with a constructive mind, one can conjure twenty different forms. The vague hesitations she had felt before the meeting at the Whispering Stones now counted for nothing, submerged beneath a final, overwhelming repulsion. If not for that day at Cardell Chase, she told herself, there would have been no barrier to her marrying Grandcourt. On that day, and in its wake, she had not reasoned or weighed options; she had acted with an impulsive force against which all questioning was merely a whisper against a roaring torrent. This impulse stemmed not only from maidenly pride and jealousy, nor solely from the shock of another woman's calamity thrust violently into her vision, but from a profound dread of wrongdoing. It was a vague, distant fear, removed from the daily textures of her life, yet undeniably potent. Whatever aligned with the code of a lady required no scrutiny from her conscience; but from the dim, shadowed realm of the disgraceful, the wrong, the guilty, she shrank with a mixture of pride and terror. Apart from shame, her instinct dictated that any deliberate injury inflicted upon another belonged squarely within the category of guilt.

Yet, did she truly grasp the extent of the case concerning Mrs. Glasher and her children? She had offered a sort of promise, saying, "I will not interfere with your wishes." But would another woman marrying Grandcourt actually obstruct those wishes, or cause genuine harm to the mother and son? Could it not be preferable, even beneficial, for Grandcourt to marry? After all, what could not a woman achieve once wedded, provided she knew how to assert herself? Here, too, lay the domain of constructive imagination. Gwendolen possessed as precise a concept of marriage—the intricate web of mutual influences, demands, and duties between man and woman—as she did of magnetic currents or storm laws.

"Mamma managed badly," was her summation of her mother's experience; she vowed to handle matters quite differently. The trials of matrimony were the final topic Mrs. Davilow wished to explore with this daughter.

*I wonder what Mamma and Uncle would say if they knew about Mrs. Glasher!* Gwendolen debated silently. Not that she could envision confessing it, even if silence hadn't bound her lips tighter. *I wonder what anyone would say; or what they would say to Mr. Grandcourt marrying someone else and fathering other children!* To consider the judgment of "anyone" liberated her from the burden of discerning where clarity ended and obscurity began, since feeling had ceased to be the arbiter. She merely collected her memories, which confirmed that "anyone" viewed illegitimate children as deserving of greater social ostracization and deprivation than illegitimate fathers. The verdict of the public seemed clear: she had no compelling reason to champion Mrs. Glasher and her offspring.

However, there was another dimension to their presence that troubled her. Public opinion could not extinguish a feeling best described as indignation and loathing—to be expected to unite herself with an exhausted life, laden with backward secrets that must have been acutely felt by the woman herself, yet foreign to her own experience. True, the question of her own love for Grandcourt had occupied her mind scarcely at all. The desirability of marriage had always seemed rooted in sentiments other than love; to be enamored was the province of the man, upon whom the advances rested. Gwendolen had found no fault with Grandcourt's manner of being enamored until she glimpsed his past, a revelation she resented as a deliberate affront. His advances toward *her* had been calculated and deliberate, and now she felt a retrospective disgust for them. Perhaps the lives of other men were similarly constructed—filled with secrets that reduced the ignorant assumptions of the women they sought to marry to a farce, performed with laughter hidden in their sleeves.

These feelings of disgust and indignation had sunk deep. Though subsequent troubling experiences in recent weeks had dulled them from passionate fire to mere memory, it was chiefly their lingering resonance that kept her steadfast in her understanding with herself: she would not accept Grandcourt. She had never intended to form a new determination; she had only been contemplating what might be thought or said. If anything could have swayed her, it would have been the prospect of making life easy for "poor Mamma"; that, she admitted, was a temptation. But no! She was going to refuse him. Meanwhile, the anticipation of his rejection invigorated her; she held the white reins in her hands once more. A new current surged through her frame, reviving her from the beaten-down consciousness left by her interview with Klesmer. She was no longer going to crave validation of her capabilities; she was going to exercise her power.

Was this what caused her heart to palpitate anxiously when she heard the horse's hooves crunching on the gravel? When Miss Merry opened the door to announce that Grandcourt was in the drawing-room, the hours of preparation and the triumph of the situation seemed futile. She might as well have encountered him suddenly amidst her despair. Walking into the drawing-room required her to concentrate every ounce of energy into a self-control that rendered her gravely gracious. She extended her hand to him, answering his inquiry about her health with a voice as low and languid as his own.

A moment later, seated on two wreath-painted chairs, the tableau was striking. Gwendolen sat upright with downcast eyelids; Grandcourt remained about two yards distant, leaning one arm over the back of his chair, his gaze fixed upon her, his hat held loosely in his left hand. Any observer viewing them as a painting would have concluded they were in the throes of romantic suspense. Indeed, the courtship had commenced. She already felt herself being wooed by this silent man seated at an agreeable distance, enveloped in a subtle atmosphere reminiscent of attar of roses, his attention bent wholly upon her. He, too, considered himself the suitor; he was not a man to believe his presence carried no weight, and he was precisely the type to derive the utmost piquancy from a girl he had not yet deemed fully calculable.

"I was disappointed not to find you at Leubronn," he began, his usual broken drawl tinged with a shade of amorous languor. "The place was intolerable without you. A mere kennel of a location. Do you not agree?"

"I cannot judge what it would be without myself," Gwendolen replied, turning her eyes upon him with a recovering sense of mischief. "*With* myself, I like it well enough to have stayed longer, if I could. But I was obliged to return home due to family troubles."

"It was very cruel of you to go to Leubronn," Grandcourt remarked, ignoring the troubles entirely—though Gwendolen, for reasons she barely understood, wished for a

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Lady Violet Lebas, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of London itself. Inside, the air was thick with the scent of beeswax and damp wool, a heavy perfume masking the quiet suffocation of the room. She sat at her mahogany desk, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from Mr. Andrew Lang, his sprawling, confident script detailing his latest thoughts on literature, positioning himself as the benevolent instructor to her "undefeated resolution to admire only the right things."

Violet sighed, tracing the edge of the parchment with a silver letter-opener. Lang was a man of undeniable erudition, yet his letters were suffocated by the very patriarchal complacency she so deeply despised. He wrote to her of Virgil, of modern English poetry, of Fielding and Rochefoucauld, dismissing the systemic inequalities that left women like Sophia Western with no recourse but to forgive the betrayals of their husbands. He painted a nostalgic picture of a "lusty old militant world," entirely blind to the fact that this merry England was built upon the bruised backs of women and the indentured silence of the poor.

"The right things," she murmured aloud, the words tasting like ash. What he meant, of course, was the established canon—the texts elevated by centuries of wealthy, privileged men who saw their own reflections in the exploits of conquerors and the musings of comfortable philosophers.

She turned to his pages on Fielding. Lang excused the novelist's coarse morality, arguing that men must be pardoned the "vices of young blood" so long as they were brave and generous. He positioned himself as the arbiter of taste, unaware that the very act of defining "taste" was an exercise in exclusion.

Outside, the city churned. The rain blurred the gas lamps into halos of sickly yellow, illuminating the wet cobblestones where the poor hurried to shelter from the elements that the elite indoors ignored. Violet watched a carriage splash through a puddle, the driver cursing the weather, a stark contrast to the dry, heated debates happening within walls like hers.

She picked up a quill, dipping it into the inkwell. The nib scratched against the paper, a sound like a bird breaking bone. She began to write, not in response to Lang's grand theories, but in defense of the voices he silenced. She thought of the women who walked the streets below, their faces obscured by hoods, their lives reduced to survival in a world designed by men who believed themselves above reproach.

Lang spoke of the "delicate liberality" of men like Grandcourt, a phrase that now echoed in her mind with bitter irony. How could one speak of delicacy when the foundation of such men's wealth was the exploitation of the vulnerable? How could one claim moral superiority while ignoring the suffering caused by the very systems they upheld?

Violet's pen moved faster now, her anger fueling her words. She described the hidden costs of the aristocratic lifestyle, the invisible labor that sustained the glittering facades of the upper classes. She wrote of the resilience of the marginalized, the quiet strength of those who refused to be broken by the weight of expectation.

As the storm intensified, thunder rolling overhead like the rumble of distant cannons, Violet felt a surge of defiance. She would not let Lang's words define her reality. She would write her own truth, a truth that acknowledged the pain and injustice of the world, yet celebrated the enduring spirit of those who fought against the odds.

In the dim light of the library, surrounded by the books that had shaped generations of men, she found her own voice. It was a voice that demanded change, that challenged the status quo, and that refused to be silenced by the comfortable lies of the powerful.

The rain continued to fall, washing the streets clean, preparing the ground for new beginnings. Violet smiled, a rare, fierce expression that transformed her face. She was done with the easy answers of the past. She was ready to confront the complexities of the present, to weave a narrative that honored the struggles of the oppressed and inspired hope for a better future.

With a final flourish of her pen, she capped the inkwell and stood up, stretching her arms above her head. The storm had passed, leaving the air crisp and clear. Outside, the first hints of dawn were breaking through the clouds, signaling the start of a new day.

Violet walked to the window, watching the sunrise paint the sky in hues of gold and pink. She knew the road ahead would be difficult, fraught with challenges and opposition. But she also knew that she was not alone. There were others like her, scattered across the city and beyond, fighting for justice and equality. Together, they would build a world that was truly free, a world where every voice mattered, and where no one was left behind.

As the sun fully emerged, casting its warm glow over the city, Violet felt a sense of peace settle over her. She had faced her demons, confronted her fears, and emerged stronger than ever before. She was ready to face the world, armed with her pen and her unwavering belief in the power of truth.

The story of Daniel Deronda was far from over. In fact, it was just beginning. And Violet was determined to ensure that it ended on a note of triumph, a testament to the resilience of the human spirit and the enduring power of love and justice.

With a deep breath, she turned away from the window and returned to her desk, ready to continue her work. The journey ahead would be long and arduous, but she was prepared for whatever challenges lay in store. For she knew that the fight for equality was not just a battle for the present, but a legacy for the future.

And so, with courage and conviction, she began to write, weaving a tale that would inspire generations to come.

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Gwendolen, like the frantic heartbeat of a world indifferent to her quiet suffocation. She sat at her mahogany desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from Grandcourt, his sprawling, confident script detailing his latest commands regarding their departure for Diplow.

Grandcourt was a man of undeniable erudition, yet his presence was suffocated by a patriarchal complacency she so deeply despised. He wrote to her of Virgil, of modern English poetry, of Fielding and Rochefoucauld, positioning himself as the benevolent instructor to her "undefeated resolution to admire only the right things."

"The right things," she murmured aloud, the words tasting like ash. What he meant, of course, was the established canon—the texts elevated by centuries of wealthy, privileged men who saw their own reflections in the exploits of conquerors and the musings of comfortable philosophers.

She turned to his pages on Fielding. Grandcourt excused the novelist's coarse morality, arguing that men must be pardoned the "vices of young blood" so long as they were brave and generous. He painted a nostalgic picture of a "lusty old militant world," entirely blind to the fact that this merry England was built upon the bruised backs of women and the indentured silence of the poor. He dismissed the systemic inequalities that left women like Sophia Western with no recourse but to forgive the betrayals of their husbands.

Today, a slight rain hindered riding; but to compensate, a package had come from London, and Mrs. Davilow had just left the room after bringing in for admiration the beautiful things (of Grandcourt's ordering) which lay scattered about on the tables. Gwendolen was just then enjoying the scenery of her life. She let her hands fall on her lap, and said with a pretty air of perversity,

"Why is tomorrow the only day?"

"Because the next day is the first with the hounds," said Grandcourt.

"And after that?"

"After that I must go away for a couple of days—it's a bore—but I shall go one day and come back the next." Grandcourt noticed a change in her face, and releasing his hand from under his knees, he laid it on hers, and said, "You object to my going away?"

"It's no use objecting," said Gwendolen, coldly. She was resisting to the utmost her temptation to tell him that she suspected to whom he was going—the temptation to make a clean breast, speaking without restraint.

"Yes it is," said Grandcourt, enfolding her hand. "I will put off going. And I will travel at night, so as only to be away one day." He thought that he knew the reason of what he inwardly called this bit of temper, and she was particularly fascinating to him at this moment.

"Then don't put off going, but travel at night," said Gwendolen, feeling that she could command him, and finding in this peremptoriness a small outlet for her irritation.

"Then you will go to Diplow tomorrow?"

"Oh, yes, if you wish it," said Gwendolen, in a high tone of careless assent. Her concentration in other feelings had really hindered her from taking notice that her hand was being held.

"How you treat us poor devils of men!" said Grandcourt, lowering his tone. "We are always getting the worst of it."

"_Are_ you?" said Gwendolen, in a tone of inquiry, looking at him more naively than usual. She longed to believe this commonplace badinage as the serious truth about her lover: in that case, she too was justified. If she knew everything, Mrs. Glasher would appear more blamable than Grandcourt. "_Are_ you always getting the worst?"

"Yes. Are you as kind to me as I am to you?" said Grandcourt, looking into her eyes with his narrow gaze.

Gwendolen felt herself stricken. She was conscious of having received so much, that her sense of command was checked, and sank away in the perception that, look around her as she might, she could not turn back: it was as if she had consented to mount a chariot where another held the reins; and it was not in her nature to leap out in the eyes of the world. She had not consented in ignorance, and all she could say now would be a confession that she had not been ignorant. Her right to explanation was gone. All she had to do now was to adjust herself, so that the spikes of that unwilling penance which conscience imposed should not gall her. With a sort of mental shiver, she resolutely changed her mental attitude. There had been a little pause, during which she had not turned away her eyes; and with a sudden break into a smile, she said,

"If I were as kind to you as you are to me, that would spoil your generosity: it would no longer be as great as it could be—and it is that now."

"Then I am not to ask for one kiss," said Grandcourt, contented to pay a large price for this new kind of love-making, which introduced marriage by the finest contrast.

"Not one?" said Gwendolen, getting saucy, and nodding at him defiantly.

He lifted her little left hand to his lips, and then released it respectfully. Clearly it was faint praise to say of him that he was not disgusting: he was almost charming; and she felt at this moment that it was not likely she could ever have loved another man better than this one. His reticence gave her some inexplicable, delightful consciousness.

"Apropos," she said, taking up her work again, "is there any one besides Captain and Mrs. Torrington at Diplow?—or do you leave them tête-à-tête? I suppose he converses in cigars, and she answers with her chignon."

"She has a sister with her," said Grandcourt, with his shadow of a smile, "and there are two men besides—one of them you know, I believe."

"Ah, then, I have a poor opinion of him," said Gwendolen, shaking her head.

"You saw him at Leubronn—young Deronda—a young fellow with the Mallingers."

Gwendolen felt as if her heart were making a sudden gambol, and her fingers, which tried to keep a firm hold on her work, got cold.

"I never spoke to him," she said, dreading any discernible change in herself. "Is he not disagreeable?"

"No, not particularly," said Grandcourt, in his most languid way. "He thinks a little too much of himself. I thought he had been introduced to you."

"No. Some one told me his name the evening before I came away. That was all. What is he?"

"A sort of ward of Sir Hugo Mallinger's. Nothing of any consequence."

"Oh, poor creature! How very unpleasant for him!" said Gwendolen, speaking from the lip, and not meaning any sarcasm. "I wonder if it has left off raining!" she added, rising and going to look out of the window.

Happily it did not rain the next day, and Gwendolen rode to Diplow on Criterion as she had done on that former day when she returned with her mother in the carriage. She always felt the more daring for being in her riding-dress; besides having the agreeable belief that she looked as well as possible in it—a sustaining consciousness in any meeting which seems formidable. Her anger toward Deronda had changed into a superstitious dread—due, perhaps, to the coercion he had exercised over her thought—lest the first interference of his in her life might foreshadow some future influence. It is of such stuff that superstitions are commonly made: an intense feeling about ourselves which makes the evening star shine at us with a threat, and the blessing of a beggar encourage us. And superstitions carry consequences which often verify their hope or their foreboding.

The time before luncheon was taken up for Gwendolen by going over the rooms with Mrs. Torrington and Mrs. Davilow; and she thought it likely that if she saw Deronda, there would hardly be need for more than a bow between them. She meant to notice him as little as possible.

And after all she found herself under an inward compulsion too strong for her pride. From the first moment of their being in the room together, she seemed to herself to be doing nothing but notice him; everything else was automatic performance of an habitual part.

When he took his place at lunch, Grandcourt had said, "Deronda, Miss Harleth tells me you were not introduced to her at Leubronn?"

"Miss Harleth hardly remembers me, I imagine," said Deronda, looking at her quite simply, as they bowed. "She was intensely occupied when I saw her."

Now, did he suppose that she had not suspected him of being the person who redeemed her necklace?

"On the contrary. I remember you very well," said Gwendolen, feeling rather nervous, but governing herself and looking at him in return with new examination. "You did not approve of my playing at roulette."

"How did you come to that conclusion?" said Deronda, gravely.
