The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Mrs. Glasher, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of a world that had long ceased to care for her. She sat at her mahogany desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from Mr. Grandcourt, his sprawling, confident script detailing his latest movements and intentions.

Lydia sighed, tracing the edge of the parchment with a silver letter-opener. Grandcourt was a man of undeniable erudition and fortune, yet his correspondence was suffocated by the very patriarchal complacency she so deeply despised. He wrote to her of Diplow, of Miss Arrowpoint, of the inevitable march of social propriety, positioning himself as the benevolent arbiter of her fate while simultaneously dismissing the emotional weight of their shared history.

"Fate," she murmured aloud, the words tasting like ash. What he meant, of course, was the established canon—the texts elevated by centuries of wealthy, privileged men who saw their own reflections in the exploits of conquerors and the musings of comfortable philosophers.

She turned to his pages on the upcoming marriage. Grandcourt excused the necessity of securing a suitable alliance, arguing that a woman must be pardoned the "vices of youth" so long as she was brave and generous. He painted a nostalgic picture of a "lusty old militant world," entirely blind to the fact that this merry England was built upon the bruised backs of women and the indentured silence of the poor. He dismissed the systemic inequalities that left women like Sophia Western with no recourse but to forgive the betrayals of their husbands.

Outside, the landscape mirrored this internal decay. The house stood rambling and patchy, a hybrid of gray stone and red tile, its round tower jutting awkwardly from the corner like a misplaced finger. Once, in the height of summer, the conical roof had been crowned by a weather-vane that caught the gleam of sunlight; now, in November, it was obscured by low-hanging clouds and the skeletal branches of winter trees. The grounds, once a vast meadow bordered by stone lodges that resembled little prisons, were now choked with the black soot of nearby coal mines. The country, once entirely rural and lovely, was chiefly peopled by men with candles stuck in their hats and a diabolic complexion that laid them peculiarly open to suspicion in the eyes of the children at Gadsmere—Mrs. Glasher's four beautiful daughters, who had dwelt there for about three years.

Now, with the flower-beds empty and the pool blackly shivering, the place seemed sombrely in keeping with the black roads and black mounds that put the district in mourning. Yet, Mrs. Glasher liked Gadsmere as well as she would have liked any other abode. The complete seclusion of the place, secured by the unattractiveness of the surrounding countryside, was exactly to her taste. When she drove her two ponies with a waggonet full of children, there were no gentry in carriages to be met, only men of business in gigs; at church, there were no eyes she cared to avoid, for the curate and his wife were either ignorant of anything to her disadvantage or simply ignored it. To them, she was simply a widow lady, the tenant of Gadsmere; and the name of Grandcourt was of little interest in that district compared with the names of Fletcher and Gawcome, the lessees of the collieries.

It was full ten years since the elopement of an Irish officer's beautiful wife with young Grandcourt, and a consequent duel where the bullets wounded the air only, had made some little noise. Most of those who remembered the affair now wondered what had become of that Mrs. Glasher, whose beauty and brilliance had made her rather conspicuous to them in foreign places, where she was known to be living with young Grandcourt.

That he should have disentangled himself from that connection seemed only natural and desirable. As to her, it was thought that a woman who was understood to have forsaken her child along with her husband had probably sunk lower. Grandcourt had of course got weary of her. He was much given to the pursuit of women: but a man in his position would by this time desire to make a suitable marriage with the fair young daughter of a noble house. No one talked of Mrs. Glasher now, any more than they talked of the victim in a trial for manslaughter ten years before: she was a lost vessel after whom nobody would send out an expedition of search; but Grandcourt was seen in harbor with his colors flying, registered as seaworthy as ever.

Yet, in fact, Grandcourt had never truly disentangled himself from Mrs. Glasher. His passion for her had been the strongest and most lasting he had ever known; and though it was now as dead as the music of a cracked flute, it had left a certain dull disposedness, which, on the death of her husband three years before, had prompted in him a vacillating notion of marrying her, in accordance with the understanding often expressed between them during the days of his first ardor. At that early time Grandcourt would willingly have paid for the freedom to be won by a divorce; but the husband would not oblige him, not wanting to be married again himself, and not wishing to have his domestic habits printed in evidence.

The altered poise which the years had brought in Mrs. Glasher was just the reverse. At first she was comparatively careless about the possibility of marriage. It was enough that she had escaped from a disagreeable husband and found a sort of bliss with a lover who had completely fascinated her—young, handsome, amorous, and living in the best style, with equipage and conversation of the kind to be expected in young men of fortune who have seen everything. She was an impassioned, vivacious woman, fond of adoration, exasperated by five years of marital rudeness; and the sense of release was so strong upon her that it stilled anxiety for more than she actually enjoyed. An equivocal position was of no importance to her then; she had no envy for the honors of a dull, disregarded wife: the one spot which spoiled her vision of her new pleasant world, was the sense that she left her three-year-old boy, who died two years afterward, and whose first tones saying "mamma" retained a difference from those of the children that came after. But now the years had brought many changes besides those in the contour of her cheek and throat; and that Grandcourt should marry her had become her dominant desire. The equivocal position which she had not minded about for herself was now telling upon her through her children, whom she loved with a devotion charged with the added passion of atonement. She had no repentance except in this direction. If Grandcourt married her, the children would be none the worse off for what had passed: they would see their mother in a dignified position, and they would be at no disadvantage with the world: her son could be made his father's heir. It was the yearning for this result which gave the supreme importance to Grandcourt's feeling for her; her love for him had long resolved itself into anxiety that he should give her the unique, permanent claim of a wife, and she expected no other happiness in marriage than the satisfaction of her maternal love and pride—including her pride for herself in the presence of her children. For the sake of that result she was prepared even with a tragic firmness to endure anything quietly in marriage; and she had acuteness enough to cherish Grandcourt's flickering purpose negatively, by not molesting him with passionate appeals and with scene-making. In her, as in every one else who wanted anything of him, his incalculable turns, and his tendency to harden under beseeching, had created a reasonable dread:—a slow discovery, of which no presentiment had been given in the bearing of a youthful lover with a fine line of face and the softest manners. But reticence had necessarily cost something to this impassioned woman, and she was the bitterer for it. There is no quailing—even that forced on the helpless and injured—which has not an ugly obverse: the withheld sting was gathering venom. She was absolutely dependent on Grandcourt; for though he had been always liberal in expenses for her, he had kept everything voluntary on his part; and with the goal of marriage before her, she would ask for nothing less. He had said that he would never settle anything except by will; and when she was thinking of alternatives for the future it often occurred to her that, even if she did not become Grandcourt's wife, he might never have a son who would have a legitimate claim on him, and the end might be that her son would be made heir to the best part of his estates. No son at that early age could promise to have more of his father's physique. But her becoming Grandcourt's wife was so far from being an extravagant notion of possibility, that even Lush had entertained it, and had said that he would as soon bet on it as on any other likelihood with regard to his familiar companion. Lush, indeed, on inferring that Grandcourt had a preconception of using his residence at Diplow in order to win Miss Arrowpoint, had thought it well to fan that project, taking it as a tacit renunciation of the marriage with Mrs. Glasher, which had long been a mark for the hovering and wheeling of Grandcourt's caprice. But both prospects had been negatived by Gwendolen's appearance on the scene; and it was natural enough for Mrs. Glasher to enter with eagerness into L

On the day Gwendolen Harleth became Mrs. Grandcourt, the morning air was crisp with a slight frost that curled the edges of the leaves, cutting the sunlight into sharp, bright shards. Half the village of Pennicote lined the path to the church, drawn by the spectacle of the bridal party, a procession that seemed to demand attention from every corner of the parish. The rector, acting as father to the bride, officiated alongside an old friend, lending a gravity to the proceedings that only heightened the sense of occasion. Yet, amidst the general effervescence, two faces stood out as islands of gloom: Mrs. Davilow's and Anna's. The mother's eyelids were swollen and pink, the physical toll of tears shed throughout the night, a testament to the agonizing severance from a daughter who was the very bloom of her existence. Anna's distress, however, puzzled the onlookers; she was being framed by a bridesmaid's gown of such splendor that it seemed to mock her melancholy.

The crowd reflected the brilliance of the day, especially the bride herself. Gwendolen possessed the figure and carriage of a titled lady, though one might argue a title demanded a rosier hue; yet, the groom, pale and possessing a complexion akin to the miller's wife's own husband, completed the union perfectly in the eyes of the spectators. They assumed he loved her dearly, hoping he would never hold against her the vicissitudes of her past—her years as a governess, her mother's humble cottage at Sawyer's. Even the miller's fourteen-year-old daughter, initially skeptical that high-born gentility treated its wives poorly, was corrected by her mother, who recounted tales of Squire Pelton whipping his wife in secret. "Men are men," the tailor interjected, noting how disputes begin with the tongue. "This Mr. Grandcourt has a wonderful little tongue," he added, observing the groom's demeanor. "Everything must be done dummy-like without his ordering." Mrs. Girdle nodded sagely, "Then he's the more whip, I doubt. She's got tongue enough." As the couple emerged, the tailor noted the long, dramatic corners of Gwendolen's eyes, remarking that she made people feel comical when she looked at them.

In truth, Gwendolen displayed an elasticity of bearing and a lustre in her brown glance born of intense, almost feverish excitement. It was not pain she felt, but a state akin to standing at a gambling table, watching her chips dwindle until the thrill of loss eclipsed the unease of a growing conscience. Ambition and a hunger for luxury, slowly poisoning her spirit, fueled a gratifying vanity that rendered her fears irrelevant. She did not repent her choice; rather, she surmounted the nagging doubts with an exulting defiance, daring the world to judge her as she stepped onto the board of life, eager to win or lose with éclat. She believed she was acquiring the official strength of marriage, an intoxication of youthful egoism returning stronger than before. Walking with an erect head and elastic step, she moved among illusions, unaware that beneath the surface, a subtle intoxication had already taken root.

"Thank God you bear it so well, my darling!" Mrs. Davilow cried, helping Gwendolen remove her white veil and slip into her traveling dress. The mother's trembling had been the only sign of weakness, urging Gwendolen to view the morning as a triumph. Gwendolen laughed tenderly, pressing her hands to her mother's cheeks. "Why, you might have said that if I were going to Mrs. Mompert's, you dear, sad, incorrigible mamma!" she teased, then spread her arms to display herself. "Here am I—Mrs. Grandcourt! What else would you have me?" She spoke of splendid houses, horses, diamonds, courtship, and titles, promising to love her mother better than anyone else. Mrs. Davilow accepted the declaration with a whispered plea for happiness, admitting she would bear the separation sorrowfully. Gwendolen, shaking her head with a bright smile, declared that sorrow was her mother's sauce, necessary for any joy. Clinging to each other, Gwendolen rained kisses on her mother's cheeks, wishing she could go with her, before handing her hand to Grandcourt to lead her to the carriage. The rector offered a final blessing, telling Mrs. Davilow that Gwendolen was in a position far beyond his hopes, chosen entirely for her own sake.

The journey to Ryelands covered fifty miles by rail, arriving as twilight veiled the sky. Driving through the park, Gwendolen watched the grand outlines of the estate emerge from the gloom: winding drives bordered by evergreens, vast grassy undulations dotted with dark clumps, and finally, the white house framed by a hanging wood and a rising terrace. During the ride, she was at her liveliest, chatting incessantly, treating Grandcourt's gentle grip on her hand as a playful contest to be won. She grew febrile with excitement, her heart palpating at the changing light and scenery. Was it the fulfillment of her girlish dream to be "somebody," walking corridors of lofty silence where her painted springs bloomed and zephyrs blew trumpets, surrounded by servants who revered her insolence? Or was it a dim, insistent dread mixing with the expectation of triumph? Exultation in her nature carried an inevitable infusion of dread, ready to curdle. As they approached the gates, she fell silent. When Grandcourt kissed her, she barely registered it, lost in the absorbing show of her own making. A numbness settled over her personality, replacing the half-willful excitement of the day.

Inside, the hall glowed with warmth, matting, carpets, and the imposing presence of portraits and statues. Servants were few, mostly from Diplow, plus a new maid assigned to Gwendolen. Led by Grandcourt down a subtly scented corridor, she entered an ante-room where a doorway spilled rich light and color. "These are our dens," Grandcourt said, pressing her hand to his lips before moving away, more in love than he anticipated. Gwendolen yielded her hat and mantle, throwing herself into a chair by the glowing hearth, admiring her reflection in the glass panels amidst her faint-green satin surroundings. The housekeeper lingered briefly before approaching with a packet, delivered strictly to Gwendolen's hands. "It was a present particularly ordered by Mr. Grandcourt," the housekeeper explained, bowing low. "He was not to know of its arrival till he saw you wear it."

Gwendolen placed the packet on her lap, assuming it contained the promised diamonds. Inside the sealed coverings lay a box, and within that, a jewel-case. As the gleaming stones revealed themselves, a letter lay atop them. Recognizing the handwriting instantly, she felt as if an adder had coiled upon the jewels. Her heart leaped, draining her strength; her hands trembled violently as she unfolded the thin paper. The words thrust themselves upon her: *These diamonds, once given with ardent love to Lydia Glasher, I pass on to you. You have broken your word... God is too just for that. The man you have married has a withered heart. His best young love was mine... It is dead: but I am the grave in which your chance of happiness is buried as well as mine.* The letter accused her of choosing to injure him and his children, declaring that she had taken him with her eyes open and that her willing wrong would be her curse.

At first, Gwendolen stared, spellbound by the horror of the words, reading them as a doom of penance. Suddenly, a spasm of terror seized her. She leaned forward, stretching the paper toward the fire, desperate to destroy the accusation before it could reach anyone's eyes. The note flew like a feather from her trembling fingers, consumed instantly by a great draft of flame. In her frantic movement, the casket toppled to the floor, scattering the diamonds. She paid no heed, collapsing back into her chair, utterly helpless. She could not see her own reflection anymore; she appeared to others as a woman petrified white, save for the visible tremor in her lips and hands. For a long while, she sat in a stupor, feeling ill, haunted by the repeating words of the burned letter. Truly, these were poisoned gems, and the venom had seeped into the veins of the poor young creature.

Finally, a tap at the door announced Grandcourt's entrance, dressed for dinner. The sight of him struck a new, violent nerve. Gwendolen screamed again and again, her hysteria erupting with terrifying force. Grandcourt, expecting a smiling bride ready to descend the stairs, found instead a pallid, shrieking woman with jewels scattered around her feet on the floor. Was it madness? Had the furies finally crossed his threshold?

Love, in its most potent manifestations, possesses the nature of an isolated fatality, rendering the mind's usual opinions and resolutions utterly irrelevant. Consider Daphnis, whose frenzy rendered Heraclitus's doctrines useless; or Tristan, whose philtre-induced passion would have shattered the sharpest logic of Duns Scotus; or Romeo, whose sudden devotion to Juliet made any philosophical objections regarding Ptolemy moot. Yet, not all love operates in such solitary isolation. This passion possesses a vast capacity to ally itself with every operation of the soul, acknowledging effects from the imagined light of unproven firmaments and calibrating its scale to the grander orbits of what has been and what shall be.

Upon his return to town, Deronda assured Sir Hugo that he had successfully implanted in Grandcourt's mind the distinct understanding that he could secure fifty thousand pounds by relinquishing a prospect that was likely distant and far from certain. Beyond this, however, Deronda possessed no further insight into Grandcourt's true disposition, save for the observation that the baronet remained inclined to maintain friendly communications.

"And what did you think of the future bride on a nearer survey?" Sir Hugo asked, his tone shifting to one of agreeable playfulness.

"I thought better of her than I did in Leubronn," Deronda replied. "Roulette was not a fitting setting for her; it brought out something of the demon. At Diplow, she seemed much more womanly and attractive—less hard and self-possessed. I perceived a quite different expression in her mouth and eyes."

"Don't flirt with her too much, Dan," Sir Hugo warned, feigning concern. "If you provoke Grandcourt into savagery when they convene at the Abbey for Christmas, it will interfere with my own affairs."

"I can remain in town, sir," Deronda offered.

"No, no. Lady Mallinger and the children simply cannot do without you at Christmas. Only, pray, avoid making mischief—unless you contrive a duel and manage to shoot Grandcourt, which might be worth a trifling amount of inconvenience."

"I don't believe you have ever witnessed me flirt," Deronda said, devoid of amusement.

"Oh, haven't I, though?" Sir Hugo retorted provocatively. "You are perpetually casting tender glances at women and engaging them in a manner that borders on the Jesuitical. You are a dangerous young fellow—a modern Lovelace who ensures that Clarissas run after you rather than you pursuing them."

Was there any point in allowing oneself to be exasperated by such a tasteless jest? Exasperation, after all, precedes reflection on utility. Few friendly remarks are more irritating than the assertion that we constantly appear to be doing precisely what we never intend to do. Sir Hugo's definition of flirting was, it was hoped, peculiar; for his own part, Deronda was certain he had never flirted. Nevertheless, he felt a relief that the baronet lacked knowledge regarding the repurchase of Gwendolen's necklace, thereby sparing him from feeding the baronet's appetite for such gallant rallying.

Resolved to exercise greater caution in the future, particularly during his upcoming visit to Mrs. Meyrick's—the first since his arrival from Leubronn—Deronda knew he would tread carefully. Mirah was undoubtedly a creature in whom it was perilously easy to display a tender interest, both in demeanor and speech.

Mrs. Meyrick had recently sent Deronda a detailed report on Mirah's well-being within her household. "We are growing fonder of her every day," she had written. "At breakfast, we all turn toward the door with expectation to see her enter; we watch her and listen to her as if she were a native from a newly discovered land. I have heard not a single word from her lips that casts the slightest doubt upon her. She is perfectly contented and overflowing with gratitude. My daughters are learning from her, and they eagerly hope to acquire her other pupils, for she is anxious not to eat the bread of idleness but to work, just as my girls do. Mab declares our life has transformed into a fairy tale, and her sole fear is that Mirah will revert to becoming a nightingale once more and fly away from us. Her voice is simply perfect—not loud and commanding, but searching and melting, akin to the contemplation of what has passed. That is precisely how old people like myself perceive a beautiful voice."

Yet, Mrs. Meyrick omitted details that would have required her to admit that Amy and Mab, who had accompanied Mirah to the synagogue, found the Jewish faith less reconcilable with their aspirations in Mirah's case than in that of Scott's Rebecca. Out of delicacy toward Mirah, whose religion was too tender a subject to be touched lightly, they maintained a silence. However, eventually, Amy, driven by her nature as a practical reformer, could not restrain a question.

"Excuse me, Mirah, but does it truly seem right to you that women should sit behind rails in a separate gallery?" Amy asked.

"Yes, I never thought of anything else," Mirah replied with mild surprise.

"And you prefer seeing the men with their hats on?" Mab ventured cautiously, proposing the smallest possible point of divergence.

"Oh, yes. I cherish what I have always seen there, because it evokes in me the same feelings—the feelings I would not exchange for anything else in the entire world."

Following this declaration, any criticism, whether of doctrine or practice, would have appeared to these generous young women as an inhospitable cruelty. Mirah's religion was woven into the very fiber of her affections and had never presented itself to her as a collection of abstract propositions.

"She says herself she is a very bad Jewess and does not fully know her people's religion," Amy remarked later, once Mirah had retired to bed. "Perhaps it will gradually melt away from her, and she will pass into Christianity like the rest of the world, provided she grows to love us very much and never finds her mother. It is so strange to belong to the Jewish religion now."

"Oh, oh, oh!" cried Mab. "I wish I were not such a hideous Christian. How can an ugly Christian, who is perpetually dropping her work, convert a beautiful Jewess who possesses not a single fault?"

"It may be wicked of me," admitted shrewd Kate, "but I cannot help wishing that her mother may not be found. There might be something unpleasant."

"I do not think so, my dear," Mrs. Meyrick reassured her. "I believe Mirah is cast in the mold of her mother. And what a joy it would be for her to have such a daughter returned! But a mother's feelings are not worth reckoning, I suppose"—she shot a mischievous glance at her own daughters—"and a dead mother is worth more than a living one?"

"Well, and so she may be, little mother," Kate conceded; "but we would rather hold you cheaper and have you alive."

It was not only the Meyricks, whose varied knowledge had been acquired through the irregular foraging typical of clever girls, but Deronda himself—who, despite his extensive masculine instruction—had been roused by this apparition of Mirah to the realization that he knew hardly anything about modern Judaism or the inner history of his own race. The Chosen People had commonly been treated as a group selected for the benefit of others; their thinking regarded as something (whatever it might be) that ought to have been entirely different. Deronda, like his neighbors, had viewed Judaism as a sort of eccentric, fossilized form that an accomplished man might dispense with studying and leave to specialists. But Mirah, with her terrified flight from one parent and her ardent yearning for the other, flashed upon him the hitherto neglected reality that Judaism was something still throbbing in human lives, still forging for them the only conceivable vesture of the world. During the idle excursion he immediately undertook with Sir Hugo, he began to seek out the exteriors of synagogues and the titles of books concerning the Jews.

This awakening of a new interest—this transition from the supposition that we hold correct opinions on subjects we are careless about, to a sudden care for them and a recognition that our previous views were merely ignorance—proved an effective remedy for ennui, a malady unfortunately incapable of being cured by a physician's prescription. Yet, Deronda carried this change with him, enduring his weeks of lounging all the better. It was on this journey that he first entered a Jewish synagogue at Frankfort, where his party rested on a Friday. While exploring the Juden-gasse, which he had observed long before, he recalled its picturesque old houses; what his eyes now chiefly dwelt upon were the human types inhabiting them. His thoughts, busily connecting them with the past phases of their race, stirred that fiber of historic sympathy which had helped determine in him certain traits worth noting for those interested in his future.

True, when a young man possesses a fine appearance, lacks eccentricities of manner, bears the education of a gentleman, and enjoys a present income, it is not customary to feel a prying curiosity about his way of thinking or his peculiar tastes. He may very well settle into life as an agreeable, clever youth without passing a special examination on such matters. Later, when he becomes somewhat slovenly and portly, his peculiarities become more distinctly discernible, and it is considered a mercy if they are not highly objectionable. But anyone wishing to comprehend the effect of subsequent events on Deronda should know a little more of what he was at twenty-five than was evident in ordinary intercourse.

It happened that the very vividness of his impressions often rendered him more enigmatic to his friends, contributing to an apparent

"No man," a Rabbi once declared, citing an indisputable law, "shall turn the bones of his father and mother into spoons." The statement hung in the air, a moral anchor designed to halt the drift of economic pragmatism. Yet, the market for spoons had never swollen sufficiently to provoke a counter-argument suggesting that human progress might indeed lie in such a repurposing of material. The only barrier was sentiment—a soft, intangible force that coerced only those who already believed feelings constituted the greater portion of worldly wealth.

Gwendolen Deronda abandoned the fashionable circuits of Rotten Row for a more rugged terrain. He wandered through the districts of London most densely populated by common Jews, drifting toward synagogues during services and peering into shopfronts. It was a reconnaissance mission yielding little promise of specific revelation. Why not seek counsel from an influential Rabbi regarding the odds of locating a mother named Cohen, a son named Ezra, and a lost daughter named Mirah? He considered this approach, pushing it aside until after Christmas. The truth was, despite his capacity for finding poetry in the mundane, Deronda, whenever a keen personal interest ignited within him, could not escape the crushing weight of the Actual. Reality, unyielding and unselective, refused to accommodate his tastes. Enthusiasm thrives comfortably among abstract ideas; it tolerates the pungent breath of garlic in medieval markets and finds nobility in the ceremonial trappings of classic processions. But when ideals press against the flesh, demanding warm incarnation, enthusiasm grows squeamish, prone to fainting under the burden of the tangible.

Lying dreamily in a boat, imagining a quest for a beautiful maiden's kin in Cordova amidst the bustling Jews of Ibn-Gebirol's era, one bears the physical inconveniences without shock. Similarly, if the grim scenery of St. Mary Axe and Whitechapel were magically transposed to the Rhine's banks in the eleventh century, transforming the cries of Crusaders into the bay of bloodhounds and forcing the reviled Jew to stand erect and heroic against sword and firebrand, the dingy shops and unprepossessing faces would dissolve into the thrill of contemplative emotion. Yet, the fervor we muster for grandiose martyrdom pales beside the sustained enthusiasm required when there is no danger, no challenge—only the impartial midday sun falling upon commonplace, perhaps half-repulsive objects that are, in truth, beloved ideas made flesh. Here resides the chief poetic energy: the force of imagination piercing or exalting solid fact, rather than floating aimlessly among cloud-pictures. To glory in a prophetic vision of knowledge sweeping the globe is an easier exercise for the believing mind than witnessing its inception in newspaper placards staring from bridges beyond cornfields. For many delicate souls, it is entirely possible to be in the thick of Armageddon without perceiving anything more than the annoyance of a puff of explosive smoke and the struggle of bodies on the ground immediately surrounding them.

It was contrary to Deronda's nature to scorn the feeble, fastidious sympathy that recoils from the broad sweep of human life. But now, with Mirah standing before him as a living reality, whose fate he was compelled to safeguard, he viewed every common Jew and Jewess through the lens of comparison with her. He harbored a presentiment of the collision between her idealized notion of an unknown mother and brother and the harsh discovered fact—a premonition sharpened by a suppressed awareness that a similar possibility of collision might lurk hidden within his own destiny. He would not have looked with complacent expectation upon wealthy Jews, those who outdid the lords of the Philistines in their diversions; yet, since there was no likelihood of Mirah's friends residing among that class, their habits did not immediately engage his concern. In this mood, he rambled without anticipating a more fruitful result than a subtle preparation of his own mind, perhaps for future theorizing as well as practice—much as if Mirah were related to Welsh miners, he had gone to scrutinize their ways, hoping simultaneously to glean details on the history of strikes.

He did not truly yearn to find anyone in particular. When, as was his habit, he scanned the names above shop doors, he was content that none bore the inscription "Ezra Cohen." He confessed, however, that he particularly wished Ezra Cohen should not keep a shop. Wishes are often deemed ominous; according to this superstition, the order of the world is arranged such that if one holds an impious objection to a squint, one's offspring is more likely to be born with one; conversely, if one desires a squint, it will not be granted. This despondent view of probability is entirely rejected by the hopeful, who take their wishes as sufficient security for fulfillment. Who remains absolutely neutral? One morning, Deronda turned into a small side street escaping the noise and obstructions of Holborn, feeling the scales tip decisively toward the despondent side.

Rather weary of the streets, he paused to hail a hansom cab approaching when his attention was snagged by fine old clasps in chased silver displayed in the window to his right. His initial thought was that Lady Mallinger, possessing a strictly Protestant taste for such Catholic relics, might appreciate these missal-clasps transformed into a bracelet. Then his eyes swept the remainder of the window's contents, revealing that this was a pawnbroker's establishment where lead was exchanged for jewelry, lace, and all equivocal objects masquerading as *bric-à-brac*. A placard in the corner announced: *Watches and Jewelry exchanged and repaired*. But his scrutiny had been noticed from within. A figure emerged from the doorway, looking around with a tone of cordial encouragement, "Good day, sir." The instant was sufficient for Deronda to recognize the face—unmistakably Jewish—belonging to a young man of about thirty. Wincing from the shopkeeper's inevitable persuasiveness, he returned the greeting only to pass swiftly to the other side of the street, beckoning the cabman to draw up there. From that vantage point, he read the sign above the window: *Ezra Cohen*.

There might be a hundred Ezra Cohens lettered above shop windows, yet Deronda had not encountered them before. Presumably, the young man interested in a potential customer was Ezra himself; he was of the age expected for Mirah's brother, who had grown up while she remained a child. However, Deronda's first endeavor as he drove homeward was to convince himself that there was no warrantable presumption linking this Ezra to Mirah. Next, he reasoned that even if, against all logic, this man proved to be the brother, and inquiries revealed the mother to be deceased, it was not his duty to disclose the discovery to Mirah. An inconvenient disturbance arose in this conclusion: his recently acquired knowledge that Mirah possessed a religious desire to know of her mother's death and to ascertain whether her brother lived. How far was he justified in determining another life by his own notions? Was it not his secret grievance against the manner in which others had ordered his own existence that he lacked open daylight on all its relations, depriving him, unlike other men, of the full guidance of primary duties?

Immediate relief from this internal debate came from the reflection that he had made no real discovery yet, and that closer inspection of the facts would certify that no demand existed for any decision whatsoever. He intended to return to the shop as soon as convenient to purchase the clasps for Lady Mallinger. But he was hindered for several days by Sir Hugo, who, preparing to deliver an after-dinner speech on a burning topic, required Deronda to research the legal aspects of the question, alongside wasting daily hours in arguments that invariably ended in a drawn battle. On many issues, they held opposing views, yet Sir Hugo cared little for this. When Deronda articulated his point effectively, Sir Hugo replied with a mixture of satisfaction and regret, "Confound it, Dan! Why don't you make an opportunity of saying these things in public? You're wrong, you know. You won't succeed. You've got the massive sentiment—the heavy artillery of the country against you. But it's all the better ground for a young man to display himself on. When I was your age, I should have taken it. And it would be quite as well for you to be in opposition to me here and there. It would throw you more into relief. If you would seize an occasion of this sort to make an impression, you might be in Parliament in no time. And you know that would gratify me."

"I am sorry not to do what would gratify you, sir," Deronda said. "But I cannot persuade myself to look at politics as a profession."

"Why not? If a man is not born into public life by his position in the country, there is no way for him but to embrace it by his own efforts. The business of the country must be done—Her Majesty's Government carried on, as the old Duke said. And it never could be, my boy, if everybody looked at politics as if they were prophecy, and demanded an inspired vocation. If you are to get into Parliament, it won't do to sit still and wait for a call either from heaven or constituents."

"I don't want to make a living out of opinions," Deronda countered, "especially out of borrowed opinions. Not that I mean to blame other men. I dare say many better fellows than I don't mind getting on to a platform to praise themselves, and giving their word of honor for a party."

"I'll tell you what, Dan," Sir Hugo interjected, "a man who sets his face against every sort of humbug is simply a three-cornered, impracticable fellow. There's a

When Deronda arrived at five o'clock, the shop's iron-bound door yielded to the touch of a Christian servant, ushering him into a space that defied the grim expectations of a pawnbroker's rear chamber. The house, old and sprawling, concealed a sanctuary behind the storefront; by daylight, the room might have been a cavern of gloom, but now it breathed with the warm, amber luminescence of a fine brass lamp. Seven oil-flames danced above a table draped in snow-white linen, casting the smoky ceiling and shadowed walls into a chiaroscuro that rendered the gathered figures with the luminous intensity of a Venetian painting.

Here, the domestic tableau was arranged with theatrical precision. The grandmother, a woman of formidable presence, wore a gown of yellowish-brown, accented by a heavy gold chain that replaced a missing necklace. Under the lamp's glow, her face, marked by age and framed by a roll of gray hair, possessed a picturesque severity. Beside her, young Mrs. Cohen cut a striking figure in red and black, her throat encircled by a garland of large, artificial pearls. Between them, the household slept or waited: a baby nestled beneath a scarlet counterpane in the cradle, Adelaide Rebekah radiant in braided amber silk, and Jacob Alexander stooping in black velveteen with scarlet stockings.

As the four pairs of dark eyes glistened with a sudden, collective welcome, Deronda felt a flush of shame rise in his cheeks. By the harsh light of day, these creatures had inspired in him a supercilious distaste, a judgment born of prejudice and distance. Yet here, in the intimate theater of the private hearth, that disdain evaporated. The greeting was cordial, the atmosphere thick with a dignity that seemed to bloom specifically from the act of hosting. Deronda glanced around, marveling at the clutter of the old furniture. The oaken bureau and the high side-table appeared less like expressions of refined taste and more like artifacts of economic necessity, yet they were set with a deliberate care. A large dish of blue-and-yellow ware dominated the side-table, flanked by tarnished silver vessels, before which rested a massive volume bound in darkened vellum with a deeply ribbed spine. An open doorway at the far end revealed a second room, also aglow with light.

While absorbing these details through parenthetic glances, Deronda was drawn into the immediate transaction by Jacob's earnest solicitude regarding a knife. The boy had procured a specific tool, complete with a hook and a white handle, presenting it with the gravity of a merchant closing a deal. "Is that the sort of thing you want, Jacob?" Deronda asked, noting the item's utility. Jacob subjected the blade to a rigorous inspection, opening the hooks and comparing it to a cork-screw he produced from his pocket. "Why do you like a hook better than a cork-screw?" Deronda queried. "'Caush I can get hold of things with a hook," Jacob replied, his logic sound and practical. "A cork-screw won't go into anything but corks. But it's better for you, you can draw corks."

"You agree to change, then?" Deronda asked, catching the grandmother's delighted attention. "What else have you got in your pockets?" Jacob pressed, his demeanor shifting from childlike curiosity to deliberative seriousness. "Hush, hush, Jacob, love," the grandmother chided gently. Mindful of propriety, Deronda demurred, "I think I must not tell you that. Our business was with the knives." Jacob scanned Deronda's face for a moment, seemingly weighing the risk, before nodding gravely. "I'll shwop," he declared, handing over the cork-screw knife. Deronda pocketed it with corresponding solemnity.

Immediately, the small son of Shem darted into the adjacent room, his voice a rapid chatter that soon drew him back. Seeing his father enter, the boy snatched a little velveteen hat from a chair and donned it, rushing forward to greet the visitor. Cohen, however, retained his own hat, standing still as the two children climbed onto his legs, clasping his knees. He laid his hands upon each in turn, uttering a Hebrew benediction. His wife, having lifted the sleeping infant from the cradle, brought the child forward, holding it beneath her husband's outstretched palms to receive the blessing even in slumber. For a fleeting moment, Deronda perceived that this pawnbroker, proud of his trade, was not entirely prosaic; there was a sacred rhythm to his actions that transcended mere commerce.

"Well, sir, you found your welcome in my family, I think," Cohen said, removing his hat and assuming his former, bustling persona. "And you've been punctual. Nothing like a little stress here," he added, tapping his side pocket as he settled into a chair. "It's good for us all in our turn. I've felt it when I've had to make up payments. I began to fit every sort of box. It's bracing to the mind."

"Now then!" Cohen exclaimed, leaning forward. "Let us see, let us see." Deronda produced the ring he had spoken of earlier. "That is the ring I spoke of," he said, sliding it from his finger. "I believe it cost a hundred pounds. It will be a sufficient pledge to you for fifty, I think. I shall probably redeem it in a month or so."

Cohen's glistening eyes narrowed slightly as they met the ingenuous gaze of this young gentleman, who seemed to operate under the naive assumption that redemption was a source of satisfaction for a lender. Cohen took the ring, examined it with casual indifference, and returned it. "Good, good. We'll talk of it after our meal. Perhaps you'll join us, if you've no objection. Me and my wife'll feel honored, and so will mother; won't you, mother?" The invitation was doubled, and Deronda gladly accepted.

All turned and stood around the table. No dish was visible save one covered by a napkin; Mrs. Cohen had placed a china bowl near her husband for the ritual washing of hands. But before proceeding, Cohen paused, donning his hat again, and called out in a loud voice, "Mordecai!"

Deronda wondered if this was part of some religious ceremony, unsure what ancient rites might be expected of the household. From the next room came a distinct "Yes," prompting Deronda to look toward the open door. To his astonishment, he saw the figure of the enigmatic Jew he had encountered that morning in the bookshop. Their eyes met, and Mordecai appeared as surprised as Deronda; neither made any sign of recognition. Yet, as Mordecai moved to seat himself at the far end of the table, he bowed his head to the guest in a cold, distant manner, as if the disappointment of the morning remained a bitter stain on this new acquaintance.

Cohen now washed his hands, pronouncing Hebrew words throughout the motion. He removed the napkin to reveal two long, flat loaves sprinkled with seeds—the memorial of the manna that sustained the wandering forefathers. Breaking off small pieces, he distributed them to each member of the family, including Adelaide Rebekah, who stood on a chair, her entire length displayed in her amber garment, her little Jewish nose comically elongated by the compression of her lips in an effort to maintain a dignified posture. Cohen recited another Hebrew blessing, and then, uncovering the male heads, the family seated themselves. The meal commenced without any peculiarity that initially arrested Deronda's attention.

Preoccupied with a desire to steer the conversation toward a leading question, and haunted by the lingering image of Mordecai—who shared with him a series of fascinated, half-furtive glances—Deronda ate with little consciousness of the food. Mordecai wore no handsome Sabbath garment; instead of the threadbare, rusty black coat of the morning, he donned a light drab coat that appeared to have once been a handsome loose paletot, now shrunk tight with repeated washings. This change of attire accentuated his dark-haired, eager face, which bore a resemblance to the prophet Ezekiel, certainly not modish in contemporary eyes.

It was noticeable that the thin tails of the fried fish were reserved for Mordecai, a share typically assigned to a poor relation—a survival of prehistoric practice, perhaps, no longer widely admitted as superstition. Mr. Cohen maintained the conversation with lively vigor, introducing subjects in good taste: the Queen and the Royal Family, the Emperor and Empress of the French. Both the grandmother and wife entered these topics with zest. Mrs. Cohen the younger displayed an accurate memory of distinguished birthdays, while the elder assisted her son in recounting the visit of the Imperial couple to the city ten years prior.

"I dare say you know all about it better than we do, sir," Cohen prefaced, eager to impart full information. "Our baby is named Eugenie Esther," young Mrs. Cohen announced vivaciously. "It's wonderful how the Emperor's like a cousin of mine in the face," the grandmother remarked, her eyes widening. "It struck me like lightning when I caught sight of him. I couldn't have thought it."

"Mother and me went to see the Emperor and Empress at the Crystal Palace," Mr. Cohen recounted. "I had a fine piece of work to take care of, mother; she might have been squeezed flat—though she was pretty near as lusty then as she is now. I said if I had a hundred mothers I'd never take one of 'em to see the Emperor and Empress at the Crystal Palace

If the scale of one's guilt were the sole metric of moral weight, the annals of humanity would present a singular, harrowing tableau: the architects of imperial greed and the titans of financial predation marching shoulder-to-shoulder with petty thieves, pickpockets, and the butcher who slaughters with his own hands. Yet, wickedness does dispense its rewards, albeit unevenly. Those who triumph in this diabolical game inevitably descend below the baseline of human existence, becoming more cruel, more brutish than the fragile vessel of the earth can sustain for the vast majority of womankind. Most carry within them a form of conscience—a shadow of justice that manifests as fear, a shadow of love that appears as pity—barriers that prevent the attainment of serene, unburdened malice, a state of being notoriously difficult to maintain in our composite, vulnerable flesh.

On the twenty-ninth of December, Deronda knew the Grandcourts had arrived at the Abbey, though he had not glimpsed them until he prepared for dinner. A magnificent snowfall had blanketed the grounds, granting the children the rare, crystalline joy of snowball fights and castle building. During the Christmas holidays, the Mallinger girls demanded no amusement unless it was orchestrated and shared by "Cousin," as they invariably addressed Deronda. Following this vigorous outdoor exertion, he had engaged in a game of billiards, allowing the hours to slip by without his mind dwelling on the impending encounter with Gwendolen at dinner. Nevertheless, the prospect held a distinct fascination. As he retired to his room, slightly weary and flushed from the sport, before the half-hour bell tolled, he began to speculate on the transformative influence her marriage to Grandcourt would exert upon her spirit. He wondered if there would be perceptible shifts in her demeanor compared to his observation at Diplow, mirroring the changes he had noted since his first vision of her at Leubronn.

"I suspect there are natures that one could watch grow or degenerate daily if one observed them closely," he mused. "Some of us move faster than others. And I am certain she retains the strong imprints of anything that has once marked her. That incident with the necklace, the whisper that someone deemed her gambling improper, had clearly taken root. Yet such susceptibility cuts both ways; it can propel one toward desperation just as readily as toward redemption. Whatever fascinations Grandcourt holds for capricious tastes—heaven forbid one believes he possesses the power to summon tenderness in daily intimacy! One might be tempted to strike him for the mere spectacle of passion flashing across his face and lips. I fear she married him out of ambition, driven to escape poverty. But why did she initially flee from him? The destitution came later, surely. Poor creature! She may have been coerced into it. How can one feel anything but pity for a young being, brimming with untapped vitality, recklessly ignorant, hanging all her blind hopes upon the remains of a human soul?"

Naturally, the terms Deronda applied to the bridegroom were less charitable than the excuses and pity draped over the bride. His conception of Grandcourt as a "remnant" stemmed not from specific knowledge, but from the cumulative impression of polite interaction suggesting that Grandcourt had exhausted all natural, healthy interest in the world. Generally, when a marriage of consequence occurs, male acquaintances pity the bride and female acquaintances pity the groom; each is presumed capable of finding better fortune. Especially when the bride is charming, young men on the scene often conclude she lacks genuine attachment to a husband so uninteresting to themselves, assuming she wed on pragmatic grounds. In such scenarios, who pities the husband? Even his female friends often deem his position retributive, believing he should have chosen differently. Perhaps Deronda could be excused for withholding pity for Grandcourt, who had never struck observers as a man likely to emerge from experience with greater suffering than he inflicted upon others. Conversely, regarding Gwendolen—young, impulsive, eager for pleasure, nourished by flattery that convinces a lovely girl of her divine right to rule—how swiftly might life shift from expectation to a bitter realization of the irremediable! Having witnessed her, he must possess rather muted feelings not to anticipate her entrance into the room with some degree of interest. Still, since the honeymoon lay three weeks in the past, and Gwendolen had been enthroned not only at Ryelands but at Diplow, she was likely to have composed her countenance, manifesting or concealing her emotions with suitable discretion, unwilling to indulge curiosity with a helpless exposure of her inner turmoil.

A diverse assembly awaited the new couple. The old aristocracy was represented by Lord and Lady Pentreath; the old gentry by young Mr. and Mrs. Fitzadam of the Worcestershire branch; politics and the public good, specialized in the cider interest, by Mr. Fenn, Member for West Orchards, accompanied by his two daughters; Lady Mallinger's family by her brother, Mr. Raymond, and his wife; and the useful bachelor element by Mr. Sinker, the eminent counsel, and Mr. Vandernoodt, whose acquaintance Sir Hugo had found pleasant enough at Leubronn to warrant adoption in England.

All had gathered in the drawing-room before the new couple appeared. Time was spent chiefly observing the children—various little Raymonds, nephews and nieces of Lady Mallinger, alongside her own three girls, who were always permitted to appear at this hour. The scene was genuinely delightful, enlarged by full-length portraits with deep backgrounds inserted into the cedar paneling, surmounted by a ceiling glowing with the rich colors of coats of arms ranged between the sockets. Illuminated nearly as much by the red fire of oak boughs as by pale wax candles, the room was stilled by the deep-piled carpet and the high English breeding that subdued all voices. The mixture of ages, from the white-haired Lord and Lady Pentreath to the four-year-old Edgar Raymond, lent a varied charm to the living groups. Lady Mallinger, with her fair, matronly roundness and mildly prominent blue eyes, moved about in black velvet, carrying a tiny white dog on her arm as a finishing touch to her costume. The children were scattered among the ladies, while most gentlemen stood somewhat apart, conversing with a moderate vivacity typical of the long minutes preceding dinner. Deronda stood slightly apart from the main circle, engaged in a dialogue fixed upon him by Mr. Vandernoodt, a man of the finest Dutch blood imported at the Revolution. For the rest, he was one of those commodious figures in society who are nothing particular themselves but are understood to be acquainted with the best in every department: close-clipped, pale-eyed, nonchalant, serving as an excellent foil to the intense coloring and vivid gravity of Deronda.

They discussed the bride and bridegroom, whose arrival was anticipated. Mr. Vandernoodt, an industrious gleaner of personal details who could likely recount everything about a great philosopher or physicist save their theories or discoveries, implied he had learned many facts about Grandcourt since meeting him at Leubronn.

"Men who have seen a good deal of life do not always end by choosing their wives wisely. He has had rather an anecdotal history—gone rather deep into pleasures, I fancy, lazy as he is. But, of course, you know all about him."

"No, really," Deronda replied in an indifferent tone. "I know little more of him than that he is Sir Hugo's nephew."

Just then, the door opened, deferring any further satisfaction of Mr. Vandernoodt's communicativeness. The scene was one designed to highlight any figure of distinction entering it, and certainly, when Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt appeared, no observer could deny their figures possessed distinction. The bridegroom wore neither more nor less easy perfection of costume, neither more nor less well-cut impassibility of face, than before his marriage. It was to be assumed he would accept nothing less than the best in outward equipment, wife included; and the bride was precisely what one might expect him to choose. "By George, I think she's handsomer, if anything!" exclaimed Mr. Vandernoodt. Deronda agreed but remained silent. The white silk and diamonds—it might seem strange, but she did wear diamonds on her neck, in her ears, in her hair—may have contributed to the new imposingness of her beauty, which flashed upon him as more unquestionable, if not more thoroughly satisfactory, than when he had first seen her at the gaming table. Some faces, peculiar in their beauty, are like original works of art; for the first time, they are almost always met with scrutiny. But in seeing Gwendolen at Diplow, Deronda had discerned in her more than he had expected of that tender, appealing charm we call womanly. Was there any new change since then? He distrusted his impressions; yet as he saw her receiving greetings with what seemed a proud, cold quietude and a superficial smile, there seemed to operate within her the same demonic force that had possessed her when she took him in her resolute glance and turned away a loser from the gaming table. There was no time for further conclusion, no time even for him to offer his greeting before the summons to dinner.

He sat not far from opposite her at the table and could occasionally hear her answers to Sir Hugo, who was at his liveliest in conversation with her. Though he looked toward her with the intention of bowing, she gave him no opportunity for some time. Finally, Sir Hugo, who might have imagined they had already spoken, said, "Deronda, you will like to hear what Mrs. Grandcourt tells me about your favorite Klesmer."
