The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Lady Violet Lebas, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of London itself. She sat at her mahogany desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from Mr. Andrew Lang, his sprawling, confident script detailing his latest thoughts on literature.

Violet sighed, tracing the edge of the parchment with a silver letter-opener. Lang was a man of undeniable erudition, yet his letters were suffocated by the very patriarchal complacency she so deeply despised. He wrote to her of Virgil, of modern English poetry, of Fielding and Rochefoucauld, positioning himself as the benevolent instructor to her "undefeated resolution to admire only the right things."

"The right things," she murmured aloud, the words tasting like ash. What he meant, of course, was the established canon—the texts elevated by centuries of wealthy, privileged men who saw their own reflections in the exploits of conquerors and the musings of comfortable philosophers.

She turned to his pages on Fielding. Lang excused the novelist's coarse morality, arguing that men must be pardoned the "vices of young blood" so long as they were brave and generous. He painted a nostalgic picture of a "lusty old militant world," entirely blind to the fact that this merry England was built upon the bruised backs of women and the indentured silence of the poor. He dismissed the systemic inequalities that left women like Sophia Western with no recourse but to forgive the betrayals of their husbands.

But today, the ink on the page seemed to shift, bleeding into a darker narrative of her own making. In the margins of Lang's preface, where he spoke of "living authors" and the freedom of personal bias, Violet imagined a different kind of correspondence—one that did not seek to instruct, but to witness. She pictured a man, Daniel Deronda, walking beside her in the rain, not as a student of the canon, but as a seeker of the hidden. He would not ask for permission to speak of the "right things"; instead, he would lean in, his eyes sharp and unblinking, to reveal the fractures in the polished surface of society.

She thought of Grandcourt, the man who had recently married Gwendolen. To Lang, he was a specimen of masculine vigor, a "keen fellow" with estates to divide. But to Violet, he was a monument to the cruelty of the elite, a man who treated human lives as mere accessories to his own ambition. She recalled the whispers of the city, the rumors of a woman named Mrs. Glasher, a fiery dark-eyed beauty who had once held Grandcourt in thrall. Lang would dismiss this as a trivial scandal, a "dog's part" played by a new Jason. But Violet saw the tragedy beneath the gossip: the way power operated not just through force, but through the silent erosion of souls.

She closed the letter, the sound final and decisive. Outside, the rain continued its relentless rhythm, washing the streets clean but leaving the walls of the institution untouched. Yet, in her mind, a new story was taking shape. It was a story not of heroes and villains, but of the intricate web of connections that bound them all together—the shared pain of the marginalized, the hidden histories of the powerful, and the quiet, persistent hope that someone, somewhere, might finally see the truth behind the mask.

As she picked up her pen, Violet began to write, not to Lang, but to herself. She would craft a tale that refused to be categorized, a narrative that celebrated the resilience of the overlooked and challenged the very foundations of the world she inhabited. It was a story of defiance, of love that transcended boundaries, and of the enduring spirit of those who dared to dream of a better future. And in doing so, she found her own voice, clear and strong, rising above the noise of the city and the constraints of the past.

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Daniel Deronda, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of London itself. He sat at his mahogany desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, his gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from Sir Hugo Fronsberg, his sprawling, confident script detailing a pardon held safely in bank for Sigismund Aspern, a man Deronda suspected was plotting some mad project with broken wings.

But Deronda pushed the letter aside. His mind was elsewhere, caught in the trap of a peculiar test of mettle. In spite of his anxiety for Mirah and his curiosity regarding the enigmatic Mordecai, he had failed to reach the city before Sir Hugo, who had preceded his family to prepare for the opening of Parliament. Instead, Deronda found himself quartered in Park Lane, unaware that his chambers were already tenanted by Hans Meyrick. He had expected this; he had not expected the transformation awaiting him inside.

Most of us remember Retzsch's drawing of destiny: Mephistopheles playing chess with man for his soul, making feints of unintended moves to beguile the mortal into defending against the wrong point of attack. The fiend mocks preparation, persuading us against taking out waterproofs when the sky is clearing, foreseeing that our imbecile caution will become a prejudice against the very signs we ignored. It is a cruel irony when, after painfully adjusting oneself to what seems a wise provision, one finds all mental precaution beside the mark, excellent intentions reduced to miscalculated dovetails cut from a wrong starting-point. Deronda's magnanimity had prepared itself to meet misbehavior, only to find a far stranger call upon it.

His first impression was one of pure pleasure and amusement at finding his sitting-room transformed into an *atelier*, strewn with miscellaneous drawings and the contents of two chests from Rome. The lower half of the windows were darkened with baize, and the blonde Hans presided over the littered place as its weird genius. Hans's hair was longer than of old, his face more whimsically creased, his high voice rising further under the excitement of rapid talk. The friendship between them, kept warm since Cambridge through correspondence and episodes of companionship abroad, had evolved into a practiced exchange of spiritual borrowing and indulgence.

"I knew you would like to see my casts and antiquities," Hans said, after the first hearty greetings, "so I didn't scruple to unlade my chests here. But I've found two rooms at Chelsea not many hundred yards from my mother and sisters, and I shall soon be ready to hang out there—when they've scraped the walls and put in some new lights. That's all I'm waiting for. But you see I don't wait to begin work: you can't conceive what a great fellow I'm going to be. The seed of immortality has sprouted within me."

"Only a fungoid growth, I dare say—a growing disease in the lungs," Deronda replied, treating Hans in brotherly fashion despite the absurdity. He walked toward some drawings propped on the ledge of his bookcases: five rapidly sketched heads, different aspects of the same face. He stood at a convenient distance, making no remark. Hans, too, fell silent for a minute, took up his palette, and began touching the picture on his easel.

"What do you think of them?" he asked finally.

"The full face looks too massive; otherwise the likenesses are good," Deronda said, more coldly than was usual with him.

"No, it is not too massive," Hans decided. "There is always a little surprise when one passes from the profile to the full face. But I shall enlarge her scale for Berenice. I am making a Berenice series—look at the sketches along there—and now I think of it, you are just the model I want for the Agrippa." Hans moved to Deronda's side, pencil and palette in hand, adding hastily, "No, no, I forgot; you don't like sitting for your portrait, confound you! However, I've picked up a capital Titus. There are to be five in the series. The first is Berenice clasping the knees of Gessius Florus... Then, this, where she stands on the Xystus with Agrippa... The third is Berenice exulting in the prospects of being Empress..."

"You must put a scroll in her mouth, else people will not understand that. You can't tell that in a picture."

"It will make them feel their ignorance then—an excellent aesthetic effect. The fourth is Titus sending Berenice away... *invitus invitam*... I've found a model for the Roman brute."

"Shall you make Berenice look fifty? She must have been that."

"No, no; a few mature touches to show the lapse of time. Dark-eyed beauty wears well, hers particularly. But now, here is the fifth: Berenice seated lonely on the ruins of Jerusalem. That is pure imagination. That is what ought to have been—perhaps was. Now, see how I tell a pathetic negative. Nobody knows what became of her—that is finely indicated by the series coming to a close. There is no sixth picture." Hans pretended to speak with a gasping sense of sublimity, drawing back his head with a frown as if searching for a like impression on Deronda. "I break off in the Homeric style. The story is chipped off, so to speak, and passes with a ragged edge into nothing—*le néant*; can anything be more sublime, especially in French? The vulgar would desire to see her corpse and burial... But now come and look at this on the easel. I have made some way there."

"That beseeching attitude is really good," Deronda said after a moment's contemplation. "You have been very industrious in the Christmas holidays; for I suppose you have taken up the subject since you came to London." Neither of them had yet mentioned Mirah.

"No," Hans said, putting touches to his picture, "I made up my mind to the subject before. I take that lucky chance for an augury that I am going to burst on the world as a great painter. I saw a splendid woman in the Trastevere—the grandest women there are half Jewesses—and she set me hunting for a fine situation of a Jewess at Rome. Like other men of vast learning, I ended by taking what lay on the surface. I'll show you a sketch of the Trasteverina's head when I can lay my hands on it."

"I should think she would be a more suitable model for Berenice," Deronda said, struggling to express his discontent.

"Not a bit of it. The model ought to be the most beautiful Jewess in the world, and I have found her."

"Have you made yourself sure that she would like to figure in that character? I should think no woman would be more abhorrent to her. Does she quite know what you are doing?"

"Certainly. I got her to throw herself precisely into this attitude. Little mother sat for Gessius Florus, and Mirah clasped her knees." Hans stepped back to inspect the effect of his touches.

"I dare say she knows nothing about Berenice's history," Deronda said, feeling more indignation than he could justify.

"Oh, yes, she does—ladies' edition. Berenice was a fervid patriot, but was beguiled by love and ambition into attaching herself to the arch-enemy of her people. Whence the Nemesis. Mirah takes it as a tragic parable, and cries to think what the penitent Berenice suffered as she wandered back to Jerusalem and sat desolate amidst desolation. That was her own phrase. I couldn't find it in my heart to tell her I invented that part of the story."

"Show me your Trasteverina," Deronda said, chiefly to hinder himself from saying something else.

"Shall you mind turning over that folio?" Hans asked. "My studies of heads are all there. But they are in confusion. You will perhaps find her next to a crop-eared undergraduate."

After Deronda had been turning over the drawings a minute or two, he said, "These seem to be all Cambridge heads and bits of country. Perhaps I had better begin at the other end."

"No; you'll find her about the middle. I emptied one folio into another."

"Is this one of your undergraduates?" Deronda asked, holding up a drawing. "It's an unusually agreeable face."

"That! Oh, that's a man named Gascoigne—Rex Gascoigne. An uncommonly good fellow; his upper lip, too, is good. I coached him before he got his scholarship. He ought to have taken honors last Easter. But he was ill, and has had to stay up another year. I must look him up. I want to know how he's going on."

"Here she is, I suppose," Deronda said, holding up the sketch of the Trasteverina.

"Ah," Hans said, looking at it rather contemptuously, "too coarse. I was unregenerate then."

Deronda was silent while he closed the folio, leaving the Trasteverina outside. Then, clasping his coat-collar and turning toward Hans, he said, "I dare say my scruples are excessive, Meyrick, but I must ask you to oblige me by giving up this notion."

Hans threw himself into

There exists a school of tragic thought that posits the truest sorrow of Prometheus was not his binding *after* he had stolen the celestial fire and wedged it into the reed (*narthex*) to convey it to mankind, but rather the torment that preceded the theft. It is the agony of being thrust by the brute forces of institutionalized power—Kratos and Bia—into a solitude of despised ideas, shackled by the throbbing paralysis of poverty and disease. It is a specific kind of exile where thousands walk past, yet none truly see.

"Second-sight" serves as a banner over contested territory, yet it remains an empirical certainty that certain souls possess yearnings and conceptions—indeed, traveled conclusions—that inevitably crystallize into images possessing a prophetic foresight. The act they intend to perform rises before them in its entirety, casting a coercive shadow; the event they hunger for or dread grows with the rapidity of a seed, fed by countless unseen impressions. These visionaries are not necessarily less capable of rigorous argument nor less sane than the common calculators of the marketplace. Sometimes, their natures offer manifold openings, reminiscent of the hundred gates of Thebes, allowing a greater and more miscellaneous influx of experience than flows through a narrow, beadle-guarded portal. Of course, there are abject specimens of the visionary, just as there is a diminutive mammal one might trap within the fold of a glove. This tiny relative of the elephant harbors no malice; yet, what great mental or social archetype is immune to producing offspring whose insignificance is both grotesque and toxic? One hesitates to consider the entire genus of "patriots"; or to imagine the elbow-shoving that might occur on Judgment Day among those who styled themselves authors, clutching volumes in their hands or stacked on carts.

This apologia for inevitable kinship serves to introduce the facts concerning Mordecai, whose silhouette had gnawed itself into Daniel Deronda's mind as a new, unanswered question. Yet, Deronda's interest was merely a vague, expectant suspension. The consumptive-looking Jew, appearing to be a fervid scholar subsisting on a quiet handicraft much like Spinoza, fit into none of Deronda's anticipations.

For Mordecai, however, the impact of their meeting was transformative. For many winters, while acutely aware of his ebbing physical vitality and a widening spiritual loneliness, his passionate desire had concentrated itself in a singular yearning: to find a young ear into which he could pour his mind as a testament. He sought a soul kindred enough to accept the spiritual product of his own brief, painful life as a mission to be executed. Remarkably, the hopefulness that often acts as a beneficent delusion for consumptive patients was in Mordecai wholly diverted from the prospect of bodily recovery and channeled entirely into this yearning for transmission. The desperate cry that had panted upward from overwhelming discouragement had grown into hope, and that hope hardened into a confident belief. Instead of being checked by his clear conception of hastening decline, this belief intensified into an expectant faith in a prophecy destined to fulfill itself in a fleeting window of time.

Several years had passed since he first began measuring men with a keen glance, searching for a possibility that evolved into a distinct conception. Initially, this clarity was achieved primarily through contrast: he sought a man who differed from himself. Tracing the reasons within his own self for the rebuffs he endured and the hindrances that besieged him, he imagined a counterpart who possessed all the elements necessary for sympathy, yet embodied them differently. This man must be a Jew, intellectually cultured and morally fervid—a nature ready to be filled from Mordecai's—but his face and frame must be beautiful and strong. He must be accustomed to the refinements of social life, his voice flowing with a full and easy current, his circumstances free from sordid need. He must glorify the possibilities of the Jew, refusing to sit and wonder as Mordecai did, bearing the stamp of his people amidst the signs of poverty and waning breath.

Sensitive to physical characteristics, Mordecai had, both abroad and in England, scrutinized pictures as well as men. In vacant hours, he would linger in the National Gallery, searching for paintings that might feed his hopefulness with grave and noble types of the human form, suitable to his own race. Yet, he returned in disappointment. Instances are scattered thinly across the galleries of Europe where the fortune or selection of even the chief masters has endowed art with a face that is simultaneously young, grand, and beautiful. Where melancholy exists, it is not a feeble passivity but enters into the foreshadowed capability of heroism.

Observers may recall his emaciated figure and dark eyes deep in their sockets as he stood before a picture that touched him to new or habitual meditation. He commonly wore a cloth cap with black fur trim, which no painter would have asked him to remove. Spectators were likely to perceive him as an odd-looking Jew, perhaps one who extracted money from pictures. Mordecai, when he looked at them, was perfectly aware of the impression he made. Experience had rendered him morbidly alive to how a man's poverty and other physical disadvantages cheapen his ideas, unless those ideas belong to a Peter the Hermit ringing a tocsin for the rabble. Yet, he was too sane and generous to attribute his spiritual banishment solely to the excusable prejudices of others; certain incapacities of his own had sealed the sentence of exclusion. Hence, his imagination constructed another man—one who would be something more ample than the "second soul" bestowed, according to Cabalist notions, to aid the insufficient first. This figure would be a blooming human life, ready to incorporate all that was worthiest in an existence whose visible, palpable part was burning itself fast away. His inward need for the conception of this expanded, prolonged self was reflected as an outward necessity. The thoughts of his heart—that ancient phrase best shadows the truth—seemed to him too precious, too closely interwoven with the growth of things to lack a further destiny. As the more beautiful, stronger, and more executive self took shape in his mind, he loved it beforehand with an affection that was half-identifying, half-contemplative and grateful.

Mordecai's mind worked so constantly in images that his coherent trains of thought often resembled the significant dreams attributed to sleepers by awake persons in their most inventive moments. Nay, they often mirrored genuine dreams in their manner of breaking off the passage from the known to the unknown. For a long while, he habitually conceived of the Being answering to his need as one distantly approaching or turning his back toward him, darkly painted against a golden sky. The reason for the golden sky lay in one of Mordecai's habits. He was keenly alive to the poetic aspects of London; a favorite resort, when strength and leisure allowed, was the bridges, particularly about sunrise or sunset. Even when bending over watch-wheels and trinkets, or seated in a small upper room overlooking dingy bricks and cracked windows, his imagination spontaneously planted him on a spot commanding a far-stretching scene. His thoughts operated in wide spaces; whenever possible, he tried to subject himself to the influences of a large sky. Leaning on the parapet of Blackfriars Bridge, gazing meditatively, the breadth and calm of the river—with its long vista half-hazy, half-luminous—the grand, dim masses of tall buildings signaling world commerce, and the oncoming of boats and barges from the distant stillness into sound and color—all entered his mood and blended indistinguishably with his thinking. It acted as a fine symphony to which we can hardly be said to listen, creating a medium that bears up our spiritual wings. Thus, the figure representing Mordecai's longing was mentally seen darkened by the excess of light in the aerial background. But in the inevitable progression of his imagination toward fuller detail, he ceased to see the figure with its back turned. It began to advance, and a face became discernible. The attributes of youth, beauty, refinement, Jewish birth, and noble gravity coalesced into a form and color that were hardly individual but typical, gathered from his memory of faces seen among the Jews of Holland and Bohemia, and from paintings that revived that memory.

Reverently, it may be said of this mature spiritual need that it was akin to the boy's or girl's picturing of a future beloved. However, the stirrings of such young desire are feeble compared with the passionate current of an ideal life straining to embody itself, made intense by resistance to imminent dissolution. The visionary form became a companion and auditor, keeping a place not only in the waking imagination but in those dreams of lighter slumber wherein it is truest to say, "I sleep, but my heart waketh"—when the disturbing trivial story of yesterday is charged with the impassioned purpose of years.

Of late, the urgency of irremediable time, measured by the gradual choking of life, had turned Mordecai's trust into an agitated watch for the fulfillment that must be at hand. Was the bell on the verge of tolling, the sentence about to be executed? The deliverer's footsteps must be near—the deliverer who was to rescue Mordecai's spiritual travail from oblivion and grant it an abiding place in the best heritage of his people. Many would count this yearning an insane exaggeration of his own value, even if his ideas were as true and precious as those of Columbus or Newton. They might dismiss it as the sublimer part for a man to declare, "If not I, then another," and hold cheap the meaning of his own life. But the fuller nature desires to be an agent, to create, and not merely to look

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the Chelsea windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Mirah, like the frantic heartbeat of a city indifferent to the quiet suffocations of its women. Inside, the air was thick with the scent of beeswax and damp wool, a heavy perfume masking the tension of the afternoon. The two rooms had been thrown together, a makeshift sanctuary where the boundaries between domestic duty and artistic aspiration blurred. Across the front room, a great length of embroidery stretched like a tapestry of interrupted dreams, a *pièce de résistance* in needlework that demanded the attention of any clever fingers at liberty. Mrs. Meyrick bent over one corner, Mab in the middle, and Amy at the far end, their movements synchronized in a quiet industry.

In the center of this tableau sat Mirah, perched on a camp-stool that seemed too small for her spirit. Her education in the arts of seamstress had been neglected, her stitches falling on the make-shift level of the tailor-bird's nest, yet today she served a dual purpose. She was the reader, her voice carrying the delicate cadences of Charles Lamb's "Essay of Elia," specifically the "Praise of Chimney-Sweeps," and simultaneously the model for Kate's title-page vignette—a fair public absorbed in the successive volumes of the family tea-table. She spoke with charming distinctness, all smiles over the "innocent blackness" of the chimney sweeps, until the sound of a knock shattered the spell.

It was not the gentle tap of a neighbor, nor the familiar rattle of the muffin-man. This was an imposing signal, a knock accompanied by a ring that magnified the sense of social existence, demanding immediate reverence. The five bright faces lifted in unison, their amusement curdling into anticipation.

"Dear me!" Mrs. Meyrick exclaimed, smoothing her apron. "Can it be Lady Mallinger? Is there a grand carriage, Amy?"

"No—only a hansom cab," Kate replied dryly, her eyes narrowing. "It must be a gentleman. Or perhaps the Prime Minister. Hans says the greatest man in London may get into a hansom cab."

"Oh, oh, oh!" cried Mab, her imagination already spinning tales of royalty. "Suppose it should be Lord Russell!"

The door opened, and the old maid-servant, distracted by the sheer magnitude of the visitor, left it ajar. Bowing toward Mrs. Meyrick was a figure quite unlike the respectable Premier they had imagined. He was tall and physically imposing, dressed in kid and kerseymere that strained against his frame, his face massive and flamboyant, crowned with wild hair and gold spectacles. As Mrs. Meyrick glanced at the card, the name confirmed the visual shock: Julius Klesmer.

Even embarrassment could hardly have made the "little mother" awkward, but her quick perceptions seized the significance of the moment instantly. The great personage had come to Mirah, sparing her the humiliation of approaching him; it was a sign of active interest, a validation of her talent. Yet, as Klesmer entered, the cozy dimensions of the cottage seemed to shrink into closets. The piano, once a centerpiece of domestic harmony, now seemed a ridiculous toy. The entire family existence felt petty and private, like an establishment of mice in the Tuileries, dwarfed by the vast, shadowy presence of the man who carried his consciousness in a theater of thousands.

Klesmer's glance swept the room, suggesting vast areas and a multitudinous audience that formed the usual scenery of his mind. Vanity, yes, but not the petty kind; it was the vanity of a conductor facing a symphony hall, natural to him as the length of his fingers. His grandiose air made Mab feel like a doll beside the cottage piano, yet beneath the performance lay a keen, thoroughly kind sensibility. He remembered homes no larger than this on the outskirts of Bohemia, and in the figurative Bohemia of small incomes, he knew the variety and romance that belonged to limited means.

"I hope I have not taken too great a freedom," he began, addressing Mrs. Meyrick with the utmost deference, his voice resonating slightly too loudly for the small space. "Being in the neighborhood, I ventured to save time by calling. Our friend, Mr. Deronda, mentioned to me an understanding that I was to have the honor of becoming acquainted with a young lady here—Miss Lapidoth."

Klesmer had discerned Mirah in the first moment of entering, but with subtle politeness, he looked round bowingly at the three sisters, feigning uncertainty as to which was the young lady in question.

"Those are my daughters: this is Miss Lapidoth," Mrs. Meyrick said, waving her hand toward Mirah.

"Ah," Klesmer said, in a tone of gratified expectation. He turned a radiant smile and a deep bow toward Mirah. Instead of being taken aback, she met his gaze with a calm pleasure. She liked the look of him, feeling a strange certainty that he would scold her, like a great musician and a kind man correcting a novice.

"You will not object to beginning our acquaintance by singing to me," he added, aware that they would all be relieved by getting rid of preliminaries.

"I shall be very glad. It is good of you to be willing to listen to me," Mirah said, moving to the piano. "Shall I accompany myself?"

"By all means," Klesmer replied, seating himself where Mrs. Meyrick indicated, ensuring he had a clear view of the singer. The acute little mother would not have acknowledged the weakness, but she whispered to herself, "He will like her singing better if he sees her."

All the feminine hearts except Mirah's beat fast with anxiety. Klesmer sat with a listening frown, terrifying in his intensity, and they dared only look at him furtively. If he said anything severe, it would be unbearable. They comforted themselves with the memory of Prince Camaralzaman, who had heard the finest things and still preferred Mirah's singing. She appeared to be doing her very best, radiating a confidence that belied her terror.

The song she chose was a fine setting of words from Leopardi's grand Ode to Italy. First came the recitative: *"O patria mia, vedo le mura e gli archi / E le colonne e i simulacri e l'erme torri / Degli avi nostri"*—seeing the walls, the arches, the columns, the empty towers of their ancestors. Then followed the mournful melody, a rhythmic plaint: *"Ma la gloria—non vedo"*—but glory, I do not see. After this came a climax of devout triumph, passing from the subdued adoration of a happy Andante in the words *"Beatissimi voi. Che offriste il petto alle nemiche lance / Per amor di costei che al sol vi diede"* to the joyous outburst of an exultant Allegro: *"Oh viva, oh viva: Beatissimi voi / Mentre nel mondo si favelli o scriva."*

When she ended, Klesmer said after a moment, "That is old Leo's music."

"Yes, he was my last master—at Vienna: so fierce and so good," Mirah said, with a melancholy smile. "He prophesied that my voice would not do for the stage. And he was right."

*"Continue, if you please,"* Klesmer commanded, putting out his lips and shaking his long fingers, while he went on with a smothered articulation quite unintelligible to the audience.

The three girls detested him unanimously for not saying one word of praise. Mrs. Meyrick was a little alarmed. Mirah, simply bent on doing what Klesmer desired and imagining that he would now like to hear her sing some German, went through Prince Radziwill's music to Gretchen's songs in the *Faust*, one after the other without any interrogatory pause. When she had finished, he rose and walked to the extremity of the small space at command, then walked back to the piano. Mirah had risen from her seat and stood looking toward him with her little hands crossed before her, meekly awaiting judgment.

Then, with a sudden unknitting of his brow and beaming eyes, he stretched out his hand and said abruptly, "Let us shake hands: you are a musician."

Mab felt herself beginning to cry, and all the three girls held Klesmer adorable. Mrs. Meyrick took a long breath.

But straightway the frown came again. The long hand, back uppermost, was stretched out in quite a different sense to touch with fingertip the back of Mirah's, and with protruded lip he said, "Not for great tasks. No high roofs. We are no skylarks. We must be modest." Klesmer paused here. And Mab ceased to think him adorable: "as if Mirah had shown the least sign of conceit!"

Mirah was silent, knowing that there was a specific opinion to be waited for. Klesmer presently went on—"I would not advise—I would not further your singing in any larger space than a private drawing-room. But you will do there. And here in London that is one of the best careers open. Lessons will follow. Will you come and sing at a private concert at my house on Wednesday?"

"Oh, I shall be grateful," said Mirah, putting her hands together devoutly. "I would rather get my bread in that way than by anything more public. I will try to improve. What should I work at most?"

The soul possesses a faculty capable of weaving veils that conceal and illuminate alike, transforming mere contingencies into the grandeur of pomp. Like the moon rising behind a dense, lofty grove on a summer evening, burning with an unconsuming fire that kindles the leafy shadows until the dusky foliage becomes a substance glorious and incorporated with her own radiance, so too does this inner power operate.

Deronda emerged from the narrow confines of his Chelsea lodging with a spirit heavy enough to demand physical exertion, a remedy for the fumes of his own irritable temper. Heading toward the city, the sight of the Chelsea Stairs and the waiting boats instantly resolved his dilemma: he would reject the stagnant passivity of a carriage ride. Instead, he hailed a wherry, seizing an oar to propel himself through the water.

His destination was Ram's bookshop. He had arrived too late the previous day to catch Mordecai during his midday vigil, having been informed that the remarkable inmate of the Cohen household invariably returned between five and six. Deronda felt a pressing necessity to deepen his acquaintance with this enigmatic figure, viewing it as a prerequisite for reclaiming his ring. He feared their conversations would once again terminate abruptly, severed by that fleeting drop of Mordecai's interest akin to a retracting drawbridge, threatening to seal off any future ease of communication. As the rhythm of rowing warmed his limbs and sharpened his focus on the errand at hand—specifically regarding Mirah—he underwent a familiar shift in mental clarity. He moved his perspective from seeing Mordecai merely as a tool for his own ends to recognizing the humanity of the man he had been taunting internally as little more than a recruiting sergeant, oblivious to the dramatic tragedy that supplied his recruits.

"If I secured the information I desperately seek from this man," Deronda mused, "I would be content provided he held no inclination to reveal more of himself, or explain why he seemed to harbor an expectation from me that remained unfulfilled. Such curiosity would likely fade; yet, perhaps they had passed each other like two ships, each carrying an exile who would have recognized the other had they been able to gaze face-to-face. Though there seems little chance of a peculiar bond between myself and this poor fellow, whose voyage, I suspect, is drawing to a close... I wonder if there exists a profound mutual longing between strangers who exchange vacant glances, or even those who secretly crave one another's absence amidst a crowd. Regardless, one creates opportunities for connection by adopting the recruiting sergeant's strategy."

As the wherry approached Blackfriars Bridge, where Deronda intended to disembark, the clock struck half-past four. The gray daylight was dying gloriously, its western clouds fractured into narrowing strata of purple before a vast, spreading saffron clarity. This hue possessed a monumental calm in the sky, yet upon the river, animated by its shifting forms, it manifested as a luminous dance—the alternating flash of ripples, the sudden glow of a brown sail, the transition of laden barges from shadow to color—all responding actively to that brooding splendor.

Now sufficiently heated, Deronda abandoned the oar and draped his Inverness cape over his shoulders. Just as he fastened the final button, his gaze met a well-known face framed against the bridge's parapet, illuminated by the western light into startling brilliance. It was a visage of bodily emaciation and spiritual urgency. It was Mordecai. Caught in his own watch toward the west, Mordecai had spotted the approaching boat, holding it fast in his gaze. First drawn by motion, then seized by a recovering intuition that made him tremble with foreknowledge, he watched until the nearing figure lifted its face toward him—the face of his visions. Immediately, with a white hand raised high, Mordecai beckoned repeatedly.

Anticipating recognition, Deronda had signaled without delay, and the reply came instantaneously. Mordecai lifted his cap and waved it, feeling in that singular moment that his internal prophecy was realized. Obstacles, inconsistencies, everything dissolved into the overwhelming sense of completion flooding his soul with the satisfaction of his longing. His exultation mirrored that of an experimenter observing the first stirrings of change corresponding to what his feverish concentration had previously foreseen. The prefigured friend emerged from the golden background to signal him; this was actuality; the remainder was yet to be written.

Within three minutes, Deronda had landed, settled his fare with the boatman, and joined Mordecai, who stood perfectly still, waiting for him by instinct.

"I was exceedingly glad to find you standing here," Deronda said. "I intended to proceed to the bookshop and search for you again. I was there yesterday—did they mention it?"

"Yes," Mordecai replied, his tone simple yet profoundly grave. "That was precisely why I came to the bridge."

This answer, delivered with such weight, struck Deronda as mysteriously cryptic. Did the peculiarities of this man truly stem from some form of mental alienation, as Cohen had hinted?

"You knew nothing of my presence in Chelsea?" Deronda asked after a pause.

"No; but I expected you to descend the river. I have been waiting for you these five years." Mordecai's deep-set eyes locked onto those of the friend who had finally arrived, filled with a look of affectionate dependence that was both heartbreaking and solemn. Deronda's sensitivity responded fully, unable to deny that this strangely revealed relationship was rooted in an illusion.

"It will be a satisfaction to me if I can prove of any genuine utility to you," Deronda answered earnestly. "Shall we take a cab and drive wherever you wish? You have surely walked enough with your short breath."

"Let us go to the bookshop. It will soon be time for me to be there. But now, look up the river," Mordecai said, turning back toward the water. He spoke in tones of an excited calm, so consumed by a sense of fulfillment that he perceived no barriers to a total understanding between them. "Observe the sky as it slowly fades. I have always loved this bridge; I stood upon it as a child. It is a gathering place for spiritual messengers. It is true, as the Masters said, that every order of existence has its angel—that signifies the full message of each from afar. Here, I have listened to the messages of earth and sky. When I was stronger, I would linger to watch the stars in the deep heavens. Yet, this time, just about sunset was what I cherished most. It has sunk into me, dwelling within me—fading, slowly fading. It was my own decline; it paused, it waited, until finally it brought me my new life, my new self, who will live when this breath is entirely spent."

Deronda remained silent, feeling strangely transformed. The initial suspicion that Mordecai might suffer from hallucinations, or be a monomaniac obsessed with a subject that had strained his diseased organism, yielded to a more submissive expectancy. His nature was too expansive, too willing to conceive realms beyond his immediate experience, to settle quickly on the facile diagnosis of "madness" whenever a consciousness displayed such fullness and conviction where his own was empty. Consistent with his habitual disposition, he found himself inclined to meet rather than resist claims arising from another's need. This claim carried a solemnity that seemed to radiate from Mordecai, utterly nullifying his outward poverty and elevating him into authority, as if he were that preternatural guide seen in universal legends who suddenly sheds his mean disguise to stand as a manifest Power. This impression was reinforced by a resolved quietude in Mordecai's demeanor, born of his persuasion of fulfillment. After a moment of shared silence, Mordecai said, "Let us go now." Once they were in the cab, he added, "We will alight at the end of the street and walk to the shop. You may examine the books; Mr. Ram is departing shortly and will leave us alone."

It appeared this enthusiast was as cautious and attuned to the judgments of others as if he were the antithesis of all enthusiasm, known as "a man of the world."

As the cab rattled along, Mirah remained present in Deronda's mind amidst this strange experience, though he foresaw that the dialogue would be dictated by Mordecai, not himself. He was no longer certain what questions he could pose. Reacting to his own mood, he thought inwardly, "I suppose I am in a state of complete superstition, as if awaiting a destiny capable of interpreting an oracle. Yet, some powerful connection must exist between me and this man, for he feels it intensely. Great heaven! What relation has proven more potent in the world than faith, even when mistaken? Than expectation, even when perpetually disappointed? Will my side of this relation be disappointing or fulfilling? Well, if it is ever possible for me to fulfill, I will not disappoint."

Ten minutes later, the two men, possessing an intensity of consciousness akin to two undeclared lovers, found themselves alone in the small, gas-lit bookshop. They turned face to face, each bare-headed by an instinctive urge to see the other fully. Mordecai stepped forward to lean his back against the small counter, while Deronda stood against the opposite wall, barely four feet apart. One wishes to capture those two faces forever, as Titian's *Tribute Money* has preserved two contrasting types. Imagine—the pathetic mark of consumption, paired with the brilliance of its glance, set upon a sharply defined facial structure reminiscent of a forsaken temple, lending an air of someone retreating unwillingly into distance. Now imagine this upon a Jewish face, naturally accentuated for expressing an eager mind—the count

On the evening following his encounter with Mordecai, Daniel Deronda sat in a room where the air felt charged with the static of unresolved questions. For a mind of lesser susceptibility, the adventure might have been merely a curious diversion, a fleeting anomaly to be filed away; but for Deronda, the event had struck a chord so resonant that it demanded immediate scrutiny. His intellect, reacting to the intensity of his emotion, began to dissect the very foundations of his agitation, wrestling with the terrifying prospect of surrendering to its guidance. A cold alarm prickled beneath his skin: he realized he was being half-dominanted by Mordecai's energetic certainty, and far more so by the fervent, almost desperate trust radiating from the older man.

This realization triggered his characteristic defense mechanism: a recoil from the moral stupidity of undervaluing what had touched him closely, and a dread of missing the seismic shift occurring in his own life—a crisis he sensed was monumental in the grand, historic tapestry of humanity. Had he read of such an incident transpiring centuries prior in Rome, Greece, or the dusty streets of Cairo, involving a young man equally dissatisfied with neutrality and craving a specific, arduous duty, he would have deemed it entirely natural. Such a figure, cloaked in the garments of a bygone era, would have been understood as a product of an age defined by its weightier consequences. Yet, here he was, dressed for dinner in a white tie, surrounded by a circle where possessing a conscience was often mocked as the solemn folly of taking oneself too seriously. Deronda shrank from this cowardice before fashionable ignorance, yet he simultaneously recoiled from letting his course be dictated by mere contagion, devoid of reason's consent, or allowing a reverential pity for spiritual struggle to rush him down a dimly lit path.

"What, after all, had truly happened?" he asked himself, mentally rehearsing the clinical diagnosis Sir Hugo would surely offer. "A consumptive Jew, driven by a fanaticism intensified by obstacles and hastening death, has fixated on Deronda as the living embodiment of some visionary image born of wedded hope and despair: despair of his own life, coupled with an irrepressible hope in the propagation of his fanatical beliefs." The instance was perhaps odd, exceptional in its form, but substantively, it was not rare. Fanaticism was not uncommon as bankruptcy; taken in all its aspects, it was abundant. While Mordecai waited on the bridge for the fulfillment of his visions, another man was convinced he held the mathematical key to the universe, viewing all known physicists as conspirators; another claimed the metaphysical key, differing only by a hair's breadth from the old wards. Scattered everywhere were terrible souls, eyes either glittering or preternaturally dull, searching for the listener, armed with volumes difficult to print or impossible to read. Mordecai possessed a more pathetic aspect, a speech more passionate and penetrative than the usual monomaniac; he was more poetical than a social reformer mapping the new moral world in parallelograms or an enthusiast preaching about sewage. Still, he fell under the same classification. It would be right and kind to indulge him slightly, to offer such practical comfort as was feasible, but what likelihood existed that his notions held the value he ascribed to them? In such cases, a man of the world knows what to think beforehand. As for Mordecai's conviction that he had found a new executive self, it might be preparing him for the worst of disappointments—that which presents itself as final.

Deronda's ear caught these negative whispers, repeating them distinctly to himself. It was not the first, nor the most pressing, occasion on which he had faced this question of family likeness among the heirs of enthusiasm—whether prophets, dreamers, "great benefactors of mankind," or devotees of phantasmal discovery. From the first believer in his own unmanifested inspiration down to the last inventor of a perpetual motion machine, the kinship of human passion and the sameness of mortal scenery inevitably filled fact with burlesque and parody. Error and folly had their hecatombs of martyrs. Reduce the grandest type of man known to history to an abstract statement of his qualities and efforts, and he appears in dangerous company: say that, like Copernicus and Galileo, he was immovably convinced in the face of hissing incredulity; so too is the contriver of perpetual motion. One cannot fairly judge spirits by this test alone. To avoid administering the dose of hemlock or the sentence of banishment in the wrong case, nothing suffices but a capacity to understand the subject-matter on which the immovable man is convinced, and a fellowship with human travail, both near and afar, to prevent us from scanning any deep experience lightly. Should we say, "Let the ages try the spirits"? No, for we are the beginning of the ages, justified only by just judgments formed in separate human breasts—separate yet combined. Even steam engines could not have been made without that condition; they must have remained in the mind of James Watt.

This train of thought was familiar enough to Deronda to save him from any contemptuous prejudgment of Mordecai, even if their communication had been free from that peculiar claim on himself, strangely ushered in by some long-growing preparation in the Jew's agitated mind. Considered in a purely rational way, this claim might seem dismissible as illusory and preposterous; yet, it was precisely what transformed Mordecai's hold on him from an appeal to ready sympathy into a clutch on his struggling conscience. Consciences are not all cast in the same mold, delivering fixed laws; they are the voice of sensibilities as varied as our memories, which also possess their own kinships and likenesses. Deronda's conscience included sensibilities beyond the common, enlarged by his early habit of imagining himself into the experiences of others.

What was the claim this eager soul made upon him? "You must believe my beliefs—be moved by my reasons—hope my hopes—see the vision I point to—behold a glory where I behold it!" To accept such a demand as a direct obligation would have been preposterous, bordering on dishonesty. Looking on the agitation of those moments, Deronda felt thankful that amidst his compassion, he had preserved himself from the bondage of false concessions. The claim rested, too, on a supposition that might be—and probably was—in discordance with the full fact: the supposition that he, Deronda, was of Jewish blood. Was there ever a more hypothetical appeal?

Yet, since the age of thirteen, Deronda had associated the deepest experience of his affections with a pure supposition: that Sir Hugo was his father. That hypothesis had been the source of passionate struggle within him; by its light, he had learned to subdue feelings and cherish them. He was well-used to finding a motive in a conception that might be disproved, and accustomed to contemplating revelations that could alter his view of his particular duties. To exist in a state of suspense, one of emotive activity and scruple, was a familiar posture of his conscience.

Now, suppose that wish-begotten belief in his Jewish birth, and that extravagant demand of discipleship, were the foreshadowing of an actual discovery and a genuine spiritual result. Suppose Mordecai's ideas made a real conquest over Deronda's conviction? Nay, it was conceivable that as Mordecai needed and believed that connection to replenish himself, so Deronda might receive from Mordecai's mind the complete ideal shape of that personal duty and citizenship lying dormant in his own thought like sculptured fragments certifying some beauty yearned after but not traceable by divination.

As this possibility presented itself in his meditations, he became aware that it would be labeled dreamy, and he began to defend it. If the influence he imagined submitting to had been that of an honored professor, an authority in a seat of learning, or a philosopher accepted as the voice of the age, would thorough receptiveness toward direction have been ridiculed? Only by those who deem it a sign of weakness to be obliged for an idea, preferring to hint that they have implicitly held in a more correct form whatever others have stated with sad explicitness. After all, what was there but vulgarity in taking the fact that Mordecai was a poor Jewish workman, perhaps to be met on a sanded floor in the parlor of the *Hand and Banner*, as a reason for deciding beforehand that no spiritual force within him could determine the fate of a white-handed gentleman? There is a legend of Emperor Domitian, who, hearing of a Jewish family of the house of David from whom the ruler of the world was to spring, summoned them in alarm but quickly released them upon seeing they had the hands of workmen. Conversely, a Rabbi stood waiting at the gate of Rome, confident the Messiah would be found among the destitute who entered. Both Emperor and Rabbi were wrong in their trust of outward signs: poverty and poor clothes are no sign of inspiration, Deronda told his inward objector, yet they have accompanied it in remarkable cases. To regard discipleship as out of the question because of these factors would be mere dullness of imagination.

A more plausible reason for dismissing discipleship was the strain of visionary excitement in Mordecai, which turned his wishes into overmastering impressions and made him read outward facts as fulfillments. Was such a temperament likely to accompany the wise estimate of consequences, the only safeguard from fatal error even for an ennobling motive? It remained to be seen whether that rare conjunction existed in Mordecai: perhaps his nature was one where a wise estimate of consequences was fused in the fires of passionate belief, thereby determining the very consequences it believed in. The inspirations of the world have come thus; even strictly

"If there are rungs on the ladder of suffering, Israel stands at the summit; if endurance sanctifies pain, the Jews claim the nobility of every land; and if a literature is deemed rich because it possesses a handful of classical tragedies, what judgment awaits a National Tragedy sustained for fifteen hundred years, authored and enacted by the heroes themselves?" These were the words of Zunz, echoing in Daniel Deronda's mind as he approached the Cohen household. Yet, standing before the shopfront, the weight of those verses felt strangely distant. Ezra Cohen wore no mantle of martyrdom; instead, his eyes gleamed with the pragmatic triumph of a man who had mastered the alchemy of profit, a success that grated against the very essence of the Jewish dispersion. This pawnbroker, a Jeshurun of commerce, was no living symbol of the great national sorrow. And yet, a disquieting irony settled over Deronda: how could a fragile vessel of collective consciousness, like Mordecai's, breathe so tenuously within the self-congratulatory ignorance of the Cohens' prosperity?

When Deronda reappeared, the gloom of the street vanished, replaced by a radiant, almost blinding gladness on the faces of the family. Ezra Cohen seized the moment to explain that while a diamond ring—or indeed, a little more capital—might have yielded greater returns, he cared naught for the coin when weighed against the sheer delight of his women and children in hosting a young gentleman whose arrival had become the sole topic of their discourse. Young Mrs. Cohen expressed regret that the infant slept, only to pivot instantly to the joy that Adelaide remained awake, urging Deronda to abandon the shop and retreat to the parlour to meet "mother and the children." Accepting the invitation, Deronda carried his portable gifts: a set of paper figures for Adelaide and an ivory cup-and-ball game for Jacob.

Inside, the grandmother presided over a chaotic tableau of card-playing, tossing plates into the air with a dexterity that kept them miraculously intact. "Stop!" cried Jacob, darting toward Deronda as he crossed the threshold. "Do not tread on my plate. Watch me throw it up again." Deronda paused, sharing a knowing glance with the grandmother, and watched the porcelain arc through the air, shattering only after several attempts. Once the child was satisfied, Deronda stepped forward to take his seat. He noted that the door from which Mordecai had emerged on his previous visit was now firmly shut, a barrier he intended to breach only after establishing a rapport with the family.

Only when Adelaide rested on his lap, arranging her paper figures in a miniature ballet, and Jacob practiced his throws with the ivory ball, did Deronda finally inquire, "Is Mordecai in just now?"

Ezra Cohen, pausing his business to peer over his shoulder, asked, "Where is he, Addy?"

"In the workroom," his wife replied, nodding toward the sealed door.

"The truth is, sir," Cohen began, his tone shifting to a mixture of apology and pride, "we cannot fathom what has come over him these last days. He is... 'touched,' as one might say—not entirely rational in all things, unlike you and me—but he has been wonderfully regular and industrious, taking as much delight in the boy as anyone could. Lately, however, he moves like a sleepwalker or sits as still as a wax figure."

"It is the disease, poor soul," the grandmother whispered tenderly. "I fear he cannot withstand it much longer."

"No, no," corrected the younger Mrs. Cohen. "It is merely something in his head. He turns over writing constantly, and it takes him a long time to hear and respond when I speak to him."

"You may consider us weak ourselves," Cohen admitted apologetically. "But neither my wife nor my mother would part with him, even if he became a heavier burden. We understand the long and short of matters; it is simply our principle. Some fools conduct business at a loss without realizing it. I am not one of them."

"Oh, Mordecai carries a blessing within him," the grandmother insisted.

"He has something wrong inside him," Jacob interjected, stepping forward to amend his grandmother's error. "He said he couldn't talk to me, and he refused a single bun."

"Far from being surprised at your concern," Deronda said gently, "I share that sentiment myself. I recently spoke with him at Ram's bookshop—in fact, I promised to bring him here so we might walk together."

"That explains it!" Cohen slapped his knee, his demeanor brightening. "He has been waiting for you. It seems to have lifted him. He must be talking about his learning to you. How kind of you, sir; though I suspect there is little to extract from it, or he would not be where he is. But here comes the shop." Hurrying away, Jacob leaned close to Deronda's elbow with obliging familiarity. "I'll fetch Mordecai for you, if you wish."

"No, Jacob," his mother commanded softly. "Open the door for the gentleman and let him go in himself. Hush! Make no noise."

Jacob, playing his part with skillful discretion, turned the handle with barely a whisper. Deronda followed, standing on the threshold of the small room. Lit only by a dying fire and a single shaded candle, the space held a somber intimacy. Scattered across the board beneath the window lay various articles of jewelry, while books were heaped in the corner. Mordecai sat on a high chair with his back to the door, his hands clasped on the board, a watch propped on a stand before him. He sat in a state of agonizing anticipation, akin to a prisoner awaiting a delayed release. When he heard Deronda's voice—"I am come for you. Are you ready?"—he turned instantly, seizing his fur-lined cap and rising to join him.

Within moments, they stood in the sitting-room. Jacob, noting the shift in his friend's demeanor, grabbed Mordecai's arm. "Look at my cup and ball!" he exclaimed, launching the ball high near Mordecai's face, hoping to cheer a recovering spirit. The gesture worked; the tension in Mordecai's mind visibly relaxed, allowing a smile to break through. "Fine, fine!" he breathed.

"You have forgotten your greatcoat and comforter," young Mrs. Cohen reminded him, and he retreated briefly to retrieve them.

"He has returned to life, do you see?" Cohen whispered, re-entering the room with a triumphant undertone. "I told you so; I am mostly right." Then, in his normal voice, he added, "Well, sir, we need not detain you further, I trust. But I hope this is not the final time we shall see you."

"Will you come again?" Jacob asked, stepping forward eagerly. "Watch, I can catch the ball! I'll wager I can catch it without stopping if you return."

"He has remarkably skilled hands," Deronda remarked, turning to the grandmother. "From which side of the family does he inherit them?"

The grandmother merely nodded toward her son, who responded promptly, "My side. My wife's family possess no such aptitude. But bless your soul! Ours is a cleverness as durable as gutta-percha; you can twist it whichever way you please. There is nothing some elderly gentlemen will not accomplish if you set them to it." Cohen winked down at Jacob's retreating back, though the effect was dubious; the child emitted a nasal whinnying laugh and stamped his feet, singing, "Old gentlemen, old gentlemen," in a chiding cadence.

Deronda found himself thinking, *I shall never grasp the full measure of these people until I ask Cohen directly whether he lost a sister named Mirah when she was six.* The moment of inquiry did not yet feel accessible to him. Nevertheless, his initial repulsion at their commonness began to soften, tempered by a kinder observation. Despite their unrefined mannerisms and speech, he was compelled to acknowledge a genuine moral refinement in their care for the consumptive workman, whose intellectual distinction they perceived primarily as a harmless, silent rambling.

"The Cohens seem to harbor a special affection for you," Deronda observed as he and Mordecai stepped onto the doorstep.

"And I for them," came the immediate reply. "They possess the heart of the Israelite within them, though they remain like horses and mules, lacking understanding beyond the narrow path they tread."

"I fear I have caused you unease," Deronda said, "by my delay in keeping my promise. I intended to come yesterday, but found it impossible."

"Yes—yes, I trusted you. Yet I was truly uneasy, for the spirit of my youth has been stirred within me, and this body is too frail to bear the beating of its wings. I am as a man bound and imprisoned for many years: behold him brought to speech with his fellow, his limbs set free. He weeps, he totters; the joy within him threatens to break and overturn the tabernacle of flesh."

"You must not speak too freely in this evening air," Deronda advised, feeling the weight of Mordecai's reliance like cords binding him painfully. "Wrap your mouth in the woolen scarf. We are heading to the *Hand and Banner*, I presume, and shall find privacy there?"

"No, that is precisely my grievance regarding your absence yesterday. Tonight is the evening of the club I mentioned, and we might not secure any minutes alone until late, when everyone else has departed. Perhaps we should seek another venue. But I am accustomed to that solitude. In new places, the outer world

The silence that settled over the room was not empty; it was a heavy, sacred suspension, a held breath between worlds. Mordecai lay still, his consciousness drifting through the fog of fever until the unwonted quiet pierced through, anchoring him. He looked up at Deronda, and there was no trace of the bewildered stranger usually found in such encounters. Instead, his gaze was one of reposing satisfaction, a calm acceptance of a destiny being fulfilled.

Deronda rose immediately, drawing his chair closer until the distance between them vanished, eliminating the need for raised voices or strained projection. The movement struck Mordecai with the profound relief of a patient sensing the gentle adjustment of a pillow beneath his head. He began to speak in a low, intimate murmur, articulating thoughts as if speaking only to himself, yet every syllable carried the weight of a sermon.

"In the doctrine of the Cabbala," Mordecai whispered, his voice thin but resonant, "souls are born again and again in new vessels until they achieve perfection and purification. When a soul is liberated from a worn-out shell, it seeks the fellow-soul that requires it, that they may be perfected together, completing their earthly labors before departing the mortal realm to return to the eternal bosom. It is the lingering imperfection of souls already trapped in flesh that delays the birth of new souls and postpones the Messianic age. Thus, the mind gives shape to the hidden, casting a shadow of what is known, speaking truth even in parable. When my long-wandering spirit is finally freed from this weary body, it will join yours, and our shared work will be completed."

His pause hung in the air, an implicit plea that Deronda's empathy refused to ignore. He searched for words that were truthful, yet for Mordecai's listening ear, they were inevitably saturated with unspoken significance. "Everything I can, in conscience, do to make your life effective, I will do," Deronda said, his voice steady.

"I know it," Mordecai replied, his tone radiating a quiet certainty that rendered further reassurance unnecessary. "I heard it. You see it all—you stand beside me on the mountain of vision, beholding the paths of fulfillment that others deny." He fell silent for a moment, gathering his scattered memories, before continuing meditatively. "You will take up my life exactly where it was severed. I feel myself transported back to that day when my life broke. The bright morning sun was blazing on the quay at Trieste; the garments of men from every nation shimmered like jewels; the boats pushed off into the harbor. The Greek vessel destined to carry us to Beirut was to depart within the hour. I was traveling with a merchant as his clerk and companion. I told myself, 'I shall behold the lands and peoples of the East, and I shall speak with a fuller vision.' I breathed then as you breathe now, without labor; I possessed the light step and the enduring vitality of youth. I could fast, I could sleep on the hardest ground. I had wedded poverty, and I loved my bride—for to me, poverty was freedom. My heart exulted as if it belonged to Moses ben Maimon, strengthened by the vigor of sixty years, knowing the vast work that awaited. It was the first time I had ventured south; the soul within me felt its former sun. Standing on the quay, where the earth seemed to emit light and the shadows wore an azure glory as if spirits had become visible, I felt myself immersed in a flood of glorious life, wherein my own small, counted existence melted away until I knew it no more. A great sob rose within me, akin to the rush of waters too powerful to resist—a bliss so overwhelming it nearly drowned me. So I stood there, awaiting my companion, and I saw him not until he said, 'Ezra, I have been to the post; there is your letter.'"

"Ezra!" Deronda exclaimed, unable to suppress the shock that surged through him.

"Ezra," Mordecai repeated, affirmative and utterly engrossed in the memory. "I was expecting a letter; for I wrote continually to my mother. And that sound of my name was like the touch of a wand that recalled me to the body from whence I had been released, as if to mingle once more with the ocean of human existence, free from the crushing pressure of individual bondage. I opened the letter; and the name came again as a cry that would have disturbed me even in the bosom of heaven, making me yearn to reach out to that sorrow—'Ezra, my son!'"

Mordecai paused again, his imagination arrested by the sheer grip of that long-past moment. Deronda's mind hovered breathless on the precipice of what was coming. A strange, terrifying possibility had suddenly crystallized in the air. Mordecai's eyes were cast downward in abstracted contemplation, and shortly, he continued, "She was a mother of whom it might rightly be said, 'Her children arise up and call her blessed.' In her, I understood the meaning of that Master who, perceiving the footsteps of his mother, rose and declared, 'The Majesty of the Eternal cometh near!' That letter was her cry from the depths of anguish and desolation—the scream of a mother robbed of her little ones. I was her eldest. Death had taken four babes one after another. Then came, late, my little sister, who was, more than all the rest, the desire of my mother's eyes. And the letter was a piercing cry to me—'Ezra, my son, I am robbed of her. He has taken her away and left disgrace behind. They will never come again.'"

Here, Mordecai lifted his eyes suddenly, laying his hand firmly on Deronda's arm. "Mine was the lot of Israel. For the sin of the father, my soul must go into exile. For the sin of the father, the work was broken, and the day of fulfillment delayed. She who bore me was desolate, disgraced, destitute. I turned back. On the instant I turned—her spirit and the spirit of her fathers, who had worthy Jewish hearts, moved within me and drew me. God, in whom dwells the universe, was within me as the strength of obedience. I turned and traveled with hardship—to save the scant money which she would need. I left the sunshine and traveled into freezing cold. In the final stage, I spent a night exposed to the biting cold and snow. And that was the beginning of this slow death."

Mordecai let his eyes wander again, removing his hand. Deronda resolutely suppressed the questions that urgently demanded expression. While Mordecai remained in this state of raw emotion, no other confidence could be sought than what emerged spontaneously; indeed, Deronda felt a kindred emotion that made him dread his own speech as too momentous.

"But I worked. We were destitute—everything had been seized. And she was ill: the clutch of anguish was too strong for her, working alongside some lurking disease. At times she could not stand for the pounding of her heart, and the images in her brain became chambers of terror, where she beheld my sister reared in evil. In the dead of night, I heard her crying for her child. Then I rose, and we stretched forth our arms together and prayed. We poured forth our souls in desire that Mirah might be delivered from evil."

"Mirah?" Deronda repeated, wishing desperately to assure himself that his ears had not been deceived by a forecasting imagination. "Did you say Mirah?"

"That was my little sister's name. After we had prayed for her, my mother would rest awhile. It lasted hardly four years, and in the minute before she died, we were praying the same prayer—I aloud, she silently. Her soul went out upon its wings."

"Have you never since heard of your sister?" Deronda asked, as quietly as he dared.

"Never. Never have I heard whether she was delivered according to our prayer. I know not, I know not. Who shall say where the pathways lie? The poisonous will of the wicked is strong. It poisoned my life—it is slowly stifling this breath. Death delivered my mother, and I felt it a blessedness that I was alone in the winters of suffering. But what are the winters now?—they are far off"—here Mordecai again rested his hand on Deronda's arm, and looked at him with that joy of the hectic patient which pierces us to sadness—"there is nothing to wail in the withering of my body. The work will be the better done. Once I said the work of this beginning was mine, I am born to do it. Well, I shall do it. I shall live in you. I shall live in you."

His grasp had become convulsive in its force. Deronda, agitated as he had never been before—the certainty that this was Mirah's brother suffusing his own strange relation to Mordecai with a new solemnity and tenderness—felt his strong young heart beating faster and his lips paling. He shrank from speech. He feared, in Mordecai's present state of exaltation (already an alarming strain on his feeble frame), to utter a word of revelation about Mirah. He feared to make an answer below that high pitch of expectation which resembled a flash from a dying fire, causing watchers to fear to see it die the faster. His dominant impulse was to do as he had once done before: he laid his firm, gentle hand on the hand that grasped him.

Mordecai's hand, as if it had a soul of its own—for he was not distinctly willing to do what he did—relaxed its grasp and

The air in the garden hummed with a latent vitality, as if the earth itself were tuning a string instrument for unseen listeners. Fairy folk, invisible to the eye but keen of ear, listened intently as seeds broke their winter slumber beneath the soil. For their music, they needed no instruments; the hedgerows woke from a trance, sending sap trembling upward into budding green, creating little rhythmic floods of sound that filled the ears of spirits clad in light. Beauty, in this realm, was born of sound, vibrating into existence for senses refined beyond the mundane.

But Gwendolen was not listening to the symphony of the natural world. Her mind was a closed circuit, looping obsessively on Daniel Deronda. She pondered his thoughts with a intensity that bordered on feverish, wondering what constituted his "ideas about things" and how his days were truly spent. Yet, to frame the motives and adventures of her own life—or indeed, of anyone else's—required a complexity she lacked. It was as futile as expecting a lapdog to comprehend the grand strategy of canine existence. Just as impossible was it for her to conceive that Deronda's life was governed by the sweeping, historical destiny of the Jewish people; nor could she imagine him rising atop a brazen horse to vanish from her horizon as a twinkling star.

Despite the crushing weight of inferiority forced upon her by her marriage, Gwendolen inevitably imagined a far larger space for herself in Deronda's affections than she actually occupied. One assumes that older, wiser souls are immune to seeing their own anxieties or elations reflected in the minds of others, but Gwendolen, trapped in her youthful solitude, could not resist dwelling on the faintest signs of special interest he showed her. She mistook the color and proportion of those signals, projecting onto him a depth of regard that was not there.

So, what counsel would he offer? *He* had told her she must cultivate interest in others, acquire knowledge, and care for the "best things"—but how was she to begin? She wandered mentally through his potential recommendations, recalling famous writers she had either avoided or found impenetrable, harboring a half-smiling wish to mischief ask if they were simply "medicine for the mind." Then, guilt pricked her conscience. When safely away from observation, she ascended to her room carrying a miscellaneous selection: Descartes, Bacon, Locke, Butler, Burke, Guizot. As a clever young lady of education, she knew these authors were ornaments of mankind, assuming Deronda had devoured them. Hoping that by dipping into them sequentially, relying on her rapid intellect, she might bridge the chasm between their worlds, she sought a viewpoint closer to his level.

Yet, time for these vast mental excursions was a scarce commodity. Constantly, she had to appear on the scene as Mrs. Grandcourt, acutely aware of being watched by the exacting eyes of a husband who had found a potent motive to exercise his tenacity: ensuring his marriage answered every end he chose, and achieving that goal with greater completeness the more he discerned any opposing will in her. Whatever rebellions churned within her, Gwendolen could not reconcile herself to failure in her performance. No feeling had yet softened her toward any act, word, or look that might serve as a confession to the world. What she most dreaded was a violent impulse that would force an involuntary admission. Consequently, the will to remain silent in every other direction had thrown a frantic impetuosity into her confidences toward Deronda, to whom her thoughts continually turned as a lifeline against herself. Her riding, hunting, and socializing were performed in a spirit of grim achievement, devoid of zest or young gladness. Around Diplow, during those weeks of the new year, Mrs. Grandcourt was regarded as wearing her honors with triumphant detachment.

"She disguises it under an air of taking everything as a matter of course," observed Mrs. Arrowpoint. "A stranger might suppose she had condescended rather than risen. I always noticed that doubleness in her."

To her mother, above all, Gwendolen was bent on projecting complete satisfaction. Poor Mrs. Davilow was so effectively deceived that she interpreted the unexpected emotional distance maintained by her daughter—as enforced by Grandcourt's handsome provision for her—as a mere comparative indifference, now that marriage had supposedly created new interests. Being fetched for lunch and then dinner alongside the Gascoignes, driven back shortly after breakfast the following morning, and receiving brief calls where her husband waited outside on horseback or in the carriage, constituted the entirety of her permitted intercourse.

The truth was stark. The second time Gwendolen proposed inviting her mother with Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne, Grandcourt had initially remained silent before drawling, "We can't be having *those people* always. Gascoigne talks too much. Country clergy are always bores—with their confounded fuss about everything."

That speech brimmed with foreboding for Gwendolen. To have her mother categorized under "those people" confirmed her previous dread of drawing her too near. Still, she could not articulate the true reasons; she could not tell her mother, "Mr. Grandcourt wishes to recognize you as little as possible; moreover, it is better you see little of my married life lest you discover I am miserable." So, she waved aside every allusion to the subject with practiced lightness. When Mrs. Davilow again hinted at securing a house near Ryelands, Gwendolen replied, "It would not be so nice for you as being near the rectory here, mamma. We shall perhaps be very little at Ryelands. You would miss my aunt and uncle."

All the while, this contemptuous veto by her husband on any intimacy with her family, driving her to proudly shrink from presenting them as troublesome pensioners, was stirring a deeper, inward inclination toward them. She had never felt so kindly toward her uncle, so disposed to recall his cheerful, complacent activity and spirit of kind management—even when mistaken—as a genuine comfort compared to the neutral, chilling loftiness that permeated her daily existence. Here, perhaps, she was unconsciously finding fragments of that mental enlargement that proved elusive from her occasional dashes into difficult authors, who demanded dismissal rather than blending with her agitations.

One day, a delightful surprise awaited the household at Offendene. Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne were present when Gwendolen rode up without her husband, accompanied only by the groom. All, including the four girls and Miss Merry, seated in the dining-room at lunch, witnessed the welcome approach. Even the elder sisters were not without a trace of Isabel's romantic sensibility, viewing the beautiful sister astride the splendid chestnut—which held its head as if proud to bear her—as a reincarnation of Harriet Byron or Miss Wardour emerging from her "happiness ever after."

Her uncle stepped to the door to offer his hand, and she sprang from her horse with an air of alacrity that encouraged that notion of guaranteed bliss. Gwendolen was particularly bent today on setting her mother's heart at rest; her unusual sense of freedom in making this solitary visit enabled her to endure the pressure of painful facts that were urging themselves anew. The seven family kisses were not so tiresome as they had been.

"Mr. Grandcourt is gone out, so I determined to fill up the time by coming to you, mamma," Gwendolen said, laying down her hat and seating herself beside her mother. Then, fixing her with a playfully monitory gaze, she added, "That is a punishment to you for not wearing better lace on your head. You didn't think I should come and detect you—you dreadfully careless-about-yourself, mamma!" She gave a caressing touch to the dear, worn head.

"Scold me, dear," said Mrs. Davilow, her delicate face flushing with delight. "But I wish there was something you could eat after your ride—instead of these scraps. Let Jocosa make you a cup of chocolate in your old way. You used to like that."

Miss Merry immediately rose to leave, though Gwendolen interjected, "Oh, no, a piece of bread, or one of those hard biscuits. I can't think about eating. I am come to say good-bye."

"What! Going to Ryelands again?" asked Mr. Gascoigne.

"No, we are going to town," Gwendolen said, beginning to break a piece of bread but putting no morsel into her mouth.

"It is rather early to go to town," remarked Mrs. Gascoigne, "and Mr. Grandcourt not in Parliament."

"Oh, there is only one more day's hunting to be had, and Henleigh has some business in town with lawyers, I think," Gwendolen replied. "I am very glad. I shall like to go to town."

"You will see your house in Grosvenor Square," said Mrs. Davilow. She and the girls were devouring every movement of their goddess, soon to vanish.

"Yes," Gwendolen said, her tone assenting to the interest of that expectation. "And there is so much to be seen and done in town."

"I wish, my dear Gwendolen," Mr. Gascoigne said, offering a kind of cordial advice, "that you would use your influence with Mr. Grandcourt to induce him to enter Parliament. A man of his position should make his weight felt in politics. The best judges are confident that the ministry will have to appeal to the country on this question of further Reform, and Mr. Grandcourt should be ready for the opportunity. I am not quite sure that his opinions and mine accord entirely; I have not heard him express himself very fully. But I don't look at
