The carriage halted, a sudden pause in the city's rhythm, attended by a powdered lackey and a bay horse that seemed to shimmer with the same artificial brilliance as the house they served. She swept the matting, trod the crimson stairs, performing her arduous function with the singular, hollow mandate simply *to be there*. Like Sirius rising over a silent, indifferent sea, she hid her heart behind a glittering facade, projecting a starlight that concealed the cold vacuum beneath.

Thus, the Grandcourts arrived at Grosvenor Square, timely enough to intercept a card for the musical gathering at Lady Mallinger's. Sir Hugo, driven by business imperatives, had anticipated the arrival of his ill-favored nephew, ensuring the household was primed for the intrusion. It was merely the third evening since their installation in this gilded cage, yet Gwendolen moved through the new ceilings and furniture with a distracted vagueness. Her mind was not on the decor, but on the impending confrontation with Deronda, and the anticipation of seeing Miss Lapidoth, a woman who had endured so much and possessed the terrifying capacity to submit to any duty. Gwendolen recalled nearly every syllable Deronda had spoken regarding Mirah, particularly a phrase she now repeated to herself with bitter acrimony. She harbored a vague, gnawing consciousness that her own submission bore no resemblance to duty. If anyone had articulated it then, she would have been forced to admit that what she submitted to could not possibly take the shape of obligation; it was a submission to a yoke forged by her own shameful actions, tightened by a selfish strength so absolute that it left no room for the weight of duty to rest upon it.

Before the Grandcourts entered, the drawing rooms of Park Lane, bathed in whites, golds, and pale crimsons, were agreeably furnished and sparsely populated. An hour of instrumental music had yielded to a languid interlude of chatter. Klesmer was present, accompanied by his wife, and in his generous patronage of Mirah, he had proposed accompanying her rendition of Leopardi's "O patria mia"—a piece he had previously recommended as uniquely suited to her spirit compared to the safer, more familiar repertoire. Already seated at the piano, Klesmer waited as Mirah stood conspicuously poised. Then, Gwendolen was ushered forward, magnificent in pale green velvet encrusted with poisoned diamonds, to a seat of honor commanding a clear view of the scene.

With her exceptional sight and iron self-command, Gwendolen possessed the rare ability to map a room instantly. As she turned her gaze toward Mirah, she did not neglect to exchange a bow with Klesmer, a gesture that sparked a lightning-flash of resentment within her. It recalled that morning when her ambition had been to stand where the "little Jewess" now stood—to survey a grand audience from the elevated platform of her own talent. Instead, she was merely part of the ordinary throng in silk and gems, whose highest achievement was to admire or find fault. *"He thinks I am in the right road now,"* whispered the lurking bitterness inside her.

In her transit, Gwendolen had failed to catch sight of Deronda. Seated now, engaging in polite chat with Sir Hugo, she scanned the room with careful ease, bowing to acquaintances, terrified lest an anxious, searching look for Deronda be interpreted by her husband as something "damnably vulgar." Yet, travel—even the slow, deliberate perusal of a room—carries the risk of unwanted encounters. Among the eyes meeting hers, forcing a slight nod, were those of Mr. Lush, the "amateur too fond of Meyerbeer," whom Sir Hugo deemed useful as a half-caste gentleman. Standing near her husband, Lush was ostensibly listening to Lord Pentreath, though Sir Hugo turned a shoulder toward him. Suddenly, a disagreeable sensation pierced Gwendolen: the realization that this man knew everything about her husband's life. Banished from her sight by her own will, Lush had sunk into the background of her thoughts, screened away by the agitating figures of her internal drama where he held no place. Now, he reappeared at her husband's elbow. Instantly, like a hallucination conjured in sleep, arose the sense that he was connected to the secrets that rendered her wretched. She felt the physical effort of turning her head away, feigning a wandering survey of the wall pictures as if nothing of consequence had occurred, until she finally located Deronda.

He was not looking toward her. She withdrew her eyes, offering no recognition, comforting herself with the delusion that he must have seen her entrance. In truth, he stood near the door with Hans Meyrick, whom he had carefully included in Lady Mallinger's guest list. Both men were slightly more anxious than comfortable, fearing Mirah might not be heard to advantage. Deronda felt himself on the brink of betrayal, his emotions stirred by Mirah's presence, which triggered a cascade of images—past and future—all converging on the brother he was soon to reveal to her. Having escaped the side of Lady Pentreath, who had played her violoncello voice to say, *"Well, your Jewess is pretty—there's no denying that. But where is her Jewish impudence? She looks as demure as a nun. I suppose she learned that on the stage,"* Deronda retreated inward.

He was beginning to feel, on Mirah's behalf, a kinship to the seraphic boyhood feelings he once held for himself, when Sir Hugo asked if he wished to become a great singer. This query provoked an indignant recoil; Deronda loathed the casual remarking upon her as if she were an imported commodity disdainfully purchased by the fashionable public. He winced, knowing that Mordecai would perceive the term "Jewess" as a brand mark, akin to the lettering on Chinese silk. In this vulnerable state, he witnessed the Grandcourts' entry and was immediately summoned by Hans regarding "that Vandyke duchess of a beauty." A transient renewal of his initial repulsion toward Gwendolen surged within him—as if her beauty and her failings were responsible for the undervaluation of Mirah as a woman—a sentiment bordering on class animosity, a reaction rarely escaped by those who cherish what remains unrecognized by others, whether in persons or poetry. To Hans, admiring Gwendolen with his customary hyperbole, Deronda replied with a sarcasm lacking warmth: *"I thought you could admire no style of woman but your Berenice."*

"That is the style I worship—not admire," Hans corrected. "Other styles of women I might make myself wicked for, but for Berenice I could make myself—well, pretty good, which is something much more difficult."

"Hush," Deronda murmured, feigning urgency as the singing was about to commence. He was not truly delighted by the answer, finding relief only in Hans's retreat to a more distant vantage point.

Deronda had never heard Mirah sing "O patria mia" before. He knew Leopardi's fine ode intimately—the image of Italy sitting like a disconsolate mother in chains, hiding her face on her knees and weeping—and the few selected words resonated for him with the grandeur of the whole, breathing an inspiration through the music. When Mirah sang, she made Mordecai a palpable presence beside her. Words excluded from the song nonetheless rang within Deronda as harmonies from the invisible: *"Non ti difende Nessun de' tuoi! L'armi, qua l'armi: io solo Combatterò, procomberò sol io"*—"Do none of thy children defend thee? Arms! bring me arms! alone I will fight, alone I will fall." These seemed the very voice of that heroic passion falsely claimed to be devoted in vain when it achieves the godlike end of manifesting unselfish love. That passion was present to Deronda now as the vivid image of a man dying helplessly, severed from the possibility of battle.

Mirah rose to his expectations. As the general applause swelled, Klesmer offered a private testament, audible only to her: *"Good, good—the crescendo better than before."* Yet her primary anxiety was to know she had satisfied Mr. Deronda; any failure tonight would pain her as a special injury to him. Naturally, all her prospects stemmed from his intervention, but this occasion of singing in the house that was his home imposed a peculiar demand. She looked toward him in the distance, and he saw her gaze; yet he remained stationary, watching the streams of emulous admirers close around her until they parted to make way for Gwendolen, who was escorted forward by Mrs. Klesmer.

Now easier regarding "the little Jewess," Daniel softened toward poor Gwendolen in her splendor. His memory returned, tinged with penitence for his momentary harshness, recalling the signs and confessions that she too required a rescue—one far more difficult than that of the wanderer by the river, a salvation for which he felt utterly powerless. The silent question, *"But is it not cowardly to make that a reason for turning away?"*, became the framework for his resolve to approach her on the first opportunity, honoring her past confidence despite Sir Hugo's unwelcome hints.

Having risen to greet Gwendolen as she approached, Klesmer lingered nearby, included in the opening conversation between the two young women seated on the red divan. His smile was more evident in his eyes than on his lips, observing the piquant contrast between the splendid one and the modest one. The solicitude appeared to flow entirely from the direction of the splendid one.

"You must let me say how much I am obliged to you," Gwend

"If anyone should demand of me the reason for my love, I can offer no explanation save this: 'Because it was he, because it was I.' Beyond the words I can articulate lies an inexplicable force that drew us together." —Montaigne, *On Friendship*.

The moment had arrived to prepare Mordecai for the unveiling of the restored sister and the necessary relocation of his dwelling, a transition required before Mirah could reunite with her brother. Mrs. Meyrick, to whom Deronda had entrusted every secret save Mordecai's unique bond with himself, had tirelessly assisted in securing a suitable lodgment in Brompton, mere minutes' walk from her own residence. This proximity ensured that the siblings remained within the tender reach of her maternal vigilance. Her vibrant fusion of Scottish fervor and Gaelic liveliness had successfully guarded the secret from the girls and from Hans; any breach of confidence to them risked triggering suspicions in Mirah that would shatter the delicate foundation of the endeavor designed to secure her independence—a dignity derived, paradoxically, from the very arduous forms of dependence upon others. Both Mrs. Meyrick and Deronda possessed motives deeper than they could fully articulate for insisting that Mirah sustain herself. Perhaps the "little mother" was aided in her secrecy by a lingering ambiguity regarding the remarkable brother described to her; certainly, if she harbored any joy or anticipatory admiration, it stemmed solely from her faith in Deronda's judgment. The consumption was a sorrowful fact that appealed to her tenderness, yet how could she truly celebrate an enthusiasm she could only perceive as Jewish pertinacity, an undesirable infusion of a man whose discourse lacked the modern encouragement of Scott's Covenanters? Her mind was far from prosaic; she retained a sober share of Mab's delight in the romance of Mirah's story and her anticipated abode with them. Yet the romantic or unusual in real life demands adaptation. We stay awake at night to read of Sakya-Mouni, St. Francis, or Oliver Cromwell; but whether we should welcome someone exactly like them calling upon us the next morning, still more revealing themselves as a new relation, is a matter of quite another order. Furthermore, Mrs. Meyrick had hoped, as her children did, that the intensity of Mirah's devotion to Judaism would gradually subside, merging into the deepening current of loving interchange with her new friends. Indeed, her secret favorite continuation of the romance had been no discovery of Jewish relations, but something far more congenial to the hopes she discerned in Hans. And now—here stood a brother who would plunge Mirah's mind once again into the deepest dye of Jewish sentiment. She could not refrain from telling Deronda,

"I am as glad as you are that the pawnbroker is not her brother: there are Ezras and Ezras in the world; and truly it is a comfort to think that all Jews are not like those shopkeepers who _refuse_ to let you depart from their premises: and besides, what he said to you concerning his mother and sister makes me bless him. I am sure he is good. But I have never liked anything fanatical. I suppose I heard a little too much preaching in my youth and lost my palate for it."

"I do not believe you will find that Mordecai imposes any preaching," Deronda replied. "He is not what I would term fanatical. I reserve the label 'fanatic' for a man whose enthusiasm is narrow and hoodwinked, devoid of proportion, rendering him unjust and unsympathetic to those outside his own orbit. Mordecai is an enthusiast; I wish to keep that word for the highest order of minds—those who care supremely for grand and universal benefits to mankind. He is not a strictly orthodox Jew, and is replete with allowances for others; his conformity in many matters serves as an allowance for the condition of other Jews. Those with whom he lives cherish him as much as possible, yet they cannot comprehend his ideas in the slightest."

"Oh, well, I can rise to the level of the pawnbroker's mother, and like him for the goodness I perceive; for what I do not see, I will take your word. By your definition, one might arguably be fanatical in worshipping common-sense; for my poor husband used to remark that the world would be a poor place if it contained nothing but common-sense. Nevertheless, Mirah's brother shall have excellent bedding—that I have arranged; and I shall have this extra window papered to ward off drafts." (The conversation unfolded within the destined lodging.) "It is comforting to know that the householders are no strangers to me—no hypocritical harpies. And once the children are aware, we shall be able to render the rooms much more charming."

"The next phase of the affair involves informing all of Mordecai and persuading him to move—which may prove a more arduous undertaking," Deronda observed.

"And will you disclose this to Mirah before I mention anything to the children?" Mrs. Meyrick asked. Deronda hesitated, and she continued in a tone of persuasive deliberation—"No, I think not. Let me inform Hans and the girls the evening prior, and they will be gone the following morning?"

"Yes, that is best. But do justice to my account of Mordecai—or Ezra, as I suppose Mirah will wish to address him: do not assist their imaginations by invoking the name of Habakkuk Mucklewrath," Deronda smiled, noting that Mrs. Meyrick herself had employed the comparison of the Covenanters.

"Trust me, trust me," the little mother assured him. "I shall have to persuade them so ardently to be glad that I shall convert myself. When I am frightened, I find it beneficial to have someone to be angry with for lacking bravery; it warms the blood."

Deronda might have been more argumentative or persuasive regarding the proper view of Mirah's brother had he not been anxiously preoccupied with the more immediate and vital task before him, one he desired to accomplish without wounding the Cohens. Mordecai, through a memorable response, had made it evident that he would be acutely sensitive to any inadvertence concerning their feelings. In the interim, he had met Mordecai at the *Hand and Banner*, but now, after due reflection, he penned a letter stating that he had particular reasons for wishing to see him at his own home the following evening, requesting permission to sit in his workroom for an hour, provided the Cohens did not deem it an intrusion. He would call with the understanding that if there were any objection, Mordecai would accompany him elsewhere. Deronda hoped thereby to cultivate a modest expectation that would serve a preparatory function.

He was received with customary friendliness, accompanied by additional costumes worn by the women and children, and in all the elders, a slight air of wonderment that even in Cohen did not transgress the bounds of silence—the guest's dealings with Mordecai constituting a mystery of which he was rather proud, believing it lay outside the sphere of light enclosing his own understanding. Yet when Deronda remarked, "I suppose Mordecai is at home and expecting me," Jacob, who had profited from the family remarks, approached his knee and asked, "What do you want to talk to Mordecai about?"

"Something exceedingly interesting to him," Deronda replied, pinching the boy's ear, "though you cannot comprehend it."

"Can you say this?" Jacob demanded, immediately unleashing a string of rote-learned Hebrew verses with a marvelous mixture of throaty and nasal tones, nodding his small head at his listener with a sense of presenting formidable evidence capable of altering their mutual standing.

"No, indeed," Deronda answered, maintaining gravity; "I cannot say anything like that."

"I thought not," Jacob declared, performing a dance of triumph with his small scarlet legs, extracting various objects from the deep pockets of his knickerbockers and returning them, signaling his resources; thereafter, darting to the door of the workroom, he flung it wide, set his back against it, and announced, "Mordecai, here comes the young swell"—a borrowing of his father's phrase, which seemed to him aptly suited to cap the recitation of Hebrew.

He was recalled with hushed warnings by mother and grandmother. Deronda entered, closing the door behind him, to find that a piece of carpet had been laid down, a chair positioned, and the fire and lights tended to, tokens of the Cohens' respect. As Mordecai rose to greet him, Deronda was struck by the air of solemn expectation in his countenance, such as would have appeared perfectly natural if his letter had proclaimed that some revelation concerning the lost sister was imminent. Neither spoke until Deronda, with his accustomed tenderness, drew the vacant chair from the opposite side of the hearth and seated himself near Mordecai, who then said, in a tone of fervid certainty,

"You are coming to tell me something that my soul longs for."

"It is true I have something very weighty to impart to you—something I trust you will rejoice in," Deronda stated, guarding against the probability that Mordecai had been preparing himself for something entirely distinct from the facts.

"It is all revealed—it is made clear to you," Mordecai exclaimed more eagerly, leaning forward with clasped hands. "You are even as my brother that sucked the breasts of my mother—the heritage is yours—there is no doubt to divide us."

"I have learned nothing new about myself," Deronda replied. The disappointment was inevitable; it was better not to allow the feeling to strain further in a mistaken hope.

Mordecai sank back in his chair, momentarily unable to care what was truly forthcoming. All day his mind had been

One might be tempted to envy Deronda the quiet theater of preparation, watching as he draped fresh gray flannel shirts and a dressing gown reminiscent of a Franciscan's brown frock over Mordecai's frame. Half of his delight lay in seeing Mirah's brother through her eyes, shielding her fervent joy from any shadow of doubt. Yet, even as he arranged the scene, a creeping suspicion gnawed at him: was he mistaking the lower for the higher? Was Mirah truly incapable of appreciating the distinction in her brother precisely *because* of the poverty that marked his past, or was he projecting his own need to sanitize history onto her? The Meyricks demanded appeasement; their minds, untouched by that sacred "seeing"—that bath of solemnity born of reverent love—were prone to immediate repugnance. Deronda, driven by this social friction, resolved to hide every trace of the Jewish identity that might trigger their instinctive recoil. This impulse would have hardened further had he listened to the dialogue crackling around Mrs. Meyrick's hearth that evening, after Mirah retired.

Hans, now settled in his Chelsea rooms, lingered late. As Mrs. Meyrick poked the fire into a roaring blaze, she announced, "Now, Kate, extinguish your candle. Come all round the fire cosily. Hans, dear, cease your laughter at those poems for the ninety-ninth time and join us. I have something wonderful to tell."

"As if I didn't know that, ma," Kate retorted, her voice dripping with practiced sarcasm. "I've seen it in the corner of your eye for ages, hiding behind your endless 'errands.'" The girls gathered, propping their feet on the fender, while Hans swung his chair forward, resting his fists and chin on the backrest.

"Well, since you're so wise," Mrs. Meyrick declared, her voice clear and bright, "perhaps you know Mirah's brother has been found!"

"Oh, confound it!" Hans exploded instantly.

"Hans, that is wicked," Mab whispered, horrified. "Suppose we had lost you?"

"I cannot help being rather sorry," Kate admitted, her tone shifting to genuine regret. "And her mother? Where is she?"

"She is dead."

"I hope the brother isn't a bad man," Amy ventured timidly.

"Nor a fellow all smiles and jewelry—a Crystal Palace Assyrian with a hat on," Hans sneered, his mood souring. "Were there ever such unfeeling children?"

"You don't think the least bit of Mirah's joy in this, do you?" Mrs. Meyrick pressed, emboldened by the need for opposition.

"You know, ma, Mirah hardly remembers her brother," Kate countered. "People lost for twelve years shouldn't come back. They're always in the way."

"Hans!" Mrs. Meyrick reproached sharply. "If you had lost me for twenty years, I should have thought—"

"I said twelve years," Hans interrupted, pacing with agitation. "Anywhere near twelve is the exact duration lost relations should keep out of the way."

"But it's nice finding people—there's something to tell," Mab interjected, clutching her knees. "Did Prince Camaralzaman find him?"

Mrs. Meyrick, in her neat, narrative style, recounted everything she knew without pause. "Mr. Deronda holds the highest admiration for him," she concluded. "He seems quite to look up to him. And he says Mirah is just the sister to understand this brother."

"Deronda is getting perfectly preposterous about those Jews," Hans spat, rising and banging his chair back with violent finality. "He wants to do everything he can to encourage Mirah in her prejudices."

"Oh, for shame, Hans! To speak thus of Mr. Deronda!" Mab cried out. A subtle, undercurrent of pain flickered across Mrs. Meyrick's face, suppressed but visible.

"And now we shall never be all together," Hans rambled, wandering the room with his hands buried in the pockets of his brown velveteen coat. "But we must invite this prophet Elijah to tea. Mirah will think of nothing but sitting on the ruins of Jerusalem. She'll be spoiled as an artist—mind that—she'll get as narrow as a nun. Everything will be ruined—our home and everything. I shall take to drinking."

"Oh, really, Hans," Kate snapped, her patience fraying. "I do think men are the most contemptible animals in all creation. Every one of them must have everything to his mind, or he becomes unbearable."

"Oh, oh, oh, it's very dreadful!" Mab wailed. "I feel as if ancient Nineveh were come again."

"I should like to know what's the good of attending university and knowing everything if you're so childish, Hans," Amy chided. "You ought to put up with a man Providence sends you to be kind to. We shall have to put up with him."

"I hope you'll all like the new *Lamentations of Jeremiah*—'to be continued in our next'—that's all," Hans muttered, grabbing his wide-awake. "It's no use being anything special if you have to endure the company of those men with fixed ideas, staring blankly at you and demanding your remarks serve as mere footnotes to their text. If you're trapped under a petrifying wall, you'd better be an old boot. I don't feel myself an old boot."

Abruptly, he leaned forward to kiss his mother's brow in a hasty, desperate gesture, then offered a condescending nod to the girls on his way out. "Good night, little mother... Good night, girls."

"Suppose Mirah knew how you're behaving," Kate called after him.

His reply was the slam of the door. Turning back to her mother, she added, "I *should* like to see Mirah when Mr. Deronda tells her. I know she will look so beautiful."

Yet Deronda, reconsidering his approach, had penned a letter the previous day. He instructed Mrs. Meyrick to reveal the news herself rather than waiting for his arrival, omitting the true reason: his shrinking from re-entering a narrative where he risked appearing as a benefactor playing a role of general goodness. Instead, he framed it as a desire to stay with Mordecai while Mrs. Meyrick brought Mirah for a visit, creating a necessary buffer before the inevitable move to a new dwelling that Mirah herself would soon propose. Secretly, Deronda harbored anxious wonderment regarding Mordecai. After years of solitary immersion in ideas that had narrowed with his diminishing physical strength, would the elder Cohen allow space for Deronda's tender interest in his sister beyond the bounds of pious duty?

Mordecai's feelings for the Cohens, particularly little Jacob, displayed a persistent warmth, yet these were familiar presences woven into his daily life for years. Deronda noted with concern that Mordecai asked no new questions about Mirah, maintaining an unusual silence on all subjects and seemingly submitting passively to the upheaval of his personal life. Obediently, Mordecai donned the new clothes, but afterward, he smiled faintly at Deronda. "I must keep my old garments by me for a remembrance."

When they sat awaiting Mirah, Mordecai remained silent, his eyelids lowered, yet his face and hands betrayed a restless turmoil. Indeed, Mordecai was undergoing a peculiar nervous perturbation, known only to those whose minds, long flowing with strong impetus in a single current, are suddenly forced into a new or reopened channel. People susceptible because their strength has been drained by dormant biases dread interviews that imperiously revive the past with the same terror they feel facing a threatening illness. Joy may be present, but joy, too, is terrible.

Deronda caught the infection of this excitement. Upon hearing the ring at the door, he stepped out, unsure why, needing only to see and greet Mirah beforehand. He was startled to find her wearing the hat and cloak in which he had first encountered her—the memorable cloak that had once been wetted to serve as a winding sheet. She had descended stairs clad in this attire. When Mrs. Meyrick asked in a questioning tone, "You like to go in that dress, dear?" Mirah replied, "My brother is poor, and I want to look as much like him as I can, else he may feel distant from me," imagining she would meet him in a worker's garb.

Deronda could offer no comment, feeling a secret shame for his own fastidious preparations. They shook hands silently; Mirah appeared pale and awestruck. As Deronda held the door open, Mordecai had risen, his eyes turning toward the entrance with an eager intensity. Mirah took only two or three steps before stopping. They stood motionless, locked in a gaze that felt less like their own presence than the collision of another's memory. Compared to the weight of shared recollection, physical touch was no union.

Mirah broke the silence first, standing firm where she was. "Ezra," she said, using the exact tone she employed when recounting her mother's call to him. With a sudden movement, Mordecai advanced and laid his hand on her shoulders. He towered over her, looking down tenderly. "That was our mother's voice. Do you remember her calling me?"

"Yes, and how you answered her—'Mother!'—and I knew you loved her."

Mirah threw her arms around her brother's neck, clasping her small hands behind it, drawing his face down to kiss it with childlike lavish

It is a perilous and poorly compensated endeavor to attempt to pre-order the chaotic river of existence according to the strict blueprints of one's own safety, a folly Machiavelli attributed to Cesare Borgia. The condottiere, in his grim foresight, calculated every contingency following his father's demise, fortifying against every conceivable misfortune except one singular, catastrophic oversight: he never conceived that the shadow cast by his father's death would inevitably swallow his own life in its wake.

Grandcourt's significance within this realm was of a grandly passive magnitude, rooted solely in the inheritance of acres. The tides of political upheaval and social reform washed over him only through the slender wire of his rental income; his biography could remain untouched by the complexities of Schleswig-Holstein, Bismarck's iron policies, the rise of trade unions, household suffrage, or the latest commercial panics. He skimmed the broadsheets on these tumultuous subjects, his opinions lacking any genuine breadth, categorizing all Germans, all merchants, and all voters prone to using inferior soap under the sweeping, dismissive label of "brutes." Yet, he took no tangible action on these agitated matters beyond glancing from beneath his lashes at anyone who dared mention them, cultivating a silence so profound it shook the foundations of timid intellects.

Within his own narrow sphere, however, Grandcourt displayed the qualities of triumphant diplomacy in its wildest continental incarnation. Not a movement of Gwendolen in relation to Deronda escaped his scrutiny. He would vehemently deny possessing jealousy, for such an admission would imply a doubt in his absolute power to obstruct what he had resolved against. That his wife should harbor inclinations toward another man's society caused him no pain; what he demanded was that she remain as acutely aware of her confinement as one is of a locked handcuff, rendering her inclination helpless to contradict his resolve. Though his entry into matrimony may have been marked by vacillating whims, his interpretation of the marital bond was unyielding. He harbored no repentance; indeed, the union had injected a new aim into his life, fresh objects upon which to exert his will. He had not regretted his choice. His taste was fastidious, and Gwendolen satisfied it. He would not tolerate a wife who had not ascended in rank through him, nor one who failed to command admiration through her bearing and beauty, nor one whose nails lacked proper shape, nor one whose ear lobes were too large and red. Nor would he accept a woman who, despite physical perfection, remained a ninny, incapable of delivering spirited replies. These demands may not seem excessive to refined contemporaries whose capacity for love hangs suspended due to a lack of indispensable details, yet few could match his satisfaction in securing a wife inclined to fly out if she dared, and urged into marrying him by sentiments other than passionate attachment. Still, for those who prefer command to love, the necessity of altering one's mindset precisely at the threshold of matrimony remains obscure.

Grandcourt did not believe he had selected the wrong wife; having assumed the role of husband, he refused to be duped or viewed in a light that invited pity. This was his state of mind—not jealousy, yet his behavior mirrored it as closely as yellow resembles yellow, a hue known to stem from vastly different origins.

He had returned to town earlier than usual, driven by the need for legal counsel regarding his will, the transfer of mortgages, and the transaction with his uncle concerning the succession to Diplow. The lure of ready money, skillfully dangled without importunity, had finally secured his agreement. Another acceptable justification for his presence in the city was the presentation of himself alongside the beautiful bride he had chosen, defying all external expectations. True, Grandcourt moved with the conviction that he cared not a languid curse for anyone's admiration; yet this state of non-care, like desire itself, required an object—a world of admiring or envious spectators. For if one delights in staring stonily at smiling persons, those persons must smile, a rudimentary truth often forgotten by those who lament mankind's general contemptibility, since any other aspect of the race would fail to satisfy the voracity of their disdain. In town for the first time with his wife, Grandcourt expanded his abstention from curses through splendid receptions, conspicuous rides, and public appearances designed to showcase her. He desired that she be sought after; he relished seeing "fellows" eager to converse with her and escort her within his line of sight. There was even a certain lofty coquetry on her part that he found acceptable. What he detested, however, were her interactions with Deronda.

Following the musical gathering at Lady Mallinger's, where Grandcourt had watched the dialogue on the settee with the intensity of a hawk, it was characteristic of him to invite Deronda alongside the Mallingers, tenaciously avoiding any implication that Deronda's presence mattered to him in the slightest. He refrained from commenting directly on Gwendolen's behavior that evening, fearing his disgust might be too potent to satisfy his own pride. A few days later, however, he remarked, disregarding propriety, "Nothing makes a woman more of a gawky than looking out after people and showing tempers in public. A woman ought to have good manners. Else it's intolerable to appear with her."

Gwendolen made the expected connection, alarmed by the prospect of being deemed a gawky. Like him, she possessed a melancholy distaste for things, preferring that her aversion extend to admirers as well. Yet, the looming threat of rebuke only intensified the tension surrounding any potential encounter with Deronda. The novelty and excitement of her urban existence resembled the hurried, constant flux of foreign travel; regardless of her internal despondency, there was a program to fulfill, yielding gratification to her multifaceted self. As always occurs with deep interest, the relatively rare opportunities to exchange words with Deronda created a diffusive effect in her consciousness, magnifying their communications and inflating the significance she imagined they held in his mind. How could Deronda prevent this? He certainly did not avoid her; rather, he endeavored to convince her through every delicate, indirect means that her confidence in him had not been indiscreet, as it had not diminished his respect. Furthermore, he enjoyed her proximity—how could it be otherwise? She was more than a problem; she was a lovely woman, for whose mind and fate he harbored a care that, however futile, solicited him as a responsibility. Indeed, this burden grew heavier when he contemplated his own future, envisioning it lying far distant from this splendid, sad-hearted creature. Because he had once been impelled to arrest her attention momentarily—as if seizing her arm to warn her from stepping into danger—she had turned to him with a beseeching, persistent need.

One instance where Grandcourt stimulated a feeling in Gwendolen that he wished to suppress without appearing concerned involved Mirah. Gwendolen's inclination to pursue singing lessons lingered as a form of obedience to Deronda's advice, but day followed day with that palpable lack of leisure characteristic of lives devoid of work to mark intervals. The constant liability of Grandcourt's presence and surveillance flattened every effort to the level of the boredom his manner expressed; his negative mind was as diffusive as fog, clinging to all objects and spoiling all contact.

Yet, one morning during breakfast, Gwendolen, in a recurring fit of determination to revive her old spirit, said, dallying prettily over her prawns without consuming them, "I think of making myself accomplished while we are in town, and having singing lessons."

"Why?" Grandcourt asked, languidly.

"Why?" Gwendolen echoed, playing at sauciness. "Because I can't eat *pâté de foie gras* to make me sleepy, and I can't smoke, and I can't go to the club to make me like to come away again—I want a variety of *ennui*. What would be the most convenient time, when you are busy with your lawyers and people, for me to have lessons from that little Jewess, whose singing is getting all the rage?"

"Whenever you like," Grandcourt replied, pushing his plate aside and leaning back in his chair, regarding her with his most lizard-like expression while playing with the ears of the tiny spaniel on his lap—a dog Gwendolen had come to dislike for fawning upon him. Then, in the same languid tone, he added, "I don't see why a lady should sing. Amateurs make fools of themselves. A lady can't risk herself in that way in company. And one doesn't want to hear squalling in private."

"I like frankness: that seems to me a husband's great charm," Gwendolen said, executing a little upward movement of her chin as she turned her eyes away from his. Lifting a prawn before her, she regarded the boiled ingenuousness of its eyes as preferable to the reptile's. "But," she added, having devoured her mortification, "I suppose you don't object to Miss Lapidoth's singing at our party on the fourth? I thought of engaging her. Lady Brackenshaw had her, you know: and the Raymonds, who are very particular about their music. And Mr. Deronda, who is a musician himself and a first-rate judge, says there is no singing in such good taste as hers for a drawing-room. I think his opinion is an authority."

She intended to sling a small stone at her husband in that manner.

"It's very indecent of Deronda to go about praising that girl," Grandcourt said in a tone of indifference.

"Indecent!" Gwendolen exclaimed, reddening and fixing her gaze upon him, overwhelmed by

In the architecture of the soul, there exists a higher justice, one that allows gratitude to eclipse resentment, a hallmark of regal natures endowed with a broader life and a deeper capacity for joy. These are not the minds that gleefully dissect virtue until it dissolves into pulp, deeming it unworthy of thanks; those are the devil's friars, sworn to poverty in love and trust, yet forced to beg from a world that hoards human property.

Deronda, as he parted from Gwendolen, carefully withheld the declaration, *"I shall not see you again for a long while; I am going away,"* fearing that Grandcourt might interpret this absence as significant to her. In truth, he departed under circumstances of monumental weight to himself. As he set out to keep his promise of visiting her, he carried within him a solemn emotion that resurrected the most profound experiences of his existence.

Sir Hugo had summoned him to his chambers with a terse note: *"Come immediately. Something has happened."* This summons brought a measure of relief when Deronda entered the baronet's study and was met not with the dread he anticipated, but with grave affection.

"It is nothing to grieve you, sir?" Deronda asked, his tone vibrating with a restored confidence rather than inquiry, as he grasped the hand extended to him.

There was an unusual intensity in Sir Hugo's gaze, a suppressed turmoil in his voice, as he replied, "No, Dan, no. Sit down. I have something to say."

Deronda complied, though a presentiment weighed upon him. It was exceedingly rare for Sir Hugo to display such profound seriousness. "Not to grieve me, my boy, no. At least, if there is nothing in it that will grieve you too much. But I hardly expected that this—just this—would ever occur. There have been reasons why I never prepared you for it. Reasons why I never spoke of your parentage. Yet, I strove in every way to ensure this did not become an injury to you."

Sir Hugo paused, but Deronda remained silent. He could not articulate, *"I have never felt it an injury."* Even if that sentiment were true, his throat tightened, rendering speech impossible. More hung in the balance than anyone but himself could comprehend as the veil of secrecy began to tear. Sir Hugo had never witnessed the grand face he cherished so dearly grow so pale, nor the lips pressed together with such agonizing restraint.

Continuing with an anxious tenderness, as if dreading a fresh wound, Sir Hugo confessed, "I acted in obedience to your mother's wishes. The secrecy was hers. But now she desires to remove it. She wishes to see you. I will place this letter in your hands; you may examine it later. It will simply state what she bids you do and where you may find her."

He handed over a letter penned on foreign stationery. Deronda shoved it into his breast pocket, grateful for the reprieve from immediate reading. The storm of emotion on Daniel's face had overtaken the baronet's composure, visibly shaking him. Finding it difficult to proceed further, Sir Hugo waited.

But Deronda's entire being was seized by a question, the most arduous in the world to voice. Yet, he could not endure the delay. This was a sacramental instant; if he allowed it to slip, he could never again access the spiritual gravity required to speak the words and receive the answer. For several moments, their gazes dropped, and it seemed as though unspoken thoughts drifted in the air between them. Finally, Deronda lifted his eyes to Sir Hugo, speaking with a tremulous reverence, terrified of inadvertently conveying the reproach that years of stifled affection had bred: "Is my father also living?"

The response came instantly, low and emphatic: "No."

In the turbulent mix of emotions that followed, it became impossible to disentangle joy from sorrow. A new illumination had fallen upon the past for Sir Hugo as well during this encounter. After a silence in which Deronda felt akin to someone whose faith has crumbled before they have fully embraced a new creed, the baronet spoke in a tone of confession, "Perhaps I was wrong, Dan, to undertake what I did. And perhaps I liked it a little too well—having you all to myself. But if you have suffered any pain which I might have alleviated, I ask you to forgive me."

"The forgiveness has long been there," Deronda replied. "The chief pain has always stemmed from someone else—whom I never knew—whom I am now to know. It has not prevented me from feeling an affection for you that constitutes a vast portion of every life I recall."

It appeared to be a single, shared impulse that drove the two men to clasp each other's hands for a fleeting moment.

"If some mortal, born too soon, Were laid away in some great trance—the ages Coming and going all the while—till dawned His true time's advent; and could then record The words they spoke who kept watch by his bed, Then I might tell more of the breath so light Upon my eyelids, and the fingers warm Among my hair. Youth is confused; yet never So dull was I but, when that spirit passed, I turned to him, scarce consciously, as turns A water-snake when fairies cross his sleep." —BROWNING: *Paracelsus*.

This epigraph framed the letter Sir Hugo pressed into Deronda's hands, a document that felt less like correspondence and more like a summons from a distant shore.

My good friend and yours, Sir Hugo Mallinger, will have told you that I wish to see you. My health is shaken, and I desire there should be no time lost before I deliver to you what I have long withheld. Let nothing hinder you from being at the *Albergo dell' Italia* in Genoa by the fourteenth of this month. Wait for me there. I am uncertain when I shall be able to make the journey from Spezia, where I shall be staying. That will depend on several things. Wait for me—the Princess Halm-Eberstein. Bring with you the diamond ring that Sir Hugo gave you. I shall like to see it again.—Your unknown mother, LEONORA HALM-EBERSTEIN.

The letter's colorless wording offered Deronda no clue to the storm brewing behind its calm surface. Yet, bound by Sir Hugo's reticence—a silence that felt less like omission and more like a sacred pledge—he accepted the delay without protest. The revelation that his lifelong conjectures regarding his lineage had been mistaken struck him with a sudden, chilling clarity, halting any further speculation in its tracks. He allowed his imagination to skim briefly over the landscape of possibilities, but he refused to anchor himself to any single vision, terrified that nurturing one specific hope or dread would distort his ability to face the truth, whatever shape it might take.

In this suspended state, Deronda found himself unable to articulate the gravity of his departure to anyone, least of all to Mordecai. To speak plainly—"I am going to learn the truth about my birth"—would ignite a dangerous excitement in Mordecai's hopeful heart, a fire that could consume them both. Instead, he cloaked his mission in the guise of Sir Hugo's wishes, projecting an air of casual indifference onto a journey that weighed heavily upon his soul. He claimed uncertainty regarding its duration, suggesting it might be fleeting, though his eyes betrayed a deeper unease.

"I will ask to have the child Jacob to stay with me," Mordecai said, finding comfort in the practical arrangements, his face softening after the initial pang of loss.

Mirah followed suit, her voice brightening. "I will drive round and ask Mrs. Cohen to let him come."

"The grandmother will deny you nothing," Deronda replied gently.

"I'm glad you were a little wrong as well as I," he added, offering a small smile to Mordecai. "You thought that old Mrs. Cohen would not bear to see Mirah."

"I undervalued her heart," Mordecai confessed, his tone reverent. "She is capable of rejoicing that another's plant blooms though her own be withered."

"Oh, they are dear good people; I feel as if we all belonged to each other," Mirah said, a touch of merriment coloring her smile.

"What should you have felt if that Ezra had been your brother?" Deronda asked, mischief flickering in his voice—a slight provocation born of the fact that she had already embraced people who stood poised to cause him significant distress.

Mirah regarded him with a flash of surprise before lowering her gaze. "He is not a bad man—I think he would never forsake anyone." As the words left her lips, she flushed deeply, casting a timid glance toward Mordecai before turning away to a nearby task. Her father loomed in her thoughts, a specter shared painfully between siblings, a subject that rendered the daylight of the street as shadowy as a haunted forest where every corner concealed an imaginary apparition.

Deronda perceived her involuntary allusion instantly, understanding the blush that stained her cheeks. How could he remain slow to recognize feelings that now seemed closer to his own than ever before? For the words of his mother's letter implied that his filial bond was not to be severed from painful conditions; indeed, paradoxically, the very missive that rendered his mother a tangible reality had cast her into greater emotional remoteness. The tender yearning for a being whose life might have been diminished by lack of his care, the image of a mother denied her due of reverence or compassion, had long simmered secretly within him, coloring his observations of every woman he encountered. Now, however, this mental portrait seemed to fit the facts no better than his previous misconceptions about Sir Hugo. He marveled at how the mere appearance of his mother's handwriting, carrying her actual sentiment, had caused his affections to shrink into a comparative neutrality. A veiled figure speaking in riddles had displaced the image he had carefully molded in his mind, the one possessing his tenderness and dutiful longing.

When he finally departed for Genoa, the interest dominating his mind bore little resemblance to his mother; it was anchored firmly in Mordecai and Mirah.

"God bless you, Dan!" Sir Hugo had said, clasping his hand. "Whatever else changes for you, it can't change my being the oldest friend you have known, and the one who has all along felt the most for you. I couldn't have loved you better if you'd been my own—only I should have been better pleased with thinking of you always as the future master of the Abbey instead of my fine nephew; and then you would have seen it necessary for you to take a political line. However—things must be as they may."

It was a defensive maneuver by the baronet, mingling purposeless chatter with expressions of profound seriousness.

Upon arriving at the *Italia* in Genoa, Deronda found no sign of Princess Halm-Eberstein. Only on the second day did a letter arrive, informing him that her arrival might occur within a week or be delayed by a fortnight or more. Circumstances prevented her from fixing her journey precisely, and she implored him to wait with as much patience as he could muster.

Faced with this indefinite prospect of suspense concerning matters of supreme importance, Deronda undertook the arduous task of seeking amusement on philosophical grounds, attempting to quiet his excited feelings and lend patience wings over a weary road. His previous visit to the magnificent city had been cursory, leaving much to explore beyond the standard tourist circuit. He spent the cooler hours wandering observantly through the streets, the quay, and the environs, often taking a boat to behold the city and harbor from the sea.

All sights, all subjects—even the anticipated reunion with his mother—found a central union in Mordecai and Mirah, and the ideas immediately associated with them. As his boat pushed through the waters near the grand harbor, one thought dominated his mind: the memory of the multitudinous Spanish Jews centuries ago, driven destitute from their homes, forced to land on this very quay for a brief respite. They had overspread the stone with a pall of famine and plague—dying mothers clutching dying children, fathers and sons staring into each other's haggard faces, like groups emerging from a hundred Hunger-towers beneath the midday sun.

Inevitably, dreamlike constructions of a possible ancestry for himself wove themselves with these historic memories, which had gained a new resonance since his discovery of Mirah. Now, influenced by Mordecai, these thoughts became irresistibly dominant. He would have sealed his mind against such speculations if he could, yet he had never fully admitted to himself that he wished the facts to validate Mordecai's conviction. He silently repeated that he had no choice in the matter, that wishing was folly—nay, on the question of parentage, wishing seemed akin to that meanness which disowns kinship before the deed is done, a disowning by anticipation. What remained was simply to accept the fact; and now that he was assured of his error regarding Sir Hugo, he possessed no strong presumption to guide him. There had been a resolved concealment rendering all inference untrustworthy, and the very name he bore might be a falsehood. If Mordecai was wrong—if the so-called Daniel Deronda was held by ties entirely aloof from the path his friend's pathetic hope outlined?—he would not say "I wish"; yet he could not help feeling on which side the sacrifice lay.

Across these two importunate thoughts, which he resisted as one resists anything in that unstrung condition of suspense, flowed continually an anxiety he made no effort to banish. He dwelt upon it with a mournfulness that often feels the best atonement one can offer for a need met too late. This anxiety was for Gwendolen.

In the wonderful mixtures of human nature, there exists a feeling distinct from the exclusive passionate love some possess, yet differing from friendship or mere benevolence. A man, for instance, hardly represents this shade of feeling toward a woman more clearly than in the phrase, "I should have loved her, if..." The "if" covers either a prior growth in inclination or circumstances that erected an internal prohibitory law, keeping emotions from quivering out of balance. In Deronda's case, the "if" carried reasons of both kinds. Throughout his relations with Gwendolen, he had never been

Errina sat, the spindle spinning in her thick-coiled raven hair, her deepest agate eyes fixed on the surging visions of a destiny that promised only insect-labor byssus. Around her, gods and men forged histories sung by poets, while she spun threads of silence. Yet, in the grand theater of fate, the spotlight shifted abruptly from the mythic to the painfully immediate.

In the antechamber of the *Italia*, the air crackled with a tension that mirrored Deronda's own premature agitations. Two servants watched him with marked surprise; the doctor their lady had summoned was not a frail elder, but a striking young gentleman whose very presence seemed to demand the evening dress he wore as a crown of adornment. But the true shock awaited him beyond the threshold.

At the far end of the vast room, a figure stood waiting, draped in black lace that cascaded from the summit of her whitening hair to the floor, trailing behind her tall, imposing silhouette. Her arms, bare to the elbow save for rich bracelets, were folded tightly before her chest. Her head held a fine, statuesque poise that rendered her even more formidable than her weary features suggested. Deronda moved forward without hesitation, closing the distance until he stood before her. She extended a hand, and he took it, lifting it reverently to his lips.

She retained her grip, studying him with an examining intensity, while his own consciousness recoiled from the sheer volatility of her gaze. Her eyes pierced him; her face was a landscape of shifting moods, capable of transforming into a stranger in the span of a heartbeat. Even as she scrutinized him, a silent dialogue played out across her brow and nostrils—a tacit language of judgment and inquiry. Deronda froze, paralyzed by the uncertainty of what emotional performance she required. He felt his cheeks flush with a heat akin to a girl's, yet he marveled at his own emotional numbness. After fifteen years of idealized imaginings, this reality felt more distant than fiction. He couldn't guess the tongue she would use; he feared it wouldn't be English.

Suddenly, she released his hand, placing both palms firmly on his shoulders. In that instant, the weariness vanished from her face, replaced by a flash of raw admiration that smoothed every line, restoring a youthful radiance.

"You are a beautiful creature!" she whispered, her voice low and melodious, carrying the faint, agreeable outline of a foreign accent. "I knew you would be."

She kissed him on each cheek, and he returned the gesture, but the intimacy felt less like a reunion and more like a formal greeting between sovereigns.

A pause settled between them, the lines of age creeping back into her expression. Then, her tone hardened into ice. "I am your mother. But you can have no love for me."

"I have thought of you more than of any other being in the world," Deronda replied, his voice trembling with nervous energy.

"I am not like what you thought I was," she stated decisively, pulling her hands away to fold them once more, inviting him to dissect her anew. He had often conjured her image in his mind as a mirror of his own soul; now, he saw fragments of that likeness buried beneath a striking, alien strangeness. She was a remarkable entity, possessing a worn beauty that felt detached from humanity—as if she were a Melusina, bound to a realm independent of theirs.

"I used to think you might be suffering," Deronda said, driven by an anxiety to avoid wounding her. "I wished to be your comfort."

"I *am* suffering. But with a suffering you cannot comfort," the Princess replied, her voice sharpening as she moved to a sofa cushioned with careful precision. "Sit down." She indicated a seat nearby. Noticing the distress etched on his face, her demeanor softened. "I am not suffering at this moment. I am at ease. I am able to talk."

Deronda sat, waiting, feeling less like a son returning home and more like a witness summoned before a mysterious Fate. He observed her with wonder, separated from her by a chasm of spiritual distance.

"No," she began, cutting through his reverie. "I did not summon you to comfort me. I could not foresee what you would feel toward me. I possess no foolish notion that you can love me simply because I am your mother, especially when we have shared no life together. But I believed I chose something better for you than mere proximity. I did not think I deprived you of anything valuable."

"You cannot wish me to believe your affection would have been worthless," Deronda countered, sensing her pause as an invitation to respond.

"I do not intend to speak ill of myself," the Princess declared with proud impetuosity. "But I possessed little affection to offer. I desired none. I had been stifled by it. I sought to live out the life inherent in me, unhampered by the weight of others' lives. You wonder what I was? I was no princess then."

She rose with a sudden, fluid motion, standing as she had before. Deronda followed suit instantly, feeling breathless.

"No princess in this tame existence I lead now. I was a great singer, and an actor as well. All others were poor beside me. Men followed me from nation to nation. I lived a myriad lives in one. I did not want a child."

There was a passionate defensiveness in her tone, casting aside all social precedent. She sought justification only in the most intense words available to describe her experience, flinging them outward as shields against potential reproach. Deronda stood clutching his coat collar, feeling as though he were clinging to a lifeline in turbulent waters, his blood rushing in a commotion reminiscent of witnessing a sacred rite that sanctified crime.

What else could she reveal? She continued with the same ferocity, a pale illumination brightening her face.

"I did not wish to marry. I was forced into wedlock with your father—not by my choice, but by my father's commands. Paradoxically, marriage was my best path to freedom. I could rule my husband, but I could never rule my father. I had a right to liberty. I had a right to escape a bondage I loathed."

She reseated herself, her eyes and closed lips performing a subtle dance of suppressed speech. Deronda remained standing. Moments later, she looked up, her defiance softening into a pleading plea.

"And the bondage I hated for myself, I sought to spare you. What greater gift could a loving mother bestow? I liberated you from the bondage of being born a Jew."

"Then I *am* a Jew?" Deronda exploded, his deep-voiced energy causing his mother to recoil slightly against her cushions. "My father was a Jew, and you are a Jewess?"

"Yes, your father was my cousin," she answered, her gaze shifting, revealing a flicker of fear.

"I am glad of it," Deronda blurted out, his voice veiled in passion. He hadn't anticipated the force of these words, nor that they would emerge in such impulsive opposition to his mother. A mixed anger shook him—one that reason struggled to quell—against a mother who seemed to have borne him unwillingly, made herself a stranger, and now revealed herself reluctantly. This final suspicion illuminated her previous speech.

But the mother was shaken by a different, equally volatile anger, one her frame could barely contain. Her physical tremors were visible, her eyes appearing larger and wilder against her pallor.

"Why do you say you are glad?" she cried violently. "You are an English gentleman. I secured that status for you."

"You did not know what you secured for me. How could you choose my birthright for me?" Deronda retorted, sinking sideways into his chair, leaning his arm over the backrest as he averted his gaze.

An intolerant fire burned within him, foreign yet undeniable. Struggling to master himself, he feared uttering words that would mark an irreversible epoch. Silence stretched between them until his mother's voice returned, firmer, its finely varied tones resisting the storm.

"I chose for you what I would have chosen for myself. How could I know you would inherit my father's spirit? How could I know you would love what I detested—if you truly loved to be a Jew?" The bitterness in her last words suggested a hatred that any listener might assume had festered between mother and son.

Yet Deronda reclaimed his composure, recalling the reality of her life: the years gone, the signs of suffering etched into her frame, and the desperate effort to recount a past that belonged to both of them. His habitual shame at accepting events as solely his burden aided him here. As he stared at her in silence, his face regained a penetrating calm, yet it exerted a strangely agitating influence. Her eyes fixed upon him with fascination, devoid of maternal delight.

"Forgive me if I speak hastily," he said with diffident gravity. "Why resolve now to disclose what you ensured I remain ignorant of? And why, since you appear angered by my gladness..."

"Oh—the reasons of our actions!" the Princess interrupted, her voice ringing with a note of sarcastic scorn. "When you reach my age, it will not seem so simple a question: 'Why did you do this?' People reduce motives to cut-and-dried formulas. Every woman is presumed to share a standard set of motivations, or else she is a monster. I am not a monster, but I have not felt exactly as other women feel—or say they feel, terrified of being deemed unlike them. When you reproach me in your heart for sending you away, you imply I ought to claim I felt as mothers

The morning light filtered through the windowpanes, illuminating a stack of correspondence where Hans Meyrick's latest missive lay waiting. Written in the family's distinctive, elegant script across four quarto pages, the letter began with a flourish of intellectual posturing that immediately signaled the sender's state of mind.

"My dear Deronda," it commenced, "in return for your sketch of Italian movements... 'time will show.' As to the present causes of past effects, it is now seen that the late swindling telegrams account for the last year's cattle plague..."

Deronda traced the lines with a weary eye. Hans was weaving a tapestry of cynical analogies, predicting a murrain among the commercial class and foreseeing the ready sale of rejected paintings as proof of his prophetic vision. He spoke of finding advantage in Deronda's absence, describing himself as "shining like Hesperus when Hyperion has departed," sitting with their Hebrew prophet, Mordecai, and claiming credit for the sage's wisdom. To Hans, the logic was circular and self-congratulatory: if whatever is best is Jewish, and B happens to be best, then B must be Jewish. He treated Mordecai not as a complex soul, but as a philosophical prop, a "better model than Rembrandt had for his Rabbi," capable of absorbing Hans's correct opinions while conveniently ignoring the vast remainder of the universe.

The letter drifted further into the realm of Hans's peculiar romantic delusions. He confessed that he could not help making declarations, lamenting that Mirah's distractions prevented him from throwing himself at her feet. He described his own hope as an "immortal, delicious maiden" forever courted, contrasting it sharply with Certainty, whom he visualized as an "ugly Janus-faced deity." With characteristic theatricality, Hans concluded that truth would prevail and that the Jewess he preferred would eventually prefer him, dismissing any counter-evidence as mere "blockhead" generalities that failed to discern the particular case.

Yet, amidst this flow of vanity, there was a thread of genuine observation regarding the household dynamics. Hans noted the arrival of Jacob Cohen, a young Israelite who treated Mordecai with a familiar ease that bordered on condescension, viewing the older man as a "second-hand Christian commodity." But when Mirah entered the room, the dynamic shifted instantly; Jacob transformed into a child, his demeanor softening until he resembled a dog trained by women, bringing sugar-plums to share with Mordecai. Hans marveled at this duality, wondering why the Jewish race hadn't already bought up the weaker generations of Europe like slave property, noting that there was one Jewess he wouldn't mind being enslaved to.

As the days passed, the tone of the letter grew lighter, filled with news of Rex Gascoigne and his sister, cousins to the Vandyke Duchess, and invitations to various social gatherings. Hans proposed painting Sir Hugo's daughters in the Gainsborough style, boasting of his ability to capture likeness while admitting his limitations in higher artistic realms. He ended with a cryptic reference to the Klesmers' departure and a final, desperate plea for Deronda to visit Topping Abbey, framing his own unrequited feelings as a noble, persistent dance among orchard blossoms.

Deronda sat alone with the letter, the paper silent in his hands. Some months prior, this correspondence would have irritated him, its romanticizing of Mirah carrying an unpleasant edge devoid of compassion for his friend's inevitable heartbreak. But the landscape of his own life had shifted irrevocably since March. The revelation of his birth had opened a new horizon, casting fresh light on the past and altering his perception of the present. Where he once might have seen Hans's hopes as mischievous unreasonableness, he now viewed them as the frantic, extravagant flight of a restless spirit.

"It is too restless, too readily excitable by novelty," Deronda murmured to the empty room, his voice low. "Too ready to turn itself into imaginative material, and wear its grief as a fantastic costume."

He looked down at the page where Hans claimed to understand Mirah's silence. Deronda realized that Hans was playing at love, treating the entire affair as a comedy, completely blind to the objections that stood between him and the woman he adored. Hans imagined the world to fit his own inclinations, projecting his own capacity for suffering onto others while failing to recognize that Deronda shared the same burden.

"Poor old Hans!" Deronda sighed, folding the letter carefully. "If we were under a fiery hail together, he would howl like a Greek, and if I did not howl too, it would never occur to him that I was as badly off as he."

He recognized the tenderness in Hans's intentions, the active imagination that sought to fill the voids of other people's lives with his own narrative. Yet, beneath the surface of this affectionate folly, Deronda sensed a deeper tragedy. Hans's inability to see the walls around him mirrored the very blindness that plagued the world at large. As Deronda prepared to set aside the letter, he felt a surge of pity—not for the foolishness of the hope, but for the isolation of the man who wore his despair as a costume, unaware that the storm he feared was already breaking within him.

"My desolation does begin to make A better life." —SHAKESPEARE: *Antony and Cleopatra.*

Before Deronda was summoned to a second audience with his mother, a day slipped by during which she sent only a single missive, stating she was not yet well enough to receive him. Yet on the third morning, a fresh note arrived: "I leave to-day. Come and see me at once."

He was ushered into the same chamber as before, though now it was shrouded in gloom, the windows choked by heavy blinds and drawn curtains. The Princess was absent initially, but she soon appeared, draped in a loose wrap of soft, dusky-orange silk. Black lace floated loosely around her head, framing a face that seemed even more formidable in the somber light; her eyes appeared larger, the lines of her visage more vigorous. One might have imagined her a sorceress, stretching forth a wonderful arm to brew youth-potions for others while scorning to mix them for herself, having drunk her fill of youth already.

She placed her arms instantly around her son's shoulders, kissing him on both cheeks, before settling among her cushions with an air of assured firmness and dignity that stood in stark contrast to the fitfulness of their first meeting. She gestured for Deronda to sit beside her. He complied, asking, "You are quite relieved now, I trust?"

"Yes, I am at ease again. Is there anything more that you would like to ask me?" she replied, speaking with the detached matter-of-factness of a queen rather than a mother.

"Can I find the house in Genoa where you used to live with my grandfather?" Deronda asked.

"No," she answered, making a deprecating gesture with her arm. "It is pulled down—not to be found. But regarding our family, and where my father dwelt at various times—you will discover all that among the papers in the chest, far better than I can recount. My father was a physician. My mother was a Morteira. I used to hear all those things without truly listening. You will find them all. I was born amidst them without my will, and I banished them as soon as I could."

Deronda struggled to mask his pained feeling, saying, "Anything else that I should desire to know from you could only be what it is some satisfaction to your own feeling to tell me."

"I think I have told you everything that could be demanded of me," the Princess said, her expression turning coldly meditative. It seemed she had exhausted her emotional reserves in their previous interview. In truth, she had reasoned to herself, "I have done it all. I have confessed all. I will not go through it again. I will save myself from agitation." And she was enacting that plan.

Yet to Deronda's nature, the moment was cruel; it transformed the filial yearning of his life into a disappointed pilgrimage to a shrine stripped of its sacred symbols. It seemed that all the woman lacking in her was present in him. With a tremor in his voice, he whispered, "Then are we to part and I never be anything to you?"

"It is better so," the Princess said, her voice suddenly softer, mellower. "There could be nothing but hard duty for you, even if it were possible for you to take the place of my son. You would not love me. Don't deny it," she interrupted abruptly, raising her hand. "I know what is the truth. You don't like what I did. You are angry with me. You think I robbed you of something. You are on your grandfather's side, and you will always harbor a condemnation of me in your heart."

Deronda felt himself under a ban of silence. Rising from his seat beside her, he preferred to stand if he had to obey that imperious prohibition against any tenderness. But his mother now looked up at him with a new admiration in her glance.

"You are wrong to be angry with me. You are the better for what I did." After a pause, she added sharply, "And now tell me what you shall do?"

"Do you mean now, immediately," Deronda clarified, "or as to the course of my future life?"

"I mean in the future. What difference will it make to you that I have told you about your birth?"

"A very great difference," Deronda said emphatically. "I can hardly think of anything that would make a greater difference."

"What shall you do then?" the Princess asked, her tone sharpening. "Make yourself just like your grandfather—be what he wished you—turn yourself into a Jew like him?"

"That is impossible. The effect of my education can never be undone. The Christian sympathies in which my mind was reared can never die out of me," Deronda replied, his tone growing increasingly tenacious. "But I consider it my duty—it is the impulse of my feeling—to identify myself, as far as possible, with my hereditary people. And if I can see any work to be done for them to which I can give my soul and hand, I shall choose to do it."

His mother watched him with wondering speculation, examining his face as if believing that close scrutiny could decipher a difficult language. He met her gaze firmly, sustained by a resolute opposition that was the expression of his fullest self. She leaned toward him slightly, speaking with decisive emphasis, "You are in love with a Jewess."

Deronda flushed and protested, "My reasons would be independent of any such fact."

"I know better. I have seen what men are," the Princess stated peremptorily. "Tell me the truth. She is a Jewess who will not accept anyone but a Jew. There *are* a few such," she added with a touch of scorn.

Deronda faced the objection we all know when speaking to those too certain of their own fixed interpretations to be enlightened by anything we may say. Besides this, the immediate point was one on which he felt a repugnance either to deny or affirm. He remained silent, and she presently continued, "You love her as your father loved me, and she draws you after her as I drew him."

Those words struck at Deronda's filial imagination, and some tenderness in his glance was taken by his mother as an assent. She proceeded with rising passion: "But I was leading him the other way. And now your grandfather is getting his revenge."

"Mother," Deronda remonstrated, "don't let us think of it in that way. I will admit that there may come some benefit from the education you chose for me. I prefer cherishing the benefit with gratitude to dwelling with resentment on the injury. I think it would have been right that I should have been brought up with the consciousness that I was a Jew, but it must always have been a good to me to have as wide an instruction and sympathy as possible. And now, you have restored me my inheritance—events have brought a fuller restitution than you could have made—you have been saved from robbing my people of my service and me of my duty: can you not bring your whole soul to consent to this?"

Deronda paused in his pleading; his mother listened intently, as if the cadence of his voice were taking her ear, yet she shook her head slowly. He began again, even more urgently.

"You have told me that you sought what you held the best for me: open your heart to relenting and love toward my grandfather, who sought what he held the best for you."

"Not for me, no," she said, shaking her head with absolute denial and folding her arms tightly. "I tell you, he never thought of his daughter except as an instrument. Because I had wants outside his purpose, I was to be put in a frame and tortured. If that is the right law for the world, I will not say that I love it. If my acts were wrong—if it is God who is exacting from me that I should deliver up what I withheld—who is punishing me because I deceived my father and did not warn him that I should contradict his trust? Well, I have told everything. I have done what I could. And *your* soul consents. That is enough. I have after all been the instrument my father wanted. 'I desire a grandson who shall have a true Jewish heart. Every Jew should rear his family as if he hoped that a Deliverer might spring from it.'"

In uttering these final sentences, the Princess narrowed her eyes, waved her head up and down, and spoke slowly with a new kind of chest-voice, as if quoting unwillingly.

"Were those my grandfather's words?" Deronda asked.

"Yes, yes; and you will find them written. I wanted to thwart him," the Princess said, with a sudden outburst of the passion she had shown in their former interview. Then she added more slowly, "You would have me love what I have hated from the time I was so high"—here she held her left hand a yard from the floor—"That can never be. But what does it matter? His yoke has been on me, whether I loved it or not. You are the grandson he wanted. You speak as men do—as if you felt yourself wise. What does it all mean?"

Her tone was abrupt and scornful. Deronda, in his pained feeling and under the solemn urgency of the moment, had to maintain a clutching remembrance of their relationship, lest his words become cruel. He began in a deep, entreating tone: "Mother, don't say that I feel myself wise. We are set in the midst of difficulties. I see no other way to get any clearness than by being truthful—not by keeping back facts which may—which

The ocean wind carried the salt of the Maremma, a brine that tasted of decay and deep, stagnant waters, yet here, upon the tiny plank-island of the yacht, the horizon stretched out in a deceptive, azure infinity. Madonna Pia, a ghost from Dante's Purgatory, haunted the edges of Gwendolen's consciousness, a pathetic figure among sinners who sought redemption too late. Like her, Gwendolen stood condemned, though her sentence was not written in ink but woven into the very fabric of her existence. She was trapped in a mesh of fatal entanglements, tighter inside than out, where the inward torture swelled far beyond the visible cause.

Grandcourt moved through the deck with the absolute, dreamy indifference of a man who believes he owns the sky. His yachting expedition was not an escape from the swampy flats, but a deliberate extension of his dominion. He desired to feel secure in his possession of her, to make her acutely aware that she was his property to mold as he pleased. To him, the open sea was merely another theater for his control, devoid of the social demands that usually shackled him. He suspected a stirring of opposition in her soul, a nascent spirit that threatened the prearranged order of their lives, particularly regarding the sentimental inclination she had shown toward Deronda. This was not jealousy in the common sense, but a cold, resolute determination to extinguish any foolery that challenged his authority. He viewed their marriage as a flawless contract: he had provided rank and luxury, fulfilling his side, while she, he assumed, had surrendered her autonomy.

Yet Gwendolen knew the truth of that transaction. She had not traded her heart for gold; she had traded her integrity. She possessed a root of conscience that had already begun its purgatorial work on solid ground, forcing her to acknowledge her own wrongs. Now, adrift on the turquoise divide separating her from the world, she felt the weight of the price she had paid. She had sold her truthfulness and her sense of justice, binding them in silence, collaring them to drag behind her wherever Grandcourt led.

Around her, the yacht was a jewel of excess. The cabin smelled of cedar and polished wood, lined with soft cushions and mirrors that multiplied the illusion of space. The crew was a collection of elegant toys, including a sailor with ringlets and a bronzed complexion, while Mr. Lush had already retreated to England, unable to endure the atmosphere. Even the sea, usually a source of nausea, seemed to offer a strange amusement; watching the rigging, forecasting the adjustments, allowed her a fleeting taste of imaginary rule. The weather was fine, the clay banks below turning purple and gem-like in the heat, inviting a dream where sorrow had finally ceased.

But what stills the hunger of the heart when the eye sickeneth for beauty? What paradise can quiet the fury of moral repulsion that gnaws like an eating pain? As Gwendolen sat throned on her cushions, watching the softening glory of sea and sky, she prayed that Grandcourt would not pause near her, that he would not turn his gaze upon her. Across the water, some anonymous woman under a smoky sky calculated the cost of eggs, unaware that her mundane worries were nothing compared to the silent scream tearing Gwendolen apart. Elsewhere, couples leaned cheek to cheek, counting their earnings for a future holiday, blissfully ignorant of the hell unfolding on this deck.

Did Grandcourt comprehend the tempest raging in his wife's breast? He knew she did not love him, and he required no more than that. He was not accustomed to comforting himself with the notion that he was universally beloved. He failed to grasp that she harbored a specific, profound repulsion for him personally. How could she?
