He knew the nature of personal repulsion better than most, cataloging the brutish habits, the smirks, the vulgar attempts at agreeableness of his fellow creatures. In this critical view of humanity, he and Gwendolen shared an affinity before their marriage, drawn together by the refined negations he presented. He understood why she hated Lush, but he remained blind to why she loathed Henleigh Grandcourt. He belonged to a school of thought that denied the existence of an external world hostile to them, convinced of his own magnetic allure. He could not conceive that beauty could become detestable through exasperation, that outward virtue could serve as a platform for hateful things to flaunt their superiority.

So how could he divine the war within her?

To any observer—even the seasoned foreign maid immune to seasickness, or his own veteran valet, let alone the picturesque crew who saw them as a model couple—their interaction was a study in well-bred silence. Grandcourt offered no humor, no chit-chat to spark disputes. He was impeccably polite, arranging garments or handing objects with a precision that bordered on the mechanical. Gwendolen, in turn, maintained a dignified distance, refusing the vulgarity of rude acceptance or rejection. When he pointed out a plantation of sugar-canes, she feigned interest, reciting lines of duty to distract herself from her personal affairs. When he smoked and walked the deck, pausing to point out sails, he would eventually settle and fix his narrow, immovable gaze upon her, treating her as merely another component of the yacht. Conscious of being watched, she strained her ingenuity to avoid meeting his eyes.

At dinner, his inquiries were clinical: the fruit was stale, the wine insufficient. A lady was obliged to respond suitably, even if her soul recoiled. Quarreling with Grandcourt was impossible; it would be like shouting insults at a beautifully coiled serpent in her cabin. What dispute could a proud woman initiate on a yacht?

This captivity gave Grandcourt intense satisfaction. It transformed their domestic life into a royal spectacle where privacy was abolished, and every private protest added only to the piquancy of his despotism. For Gwendolen, who had rarely encountered anything resembling heroism or sublimity, the entire universe narrowed to this husband and her relationship with him. Those closest to us often act as our interpreters of the world, distorting reality through their own limitations. Grandcourt was a piece of yellow, wavy glass, refracting her life into a promenade of ugly idols. His trivial sentences, petty standards, and loveless ennui poisoned her existence.

Some wives find solace in the prospect of motherhood, but Gwendolen dreaded it. To desire a child would be to consent to the finalization of the injury she had inflicted upon herself. Instead, in the irritable fluctuations of despair, glimmers of hope appeared as possibilities of accident. Dwelling on the benignity of chance became her refuge from worse temptations.

Hatred, like love, grows in secret, feeding on internal nourishment until it dominates the lifeblood. The most intense form of hatred is rooted in fear, driving one to silence and constructing a vindictive fantasy of annihilation. These hidden rites of vengeance played out in the secrecy of Gwendolen's mind, not bringing peace, but fueling a struggling terror. Alongside her dread of her husband grew a self-dread, urging her to flee the pursuing images born of her pent-up impulses. The vision of her past wrongdoings cast a pale, ghastly light over every rash effort at freedom she had made. Every act was now filtered through the lens of Deronda's potential judgment. No word of flattery or dependence could weaken his restraining power; indeed, his own efforts to restrain himself were repaid by her inability to sever the connection. Amidst the dreary uncertainties of her spoiled life, the possible remedies in his mind—and the remedy lying in her feelings for him—remained her only hope.

Deronda seemed to her a terrible-browed angel, from whom she could not hide any deed without risking an ignorant regard. Truthfulness was the only currency acceptable in their relation, for his power began in raising a self-discontent that could only be satisfied by genuine change. Yet, she had lost all confidence in concealment. Her vision of dread took the form of a fiercely impulsive deed, committed in a dream from which she would instantly awaken to find the effects terrifyingly real. She feared finding death under her hands, yet instead of darkness, she would see daylight; instead of satisfied hatred, the dismay of guilt; instead of freedom, the paralysis of a new terror—a white, dead face from which she tried to flee but was forever held back.

Deronda's words echoed in her mind, a mantra of warning: *"Turn your fear into a safeguard. Keep your dread fixed on the idea of increasing your remorse... Take your fear as a safeguard. It is like quickness of hearing. It may make consequences passionately present to you."*

And so it was. In Gwendolen's consciousness, temptation and dread met and stared like two pale phantoms, each seeing itself in the other, each obstructed by its own image. All the while, her fuller self beheld these apparitions and sobbed silently for deliverance.

Inarticulate prayers, no more defined than cries, swept out from her into the vast silence, broken only by her husband's breathing, the splash of waves, or the creaking of masts. If she ever thought of definite help, it took the form of Deronda's presence, his sympathy, the direction he might offer. Sometimes, following a white-lipped, fierce-eyed temptation where murderous fingers twitched with demonic intent, these best moments of inward crying and clinging for rescue would come. She would lie with wide-open eyes, tears rising as a blessing, whispering, *"I will not mind if I can keep from getting wicked,"* as an answer to her indefinite prayer.

When Deronda encountered Gwendolen and Grandcourt upon the staircase, his mind was already a storm-tossed sea, preoccupied by the shadow of a recent summons to his mother. Only two hours prior, he had parted from her, and shortly thereafter learned that the Princess Halm-Eberstein had departed the hotel. Logically, the path to Mainz lay clear; he could have delivered Joseph Kalonymos's letter and claimed the family chest, fulfilling the practical mandate of his journey. Yet, a paralyzing mixture of emotions, refusing to crystallize into reason, held him fast. Long after the farewell, he remained passive, weighed down by a retrospective gravity. In the solitude of his waiting, he relived the preceding scenes with a renewed, piercing intensity, as if they were mere preludes to their inevitable arrival within his soul. He allowed himself to weep, with a compassion sharper than most women's, over a life so intimately near yet agonizingly distant. He perceived the world shifted irrevocably by the certainty of bonds that had altered the balance of his hopes and fears, granting him a strange, newfound fellowship. It was as though, hidden beneath the cloak of night, he had mistakenly joined a band of wanderers, only to discover at dawn that the tents of his true kin stood miles apart. A quivering imagination seized him, sensing a profound kinship with a grandfather animated by fierce impulses and cherished thoughts, now perhaps stirring from sleep within his own heart. Through this torrent of passion, Mordecai and Mirah remained ever-present, silent partners clasping hands with him in understanding. Such was the texture of Deronda's spirit: quick, responsive fiber wrapped in a mantle of self-controlled reserve forged by early hardship.

Then, the persistent ringing of a bell shattered the trance, reminding him of the hour. He considered consulting the *Bradshaw* and making the necessary preparations for the next train to Mainz, thoughts of the letter and the chest flickering briefly alongside images of London and the beings who constituted the deepest attachments of his life. But other desires, rooted in Genoa, anchored him. These were the forces that urge us to linger when an interview carries the presage of finality or overwhelming sorrow. He did not consciously decide to stay until Friday's evening service at the synagogue, nor did he explicitly vow to seek the Grandcourts again despite Grandcourt's likely displeasure. Instead, he simply ceased moving, sitting amidst the stillness while his mind wandered to the sanctuary. He visualized the congregation: faces scarcely different from those of his grandfather's era, hearing the Spanish-Hebrew liturgy endure through seasons of wandering like a plant with drifting seeds, binding far-off lands to the exile's home. His thoughts drifted to Gwendolen, haunted by anxious recollections of the past and a half-formed conviction that it would be cruelty to depart without attempting, against Grandcourt's probable objections, to demonstrate his continued sympathy following their abrupt separation.

In this suspended state, he delayed his departure, eating dinner without taste, then rising abruptly to locate the synagogue. Passing the porter, he inquired if Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt remained in the hotel and requested their apartment number. The porter supplied the number but added that they had gone out boating. This single piece of information possessed a potency sufficient to fracture Deronda's focus, dividing his attention between the sparse *talithim* and the solemn, dark faces of worshippers who approached their prayers with an easy familiarity—a Hebrew dyed Italian—that suggested his grandfather, per the Princess's hints, must have been nearly as exceptional a Jew as Mordecai himself. Were not men of ardent zeal and far-reaching vision inherently exceptional? Those who, as Mordecai declared, created and fed the world, molding the passive lives that otherwise would have shriveled into the narrow tenacity of insects, unshaken by thoughts beyond the reach of their antennae.

A mournful impatience mingled with his solicitude for Gwendolen, fueled by his current release from immediate cares, urging him to hasten from the synagogue. He chose instead to take his evening walk toward the quay, a favored haunt that now held special allure due to the possibility of intercepting the Grandcourts returning from their boating trip. Resolving to approach them deliberately and disregard any grounds Grandcourt might have for excluding him, Deronda moved forward. The sun had sunk behind a bank of clouds, casting only a faint, dying yellow light upon the waves, which churned under an active breeze. Sauntering slowly within sight of the strand, he observed groups concentrating their attention on a sailing-boat advancing swiftly toward the shore, propelled by two men. Amidst the clamorous chatter in various tongues, Deronda deemed it safer not to ask questions but to push his way to the forefront, becoming an unobstructed witness to the unfolding drama.

Telescopes were raised, and loud declarations erupted: the boat carried someone who had drowned. One claimed it was the *milord*; another insisted the prostrate figure was *miladi*. A Frenchman, lacking a glass, ventured that it was the *milord* who had likely taken his wife out to drown her, citing a national practice—a remark an English skipper immediately dismissed in their native idiom as nonsense that had "undergone a mining operation," before decisively ruling that the reclining figure was a woman. For Deronda, terribly agitated by fluctuating terrors, the rhythm of the oars was punctuated by swift visions of possible and impossible events that might have led to this tragic conclusion, or this broken fragment thereof, with a darker half concealed—if the woman seemingly snatched from the waters was indeed Mrs. Grandcourt.

Soon, however, doubt vanished. The boat was drawn to land, and Deronda saw Gwendolen half-raising herself on her hands, driven by her own effort beneath a heavy shroud of tarpaulins and pea-jackets. She appeared pale as one of the sheeted dead, shivering, with wet hair streaming down her face, her eyes wide with a wild, amazed consciousness, as if she had awakened in a world where judgment was imminent and the surrounding beings were there to seize her. The first rower, drenched through, leaped onto the sand and ran off; the sailors crowding the boat blocked Deronda's advance, allowing him only to watch as Gwendolen cast terrified glances, shrinking in horror as she was carefully, tenderly extricated and led away by the strong arms of rough, bronzed men. Her wet clothes clung to her limbs, exacerbating the impediments of her weakness.

Suddenly, her wandering eyes fell upon Deronda, standing before her. Immediately, as if she had been expecting him and searching for him, she tried to stretch out her arms, restrained by her supporters, speaking in a muffled voice, "It is come, it is come! He is dead!"

"Hush, hush!" Deronda commanded, his tone authoritative. Turning to the assistants, he declared, "I am a connection of this lady's husband. If you will get her on to the *Italia* as quickly as possible, I will undertake everything else."

He remained to learn from the remaining boatman that her husband had gone down irrecoverably and that his vessel floated empty nearby. They had heard a cry, arrived in time to see the lady leap in after her husband, and managed to extract her swiftly, saving her from severe injury. With this confirmed, Deronda hastened to the hotel to ensure the best medical aid was secured. Once satisfied, he telegraphed the event to Sir Hugo, begging his immediate arrival, and also notified Mr. Gascoigne at the rectory, finding therein the nearest known channel to reach Gwendolen's mother. Words Gwendolen had spoken in moments of agitated confession returned to him with the force of inspiration: she had often mentioned her mother's presence as a potential aid, had she but been able to secure it.

The pain, the curse with which they died, had never passed away; Deronda could not draw his eyes from hers, nor lift them up to pray. Yet that night, he did not remove his clothes. Gwendolen, after insisting on seeing him once more before consenting to undress, had fallen into a perfectly quiet stillness, whispering with a repressed, frantic eagerness that he must come to her when she summoned him in the morning. Even so, a foreboding gnawed at him—the possibility that a change might seize her, the danger of a supervening fever, the suspicion that the late catastrophe was already seeping into her speech. He instructed her attendant that he stood ready to be called at any alarming shift in her symptoms, making it clear to everyone that he was in constant contact with her friends in England and felt bound to take every precaution for her sake—a role he assumed all the easier because he was well-known to Grandcourt's valet, the sole old servant who had survived the voyage.

When the strange, variegated emotions of the day finally claimed him, Deronda slept, undisturbed until the morning dreams arrived as a tangled web of yesterday's events, waking him with an image drawn by his pressing anxiety. It was morning, and there had been no summons—an augury that cheered him as he dressed, leading him to reflect that it was too early to inquire further. Later, he learned she had endured a too-wakeful night but showed no violent signs of agitation, eventually succumbing to sleep. He marveled at the force dwelling in this creature, so acutely alive to dread; he carried an irresistible impression that even under the shock of severe physical trauma, she was mastering herself with a terrifying determination of concealment. In contrast, he felt his own sensibilities blunted by the ordeal of meeting his mother; he seemed to himself merely fulfilling obligations, his deeper, more passionate sympathy lying in abeyance. Having lived so keenly in an experience apart from Gwendolen's lot, his current cares for her felt like a revisiting of familiar scenes, lacking the complete revival of inward response they once commanded.

In the interim, he sought a formal, legally recognized statement from the fishermen who had rescued Gwendolen. Few details emerged. The boat in which Grandcourt had departed was found drifting with its sail loose, towed safely inland. The fishermen believed he had been knocked overboard by the flapping canvas while turning the vessel, unable to swim. Yet, though they were nearby, their attention had first been arrested by a cry resembling that of a man in distress. As they hastened with their oars, they heard a shriek from the lady and saw her leap into the water.

Upon returning to the hotel, Deronda was informed that Gwendolen had risen and desired to see him. He was led into a room darkened by blinds and heavy curtains, where she sat wrapped in a white shawl, gazing toward the opening door with the uneasy vigilance of one awaiting judgment. Her long hair was gathered and coiled with careful precision, and the blue stars in her ears retained their place. As she started impulsively to her full height, encased in the shawl, her face and neck stark white save for a purple line beneath her eyes, her lips slightly parted with the peculiar expression of the accused and helpless, she appeared as the unhappy ghost of the Gwendolen Harleth Deronda had once seen turning from her losses at the gaming table with firm lips and proud self-possession. The sight pierced him with pity, stirring the effects of all their past relations anew.

"I beseech you to rest—not to stand," Deronda said as he approached, and she obeyed, falling back into her chair.

"Will you sit down near me?" she asked. "I want to speak very low."

She settled into a large armchair, and he drew a small one close to her side. The gesture touched her peculiarly; turning her pale face fully toward his, which hovered inches away, she whispered in the lowest audible tone, "You know I am a guilty woman?"

Deronda himself grew paler as he replied, "I know nothing." He dared not say more.

"He is dead." She uttered this with the same undertoned decisiveness.

"Yes," Deronda answered, in a mournful suspense that made him reluctant to continue.

"His face will not be seen above the water again," Gwendolen said, her voice not louder but charged with suppressed urgency, her hands clenched tight.

"No."

"Not by any one else—only by me. A dead face... I shall never get away from it."

With an inward voice of desperate self-repression, she spoke these final words while averting her gaze from Deronda toward something distant on the floor. Was she viewing the entire event—including her own acts—through an exaggerating medium of excitement and horror? Was she in a state of delirium, entering a realm of concealment and necessary self-suppression? Such thoughts flashed through Deronda as a fragile hope. But consider the conflict of feeling that kept him silent. She was bent on confession, and he dreaded hearing it. Against his better will, he shrank from the task laid upon him; he wished, and yet rebuked the wish as cowardly, that she could bury her secrets in her own bosom. He was not a priest. He feared the weight of this woman's soul flung upon his own with imploring dependence. Then she spoke again, hurriedly, looking at him.

"You will not say that I ought to tell the world? You will not say that I ought to be disgraced? I could not do it. I could not bear it. I cannot have my mother know. Not if I were dead. I could not have her know. I must tell you; but you will not say that any one else should know."

"I can say nothing in my ignorance," Deronda murmured, "except that I desire to help you."

"I told you from the beginning—as soon as I could—I told you I was afraid of myself." There was a piteous pleading in the low murmur to which Deronda turned his ear exclusively. Her afflicted face weighed upon him too heavily. "I felt a hatred in me that worked like an evil spirit—contriving things. Every means I could devise to free myself came to mind; and it got worse—all things got worse. That is why I asked you to come to me in town. I thought then I would tell you the worst about myself. I tried. But I could not tell everything. And *he* came in."

She paused, a shudder passing through her frame, before continuing.

"I will tell you everything now. Do you think a woman who cried, and prayed, and struggled to be saved from herself, could be a murderess?"

"Great God!" Deronda exclaimed, his voice deep and shaken. "Do not torture me needlessly. You have not murdered him. You threw yourself into the water with the impulse to save him. Tell me the rest afterward. This death was an accident that you could not have hindered."

"Don't be impatient with me." The tremor, the childlike beseeching in these words, compelled Deronda to turn his head and look at her face. The poor, quivering lips continued. "You said—you used to say—you felt more for those who had done something wicked and were miserable; you said they might get better—they might be scourged into something better. If you had not spoken in that way, everything would have been worse. I *did* remember all you said to me. It came to me always. It came to me at the very last—that was the reason why I—But now, if you cannot bear with me when I tell you everything, if you turn away from me and forsake me, what shall I do? Am I worse than I was when you found me and wanted to make me better? All the wrong I have done was in me then—and more—and more—if you had not come and been patient with me. And now—will you forsake me?"

Her hands, so tightly clenched moments before, were now helplessly relaxed and trembling on the arm of her chair. Her quivering lips remained parted as she ceased speaking. Deronda could not answer; he was obliged to look away. Taking one of her hands, he clasped it as if they were walking together like two children; it was the only way he could answer, "I will not forsake you." All the while, he felt as though he were signing his name to a blank paper that might be filled up terribly. Their posture, his averted face bearing an expression of suffering he was solemnly resolved to endure, might have told half the truth of the situation to a stranger who had suddenly entered.

That grasp was an entirely new experience for Gwendolen. She had never before received from any man a sign of tenderness that her own being desperately needed, and she interpreted its powerful effect on her as a promise of inexhaustible patience and constancy. The stream of renewed strength allowed her to proceed as she had begun—with that fitful, wandering confession where the sameness of experience seemed to nullify the sense of time or order in events. She resumed in fragments.

"All sorts of contrivances in my mind—but all so difficult. And I fought against them—I was terrified at them—I saw his dead face"—here her voice sank almost to a whisper close to Deronda's ear—"ever so long ago I saw it and I wished him to be dead. And yet it terrified me. I was like two creatures. I could not speak—I wanted to kill—it was as strong as thirst—and then directly—I felt beforehand I had done something dreadful, un

The unripe grape, the ripe, and the dried. All things are changes, not into nothing, but into that which is not at present. Marcus Aurelius had written these words, yet they hung in the air of the room like a benediction that could not quite reach the broken woman seated by the window.

In the evening, she summoned him again. It was nearly the hour when she had been brought in from the sea the previous day, a time marked by the intrusion of the ocean's violence into her domestic sphere. Now, the light was subdued, filtered through drawn blinds and open windows that breathed the cool, stagnant air of the room. She sat gazing fixedly at the darkened pane, her cheek pressed against her hand, appearing less shattered than when he had last departed, yet wearing a deep melancholy that transformed into anxious timidity as Deronda approached.

She did not offer her hand. Instead, her voice trembled with the weight of elapsed time. "How long ago it is!" she whispered. Then, with a fragile plea, "Will you sit near me again a little while?"

Deronda moved to place himself beside her, mirroring the position he had held before. He observed her turn toward him, her face etched with that indefinable expression implying a desperate need to speak, yet finding the words locked behind a barrier of fear. He waited, patient and still. Again she looked toward the window, silent, the same expression returning, refusing to break into speech. Some terror hindered her utterance. Seeking to relieve her timidity, Deronda averted his face, giving her space to gather her courage.

Presently, her voice cut through the silence, imploring and raw. "You will not say that any one else should know?"

"Most decidedly not," Deronda replied immediately. "There is no action that ought to be taken in consequence. There is no injury that could be righted in that way. There is no retribution that any mortal could apportion justly."

She remained so still during the ensuing pause that she seemed to be holding her breath, suspending her existence until she finally spoke. "But if I had not had that murderous will—that moment—if I had thrown the rope on the instant—perhaps it would have hindered death?"

"No—I think not," Deronda answered slowly, his tone measured against the chaos of her guilt. "If it were true that he could swim, he must have been seized with cramp. With your quickest, utmost effort, it seems impossible that you could have done anything to save him. That momentary murderous will cannot, I think, have altered the course of events. Its effect is confined to the motives in your own breast. Within ourselves our evil will is momentous, and sooner or later it works its way outside us—it may be in the vitiation that breeds evil acts, but also it may be in the self-abhorrence that stings us into better striving."

"I am saved from robbing others—there are others—they will have everything—they will have what they ought to have. I knew that some time before I left town. You do not suspect me of wrong desires about those things?" She spoke hesitatingly, her voice fraying at the edges.

"I had not thought of them," Deronda admitted, "I was thinking too much of the other things."

"Perhaps you don't quite know the beginning of it all," Gwendolen said slowly, as if wrestling with the reluctance to expose the rot at the core of her narrative. "There was some one else he ought to have married. And I knew it, and I told her I would not hinder it. And I went away—that was when you first saw me. But then we became poor all at once, and I was very miserable, and I was tempted. I thought, 'I shall do as I like and make everything right.' I persuaded myself. And it was all different. It was all dreadful. Then came hatred and wicked thoughts. That was how it all came. I told you I was afraid of myself. And I did what you told me—I did try to make my fear a safeguard. I thought of what would be if I—I felt what would come—how I should dread the morning—wishing it would be always night—and yet in the darkness always seeing something—seeing death. If you did not know how miserable I was, you might—but now it has all been no use. I can care for nothing but saving the rest from knowing—poor mamma, who has never been happy."

Silence settled over them again, heavy and suffocating, before she spoke with a repressed sob. "You cannot bear to look at me any more. You think I am too wicked. You do not believe that I can become any better—worth anything—worthy enough—I shall always be too wicked to—" Her voice broke off, helpless and spent.

Deronda's heart was pierced by the sound. He turned his eyes onto her poor, beseeching face. "I believe that you may become worthier than you have ever yet been—worthy to lead a life that may be a blessing. No evil dooms us hopelessly except the evil we love, and desire to continue in, and make no effort to escape from. You *have* made efforts—you will go on making them."

"But you were the beginning of them. You must not forsake me," Gwendolen said, leaning forward with her clasped hands gripping the arm of her chair. She looked at him, her face bearing piteous traces of the life-experience concentrated in the preceding twenty-four hours—that new, terrible life lying on the other side of the deed which fulfilled a criminal desire. "I will bear any penance. I will lead any life you tell me. But you must not forsake me. You must be near. If you had been near me—if I could have said everything to you, I should have been different. You will not forsake me?"

"It could never be my impulse to forsake you," Deronda said promptly, his voice carrying that unintentional resonance that made his ready sympathy feel more personal and special than it truly was. In that moment, however, he was not entirely free from a foreboding of some such self-committing effect. His strong feeling for this stricken creature could not hinder the rushing images of future difficulty. He continued to meet her appealing eyes, but with the painful consciousness that to her ear his words might carry a promise which one day would seem unfulfilled: he was making an indefinite promise to an indefinite hope.

Anxieties, both immediate and distant, crowded on his thought. It was under their influence that, after a moment's silence, he said, "I expect Sir Hugo Mallinger to arrive by tomorrow night at least; and I am not without hope that Mrs. Davilow may shortly follow him. Her presence will be the greatest comfort to you—it will give you a motive to save her from unnecessary pain?"

"Yes, yes—I will try. And you will not go away?"

"Not till after Sir Hugo has come."

"But we shall all go to England?"

"As soon as possible," Deronda said, unwilling to enter into particulars.

Gwendolen looked toward the window again with an expression which seemed like a gradual awakening to new thoughts. The twilight was perceptibly deepening, but Deronda could see a movement in her eyes and hands such as accompanies a return of perception in one who has been stunned.

"You will always be with Sir Hugo now!" she said presently, looking at him. "You will always live at the Abbey—or else at Diplow?"

"I am quite uncertain where I shall live," Deronda said, coloring slightly.

She was warned by his changed countenance that she had spoken too rashly, and she fell silent. After a little while, she began again, looking away, "It is impossible to think how my life will go on. I think now it would be better for me to be poor and obliged to work."

"New promptings will come as the days pass. When you are among your friends again, you will discern new duties," Deronda said gently. "Make it a task now to get as well and calm—as much like yourself as you can, before—" He hesitated.

"Before my mother comes," Gwendolen supplied softly.

"Ah! I must be changed. I have not looked at myself. Should you have known me," she added, turning toward him, "if you had met me now?—should you have known me for the one you saw at Leubronn?"

"Yes, I should have known you," Deronda said mournfully. "The outside change is not great. I should have seen at once that it was you, and that you had gone through some great sorrow."

"Don't wish now that you had never seen me; don't wish that," Gwendolen implored, tears gathering in her eyes.

"I should despise myself for wishing it," Deronda replied. "How could I know what I was wishing? We must find our duties in what comes to us, not in what we imagine might have been. If I took to foolish wishing of that sort, I should wish—not that I had never seen you, but that I had been able to save you from this."

"You have saved me from worse," Gwendolen said in a sobbing voice. "I should have been worse if it had not been for you. If you had not been good, I should have been more wicked than I am."

"It will be better for me to go now," Deronda said, worn in spirit by the perpetual strain of this scene. "Remember what we said of your task—to get well and calm before other friends come."

He rose as

Time, they say, measures itself in miles traveled or acres gained, yet the soul's geography defies such arithmetic. One may march south until a single bone becomes a map of new anatomy, or turn east to unlock languages that rewrite the history of races; one may endure a hero's poem of maiming and endurance only to return and find the parish gossip unchanged, the elderly gentleman still shaking his head at the butcher's boy, still staring vacantly at the same shop window. Even the swiftest thought, galloping like a greyhound, cannot outrun the limpet's crawl—a slow, sticky progress that eventually reveals itself as motion. This tension between the revolutionary surge of transformation and the quiet recurrence of the familiar defined the year's passage.

It had stripped the brilliant, self-assured Gwendolen Harleth of the Archery Meeting, crushing her into a penitent who confessed her unworthiness despite a heart that craved to be deemed worthy. Yet, at Pennicote Rectory, the change was superficial, a mere adjustment of budgets and social calendars. The rectory remained a haven of cheerful certainty; the red and pink peonies bloomed with undimmed vigor, and the rector maintained his sunny faith in patronage, diligently fulfilling his duties regardless of audience. His two unsigned ecclesiastical articles, circulated privately among the elite who knew their author but rarely read their content, were treated with polite indifference, allowing him to bask in peaceful authorship, nourished by complacency rather than the lean, hungry scrutiny of public critique. For the rector, the loss of eighteen hundred pounds a year was a trivial price for the renewed delight in his favorite son. Whatever shadow the disappointment of his first love had cast over Rex, it appeared secondary to the evidence of a more serious ambition born from family necessity. Mr. Gascoigne viewed the previous year's turmoil as merely the evaporation of excess moisture, a final step in baking the human dough.

Now, Rex returned for a summer visit, bringing Anna home. While he moved with the old vitality among his siblings, he retreated into the disciplined solitude of the eager student, rising early and closing himself away in the evenings to pursue a rigorous course of study.

"You do not regret choosing the law as a profession, Rex?" his father asked.

"There is no profession I would prefer," Rex replied firmly. "My ambition is to serve as a first-rate judge and help draft a new code. I invert the famous maxim: 'Give me something to do with making the laws, and let others compose the songs.'"

"You will inevitably have to stuff your mind with immense amounts of rubbish, I suppose—that is the drawback," the rector noted.

"I perceive no distinction between legal rubbish and the literary nonsense that clogs other minds," Rex countered. "Law does not dull the intellect. Our wisest men have often been lawyers. An orderly framework of cases and evidence offers clarity where others find only a chaotic wash of trivia. Furthermore, from a higher vantage, the foundations and evolution of law constitute the most compelling chapters of philosophy and history. Naturally, there will be tedious, drudging moments, perhaps exasperating ones. But the greatest prizes in life are never won easily, and I have learned that lesson."

"My boy," the rector said, his voice swelling with paternal pride, "the finest omen of professional success is believing one's chosen path is the noblest in existence. Most men find this conviction once they commit with will. Recall Brewitt, the blacksmith, who told me recently that his apprentice lacked interest in his trade. 'And yet, sir,' Brewitt remarked, 'what would a young fellow have if he does not like blacksmithing?' I cherish this fatherly joy, dispensing it in measured doses."

Warham, lost to India, had been parted from with ease, but Rex represented a different kind of romance for the rector—a later-life discovery of a son surpassing himself, a living testament to future eminence modeled on famous precedents. Only to his wife did he declare with absolute certainty, "Rex will be a distinguished man, Nancy, I assure you—as surely as Paley's father foresaw his son's greatness."

"Was Paley an old bachelor?" Mrs. Gascoigne queried.

"That is hardly relevant, my dear," the rector replied, forgetting the irrelevance instantly. Mrs. Gascoigne felt a pang of weakness at her own inquiry.

In the low, narrow drawing room of "Jodson's," a modest white house nestled a mile away behind a screen of evergreens, time marched in quiet rhythm. The group who had traded the faded grandeur of Offendene for this enclosure found comfort in its simplicity. Mrs. Davilow's delicate face bore only a deepening of her mild melancholy, her hair streaked with a few additional silver threads from the year's trials. Her four daughters had blossomed slightly, freed from the shadow of their mother's grief, while the good Jocosa maintained her neutral stance toward worldly pleasures, reserved for those not "in a situation."

On a July afternoon, the room opened via wide lattice windows to the intoxicating scent of monthly roses, the soft murmur of the garden, and the distant, clarifying clip-clop of hooves and wheels. With Rex and Anna joining the usual sextet, the scene became lively and crowded. Anna, beloved by her younger cousins, recounted tales of London and her new acquaintances. When she first arrived alone, questions poured forth regarding Gwendolen's residence in Grosvenor Square, her words, and the whispers surrounding her. Had Anna visited Gwendolen after learning of the yacht? No. This silence left the door open to wild speculation about the mysterious vessel, save for the fact that Gwendolen had written prior to departure announcing a Mediterranean cruise with Mr. Grandcourt, and later from Marseilles expressing her delight in the elegance of the cabins, promising a lengthy diary filled with *ditto* marks instead of further correspondence. The news had also surfaced in the newspapers, cementing this new chapter of Gwendolen's exalted life as a central thread in the sisters' romantic tapestry, prompting Isabel, the book-devouring cousin, to weave in a dash of corsair adventure to ensure a happy ending.

However, whenever Rex entered the room, the girls, adhering to strict instructions, silenced this captivating subject. Instead, today's conversation centered on the Meyricks and their extraordinary Jewish associates, sparking astonished queries from minds that viewed live Jews as a biological anomaly, akin to a strange species described by Pliny that might sleep beneath the shade of its own ears. Bertha struggled to comprehend modern Jewish beliefs, vaguely suspecting a rejection of the Old Testament due to its validation of the New; Miss Merry concluded that Mirah and her brother were impossible to reason with; and the amiable Alice simply stated she could not bear them. Mrs. Davilow gently corrected her daughter, noting that prominent Jewish families integrated into society behaved perfectly in London and Paris, though admitting that unconverted commoners were objectionable. Isabel pressed whether Mirah spoke differently, or if one could sit beside her without realizing her Jewish heritage.

Rex, possessing no partisan allegiance to the Israelites but burdened by a cram-filled acquaintance with the minutiae of their ancient history, amused himself by playfully exaggerating each speaker's prejudice. Anna interceded, begging them to understand his jest, but their laughter was cut short by the arrival of a letter for Mrs. Davilow. A messenger had delivered it with frantic haste directly from the rectory. Inside lay a telegram. As Mrs. Davilow read and reread the message in silent, agitated tremors, all eyes turned to her with mounting anxiety, yet no one dared to break the silence. Finally lifting her gaze to meet the young faces "painted with fear," she recalled that they might be imagining horrors worse than the truth, mirroring her own initial paralysis of dread. With a sob that was half-relief, she spoke,

"My dears, Mr. Grandcourt—" She paused, gathering her strength, then began again, "Mr. Grandcourt is drowned."

Rex sprang to his feet as if struck by a physical blow. He could not restrain himself, and Anna's first instinct was to look at him. But then, summoning a fragile reserve as Mrs. Davilow scanned the rector's accompanying note, he managed to ask,

"Can I do anything, Aunt? Can I carry a message to my father for you?"

"Yes, dear. Tell him I shall be ready—he is very good. He says he will accompany me to Genoa; he will be here at half-past six. Jocosa and Alice, assist me in preparing. She is safe—Gwendolen is safe—but she must be ill. I am certain she is terribly ill. Rex, dear—you and Anna—go and inform your father I shall be quite ready. I would not for the world lose another night. Bless him for his readiness; I can travel day and night until we reach her."

Rex and Anna hurried away through sunlight that had suddenly grown solemn, exchanging not a word. Anna was consumed by solicitude for any reopening of Rex's wounds, while he wrestled with a tumultuous swarm of thoughts that offended his better judgment. The chaos persisted as they reached the rectory gate.

"Nannie, I shall leave you to convey everything to my father," Rex said. "If he requires me immediately, let me know. I shall remain in the shrubbery for ten minutes—no longer."

Who among us has not succumbed to egoistic flights of imagination, envisioning favorable outcomes for our own futures amidst another's tragedy, sorrow, or death? The prospect of promotion or inheritance serves as the common lure,

"I count myself in nothing else so happy as in a soul remembering my good friends." —Shakespeare.

Sir Hugo Mallinger did not depart for Genoa with the alacrity Mr. Gascoigne had displayed, nor would Daniel Deronda leave until he had secured a final audience with the baronet. The shadow of Grandcourt's drowning loomed large, compounded by the recent, shattering revelation of Deronda's own origins; these twin tragedies demanded a face-to-face communion that letters, clumsy and distant, could never convey. It was not until the fifth evening that Deronda stood at the station platform, waiting for Sir Hugo's arrival between eight and nine. While he gazed toward the tracks, anticipating the familiar, kind visage that formed part of his earliest memories, a faint, tentative smile curved his lips. It was a smile born of gratitude for Sir Hugo's newfound mastery over his estates, his ability to provide for his daughters, or perhaps, according to a newly crystallized vision of inheritance, to adopt makeshift feminine offspring as intermediaries to secure a male heir. We are churlish indeed if we cannot share in our fellows' joys, provided they do not clash violently with our rigid theories of righteousness; otherwise, our mouths twist sour, our eyes freeze, while our own possessive desires remain stubbornly misaligned with our ideals. Yet, we must allow ourselves companionship with imperfection. It is possible, and fortunate, to harbor gratitude even when we perceive a mistake that may have caused harm, recognizing that the vehicle of that mistake was an affectionate intention pursued through a lifetime of kindly service.

Deronda's heart and judgment recoiled from Sir Hugo's role as the architect of a falsehood—a deception under which he had been reared—but the baronet likely lacked clear knowledge of the mother's breach of trust. With his characteristic lightness of touch regarding life's complexities, Sir Hugo had deemed it a reasonable preference that her son become an English gentleman, especially given the mother's eccentric refusal to sever ties with her child, effectively choosing to be absent in spirit while present in name. Daniel's affectionate gratitude drove him to seek grounds for excuse rather than condemnation; for it is as easy to be rigid in principle yet tender in blame as it is to suffer from the sight of things askew while remaining patient with the hanger-on who sees clearly. Had Sir Hugo, in his bachelorhood, been beguiled into viewing children primarily as products designed to make life agreeable for the fully grown, whose convenience dictated their disposal, he had merely shared an assumption massively enacted during that era of history. Deronda, armed with the keen memory of his own painful internal struggle, recalled how utterly excluded Sir Hugo's conception had been from such experiences. Ignorant kindness can inflict cruelty, but to rage against it as if it were direct malice would be an ignorance of its own, a *un_kindness* furthest removed from Deronda's expansive imaginative leniency. Perhaps, after the searching, harrowing scenes of the last ten days, where the curtain had been forcibly lifted on secrets unlike his own, he was more disposed than ever to curb that rashness of indignation or resentment which bears an unpleasant resemblance to the love of punishment.

When Sir Hugo's familiar figure descended from the railway carriage, the lifelong affection that had always been adept at finding excuses surged forth, submerging all newer knowledge that might have served as fresh soil for blame. "Well, Dan," said Sir Hugo, seizing Deronda's hand with a serious fervor. He uttered no further greeting; the current of mutual consciousness ran too strong for words. Next came instructions for the courier, followed by a proposal to walk slowly in the mild evening, for there was no urgency to reach the hotel. "I have taken my journey easily and am in excellent condition," Sir Hugo remarked as they emerged under the starlight, still faintly tinged with the lingering sheen of day. "I did not hasten my departure, wishing to inquire into matters a little; thus, I caught sight of your letter to Lady Mallinger before I started. But now, how fares the widow?"

"Growing calmer," Deronda replied. "She appears to be escaping the physical illness one might have feared following her plunge and terrible excitement. Her uncle and mother arrived two days ago, and she is being well cared for."

"Is there any prospect of an heir being born?"

"From what Mr. Gascoigne told me, I conclude not," Deronda said. "He spoke as if the question were merely whether the widow would retain the estates for her life."

"It will not be much of a wrench to her affections, I fancy, this loss of the husband?" Sir Hugo asked, casting a look around at Deronda.

"The suddenness of the death has been a great blow to her," Deronda answered quietly, deftly sidestepping the deeper implication.

"I wonder whether Grandcourt gave her any notion of the provisions of his will?"

"Do you know what they are, sir?" Deronda parried.

"Yes, I do," the baronet replied promptly. "Good heavens! If there is no prospect of a legitimate heir, he has left everything to a boy he had by a Mrs. Glasher—you know nothing of this affair, I presume? She was, in a manner, his wife for many years, and there are three older children, girls. The boy is to take his father's name; he is already named Henleigh, and henceforth he shall be Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt. The Mallinger fortune will be of no use to him—I am happy to say—but the young dog will have more than enough to sustain him through his fourteen years of minority. No need to have plugged the holes with my fifty thousand for Diplow that he had no right to claim. Meanwhile, my beauty, the young widow, is to endure a paltry two thousand a year and the house at Gadsmere—a nice sort of banishment for her if she chooses to lock herself away there, which I doubt she will. The boy's mother has lived there for recent years. I am perfectly disgusted with Grandcourt. Though, so far as my affairs are concerned, nothing in his life suited him better than leaving it behind."

"In my opinion, he did wrong when he married this wife—not necessarily in leaving his estates to the son," Deronda observed, his tone somewhat dry.

"I say nothing against his leaving the land to the lad," Sir Hugo countered. "But since he had married this girl, he ought to have granted her a handsome provision, sufficient for her to live in a style commensurate with the rank he had raised her to. She deserved four or five thousand a year and the London house for her life; that is what I would have done. I suppose, as she was penniless, her friends could not press for a settlement beforehand; otherwise, it is ill-trusting a man's will made after marriage. Even a wise man generally allows some folly to ooze out of him in his testament—my father did, I know. And if a fellow harbors spite or tyranny, he is likely to bottle up a good deal within such a document. It is quite clear Grandcourt intended his death to serve as an extinguisher for his wife, should she fail to bear him an heir."

"And in the other case, I suppose everything would have been reversed—illegitimacy receiving the extinguisher?" Deronda asked, with a note of scorn.

"Precisely—Gadsmere and the two thousand. It is queer. One annoyance is that Grandcourt has made me an executor; but seeing he was the son of my only brother, I cannot refuse to act. And I shall mind it less if I can be of any use to the widow. Lush suspects she was not in total ignorance regarding the family arrangements under the rose, and the purport of the will. He hints that there was no very good understanding between the couple. But I fancy you are the man who knew most about what Mrs. Grandcourt felt or did not feel—eh, Dan?"

Sir Hugo did not pose this question with his usual jocularity, but rather with a lowered tone of earnest inquiry. Deronda sensed that any evasion would be misconstrued. He answered gravely, "She was certainly not happy. They were unsuited to each other. But as to the disposal of the property—from all I have observed of her, I should predict that she will be quite contented with it."

"Then she is not much like the rest of her sex; that is all I can say," Sir Hugo remarked, giving a slight shrug. "However, she ought to be something extraordinary, for there must be an entanglement between your horoscope and hers—eh? When that tremendous telegram arrived, the first thing Lady Mallinger said was, 'How very strange that it should be Daniel who sends it!' But I have experienced something of the same sort in my own life. Once, at a foreign hotel, I encountered a lady abandoned by her husband without means. Upon hearing of her plight and stepping forward to assist, who should appear but an early flame of mine, foolish enough to marry an Austrian baron with a long mustache and short affection? But it was an affair of my own that brought me there—nothing akin to knight-errantry, any more than your coming to Genoa had to do with the Grandcourts."

A silence fell for a moment. Sir Hugo had begun discussing the Grandcourts as the less perilous topic between them, yet both wished to overcome a reluctance toward absolute frankness regarding the events touching their relationship. Deronda felt that his letter, following the first interview with his mother, had acted more as a thickening of the ice than a breaker, and that he ought to await the first opening from Sir Hugo.

The Roman Emperor, in the legend, put to death ten learned Israelites to avenge the sale of Joseph by his brethren. History remembers the cruelty, yet there have always been enough of his kin—whose piety consists in punishing those who can perceive the justice of grudges but fail to grasp the logic of gratitude. One cannot convince the stronger feeling that it lacks the stronger reason, nor bend the heart that holds no love to admit there is fertile ground for it. As language itself reveals, *love* precedes *lovable*; the impulse must exist before the object can be named.

When Deronda presented his letter at the banking-house on Schuster Strasse in Mainz, requesting Joseph Kalonymos, he was ushered into an inner chamber. Seated at a table scattered with open correspondence was the white-bearded man he had glimpsed the previous year within the Frankfurt synagogue. He wore the same battered felt hat, and beside him rested a packed portmanteau draped with a wrap and overcoat. Upon Deronda's entrance, Kalonymos rose but held his distance, offering no handshake. His gaze, small and penetrating, glittered like black gems set against the yellowish hue of his face and snow-white hair.

"Good!" Kalonymos declared in German. "Now it is you who seek me, young man."

"Yes," Deronda replied, his voice steady despite the tremor in his spirit. "I seek you with gratitude, as a friend of my grandfather's. I owe you a debt for the trouble you have taken on my behalf." He spoke the liberal German tongue with ease, a dialect that embraced strange accents into its maternal bosom.

Kalonymos extended his hand, his grip cordial. "So you are no longer angry at being something more than an Englishman?"

"On the contrary," Deronda said, relief washing over him. "I thank you heartily for saving me from the ignorance of my parentage, and for safeguarding the chest my grandfather left in trust."

"Sit," Kalonymos urged, his tone shifting to a quick, urgent whisper as he resumed his seat and gestured to a chair nearby. Deliberately removing his hat, he revealed a head thick with white hair, stroking and clutching his beard as he examined Deronda's young face with intense scrutiny.

The moment struck Deronda with the force of an electric shock. In the presence of a man still bound by zealous friendship to the grandfather whose hopes had yearned for him before birth—who, though dead, seemed to speak through the written memorials that Milton described as containing "a potency of life... as active as that soul whose progeny they are"—Deronda felt he was touching the living chain of his own ancestry. He endured Kalonymos's searching gaze with a mixture of delighted awe and solemn reverence, akin to witnessing the commemoration of ancient deeds that still profoundly shape the present. Such sensitivity might seem impossible to men of duller fiber, whose affections refuse to diffuse through the vast travel of imagination; yet it existed, stubborn and real, granting Deronda's face an expression of profound satisfaction that pleased the observer.

In Hebrew, Kalonymos quoted from the liturgy: "As thy goodness has been great to the former generations, even so may it be to the latter." After a pause, he continued, "Young man, I rejoice that I was not yet set off again on my travels, and that you have come in time for me to see the image of my friend as he was in his youth—no longer perverted from the fellowship of your people, no longer shrinking in proud wrath from the touch of him who claimed you as a Jew. You come with thankfulness to claim the kindred and heritage that wicked contrivance would have stolen from you. You come with a willing soul to declare, 'I am the grandson of Daniel Charisi.' Is it not so?"

"Assuredly it is," Deronda affirmed.

"But let me say," Kalonymos interjected, "that I should never have treated a Jew with incivility simply because he was a Jew. You can understand why I shrank from telling a stranger, 'I know nothing of my mother.'"

"A sin! A sin!" Kalonymos cried, raising a hand and closing his eyes in visceral disgust. "A robbery of our people—as when our youths and maidens were reared for the Roman Edom. But it is frustrated. I have frustrated it. When Daniel Charisi—may his Rock and his Redeemer guard him!—was a stripling and I a lad little above his shoulder, we made a solemn vow always to be friends. He said, 'Let us bind ourselves with duty, as if we were sons of the same mother.' That was his bent from first to last—to fortify his soul with bonds. He once said, 'Let us bind love with duty; for duty is the love of law; and law is the nature of the Eternal.' So we bound ourselves. Though we drifted apart in later life, the bond remained unbroken. When he died, they sought to rob him; but they could not rob him of me. I rescued that remainder of him which he prized and preserved for his offspring. And I have restored to him the offspring they had stolen."

Kalonymos left the room briefly and returned with a clerk bearing the chest. He set it on the floor, removed a leather covering, and departed again. It was not large, yet weighted by ornamental bracers and handles of gilt iron. The wood was exquisitely incised with Arabic lettering.

"So!" Kalonymos said, returning to his seat. "And here is the curious key," he added, retrieving it from a small leather pouch. "Keep it carefully. I trust you are methodic and wary." He bestowed upon Deronda the monitory, slightly suspicious glance that age often directs when entrusting precious objects to youth.

"I shall be more careful of this than of any other property," Deronda smiled, slipping the key into his breast pocket. "I have never before possessed anything that signifies so much cherished hope and effort. And I shall never forget that this effort was partly yours."

"Do you have time to tell me more of my grandfather? Or am I trespassing by staying longer?"

"Stay yet a while," Kalonymos said, glancing at his watch. "In an hour and eighteen minutes I depart for Trieste, and soon my sons will demand my attention. Shall I introduce you to them, so they may show hospitality to my friend's grandson? They dwell here in ease and luxury, though I prefer the life of a wanderer."

"I shall be glad if you will commend me to their acquaintance for a future opportunity," Deronda replied. "Pressing claims call me to England—friends who desperately need my presence. I have been kept away too long by unforeseen circumstances. But to know more of you and your family would be motive enough to draw me back to Mainz."

"Good! You will hardly find me, for I am beyond three-score years and ten, a wanderer carrying my shroud with me. But my sons and their children dwell here in wealth and unity. The days have changed since Karl the Great brought my ancestors from Italy to impart a tincture of knowledge to our rough German brethren. My generation and ours fought hard for it. Our youth fell on evil days, but we won. We accumulate wealth in safety, and the learning of all Germany is fed and fattened by Jewish brains—even if they do not always retain their Jewish hearts. Have you been left entirely ignorant of your people's life, young man?"

"No," Deronda admitted. "Recently, before I suspected my parentage, I was led to study everything pertaining to their history with more interest than any other subject. It turns out I have been preparing myself to understand my grandfather."

He was anxious lest time be wasted before this circuitous conversation could return to the core of his longing. Age rarely distinguishes between what it needs to express and what youth requires to know; distance seems to level the objects of memory. Yet Kalonymos, though keenly active, possessed an inkstand in the wrong place that would have hindered his imagination from reaching Beirut; he had long united restless travel with punctilious observation. Deronda's final sentence achieved its purpose.

"So—you would perhaps have become such a man as he if your education had not hindered? For you resemble him in features—yet not altogether, young man. He had an iron will in his face; it braced everyone around him. Even quite young, he bore a deep, upright line in his brow. I see none of that in you."

"Daniel Charisi used to say, 'Better a wrong will than a wavering; better a steadfast enemy than an uncertain friend; better a false belief than no belief at all,'" Kalonymos recited. "What he despised most was indifference. He had longer reasons than I can give you."

"Yet his knowledge was not narrow?" Deronda asked, subtly referencing the common excuse for indecision—that it stems from knowing too much.

"Narrow? No," Kalonymos shook his head, a compassionate smile playing on his lips. "From his childhood upward, he drank in learning as easily as a plant sucks up water. But he early turned to medicine and theories concerning life and health. He traveled to many lands, spending much of his substance in seeing and knowing. What he insisted upon was that the strength and wealth of mankind depended on the balance of separateness and communication. He was bitterly opposed to our people losing themselves among the Gentiles. 'It's no better,' he would say, 'than many sorts of grain going back from their variety into sameness.' He mingled all sorts of learning, just like our Arabic writers in the golden age

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the parlor windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Mirah, like the frantic heartbeat of a city indifferent to the quiet suffocations of its women. Inside, the air was thick with the scent of beeswax and damp wool, mirroring the heavy atmosphere that had settled over the Meyrick household since the news of Grandcourt's death. It was a news that caused both violent agitation and a desperate, collective effort to repress it, echoing in another breast besides Rex Gascoigne's.

Hans Meyrick stood in the center of the room, shaking the *Times* aloft with a crackling noise that cut through the silence like a physical blow. He had entered as quietly as usual with his latch-key, intending to deliver the paper during the interlude when Mirah finished teaching Mab her weekly lesson, a moment he claimed was chosen solely because he wished to hear Mirah sing. But today, the paper arrived with a vengeance, interrupting Mab's remote imitation of *Lascia ch'io pianga*. The piano keys fell silent instantly; Mirah, her hands poised over the accompaniment, jumped involuntarily, startled by the thunderous intrusion of the newspaper.

"Oh-o-o, Hans!" Mab cried, her voice trembling. "Why do you bring a more horrible noise than my singing?"

Mrs. Meyrick, the only other soul present, looked up from her seat with anxious eyes. "What on earth is the wonderful news? Anything about Italy? Have the Austrians finally given up Venice?"

"Nothing about Italy, but something *from* Italy," Hans replied, his tone possessing a peculiar cadence that immediately set his mother's mind racing toward interpretations of doom. Consider how some of us behave when an event, seemingly confirming our deepest private fears, arrives wrapped in the guise of tragedy. We lean forward, eager to share our burden with an innocent bystander who hasn't yet embarked on the same ship of despair.

"Not bad?" Mrs. Meyrick asked, her voice tight with worry, her thoughts instantly leaping to Deronda. Mirah's heart, too, had been seized by that same name.

"Not bad for anybody we care much about," Hans said quickly, though his smile did not reach his eyes. "Rather uncommonly lucky, I think. I never knew anybody die conveniently before. Considering what a dear gazelle I am, I am constantly wondering to find myself alive."

"Oh me, Hans!" Mab snapped, impatiently. "If you must talk of yourself, let it be behind your own back. What *is* it that has happened?"

Hans placed the paper before his mother, his finger pressing firmly against a specific paragraph. "Duke Alfonso is drowned, and the Duchess is alive—that's all. But more than all is this: Deronda was at Genoa in the same hotel with them. He saw her brought in by the fishermen who had saved her just in time. It seems they saw her jump in after her husband—a less judicious action than I should have expected of the Duchess. However, Deronda is a lucky fellow indeed, being there to take care of her."

Mirah sank back onto the music stool, her eyelids lowered, her hands tightly clasped in her lap. Mrs. Meyrick handed the paper to Mab, her expression softening into pity. "Poor thing! She must have been fond of her husband to jump in after him."

"It was an inadvertence—a little absence of mind," Hans said, creasing his face roguishly and throwing himself into a chair not far from Mirah. "Who can be fond of a jealous baritone, with freezing glances, always singing asides? That was the husband's role, depend upon it. Nothing can be neater than his getting drowned. The Duchess is at liberty now to marry a man with a fine head of hair, and glances that will melt instead of freezing her. And I shall be invited to the wedding."

At these words, Mirah started upright from her sitting posture. Fixing her eyes on Hans, an angry gleam ignited in them, she spoke in a voice deeply shaken by indignation. "Mr. Hans, you ought not to speak in that way. Mr. Deronda would not like you to speak so. Why will you say he is lucky? Why will you use words of that sort about life and death when what is life to one is death to another? How do you know it would be lucky if he loved Mrs. Grandcourt? It might be a great evil to him. She would take him away from my brother—I know she would. Mr. Deronda would not call that lucky to pierce my brother's heart."

A sudden transformation swept through the room. Mirah's face, usually rich with color, had paled to the hue of Ithuriel's spear, her anger stark and terrifying. Poor Hans sat transfixed, blushing under the weight of her gaze as if he were a girl caught in a storm. Nervously, he stammered, "I am a fool and a brute, and I withdraw every word. I'll go and hang myself like Judas—if it's allowable to mention him." Even in his sorrowful moments, Hans's improvisations retained a trace of drollery, a defense mechanism against the gravity of the situation.

Yet Mirah's anger was not appeased. How could it be? She had burst into indignant speech as creatures in intense pain bite and grind their teeth even through their own flesh, a visceral reaction to make their agony bearable. She said no more, but seated herself at the piano, pressing the sheet of music before her as if hoping to summon the melody to heal the wound.

It was Mab who broke the tension, her voice reflecting the discomfort radiating from her mother. "Mirah is quite right to scold you, Hans. You are always taking Mr. Deronda's name in vain. And it is horrible, joking in that way about his marrying Mrs. Grandcourt. Men's minds must be very black, I think," Mab concluded with much scorn.

"Quite true, my dear," Hans murmured in a low tone, rising and turning on his heel to walk toward the back window, avoiding eye contact.

"We had better go on, Mab; you have not given your full time to the lesson," Mirah said, her voice higher than usual, forcing a return to order. "Will you sing this again, or shall I sing it to you?"

"Oh, please sing it to me," Mab rejoiced, eager to ignore the shadow that had passed over them.

Mirah immediately began *Lascia ch'io pianga*, pouring forth its melodious sobs and cries with a new fullness and energy. Hans paused in his walk, leaning against the mantelpiece, keeping his eyes carefully averted from his mother's. When Mirah struck the final chord and rose, she announced, "I must go home now. Ezra expects me."

She extended her hand silently to Mrs. Meyrick, hesitating slightly, unable to look at her directly, instead of offering the customary kiss. But the little mother drew Mirah's face down to hers, whispering soothingly, "God bless you, my dear." Mirah felt a sharp pang of guilt, realizing she had committed an offense against her mother by angrily rebuking Hans. Mixed with her suffering was a bitter sense of pride, an ingratitude, an unbecoming assertion of superiority. Her friend, however, had divined this compunction.

Meanwhile, Hans had seized his wide-awake, ready to open the door.

"Now, Hans," Mab said, her voice masking a sister's tenderness with cunning, "you are not going to walk home with Mirah. I am sure she would rather not. You are so dreadfully disagreeable to-day."

"I shall go to take care of her, if she does not forbid me," Hans replied, opening the door.

Mirah said nothing. When he had ushered her out and closed the door behind him, he walked by her side, unforbidden. She lacked the courage to speak again, conscious that her words to him had perhaps been unbecomingly severe, yet finding only severer words hidden behind them in her own heart. A crowd of thoughts thrust themselves forward, acting as interpreters for a consciousness that remained stubbornly unchanged within her.

Hans, on his side, possessed a mind equally busy. Mirah's anger had awakened in him a new perception, accompanied by the unpleasant realization that he had been a dolt not to have seen it sooner. He wondered: suppose Mirah's heart were entirely preoccupied with Deronda in a capacity beyond that of her brother's benefactor? The supposition filled his mind with anxieties that, to his credit, were not altogether selfish. He held a strong persuasion, confirmed only by direct evidence to the contrary, that there was a serious attachment between Deronda and Mrs. Grandcourt. He had pieced together fragments of observation, gathering knowledge completed by what his sisters had heard from Anna Gascoigne. This convinced him not only that Mrs. Grandcourt harbored a passion for Deronda, but also, despite his friend's austere self-repression, that Deronda's susceptibility toward her was the sign of concealed love. Some men, holding such a conviction, would avoid allusions capable of rousing that susceptibility; but Hans's talk naturally fluttered toward mischief, driven by a form of experiment on live animals consisting in irritating his friends playfully. His experiments had ended in satisfying him that what he thought likely was true.

On the other hand, any susceptibility Deronda had manifested regarding attentions shown to Mirah, Hans attributed sufficiently to her dependent position. He credited his friend

The German epigraph hung in the air like a prophecy fulfilled: *Fortune is a light harlot, / And loves not to abide in one place.* She had watched for this moment as the realization of a threat, yet here it was, the familiar sorrow returning with the heavy, abundant luggage of a decade's neglect.

Mirah stepped out of Knightsbridge, the evening air cool against her skin. She had just finished singing at a charitable morning concert in a wealthy house, recommended by Klesmer, surrounded by the usual throngs of admirers waiting to catch a glimpse of the departing company. Her dress was simple black, draped with a dust-cloak, rendering her invisible to the prying eyes of the street. Yet, she felt the weight of footsteps keeping a precise, predatory rhythm with her own. Another kind of alarm, sharper than social scrutiny, tightened her chest. She thought instantly of her father. To turn and face him would be to invite a torrent of emotion she had previously deemed intolerable; to ignore him was impossible. She must wait for the compulsion to strike.

She walked on, neither hastening nor slowing, picturing the man behind her as her father with absolute certainty. Alongside this grim visualization came a bitter regret: she had promised Mrs. Meyrick not to conceal his existence. This regret became a driving force, urging her to intercept the inevitable collision before it caused her brother an unnecessary shock. As she approached the entrance of the small square where her home lay, she prepared to turn, to seize control of the encounter. Just as she resolved to act, a presence slipped to her side, a hand grasped her wrist, and a voice, curled with a persuasive accent, whispered, "Mirah!"

She paused instantly, unstartled. It was the voice she feared most, and the eyes she dreaded. Her face remained grave, composed as if facing an executioner, while his expression shifted rapidly between desperation and a calculated attempt at soothing. Once handsome, with vibrant color, his face was now sallow and deeply lined, bearing the peculiar stamp of impudent suavity that arises when one courts favor while simultaneously accepting disrespect. He was lightly built, active, possessing a deceptive vitality that masked his age; he was barely fifty-seven. His attire was shabby, a mirror of his previous appearances. The sight of this irreverent father struck Mirah with a compounded anguish of shame and grief, repulsion and pity—a mixture intensified now that her own world had transformed into one devoid of the comradeship needed to shield him from scorn.

"It is you, father," she said slowly, her voice tremulous.

"Why did you run away from me, child?" he began, his speech rapid, laced with a tone of tender reproof, accompanied by jerky, abbreviated gestures. "What were you afraid of? You knew I never forced you against your will! It was for your sake I broke your engagement in the Vorstadt; I saw it suited you not, and you repaid me by abandoning me to the ruin that followed. I had secured a better position for you at the Dresden theater; I withheld it to surprise you. Instead, you left me stranded, forcing me to vanish to avoid breach of contract. Hard lines for a man who sacrificed everything to secure an education that was to be your fortune! What father devoted himself more to his daughter than I to you? You know how I bore that disappointment in your voice, making the best of it. When I had no one but you, and was breaking down fighting for my livelihood with my brain alone—you chose that moment to leave. Who else did you owe everything to? Where was your gratitude? For what I cared for, I might have died in a ditch."

Lapidoth halted abruptly, not for lack of words, but having reached a pathetic crescendo. He gave a sudden sob, indistinguishable from a woman's, pulling hastily from his pocket an old yellow silk handkerchief. He genuinely believed his daughter had treated him poorly—a sentimentality natural to those who are unscrupulous yet possess a heightened sensitivity to what is owed to them, ignoring any counter-debt. Despite the sob, Mirah possessed the energy to prevent him from deceiving himself. She answered more firmly, using accusing words for the first time.

"You know why I left you, father; and I had reason to distrust you, because I felt sure you had deceived my mother. Had I trusted you, I would have stayed and worked for you."

"I never intended to deceive your mother, Mirah," Lapidoth replied, tucking the handkerchief away, his voice struggling against fresh tears. "I meant to take you back to her, but chance hindered me at the critical moment, and then news of her death arrived. It was better for you that I remain where I was; your brother could care for himself. No one claimed me but you. A particular friend managed affairs for me, and I sent him money for expenses. There is one possibility, however—" he pivoted quickly to guard against an unlikely yet plausible scenario— "that he lied to extract money from me."

Mirah offered no response. She could not bear to speak the only truth: *"I do not believe a word you say."* Instead, she signaled a desire to move forward, fearing that standing still would attract unwanted attention. Even as they walked, their pairing was conspicuous. Mirah, her beauty highlighted by the quiet, careful dignity of an English lady, formed a strange pendant to the shabby, foreign-looking, gesticulating man beside her. His air possessed an ineffaceable jauntiness, perhaps born of his bushy, grizzled curls, his diminutive stature, and his light step.

"You seem to have prospered, Mirah? You show no want," the father observed, examining her with emphatic scrutiny.

"Good friends who found me in distress assisted me in securing work," Mirah replied, her words automatic as she focused on what she must soon confess. "I teach lessons. I sing in private homes. I have just performed at a private concert." She paused, adding with significant gravity, "I have very good friends who know all about me."

"And you would be ashamed if they saw your father in this state? No wonder. I came to England with no prospects save the hope of finding you. It was a mad quest; but a father's heart is superstitious—it feels drawn like a loadstone. I might have flourished abroad; without you to burden me, I could have rolled or settled as easily as a ball. But loneliness breaks a spirit. I hoped my little Mirah would repent leaving her father when she looked back. I've suffered a sharp pinch to survive; I cannot foresee my next fall. Talents like mine are useless here. When a man is reduced to rags, no one believes in him. I could not secure decent employment with my appearance. I've been driven to depths for mere pennies."

Mirah's anxiety surged at the image of her father sinking further into degradation, a fate she felt bound to prevent. Before she could respond to his fluent, rote-like stream of inventions, he asked promptly, "Where do you live, Mirah?"

"Here, in this square. Not far from the house."

"In lodgings?"

"Yes."

"Anyone to care for you?"

"Yes," Mirah repeated, locking eyes with his keen, searching gaze. "My brother."

The father's eyelids fluttered as if struck by lightning; his shoulders twitched. After a perceptible pause, he asked, "Ezra? How did you know—how did you find him?"

"That requires a long explanation. We are at the door. My brother would not wish me to shut it on you."

Mirah stood on the doorstep, her face turned toward her father below on the pavement. Her heart hammered with the anticipation of confronting Ezra. In this posture of granting permission to the father she had once obeyed, seeing him shrink beneath the indirect demand for entry, she felt a pang of peculiar, sympathetic humiliation—the stabbed heart of reverence belonging to a nature intensely filial.

"Stay a minute, *Liebchen*," Lapidoth said in a lowered tone. "What sort of man has Ezra become?"

"A good man—a wonderful man," Mirah answered, emphasizing each word slowly as she struggled to master the agitation shaking her voice. She felt compelled to prepare him for the total revelation awaiting him. "But he was very poor when my friends discovered him—a poor workman. Twelve years ago, he was strong and happy, planning to go to the East, a place he loved to dream of. My mother called him back because... because she had lost me. He went to her, caring for her through immense suffering, working until she died in grief. Ezra, too, lost his health and strength. The cold seized him on his return, for she was forsaken. For years he has weakened, always poor, always working, yet filled with knowledge and greatness of mind. All who approach him honor him. To stand before him is like standing before a prophet of God." She ended with difficulty, her heart throbbing, "Falsehoods are no use."

She cast down her eyes to avoid witnessing the ignoble look of frustration gathering in his face. Yet, he was swift in invention and decision.

"Mirah, *Liebchen*," he cooed in his old, caressing manner, "would you not like me to appear slightly more respectable before my son sees me? With a little sum of money, I could outfit myself properly and return home as a father should. Then I could seek decent employment. With a good shirt and coat, people would welcome me. I could offer myself as a courier if I did not resemble a broken-down mounteb

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Mirah, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of London itself, utterly deaf to the quiet suffocations of its women. Inside, the air was thick with the scent of beeswax and damp wool, heavy with the residue of a day that had ended in silence and shared grief.
