Mordecai sat motionless, his head thrown back, eyes sealed shut, breathing with the labored difficulty of a man whose soul seemed to be straining against the walls of a prison cell. Beside him, Mirah remained frozen, her hand interlocked with his, her face pale as moonlight, her curls disheveled as if she had just awakened from a fever dream. The scattered papers on the floor lay in chaotic heaps, forgotten artifacts of a sudden collapse, while the ticking of the mantel-clock marked the slow erosion of twilight into a darkness that felt less like night and more like a final curtain falling.

In this suspended moment of despair, the door swung open, and the sound of a familiar voice cut through the gloom like a sudden ray of sunlight breaking through storm clouds. "Daniel Deronda—may he come in?"

"Come! Come!" Mordecai cried, his voice transforming instantly from a dying whisper to a radiant command. He rose, his face illuminated by an inner fire that banished the shadows of his exhaustion, his eyes wide and expectant as if he had awaited this visitor since dawn. Mirah startled, a flush of confused alarm rising to her cheeks, but the tension in the room shattered the moment Deronda stepped forward.

As he approached, he extended his right hand to Mirah, standing close to her brother's left, while his other hand settled firmly on Mordecai's broad shoulder. For a heartbeat, he held them both, silent and steady, reading the turmoil on their faces until he spoke, his voice tight with concern. "Has anything happened? Any trouble?"

"Speak not of trouble now," Mordecai replied, shielding Mirah from the necessity of answering. "There is joy in your face—let the joy be ours."

Mirah thought, *It is for something he cannot tell us.* Yet, as they took their seats, the atmosphere shifted with the weight of a revelation. Deronda drew a chair close before Mordecai, his demeanor carrying the gravity of a man stepping onto a stage where destiny awaited.

"That is true," he said, his tone emphatic, resonating with a newfound certainty. "I have a joy which will remain to us even in the worst trouble. I did not tell you the reason of my journey abroad, Mordecai, because... never mind. I went to learn my parentage. And you were right. I am a Jew."

The declaration hung in the air, a spark igniting dry tinder. The two men clasped hands in a gesture that seemed to carry the static charge of lightning, passing through Mirah like an electric shock that rewired her understanding of the world. Deronda continued, his voice speaking from Mordecai's mind as much as his own, bridging the gap between their souls. "We have the same people. Our souls have the same vocation. We shall not be separated by life or by death."

Mordecai's response emerged in Hebrew, barely a whisper yet vibrating with liturgical power: "Our God and the God of our fathers."

The weight of emotion pressed down on the room, rendering speech unnecessary. Mirah sank to her knees beside her brother, her gaze fixed on his face, which had transformed from deathly stillness to glowing vitality. The movement was an involuntary eruption of relief, a physical release from the crushing burden of despondency, solemn as a religious rite. In that instant, her own future receded; she lived solely through the restoration of her brother's spirit.

"And it is not only that I am a Jew," Deronda went on, basking in the clarity of a truth finally aligned with his actions. "But I come of a strain that has ardently maintained the fellowship of our race—a line of Spanish Jews that has borne many students and men of practical power. And I possess what will give us a sort of communion with them. My grandfather, Daniel Charisi, preserved manuscripts, family records stretching far back, in the hope that they would pass into the hands of his grandson. And now his hope is fulfilled, in spite of attempts to thwart it by hiding my parentage from me. I possess the chest containing them, with his own papers, and it is down below in this house. I mean to leave it with you, Mordecai, that you may help me to study the manuscripts. Some of them I can read easily enough—those in Spanish and Italian. Others are in Hebrew, and, I think, Arabic; but there seem to be Latin translations. I was only able to look at them cursorily while I stayed at Mainz. We will study them together."

Deronda concluded with a bright smile, a beacon cutting through the habitual gravity of his countenance, revealing a man fully awake to his own potential. But when this warm glance drifted from Mordecai to rest upon Mirah, it carried the intensity of too much sunshine, forcing her to shift her posture. She had knelt under an impulse so pure that any personal embarrassment felt incongruous, yet the sudden awareness of Mrs. Grandcourt's looming presence stirred unease within her. Thoughts of how her guardian might react to this new aspect of things flushed her cheeks, compelling her to rise and resume her usual pose of crossed hands and feet, striving for a quietude that masked the turbulence beneath.

Deronda, equally perceptive, sensed that the raw emotion flooding his eyes might be repugnant to her, fearing that any unexpected manifestation of passion could spoil the delicate balance of their relationship. He worried that if Mirah could harbor no love for him, any advance on his part would render their inevitable proximity a source of mutual misery. While these currents pulsed rapidly between them, Mordecai, seeing only a blessed fulfillment in his friend's presence, began to speak with his characteristic expansiveness.

"Daniel, from the first, I have said to you, we know not all the pathways. Has there not been a meeting among them, as of the operations in one soul, where an idea being born and breathing draws the elements toward it, and is fed and glows? For all things are bound together in that Omnipresence which is the place and habitation of the world, and events are of a glass wherethrough our eyes see some of the pathways. And if it seems that the erring and unloving wills of men have helped to prepare you, as Moses was prepared, to serve your people the better, that depends on another order than the law which must guide our footsteps. For the evil will of man makes not a people's good except by stirring the righteous will of man; and beneath all the clouds with which our thought encompasses the Eternal, this is clear—that a people can be blessed only by having counsellors and a multitude whose will moves in obedience to the laws of justice and love. For see, now, it was your loving will that made a chief pathway, and resisted the effect of evil; for, by performing the duties of brotherhood to my sister, and seeking out her brother in the flesh, your soul has been prepared to receive with gladness this message of the Eternal, 'behold the multitude of your brethren.'"

"It is quite true that you and Mirah have been my teachers," Deronda replied, his voice steady. "If this revelation had been made to me before I knew you both, I think my mind would have rebelled against it. Perhaps I should have felt then—'If I could have chosen, I would not have been a Jew.' What I feel now is—that my whole being is a consent to the fact. But it has been the gradual accord between your mind and mine which has brought about that full consent."

As he spoke, the memory of their first evening in the bookshop surfaced vividly, recalling the aloofness he had then felt from Mordecai's prophetic confidence. Yet now, driven by a nature that delighted in satisfying the eagerly expectant soul, he leaned forward, his eyes alight with the fervor of a man who had finally found his place in the grand design.

The mountain Dante described, heavy at the base yet growing lighter with every ascent, became Gwendolen's metaphor for her current exile. It was scarcely a week since her mother's return that Gwendolen yielded to the prospect of staying in Genoa. Paradoxically, the desire to flee that jewel of the sea rallied her fractured spirit; for what location, however verdant the valley of Enna, does not transform under the inward eye into a ring of torment where blossoms burn like tongues of fire against the soles of one's feet?

"I shall never look upon the Mediterranean again," Gwendolen confessed to her mother, who perceived the depth of her daughter's aversion even without the explicit mention of her late husband.

Mrs. Davilow, though formally bound to regard this season as one of catastrophic loss, found herself experiencing life with a vigor she had not known since Gwendolen's marriage. It appeared her beloved daughter had returned not merely with the old affection, but with a conscious, possessive cherishing of her mother's proximity, akin to clutching a treasure nearly lost forever.

"Are you there, Mamma?" Gwendolen cried out in the dead of night, a bed having been prepared for her mother beside her own. She spoke with the vulnerability of a frightened child in her early girlhood, needing the anchor of presence.

"Yes, dear; can I help you?"

"No, thank you; I simply like to know you are there. Do you mind my waking you?" This question, however, bore none of the innocence of her youth.

"I was not asleep, darling."

"It didn't seem real that you were with me. I wanted to make it real. I can endure anything if you are here. But you must not lie awake, anxious for me. You must be happy now. You must let me make you happy at last—or else, what shall I do?"

"God bless you, dear; I possess the greatest happiness possible when you hold me in such high regard."

Yet the following night, hearing her mother sighing and restless, Mrs. Davilow offered, "Let me give you your sleeping draught, Gwendolen."

"No, Mamma, thank you; I do not wish to sleep."

"It would be so beneficial for you to rest more, my darling."

"Do not speak of what *should* be good for me, Mamma," Gwendolen retorted, her voice impetuous. "You do not know what is good for me. Neither you nor Uncle Hugo must contradict me or dictate what benefits me when I feel it brings harm."

Mrs. Davilow fell silent, unsurprised that the poor child was becoming irritable. Soon, Gwendolen whispered, "I was always naughty to you, Mamma."

"No, dear, no."

"Yes, I was," Gwendolen insisted, her voice rising. "It is precisely because I was always wicked that I am miserable now."

She erupted into sobs and cries. The desperate determination to remain silent regarding the grim facts of her married life and its sudden end had reacted violently, manifesting in these bursts of enigmatic distress.

However, faint lights of interpretation began to break upon the mother's mind through information relayed from Sir Hugo to Mr. Gascoigne, and subsequently, with selective omissions, to herself. The kindly baronet, while overseeing the proper dignities surrounding his nephew's death and the possibility of recovering the body, deemed it the kindest act to utilize his cordial relationship with the rector to communicate the substance of Grandcourt's will. His aim was to spare the clergyman the additional shock of returning home with illusions intact. Perhaps Sir Hugo would have shared the news regardless of this motive, but he genuinely felt driven by it. He broke the unwelcome tidings gradually: first implying his fear that the widow was not as generously provided for as Mr. Gascoigne, or indeed as the baronet himself had anticipated; only finally, after vague references to significant claims on Grandcourt's fortune, did he reveal the prior relations that, in the unfortunate absence of a legitimate heir, had redirected all the wealth elsewhere.

The rector was deeply wounded, remembering with renewed vividness how offensively proud and repellent the deceased's manners had been toward him. He recalled also receiving hints during that transitional period before the new occupant arrived at Diplow regarding the late gentleman's former entanglements and an excessive addiction to pleasure, though he had not foresaw that the pleasures, presumably swept into private refuse heaps, would ever resurface as a swarm of live caterpillars devouring the green flesh of respectable society. Yet, he did not voice these retrospective thoughts to Sir Hugo, nor did he lower himself by expressing indignation on purely personal grounds. Instead, he conducted himself as a worldly man who had become a conscientious clergyman. His opening remark was, "When a young man makes his will in health, he usually counts on living a long while. Probably Mr. Grandcourt did not believe this document would ever have its present effect." After a pause, he added, "The effect is painful in more ways than one. Female morality is likely to suffer from this marked advantage and prominence being given to illegitimate offspring."

"Well, in point of fact," said Sir Hugo, in his comforting manner, "since the boy is there, this was truly the best alternative for disposing of the estates. Grandcourt had no one nearer than his cousin. And it is a chilling thought that you leave this life solely for the benefit of a cousin. A man derives a small pleasure in making a will if it benefits his own 'curly heads'; but it becomes a nuisance when you are bequeathing everything to a used-up fellow like yourself, someone you do not care two straws for. It is the next worst thing to retaining only a life interest in your estates. No; I forgive Grandcourt for that portion of his will. But, between ourselves, what I cannot forgive him for is the shabby manner in which he has provided for your niece—our niece, I must say—not a position superior to that of a doctor's widow. Nothing grates on me more than that posthumous stinginess toward a wife. A man ought to possess some pride and fondness for his widow. *I* certainly do. I consider it a test of a man's character that he feels at ease about his own death when he can envision his wife and daughters comfortable thereafter. I recall those fellows in the Crimean War who were willing to sink to the bottom of the sea if their widows were provided for."

"It has certainly surprised me," admitted Mr. Gascoigne, "all the more so because, standing in the place of a father to my niece, I had demonstrated my reliance on Mr. Grandcourt's apparent liberality by making no prior claims for her. That seemed to me the only honorable course under the circumstances. Probably you deem me blameworthy."

"Not blamable exactly. I respect a man for trusting another. But take my advice. If you marry another niece, though it be to the Archbishop of Canterbury, bind him down legally. Your niece cannot be married for the first time twice over. And if he is a good fellow, he will wish to be bound. As for Mrs. Grandcourt, I can only say that I feel my connection to her drawn closer because I believe she has not been well-treated. And I hope you will urge her to rely on me as a friend."

Thus spoke the chivalrous Sir Hugo, disgusted that the young and beautiful widow of a Mallinger Grandcourt was left with merely two thousand pounds a year and a residence in a coal-mining district. To the rector, that income appeared less shabby and devoid of mortifying privations; yet in this conversation, he consumed a much sharper sense of the humiliation cast upon his niece—and her closest confidants—by the public revelation of her husband's liaison with Mrs. Glasher. Like all men who are devoted husbands and fathers, he felt this humiliation vicariously through the minds of the women primarily affected by it. Consequently, the initial annoyance of hearing the facts was far outweighed by the pain of communicating them to Mrs. Davilow and anticipating Gwendolen's reaction whenever her mother deemed it appropriate to disclose them. For the good rector held an innocent conviction that his niece was unaware of Mrs. Glasher's existence, reasoning with masculine logic from what maidens and wives typically know, do, and suffer, despite having possessed a most imperfect observation of the specific maiden and wife in question.

Not so Gwendolen's mother. Now, she believed she saw an explanation for much that had remained enigmatic in her child's conduct and words before and after the engagement. She concluded that in some inconceivable manner, Gwendolen had been informed of this clandestine marriage and the existence of the children. She trusted that opportunities would arise during moments of affectionate confidence before and during their journey to England, allowing her to gradually discern how fully the truth was clear to Gwendolen and to prepare her for whatever disappointments awaited. But she was spared the burden of devising strategies on the subject.

"I hope you do not expect that I am going to be rich and grand, Mamma," Gwendolen said, not long after the rector's communication. She had been dressed and sat for a long time in quiet meditation.

Mrs. Davilow was startled but replied, after a moment's reflection, "Oh yes, dear, you will have something. Sir Hugo knows all about the will."

"That will not decide," Gwendolen stated abruptly.

"Surely, dear: Sir Hugo says you are to receive two thousand a year and the house at Gadsmere."

The rain lashed against the leaded glass of the library windows, a rhythmic drumming that felt, to Gwendolen Harleth, like the frantic, indifferent heartbeat of London itself. She sat at her mahogany desk, the heavy scent of beeswax and damp wool filling the room, her gaze fixed upon the thick sheaf of letters that had arrived by the morning post. They were from Daniel Deronda, his sprawling, confident script detailing his latest thoughts on literature and duty.

Gwendolen sighed, tracing the edge of the parchment with a silver letter-opener. Deronda was a man of undeniable erudition, yet his letters were suffocated by the very patriarchal complacency she so deeply despised. He wrote to her of Virgil, of modern English poetry, of Fielding and Rochefoucauld, positioning himself as the benevolent instructor to her "undefeated resolution to admire only the right things."

"The right things," she murmured aloud, the words tasting like ash. What he meant, of course, was the established canon—the texts elevated by centuries of wealthy, privileged men who saw their own reflections in the exploits of conquerors and the musings of comfortable philosophers.

She turned to his pages on Fielding. Deronda excused the novelist's coarse morality, arguing that men must be pardoned the "vices of young blood" so long as they were brave and generous. He painted a nostalgic picture of a "lusty old militant world," entirely blind to the fact that this merry England was built upon the bruised backs of women and the indentured silence of the poor. He dismissed the systemic inequalities that left women like her with no recourse but to forgive the betrayals of their husbands.

But the door opened, cutting through her reverie. Deronda had come.

He awaited her in the back drawing-room—part of that white and crimson space where they had sat together at the musical party, where Gwendolen had said for the first time that her lot depended on his not forsaking her, and her appeal had seemed to melt into the melodic cry—*Per pietà non dirmi addio*. But the melody had come from Mirah's dear voice.

Deronda walked about this room, which he had for years known by heart, with a strange sense of metamorphosis in his own life. The familiar objects around him, from Lady Mallinger's gently smiling portrait to the also human and urbane faces of the lions on the pilasters of the chimney-piece, seemed almost to belong to a previous state of existence which he was revisiting in memory only, not in reality; so deep and transforming had been the impressions he had lately experienced, so new were the conditions under which he found himself in the house he had been accustomed to think of as a home—standing with his hat in his hand awaiting the entrance of a young creature whose life had also been undergoing a transformation—a tragic transformation toward a wavering result, in which he felt with apprehensiveness that his own action was still bound up.

But Gwendolen was come in, looking changed; not only by her mourning dress, but by a more satisfied quietude of expression than he had seen in her face at Genoa. Her satisfaction was that Deronda was there; but there was no smile between them as they met and clasped hands; each was full of remembrance—full of anxious prevision. She said, "It was good of you to come. Let us sit down," immediately seating herself in the nearest chair. He placed himself opposite to her.

"I asked you to come because I want you to tell me what I ought to do," she began, at once. "Don't be afraid of telling me what you think is right, because it seems hard. I have made up my mind to do it. I was afraid once of being poor; I could not bear to think of being under other people; and that was why I did something—why I married. I have borne worse things now. I think I could bear to be poor, if you think I ought. Do you know about my husband's will?"

"Yes, Sir Hugo told me," said Deronda, already guessing the question she had to ask.

"Ought I to take anything he has left me? I will tell you what I have been thinking," said Gwendolen, with a more nervous eagerness. "Perhaps you may not quite know that I really did think a good deal about my mother when I married. I *was* selfish, but I did love her, and feel about her poverty; and what comforted me most at first, when I was miserable, was her being better off because I had married. The thing that would be hardest to me now would be to see her in poverty again; and I have been thinking that if I took enough to provide for her, and no more—nothing for myself—it would not be wrong; for I was very precious to my mother—and he took me from her—and he meant—and if she had known—"

Gwendolen broke off. She had been preparing herself for this interview by thinking of hardly anything else than this question of right toward her mother; but the question had carried with it thoughts and reasons which it was impossible for her to utter, and these perilous remembrances swarmed between her words, making her speech more and more agitated and tremulous. She looked down helplessly at her hands, now unladen of all rings except her wedding-ring.

"Do not hurt yourself by speaking of that," said Deronda, tenderly. "There is no need; the case is very simple. I think I can hardly judge wrongly about it. You consult me because I am the only person to whom you have confided the most painful part of your experience: and I can understand your scruples." He did not go on immediately, waiting for her to recover herself. The silence seemed to Gwendolen full of the tenderness that she heard in his voice, and she had courage to lift up her eyes and look at him as he said, "You are conscious of something which you feel to be a crime toward one who is dead. You think that you have forfeited all claim as a wife. You shrink from taking what was his. You want to keep yourself from profiting by his death. Your feeling even urges you to some self-punishment—some scourging of the self that disobeyed your better will—the will that struggled against temptation. I have known something of that myself. Do I understand you?"

"Yes—at least, I want to be good—not like what I have been," said Gwendolen. "I will try to bear what you think I ought to bear. I have tried to tell you the worst about myself. What ought I to do?"

"If no one but yourself were concerned in this question of income," said Deronda, "I should hardly dare to urge you against any remorseful prompting; but I take as a guide now, your feeling about Mrs. Davilow, which seems to me quite just. I cannot think that your husband's dues even to yourself are nullified by any act you have committed. He voluntarily entered into your life, and affected its course in what is always the most momentous way. But setting that aside, it was due from him in his position that he should provide for your mother, and he of course understood that if this will took effect she would share the provision he had made for you."

"She has had eight hundred a year. What I thought of was to take that and leave the rest," said Gwendolen. She had been so long inwardly arguing for this as a permission, that her mind could not at once take another attitude.

"I think it is not your duty to fix a limit in that way," said Deronda. "You would be making a painful enigma for Mrs. Davilow; an income from which you shut yourself out must be embittered to her. And your own course would become too difficult. We agreed at Genoa that the burden on your conscience is one what no one ought to be admitted to the knowledge of. The future beneficence of your life will be best furthered by your saving all others from the pain of that knowledge. In my opinion you ought simply to abide by the provisions of your husband's will, and let your remorse tell only on the use that you will make of your monetary independence."

In uttering the last sentence Deronda automatically took up his hat which he had laid on the floor beside him. Gwendolen, sensitive to his slightest movement, felt her heart giving a great leap, as if it too had a consciousness of its own, and would hinder him from going: in the same moment she rose from her chair, unable to reflect that the movement was an acceptance of his apparent intention to leave her; and Deronda, of course, also rose, advancing a little.

"I will do what you tell me," said Gwendolen, hurriedly; "but what else shall I do?" No other than these simple words were possible to her; and even these were too much for her in a state of emotion where her proud secrecy was disenthroned: as the childlike sentences fell from her lips they reacted on her like a picture of her own helplessness, and she could not check the sob which sent the large tears to her eyes. Deronda, too, felt a crushing pain; but imminent consequences were visible to him, and urged him to the utmost exertion of conscience. When she had pressed her tears away, he said, in a gently questioning tone,

"You will probably be soon going with Mrs. Davilow into the country."

"Yes, in a week or ten days." Gwendolen waited an instant, turning her eyes vaguely toward the window, as if looking at some imagined prospect. "I want to be kind to them all—they

Meanwhile, Ezra and Mirah, figures whom Gwendolen had effectively erased from her mental calculus regarding Deronda, found their orbit tightening around him, illuminated with a clarity that cut through the fog of her indifference. Outside, the father Lapidoth had abandoned his daughter at the threshold, seized by a sudden, desperate gamble born of the need to convert mere survival into a wager. Possessing a sum exceeding the cost of immediate starvation, he felt no weight of consequence, no calculation of alternatives. Until total ruin, he had never weighed whether to seek Mirah again or face his son; in that initial panic, he recoiled from Ezra as one might shrink from any cage of social discomfort. Yet, the possession of Mirah's purse was sufficient to exorcise the specter of future want.

The gambling instinct is a tyrant more absolute than bodily hunger, which can be sated by distraction or intellect; the craving to watch the dice roll, to hold the mind in a perpetual suspension between chance and outcome, renders all other excitations inert. In its final, demonic phase, it becomes a joyless dissipation, demons seeking diversion on the burning clay of damnation. Yet even this abstract selfishness demands sustenance. Though Lapidoth's appetite for food and drink was modest, he had slid into a shabby existence where hunger required liquid capital. When, amidst the "Pyramids" advertised on a window-blind, he had doubled, trebled, and then lost Mirah's thirty shillings, he emerged with her empty purse in his pocket. Already, his mind balanced the scales: pawn the purse for an immediate stake, or return it to her with a restored countenance, claiming the funds settled a debt owed to him.

Among the sensibilities that remained potent in Lapidoth was the belief in his own claims—a conviction that his rights to his children's property superseded the justice of his son's resentment. After all, lodging with his offspring was the optimal solution. The more he contemplated meeting Ezra, the less he flinched; his imagination was intoxicated by the prospect of effortless gain, overshadowed by the threat of private humiliation. Luck had recently conspired against him, yet he awaited its reversal, hoping it might manifest through his daughter's affairs or the benefactors she had mentioned. Lapidoth banked on the allure of his own cleverness, an old habit sanctified by early experience; it is not only women who fail to perceive their fading charm or pretend not to be exhausted.

His rapid calculations led him to the small square in Brompton, hoping that pacing and watching might reveal Mirah entering or exiting, thereby smoothing his entry into the house. But evening had fallen—the twilight following the day of his first encounter with her. Weariness soon prompted him to consider ringing the bell, asking for her expected arrival time. Yet, approaching the house, he sensed her presence; he heard her singing. Inside, Mirah sat at the piano, pouring forth *"Herz, mein Herz,"* while Ezra listened with eyes closed. Suddenly, Mrs. Adam opened the door, her voice tight with embarrassment: "A gentleman below says he is your father, miss."

"I will go down to him," Mirah started, turning immediately to glance at her brother.

"No, Mirah, not so," Ezra replied, his decision absolute. "Let him come up, Mrs. Adam."

Mirah stood frozen, fingers pinching her palms, nausea rising in her stomach as she stared at Ezra, who had also risen, visibly shaken. Yet, there was an expression on his face she had never witnessed before: brows knitted, lips hardened with a severity that mirrored the cold fire in his eyes. When Mrs. Adam admitted the father, she could not help casting a glance at the tableau. Glancing from the younger to the elder man, she whispered to herself as she closed the door, "Father, sure enough."

The resemblance was one of outline, striking in its immediacy, though the expression had been carved into stark contrast by hidden, inconspicuous differences that transformed the genius of a Cromwell within the mold of a respectable parishioner. Lapidoth had preened a melancholy demeanor, yet a genuine wince twisted his frame as he spoke, "Well, Ezra, my boy, you hardly know me after so many years."

"I know you—too well—father," Ezra returned, his tone a slow, biting solemnity that rendered the word *father* a reproach.

"Ah, you are not pleased with me. I don't wonder. Appearances have been against me. When a man falls into straits, he cannot act as he pleases... I've suffered enough, I know," Lapidoth said quickly. In speech, he always regained a slickness and hardness. Turning to Mirah, he extended her purse. "Here's your little purse, my dear. I thought you'd be anxious about that note. I've emptied it; I had a score to pay for food and lodging. I knew you'd prefer I cleared myself. Here I stand—without a single farthing in my pocket—at the mercy of my children. You can turn me out if you like, without calling a policeman. Say the word, Mirah; say, 'Father, I've had enough of you; you made a pet of me, spending your all when I needed you, but I can do better without you now.' Say that, and I'm gone, out like a spark. I shan't spoil your pleasure again." Tears welled in his voice before he finished.

"You know I could never say it, father," Mirah answered, her anguish undiminished by the falsity of his words, save for the underlying wish to keep him in the house.

"Mirah, my sister, leave us!" Ezra commanded, his tone authoritative.

She looked at her brother falteringly, beseechingly, awestruck by his resolve, yet unable to depart without pleading for this father who felt like something grown painfully within her own flesh. Moving close, she took his hand, whispering low, though not unheard by Lapidoth, "Remember, Ezra—you said my mother would not have shut him out."

"Trust me, and go," Ezra said.

She retreated, but after ascending a few steps, she collapsed onto a seat, her heart pounding. If her brother's words drove him away, Lapidoth possessed a dim sense of the preparation awaiting him, yet he began to adjust, seeking a viewpoint of cool superiority against any humiliation. This haggard son, speaking from a sepulcher, displayed the incongruity that selfish levity recognizes in suffering, until the unrelenting pincers of disease grasp its own flesh. Any sermon delivered must be accepted as routine, like a passerby ignoring religious wailing in an open cathedral. Lapidoth was not born callous; he had manufactured it.

"This home that we have here," Ezra began, "is maintained partly by the generosity of a beloved friend who supports me, and partly by the labors of my sister, who supports herself. While we have a home, we will not shut you out. We will not cast you to the mercy of your vices. For you are our father, and though you have broken your bond, we acknowledge ours. But I will never trust you. You absconded with money, leaving debts unpaid; you forsook my mother; you robbed her of her child and broke her heart; you became a gambler, and where shame and conscience once sat, now sits an insatiable desire. You were ready to sell my sister—you had sold her, but the price was denied you. A man who has done these things must never expect trust again. We will share our food with you; you shall have a bed and clothing. We perform this duty because you are our father. But you will never be trusted. You are an evil man; you made the misery of our mother. That such a man is our father is a brand on our flesh that will never cease to sting. But the Eternal has laid it upon us; and though human justice were to flog you for crimes, and your body fell helpless before public scorn, we would still say, 'This is our father; make way, that we may carry him out of your sight.'"

Lapidoth, adjusting to the storm, had not foreseen its exact intensity or trajectory—that it would strike not around him, but through him. He could not anticipate this voice from the soul of his son, touching that spring of hysterical excitability Mirah had often witnessed when he sat at home sobbing. As Ezra concluded, Lapidoth threw himself into a chair and wept like a woman, burying his face against the table. Strangely, while this hysterical crying was an inevitable reaction to his son's words, it was also a conscious resource, a tool he had honed since youth to blunt resentment and disapproval.

Ezra sat silent, exhausted by the shock of his own irrepressible utterance, the outpouring of feelings he had carried in solitude for years. His thin hands trembled on the chair arms; he would hardly have found the strength to answer a question, feeling as though he had taken a step toward beckoning Death.

Just then, Mirah's keen ear caught a sound her heart recognized. She could not remain absent. Opening the door, her immediate alarm was for Ezra, rushing to his side to take his trembling hand, which he pressed, finding support in it, though he neither spoke nor looked at her. The father, face buried, was aware of her entry. Presently, he lifted his head, pressed a handkerchief to his eyes, reached out a hand toward her, and spoke with plaintive hoarseness, "Good-b

The divine spark within us twists our noblest actions into knots of reluctance, binding our highest aspirations to our most stubborn selves. When Deronda returned from the Abbey, he found waiting for him at the Brompton lodgings a figure he had hoped to avoid: the unwanted father, now firmly installed in their sanctuary. Mirah had deemed it necessary to introduce her father to Deronda, weaving a narrative of their acquaintance that carefully skirted the precipice of truth. She spoke of Ezra's influence and the gentle sympathy that had drawn them together, but she passed lightly over Deronda's tangible aid, omitting entirely the harrowing rescue from drowning. Instead, she framed her safety as a gift from the Meyricks, allowing her father to believe that Deronda's interest stemmed solely from his proximity to these respectable friends. She could not force herself to reveal the full extent of her bond with Deronda; she could not allow the wind of her father's judgment to blow directly upon the fragile architecture of her relationship. Consequently, Lapidoth, driven by his own peculiar motivations, showed little eagerness to probe the details of her flight or her arrival in England. Yet, he displayed a keen, calculating interest in the mere fact that his children possessed a benefactor seemingly elevated in the social hierarchy.

It was Ezra's brother who delivered the news of this new, burdensome addition to their household. "I have grown calm in observing him now," Ezra concluded, his voice steady despite the turmoil beneath. "I strive to believe that my sister's tenderness, and the daily savoring of a peaceful existence, may eventually win him to stand aloof from further temptation. I have instructed her, and she has vowed, to entrust him with no funds. I have persuaded her that he will purchase his own ruin with any wealth he acquires."

Deronda first appeared three days after Lapidoth's arrival. His newly tailored garments were not yet ready, and, wishing to project an image of propriety, he refused to present himself in his old attire. Watching him depart from the window, Lapidoth was struck by a surprising youthfulness—a trait Mirah had omitted, something Deronda had instinctively categorized as impossible for a man engaged in such grave friendships and scholarly pursuits with the somber Ezra. Lapidoth began to speculate that Deronda's primary motivation must be a romantic attachment to Mirah. Such a notion pleased him; a connection to Mirah held far more promise of leniency for her father than a bond with Ezra alone. Lapidoth harbored hopes of securing Deronda's goodwill, of softening any hardened prejudices he might harbor. He conducted himself with studied amiability, striving in every conceivable manner to integrate smoothly into his children's domestic sphere. He immersed himself in Mirah's music, played the role of a docile smoker (though Mrs. Adam forbade it in her drawing room), and took walks in the square with his German pipe, fueled by the tobacco Mirah kindly supplied. Too astute to challenge openly the refusal of financial assistance—something Mirah insisted was a solemn duty owed to her brother—he contented himself with waiting.

On his subsequent visit, Lapidoth, freshly dressed and satisfied with his reflection, joined Ezra in the room. Ezra, performing a rigorous penance, was attempting to endure his father's presence whenever it was imposed. Deronda met this reunion with cold distance; the sight of the man who had blighted the lives of his wife and children provoked in him a repulsion that bordered on physical nausea. Undeterred, Lapidoth requested permission to remain, listening to the reading of documents from an old chest, and even proved useful by assisting in deciphering a difficult German manuscript. This collaboration led him to propose transcribing the document, offering his services alongside copying papers in Roman characters. While Ezra's youthful eyes were already failing, Lapidoth's remained sharp. Deronda accepted the offer, interpreting it as a sign of grace in Lapidoth's willingness to be employable, and noted the gratified expression on Ezra's face. However, Ezra soon interjected, "Let all the writing be done here; I cannot trust the papers out of my sight, lest there be an accident by fire or otherwise." Poor Ezra felt as though he were guarding a convict on temporary leave. Unless he witnessed his father at work, he could not believe in the sincerity of that labor. By this arrangement, Ezra shackled himself to the burden of his father's presence, a weight made agonizing not only by their shared, painful history but also by Lapidoth's restless temperament. As he became accustomed to his situation, Lapidoth shed any lingering awe for his son. In truth, Ezra was imposing a severe constraint upon himself, restricting his attention solely to win Deronda's favor. Like a man trapped in ill-fitting clothing, he sought relief at every opportunity: stepping out to smoke, pacing the floor, collapsing into his chair in silence, yet constantly engaging in a mute language of facial contortions and gestures. If Mirah was present, he reverted to his old habits, chatting with her about their former escapades and companions, recounting quirks and stories, and plotting scenes from the plays he once adapted, convinced he could summon the vitality of his prime at will. This was a mortal torment for Ezra; when Mirah was home, she endeavored to alleviate his suffering by escorting her father downstairs to the parlour and keeping vigil over him there. What duty arises from a single, arduous resolve? Its difficulty lies in the daily, unflinching endurance of consequences that stain the dawn with the prospect of irritation to be swallowed or shame to be borne. And such consequences were being carried by these, as by many other heroic children of an unworthy parent, with the hope, at least for Mirah, that they would stretch onward through the solid fabric of life.

In the meantime, Lapidoth's presence erected a new, intangible barrier between Deronda and Mirah. Each dreaded the contaminating inferences of the other's mind, each misinterpreting the heightened reserve and diffidence of the partner. But clarity did not take long to arrive for Deronda.

As soon as he could, following his brief excursion to the Abbey, Deronda visited Hans Meyrick's rooms. Feeling it a debt of friendship that Hans should immediately understand the reasons for his recent journey and the shifts in intention it had wrought, he arrived to find Hans absent. He was reportedly in the countryside for a few days. Leaving a note, Deronda waited a week, half-expecting a reply. Receiving none, and fearing some capricious mood in the notoriously volatile Hans—whose planned stay at the Abbey he knew had been postponed—Deronda made a second call. He was admitted to the painting-room, where he found his friend in a light coat, sans waistcoat, his long hair still damp from a bath, yet with a face that looked worn and wizened, bearing nothing of the rustic charm one might expect. Hans had picked up his palette and brushes, standing before his easel when Deronda entered, but the entire setup seemed arranged on short notice.

As they clasped hands, Deronda remarked, "You don't look much as if you'd been in the country, old fellow. Was it Cambridge?"

"No," Hans replied curtly, dropping his palette with the air of someone who has accidentally started a charade. Pushing forward a chair for Deronda, he slumped into another, leaning back with his hands behind his head. "I've been to 'I-don't-know-where'—No Man's Land—and a mortally unpleasant country it is."

"You don't mean to say you've been drinking, Hans," Deronda said, seating himself opposite with an anxious scrutiny.

"Nothing so benign. I've been smoking opium. I always intended to try it sometime, to measure how much bliss could be extracted from it; finding myself lately rather devoid of other pleasures, I judged it prudent to seize the chance. But I pledge you my word I shall never touch a cask of that bliss again. It disagrees with my constitution."

"What has been the matter? You were in high spirits when you wrote to me."

"Oh, nothing specific. The world began to look seedy—a sort of cabbage garden with all the heads cut down. A malady of genius, you may be sure," Hans said, crinkling his face into a smile. "And, in fact, I was weary of being virtuous without reward, especially in this stifling London heat."

"Nothing else? No genuine vexation?" Deronda pressed.

Hans shook his head.

"I came to tell you of my own affairs, but I cannot do so with a good grace if you intend to conceal yours."

"Haven't an affair in the world," Hans said flippantly, "except a quarrel with a bric-à-brac man. Besides, since it is the first time in our lives you've spoken to me about your own business, you're only beginning to repay a rather lengthy debt."

Deronda sensed Hans was acting artificially, yet he trusted that the old frankness would return if he offered his own confidence.

"You laughed at the mystery of my journey to Italy, Hans," Deronda began. "It was for an object that touched my happiness at its very roots. I knew nothing of my parents, and I truly went to Genoa to meet my mother. My father died long ago—when I was an infant. My mother was the daughter of an eminent Jew; my father was her cousin. Many things had led me to suspect this origin as almost probable before I departed. I was so prepared for the outcome that I was glad of it—glad to discover myself a Jew."

"You must not expect me to appear surprised, Deronda," Hans said, shifting

"All thoughts, all passions, all delights, Whatever stirs this mortal frame, All are but ministers of Love, And feed his sacred flame." —COLERIDGE.

Deronda's eagerness to confess his love burned with a ferocity that Hans's assurance regarding Mirah's need for relief from jealousy had kindled. He returned to Ezra with a singular resolve: to demand, nay, to seize the opportunity for a private audience with her. Should she accept his devotion, he felt a surge of courage that transcended mere romance; as her betrothed, he would wield a protective authority, a shield against the inevitable storms brewing within her household. Though he detected no visible tremors of restlessness in Lapidoth, nor any waning ambition in the father's attempts to court favor, Deronda harbored a gnawing dread. He foresaw a coming struggle, a mortification, or a crescendo of domestic discord that would render Ezra and Mirah helpless victims unless he intervened. These forebodings would have been sharper had he known the tempest raging in Lapidoth's mind. The same restlessness that tormented Ezra was to Lapidoth a suffocating submission to restraint, endured only because he viewed it as a temporary cage leading to a future, well-conditioned freedom.

Lapidoth had intended to wait for a providential opening, perhaps a chance to extract a substantial sum from Deronda, yet his curiosity had already begun to probe the architecture of his daughter's security. He searched frantically for the location of Mirah's funds and her keys. The imperious gambling instinct that animated every fiber of his being, weaving a continuous net of imagination around his existence, demanded release. Yet Mirah, armed with a practical clarity that bordered on the prophetic, had safeguarded all her assets except immediate necessities in Mrs. Meyrick's keeping. To Lapidoth, this created an irritating completeness of supply, akin to confinement in a lunatic asylum where every avenue of escape was meticulously blocked. In his mind, prying open a desk or drawer to pocket a banknote was not theft but a domestic appropriation, a liberty men often claim with others' property long before the law intervenes—a reason why stolen silverware is a safer investment than speculative mining shares. He chafed under his daughter's guardianship, convinced he deserved access to her earnings as readily as he had to her apple tarts. Yet, despite his simmering resentment, he remained outwardly submissive. The temptation that truly gnawed at him was not direct confrontation with Mirah, but a calculated appeal to Deronda. Those clever souls who possess nothing else to sell often command a high price for their absence. Lapidoth's desperate search for leverage birthed the notion that his family would thrive without him, and that Deronda, eager to be rid of such a burden, would advance a fortune to facilitate his departure.

However, even hardened by experience, Lapidoth retained a profound, almost superstitious awe of Ezra's formidable friend. Thus, he deferred his scheme indefinitely. On this particular day, Deronda arrived buoyed by a gladdened consciousness that radiated from his very posture and speech. Lapidoth, trapped in a crisis of discontent and longing, found his mind cluttered with fantasies of liberation, and Deronda's newfound amiability fed these delusions. This obsession became so consuming that it disrupted his usual performance of interest; he sat listlessly beside them, unable to follow the reading, until finally rising to smoke and pace the square. The atmosphere between the two friends lightened. Mirah was absent, but Deronda knew she would return before his departure, and his eyes flared with a secret anticipation. He imagined that upon seeing her again, he would witness a sweetness of recognition previously denied to him. A playful tenderness softened his demeanor toward Ezra.

"This little room is too close for you, Ezra," Deronda declared, breaking off his reading. "The weekly heat here surpasses that of Genoa, where one retreats into the shaded coolness of vast halls. You deserve a better home now. As the stronger half, I shall dictate our arrangements." He smiled at Ezra, who replied, "I am straitened for nothing but breath. You, who might dwell in a spacious palace surrounded by verdant fields, find this a narrow prison. Yet, I cannot say 'Go.'"

"Oh, the country would be a banishment while you remain here," Deronda countered, rising to traverse the cramped double room, fanning his handkerchief vigorously. "This is the happiest room in the world to me. Moreover, I shall imagine myself in the East, preparing for my eventual journey. Only, I shall not don a cravat or a heavy ring there," he concluded emphatically, pausing to strip off these accoutrements and place them on a small table behind Ezra, whose surface was cluttered with books and papers. "I have worn my memorable ring since returning home," he continued, resettling himself. "But I am such a Sybarite that I constantly cast it off as a burden during any task. I comprehend why the Romans possessed summer rings—if they did. Now, I shall function far better."

Soon, they immersed themselves in work. Deronda read passages of rabbinical Hebrew under Ezra's correction and commentary, ignoring the subtle intrusion when Lapidoth re-entered and settled into the background. His wandering gaze swiftly locked onto the ring sparkling on the dark mahogany. During his walk, his mind had rehearsed a fiction of an advantageous overseas opportunity requiring immediate capital. He envisioned presenting this need privately to Deronda, prompting a query about the necessary sum. This calculation was the most precarious part of his plan; asking too much risked rejection, while asking too little yielded insufficient reward. His own desire knew no bounds, and he lacked any metric for Deronda's generosity.

Then, amidst these airy preparations for a receipt that remained indefinite, the ring, now familiar to Lapidoth's envious eye through Deronda's ownership, suddenly appeared detached and within easy reach. Its value was certainly less than the smallest imaginary sum his mind fluctuated between, yet presented as a solid fact, his desire leaped instantly to a nascent thought—not yet an intention, but a seductive possibility: if he quietly pocketed the ring and walked away, he would secure the means for comfortable escape from current restraint, without trouble and without danger. Any property of Deronda's, accessible without formal consent, belonged to his children's estate; since their father would never prosecute them for taking it, the act was legally moot. These thoughts cascaded rapidly, coalescing into a single, vivid image. Lapidoth had never committed larceny, yet larceny was simply appropriation punished by law. Taking this ring from a virtual relative, who would otherwise offer a much heavier gift, did not constitute larceny. Still, the heavier gift was preferable if he could hasten the request. The internal melody of imagining the theft repeated, only to sink into a rejected idea. He satisfied his urgent craving by resolving to descend, waiting for the moment of Deronda's departure to invite him for a walk and execute his plan.

He rose to stare out the window, yet his vision remained fixed on the brief passage separating him from the door near the table where the ring lay. Resolved to go down, yet dominated by a thirst-like desire, he passed the table. His fingers fell noiselessly upon the ring, and he found himself in the passage clutching it. Consequently, he donned his hat and departed. The possibility of throwing himself on his children receded into an indefinite distance. By the time he reached the square, his sense of haste had crystallized into a singular mission: sell the ring and board a ship. Deronda and Ezra registered his exit; that was all.

Later, Mirah entered, causing a genuine disruption. She wore her hat, and when Deronda rose to greet her, she spoke in a confusion that troubled her deeply. "I only came in to ensure Ezra received his new draught. I must go directly to Mrs. Meyrick's to fetch something."

"Pray allow me to walk with you," Deronda urged. "I must not tire Ezra further; besides, my brains are melting. I intend to go to Mrs. Meyrick's: may I accompany you?"

"Oh, yes," Mirah replied, blushing intensely, sensing something new and electric in Deronda, before turning to pour Ezra's medicine. Meanwhile, Ezra threw back his head, eyes closed, unable to dislodge the swirling ideas occupying his mind during the reading session. For a moment, Deronda stood lost in thought of the walk alone, until Mirah returned with the draught. Suddenly remembering he had discarded his cravat, he exclaimed, "Pray excuse my dishabille—I did not mean you to see it"—and moved to retrieve it. Then, with a violent impulse of surprise, "Good heavens, where is my ring gone?" he began scouring the floor.

Ezra glanced around the corner of his chair. Mirah, swift as thought, approached where Deronda searched and asked, "Did you lay it down?"

"Yes," Deronda replied, unvisited by any explanation other than the ring having fallen into shadow, hidden beneath the variegated carpet. He shifted furniture and searched every conceivable and inconceivable spot with frantic hands and eyes. Another explanation, however, had struck Mirah, draining the color from her cheeks. She leaned to Ezra's ear and whispered, "Was my father here?"

He nodded, meeting her eyes with a terrible, shared understanding. She darted back to Deronda, who continued his hopeless exploration. "You have not found it?" she asked hurriedly. Meeting her frightened gaze, Deronda immediately sensed alarm and answered, "I perhaps put it in

The wind swept across the lawns of Diplow, carrying with it the crisp, autumnal breath of a season that had finally settled into its proper rhythm. Sir Hugo, a man whose lineage was as sturdy as his liberal convictions, had brought a peculiar warmth to the neighborhood. From the stately heights of Brackenshaw and Quetcham down to the modest parlors of Wanchester, his presence acted as a balm. He was a creature of habit and affection, believing that social order, properly fenced and adorned with hereditary rank, was the bedrock of national felicity. Thus, he transformed Diplow into a theater of polite engagement, carefully curating his guest list to ensure that the solicitor Robinson did not feel diminished by the company of village curates, nor that the curates felt excluded from the grandeur of the legal profession. It was a delicate balancing act, executed with the ease of a conductor tuning an orchestra, ensuring that every instrument played in harmony with the others.

For Mr. Gascoigne, the rector of Pennicote, the atmosphere at Diplow had shifted dramatically from the icy hauteur of Grandcourt's reign to a genial, if slightly patronizing, camaraderie. This shift was not born of genuine affection for the clergyman, but of a calculated chivalry directed toward Mrs. Grandcourt. Sir Hugo's devotion to her was a private mystery, a secret he guarded with the ferocity of a sentinel protecting a sacred relic, refusing even to whisper its contours to his own wife, Lady Mallinger. Yet, the baronet's internal logic remained unassailable in his own mind: he could not share a woman's deepest secrets with another woman, regardless of marital bonds.

In the quiet chambers of his study, Daniel Deronda composed a letter to Sir Hugo, a document that weighed heavily in his hands. Having pledged himself to Mirah, he felt compelled to articulate the full scope of his destiny, anticipating the storm that such news might unleash. He preferred the written word to spoken confrontation; the page offered a buffer against the rash impulses of speech, allowing him to frame his truth without the risk of inflicting immediate, irreversible pain. He feared the collision of voices that turns a grievable sentiment into a lasting offense.

When Sir Hugo received the missive, his composure fractured. His instinct was to deflect the blow onto Lady Mallinger, hoping her theatrical astonishment would absorb the shock of his disappointment. When she expressed her distress—that Daniel's prodigious talents and the comfort of his presence should dissolve into a frantic obsession with Jewish politics—he met her concern with a dismissive wave. "Oh, nonsense, my dear!" he declared, smoothing his cravat. "Dan will not make a fool of himself. He holds large notions about Judaism, political views you cannot fathom. Rest assured, he will keep his head above water."

Yet, regarding the impending marriage, Lady Mallinger offered no shield. With a gentle candor devoid of malice, she admitted that she had scarcely imagined the turn of events when Mirah sang at her parties or tutored Amabel. She confessed, with a hesitation that betrayed her surprise, that she had briefly entertained the notion that Daniel might wed Mrs. Grandcourt—a union that seemed plausible given his presence in Genoa at that precise moment. Though she harbored no fondness for widows, she conceded that such a match might have been preferable to Daniel's total withdrawal into the Jewish cause. Sir Hugo, sharing this sentiment, found himself unable to correct her as a mere feminine error. His displeasure at the refutation of his theories regarding Gwendolen's fate festered silently, a suppressed bitterness that he could not voice. He instructed Lady Mallinger to remain silent until further notice, reasoning that if the news struck Gwendolen as a cruel blow, the delay would soften its impact, sparing her fragile nerves. Better, he decided, that she learn the truth directly from Daniel himself.

While Sir Hugo continued his paternal attentions to Gwendolen at Diplow, supported willingly by Lady Mallinger despite her reservations about widows, the landscape of Offendene had already changed. Gwendolen had moved there, maintaining a serene calm that exceeded her mother's expectations. She inhabited a state of peaceful melancholy, a condition born of renouncing the desperate demands of the self and embracing the ordinary gifts of existence—even the kindness of a stray dog—as a blessing beyond measure. Is one who has nearly perished in a pit of darkness entitled to complain of the fresh air and the returning dawn? Life, viewed through this lens, becomes an escape; the quiet cycle of morning and evening, and particularly those rare flashes of pure empathy that pierce the inner gloom, serve as salvations that reconcile the soul to its hardships.

Gwendolen relived the terrifying chronicle of her temptations, tracing the arc from the initial illusion of self-gratification, where she had fled the grasp of conscience, to the later, more violent urge of hatred dragging her toward its fulfillment. Now, she dwelled on every word Daniel had spoken regarding her deliverance from the worst evils within herself and the damage inflicted upon others. These words became anchors, resisting the tide of self-despair. She was buoyed by the anticipation of his return, imagining him ever within reach. Her need for him blinded her to the distinct boundaries of his separate life; she filled the entirety of his existence with her own relationship to him, a passionate egoism shared by many who project their needs onto both humanity and the divine. She envisioned a future where she would constantly mold herself to a standard he held before her. Had he not first appeared as a corrective presence, initially resented but eventually loved and trusted? She could not conceive of an end to this reliance, which had become the solid ground beneath her feet, the only condition allowing her to walk.

Soon, Daniel arrived at Diplow, a journey shorter than the trip to the Abbey. He had intended to escort Ezra and Mirah to a coastal haven while securing a new home for Mirah as his bride, a sanctuary where they could jointly watch over her brother. But Ezra, weary of displacement, refused to leave unless it meant traveling eastward with them. The external pressures weighing on Ezra grew heavier, yet his mind soared with a visionary joy at the prospect of the voyage. Meanwhile, Daniel pressed forward with preparations for his wedding, hoping to conclude matters within two months. He sought a full accounting of his resources and affairs from Sir Hugo, providing a practical excuse for his visit. Yet, a deeper motive drove him: his promise to Gwendolen. A strange paradox gnawed at his heart; the beloved lover is traditionally hailed as the epitome of happiness, a state defined by indifference to external sorrow. But human experience often defies such neat categorizations. It was no betrayal of Mirah, but rather a testament to the richness of his nature, that his joy in her could coexist with a tender vigilance for another. Love, in its fullest form, is an enfolding of immeasurable cares that surpass any solitary joy.

During his visits, Daniel saw Gwendolen twice, yet returned to London without revealing the magnitude of his transformation. He chastised himself for this omission. In communications of such weight, one feels paralyzed by the need for preparation, waiting for the other's mood to align with the potential devastation of the news. In their first meeting, Gwendolen was consumed by her own inquiries—how to arrange her life, how to eradicate her ignorance, how to be kinder to all and atone for her selfishness. So absorbed was she in her own redemption that Daniel recoiled from interrupting her quest, fearing to wound her in a moment when she leaned entirely on him for support. In their second encounter, driven by a new resolve to steer the conversation, he found her submerged in a deep depression, haunted by memories that felt more tangible than any future plans. Overwhelmed by misery, she cried hysterically, accusing him of inevitable disdain. He could offer only soothing words, and when she gradually lifted her head, her eyes still beaded with tears, bearing that pathetic look of renewed, childlike curiosity, it became impossible to burden her further.

Time marched on, rendering the disclosure an imperative duty. Gwendolen, unaware of his affairs, had never questioned his absence in Genoa. This very ignorance would make the sudden revelation of facts determining his life's course a devastating blow. If he delayed the truth, letting others reveal it, he would compound his cruelty with inconsiderateness. Writing was impossible; the thought of her reading his virtual farewell alone, perhaps interpreting his words as cold triumph and indifference, broke his heart. He descended upon Diplow again, determined to endure whatever peril awaited rather than leave her in the dark.

On this third visit, Daniel encountered Hans Meyrick, who had installed his easel at Diplow, attempting to capture the three daughters on a bank in the manner of Gainsborough. Hans varied his artistic pursuits by wandering to Pennicote to sketch the village children, thereby improving his familiarity with the Gascoigne family. Hans seemed to have regained his vivacity, yet Daniel detected a layer of artifice in it, akin to the unnatural flush of a lady's complexion sustained by too much rouge. Despite his gratitude and admiration for Daniel, Hans harbored a subtle irritation. Such feelings are common in open natures when a friend's reserve breaks to reveal a reality not merely unexpected, but contrary to hopeful conjecture. Hans, who had eagerly poured out his own heart regarding Mirah, found it painful that Daniel had kept a rivalrous feeling hidden, only for it to surface as the decisive factor in his life. Friends often prove more complex than anticipated, and Hans, though excusable for his bruised sensibilities, had conducted himself honorably. As he told himself, "his metal had given a better ring than he would have sworn to beforehand

In the mosaic of human existence, the seasons do not march in rigid succession but mingle as they did in a mythical golden age: fruit hangs beside blossom, and in the same breath, the sickle cuts the grain while seeds are scattered for future growth. One hand tends the green vine, another crushes the grape; harvest and spring are eternally fused within the pulse of a single life, until the Great Gatherer calls us home to sow us anew in His unseen fields. Among the myriad gifts of love, none surpasses the exquisite privilege of binding another's destiny to our own—to stand as guardian over their joy, to weave comfort into the fabric of their hardship, and to turn the bitter wells of past suffering into springs of radiant delight. Deronda's love for Mirah burned with this sacred protectiveness. Though she had begun her journey with infantine steps upon a path strewn with thorns, the first sight of her face had struck him as the pale, girlish visage of utter despair. Now, however, she glowed like a flower of delicate ivory tipped with dark petals, bathed in the warm sunlight of contentment. She viewed every potential grief as merely a shadow cast by the immense light of her union with Deronda, a bond she could name only *good*. He watched the sober gladness that animated her movements, the habitual poise of her repose, with a delight so profound he whispered to himself that saving her from pain was sufficient recompense for his own personal joy.

Mirah remained ignorant of Hans's internal struggle or the sharp pang Gwendolen suffered; having accepted the truth that Deronda's hidden heart belonged solely to her, she interpreted Gwendolen's anxious concern as a natural expression of gratitude and dependent affection, a sentiment she herself had once known. Every word Deronda spoke regarding Mrs. Grandcourt reinforced this comforting narrative, though he carefully avoided direct confrontation, speaking only in the most distant terms. Mirah was prepared to believe he had been a rescuing angel to countless souls, and the only marvel was that she alone possessed the supreme bliss of walking constantly by his side. Thus, when the bridal veil descended upon Mirah, it concealed no trembling doubt, only a thrilling awe at receiving a magnificent gift that demanded equally magnificent stewardship. No more beautiful pair ever stood beneath a velvet canopy, nor did any couple deserve the prayers of their people for progeny more truly. Their lips, sealed with unerring honesty, touched the sacramental wine; their mutual pledge gathered a promise of fulfillment rooted in absolute integrity. Naturally, they wed according to the ancient Jewish rite. Since no faith demands that a feast exclude all but the highest ranks of acquaintance, few will likely take offense to learn that the entire Cohen family attended Deronda's modest nuptials, save for the infant daughter, who continued her intelligent battle with teething comforts at home. How could Mordecai bear the thought of shutting out those who had shared his adversity from the joy of his triumph? Mrs. Meyrick, understanding this perfectly, had fully reconciled herself to meeting the Jewish pawnbroker, arriving with her three daughters. Each woman basked in the consciousness that Mirah's marriage to Deronda crowned a romance destined to remain a sweet memory for them all. Which of them—mother or daughters—had not played a generous role, giving their best feelings and actions to the girl who needed them? If Hans had been present, it would have been preferable, but Mab had already noted that men must sometimes suffer for their inconvenience; surely, if she, Kate, and Amy had all fallen in love with Mr. Deronda, they would not be so ridiculous as men, for they were women. The Meyricks were rewarded for conquering their prejudices by hearing a speech from Mr. Cohen, possessing a rare quality among public addresses: it defied the usual mold. Jacob ate beyond his years, contributing several small, whinnying laughs as a free accompaniment to his father's words—not irreverently, but from a lively sense that his family was distinguishing itself. Meanwhile, Adelaide Rebekah, clad in a new Sabbath frock, maintained a grave air of solemn responsibility throughout. Mordecai's brilliant eyes, sunken deep in their large sockets, surveyed the scene with the cherishing benignity of a spirit already lifted into an aloofness that nullified only selfish requirements, leaving pure sympathy alive. Yet, continually, after his gaze traveled over the others, it returned to dwell on Deronda with a fresh gleam of trusting affection.

The wedding feast was humble, yet Mirah was not without splendid gifts. Upon the news of the betrothal, friends devised graceful tributes. Sir Hugo and Lady Mallinger provided a complete equipment for Eastern travel, alongside a precious locket inscribed: *"To the bride of our dear Daniel Deronda all blessings. H. and L. M."* The Klesmers sent a flawless watch, also bearing a tender inscription. But something far more precious than gold or gems arrived for Deronda from the neighborhood of Diplow on the morning of his marriage. It was a letter containing these words: *Do not think of me sorrowfully on your wedding-day. I have remembered your words—that I may live to be one of the best of women, who make others glad that they were born. I do not yet see how that can be, but you know better than I. If it ever comes true, it will be because you helped me. I only thought of myself, and I made you grieve. It hurts me now to think of your grief. You must not grieve any more for me. It is better—it shall be better with me because I have known you. GWENDOLEN GRANDCOURT.*

Preparations for the departure of the trio to the East commenced immediately, for Deronda could not deny Ezra's wish that they set sail forthwith, allowing him to accompany them rather than detain them to watch over him. Deronda held no belief that Ezra's life would endure the voyage, for symptoms indicated the final stage of his malady had set in. Yet Ezra himself had declared, "Never mind where I die, so that I am with you." He did not depart with them. One early morning, he told Deronda, "Do not quit me today. I shall die before it is ended." He chose to be dressed and sit upright in his easy chair as usual, with Deronda and Mirah on either side. For several hours, he remained unusually silent, making no effort to speak, yet looking at them occasionally with eyes full of restful meaning, as if to assure them that while this remnant of breathing time was difficult, he felt an ocean of peace beneath him. It was not until late afternoon, as the light began to fall, that he took hold of each of their hands, looking at Deronda. "Death is coming to me as the divine kiss which is both parting and reunion—which takes me from your bodily eyes and gives me full presence in your soul. Where thou goest, Daniel, I shall go. Is it not begun? Have I not breathed my soul into you? We shall live together." He paused, and Deronda waited, expecting further words. But slowly, with evident effort, Ezra pressed on their hands, raised himself, and uttered in Hebrew the confession of the Divine Unity, a prayer long spoken on the lips of the dying Israelite. He sank gently back into his chair and spoke no more. It was some hours before he ceased to breathe, enveloped in the arms of Mirah and Deronda. "Nothing is here for tears, nothing to wail Or knock the breast; no weakness, no contempt, Dispraise or blame; nothing but well and fair, And what may quiet us in a death so noble."
